# Paper 4 – Recent Advances in Urology: All Answers by Section

**Compiled from:** 2015–2025 actual Paper 4 (12 papers) and Practice Sets 1–6 Paper 4, including both the current and the earlier versions of Practice Sets 3–6 Paper 4 — 165 answers.
**Repeats merged:** questions asked more than once are printed once, with every wording and source listed under "Asked as"; their answers are combined so that no fact from any version is lost. 35 merged topics replace 91 original answers; 109 topics in all.
**Layout:** the 17 revision-book sections; in each, long answer questions (LAQ) first, then short answer questions (SAQ), then a Rapid Revision Sheet of that section's key points.

**Contents**

1. Robotics in urology (including robotic vs standard laparoscopy) *(merged: 4 questions)*

# Section 1 · Basic Principles — Long answer questions (LAQ)

## Robotics in urology (including robotic vs standard laparoscopy)
**Asked as:**
- Role of robotics in urology — *Summer 2015 Paper IV Q1 (LAQ)*
- Discuss Robot and its role in urology. — *Winter 2017 Paper IV Q1 (LAQ)*
- Robots in urology — *Practice Set 4 Paper IV Q2 (LAQ)*
- Robotic vs std lap surgeries — *Practice Set 3 Paper IV Q3b (SAQ)*

**Type:** LAQ (25 marks); also asked as SAQ (10 marks)  
(also answered in 2017-Winter Paper 4, Q1; 2015-Summer Paper 4, Q1)
(robotics also answered in 2017-Winter Paper 4, Q1 and 2015-Summer Paper 4, Q1)

### Introduction
- Surgical robots are **master–slave telemanipulators**: the surgeon controls every movement; the robot acts on nothing by itself.
- Robotic surgery is **robot-assisted laparoscopy** with a **master–slave telemanipulator** (surgeon console, patient-side cart, vision cart). Access and physiology are the same; the interface differs.
- The robot did not change the operations. It removed the barrier of laparoscopic suturing, so RARP (robot-assisted radical prostatectomy) spread where laparoscopic prostatectomy had stalled. RARP now dominates in the USA.

### History
- **PUMA 560 (1985)** first surgical robot; **PROBOT** first urological robot (TURP (transurethral resection of prostate)); "Mona" first human trial (1997).
- **da Vinci** FDA (Food and Drug Administration) approval and first RARP (**Binder and Kramer**) in **2000**; robotic adrenalectomy 1999; first paediatric robotic procedure **2002**; robotic RPLND (retroperitoneal lymph node dissection) 2006.

### da Vinci system
1. **Surgeon console**: 3D (three-dimensional) view magnified about 10 times, motion scaling, tremor filtration.
2. **Patient-side cart**: **EndoWrist** instruments with **7 DOF (degrees of freedom)**; most have a 10-use limit; ports 8 mm, 8–10 cm apart to avoid arm collisions.
3. **Vision cart**: light source, camera electronics, energy generator.

| Generation | Added feature |
|---|---|
| Standard (2000) | Three arms |
| S (2006) / Si (2009) | High definition, fourth arm / dual console for training |
| Xi (2014) | 8-mm camera in any port, finer arms, side docking (docks from either side), no stirrups |
| SP (2018) | All instruments through one 2.5-cm cannula |
| da Vinci 5 (2024) | Force feedback |

- Patents expired in 2019 (after patent expiry), so other platforms appeared (e.g. **Hugo, Versius** and the low-cost Indian **SSI Mantra**).
- **Also know:** ports 8–10 cm apart to avoid arm collisions; Xi docks from any side; retroperitoneal cases dock over the head.

### Robotic vs standard laparoscopy: comparison
| Feature | Standard laparoscopy | Robotic |
|---|---|---|
| Vision | Mostly 2D; assistant holds camera | **3D**, 10× magnified, surgeon controls camera |
| Instruments | Rigid, **4 DOF (degrees of freedom)**; fulcrum effect reverses movement | **EndoWrist, 7 DOF**, 90° articulation; intuitive movement |
| Precision | Tremor transmitted | **Tremor filtration, motion scaling** |
| Ergonomics | Standing, awkward | Seated, less fatigue |
| Suturing | Hard; steep learning curve | Easy; shorter learning curve |
| Haptic feedback | Present (limited) | Absent (force feedback only in newest system) |
| Port placement | Flexible | Ports 8–10 cm apart; docking time |
| Bedside | Surgeon at patient | Surgeon remote; emergency undocking delays |
| Cost | Low, reusable | High capital; instruments limited to about 10 uses |
| Failure | — | Robot failure about 0.4% |

### Advantages and disadvantages
| Advantages | Disadvantages |
|---|---|
| 3D vision, wristed instruments, easy suturing | High capital and instrument cost; cost-effective only at high volume |
| Tremor filtration, seated ergonomics | Limited haptic feedback |
| Less blood loss and shorter stay than open; fewer conversions than laparoscopy | Docking time, bulk, surgeon away from patient |

### Applications (with evidence)
**1. RARP**
- **Yaxley (Lancet 2016)**, RCT (randomised controlled trial): continence and potency **equal to open at 12 weeks**, with less blood loss and shorter stay; **Coughlin 2018**: equal at **24 months** on follow-up.
- Positive margins equal; the surgeon, not the platform, is the main variable. Bladder neck stricture under 2% vs up to 16% open.
- **Retzius-sparing** and **"hood"** techniques preserve continence structures and give earlier continence; ERAS (enhanced recovery after surgery) allows 23-hour or same-day discharge. Robotic surgery also widens candidacy (prior mesh, prior pelvic surgery) and allows salvage surgery.
- Versus laparoscopy: laparoscopic prostatectomy stalled on the vesicourethral anastomosis; the robot made it routine and dominant. A small RCT (randomised controlled trial; Porpiglia, 2013) favoured robotic continence and potency.

**2. RAPN (robot-assisted partial nephrectomy)**
- Versus laparoscopy (robotic vs laparoscopic gives): **fewer conversions**, **shorter warm ischaemia**, less (smaller) eGFR (estimated glomerular filtration rate) loss/fall and shorter stay; complications and margins similar. So more complex and hilar tumours are done minimally invasively.
- **Trifecta** = negative margin, no major complication within 30 days, warm ischaemia ≤25 minutes.
- ICG (indocyanine green) guides selective clamping.

**3. RARC (robot-assisted radical cystectomy)**
- **RAZOR (Lancet 2018)**: **non-inferior** 2-year PFS (progression-free survival) (72.3% vs 71.6%), with less blood loss and fewer transfusions (less transfusion).
- **iROC**: robotic cystectomy with **intracorporeal** diversion gave **more days alive and out of hospital at 90 days**, fewer VTE (venous thromboembolism) and wound complications. Continent diversion fell (11% in iROC).

**4. Other procedures**
| Procedure | Role of the robot / why the robot helps |
|---|---|
| Pyeloplasty, ureteric reimplantation | Easy intracorporeal suturing; also in children and infants (small working space: about 1 L vs 6 L in adults); similar success to laparoscopy; easier suturing |
| BMG (buccal mucosa graft) ureteroplasty | Long proximal or mid-ureteric strictures |
| RPLND (retroperitoneal lymph node dissection) | Template, nerve-sparing; short stay |
| Adrenalectomy, nephroureterectomy | Equivalent to laparoscopy; adrenalectomy suits the retroperitoneal approach |
| Simple prostatectomy | Large glands; less bleeding than open |
| Sacrocolpopexy, VVF (vesicovaginal fistula) / fistula repair | Deep pelvic suturing |
| **RAKT (robot-assisted kidney transplantation)** | Fewer wound complications in obese recipients |

- **Radical nephrectomy, adrenalectomy:** no outcome advantage over laparoscopy; robot only adds cost.
- Outcomes depend more on the **surgeon** than on the platform.

### Choosing robot vs laparoscopy
- Robot: complex excision plus reconstruction. Laparoscopy: extirpation, low-resource settings.

### Paediatric robotics
- Reconstruction (pyeloplasty, reimplantation, appendicovesicostomy) is where paediatric robotics has grown. Lower insufflation pressure; "burp" ports to enlarge the working space.

### Physiology and positioning
- **CO₂ pneumoperitoneum** (12–15 mm Hg, lower in children; another source: work at 10–12 mm Hg) causes hypercarbia, acidosis, reduced venous return and oliguria.
- **Steep Trendelenburg** for pelvic surgery increases venous return and cardiac output, and raises intracranial and intraocular pressure.
- The only strict contraindication to minimally invasive surgery is inability to tolerate pneumoperitoneum.

### Complications specific to robotics
| Problem | Details |
|---|---|
| **Steep Trendelenburg (~22–25°)** | Facial and laryngeal oedema, raised IOP (intraocular pressure), corneal abrasion |
| Nerve injury (up to ~6%) | Brachial plexus (avoid shoulder braces); femoral, peroneal |
| **Compartment syndrome, rhabdomyolysis** | Long lithotomy or flank position; fasciotomy if >30 mmHg with symptoms |
| Thermal bowel injury / bowel burns | Insulation failure, capacitive coupling; presents late |
| Robot malfunction | About 0.4% (another source: <1%); half found before the patient enters; rarely forces conversion |
| Port-site hernia | Close larger port sites; transverse incisions cut it from 5.4% to 0.4% after RARP |

- **Prevention**: secure inaccessible arms and lines before tilting, pad pressure points, limit tilt and lithotomy time, protect the eyes.

### Training and credentialing
- Box trainer, **VR (virtual reality)** simulator (simulators), animal and cadaver labs, then proctored cases (proctoring). **FRS (Fundamentals of Robotic Surgery)** curriculum improved skills in an RCT. Five-day mini-residencies also help. Dual console and simulators for training. Proficiency comes after about 40 cases, but outcomes keep improving (outcomes improve) with volume.

### Recent advances
- Single-port transvesical and extraperitoneal surgery; da Vinci SP (single port); force feedback (da Vinci 5); **AI (artificial intelligence)** video-based skill assessment; augmented reality 3D overlay; **telesurgery** (limited by latency; latency limits it).
- New platforms (Hugo RAS, Versius, Indian SSI Mantra) may cut costs.
- **Also know:** ERAS (enhanced recovery after surgery) with same-day RARP discharge; ICG (indocyanine green) fluorescence for selective clamping and ureteric perfusion.

**Diagram to draw:** console, vision cart and patient cart; RARP port placement; EndoWrist (7 DOF) beside a rigid instrument (4 DOF).

> **Key points to remember:**
> - da Vinci 2000; Xi 2014; SP 2018; patents expired 2019; 7 degrees of freedom.
> - Yaxley and Coughlin: RARP equal function to open at 12 weeks and 24 months; RAZOR non-inferior; iROC more days out of hospital.
> - RAPN trifecta: negative margin, no major complication, warm ischaemia ≤25 min.
> - Trendelenburg causes oedema and raised IOP; avoid shoulder braces.
> - Robot: 3D, 7 DOF, tremor filtration, but no haptics and high cost.
> - Robotic partial nephrectomy: shorter warm ischaemia, fewer conversions.
> - Robot made prostatectomy routine; no benefit for simple extirpation.
> - Surgeon matters more than platform.

## LASERs in urology: applications and newer generation laser technologies (new LASER for stone treatment)
**Asked as:**
- Describe applications of various LASERs in urology. — *Summer 2017 Paper IV Q2 (LAQ)*
- Newer generation laser technologies. — *Winter 2024 Paper IV Q3c (SAQ)*
- New LASER for stone Treatment — *Winter 2025 Paper IV Q3b (SAQ)* (also answered in 2024-Winter Paper 4, Q3c)
- Newer generation laser technologies. — *Practice Set 3 Paper IV Q3e (earlier version) (SAQ)* (also answered in 2024-Winter Paper 4, Q3c; stone lasers in 2025-Winter Paper 4, Q3b)
- New LASER for stone Treatment — *Practice Set 3 Paper IV Q3f (earlier version) (SAQ)* (See Practice Set 3 Paper 4, Q3e (full answer).)

**Type:** LAQ (25 marks); also asked as SAQ (10 marks)

### Principle
- LASER (light amplification by stimulated emission of radiation): a "pumped" gain medium releases identical photons. A mirror resonator amplifies them, and a partial mirror lets the beam out.
- Light is **monochromatic, coherent and collimated**, so it passes down thin fibres.
- **Chromophore** decides the effect. Water absorbs holmium and thulium light. Haemoglobin absorbs KTP (potassium titanyl phosphate) light.
- Absorption sets **penetration**. Shallow penetration vaporises and cuts. Deep penetration coagulates and later sloughs.
- Coagulation occurs at about 60 °C; vaporisation at ≥100 °C.
- **Pulse duration** decides the stone mechanism. Short pulses (<10 µs) act photomechanically (shock waves). Long pulses (>250 µs: Ho:YAG [holmium:yttrium-aluminium-garnet], TFL [thulium fibre laser]) act **photothermally**.

### Lasers used
| Laser | Wavelength (nm) | Chromophore | Penetration | Main uses |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Ho:YAG | 2120 (Ch 19 quotes 2100; often quoted as 2100 nm) | Water | 0.3–0.4 mm (other sources: 0.4 mm; 0.31 mm) | Stones, HoLEP, incisions, tumours |
| TFL | 1940 | Water (peak) | <0.1 mm (other sources: about 0.2 mm; 0.08 mm) | Dusting, enucleation |
| Tm:YAG | ~2010 (other sources: 2013) | Water | 0.2 mm | Vaporesection, enucleation |
| KTP/LBO | 532 | Haemoglobin | 0.8 mm | PVP |
| Diode | 940–1470 | Water + haemoglobin | 0.5–5 mm | Prostate vaporisation |
| Nd:YAG | 1064 | Neither strongly | Up to 10 mm | Coagulation of tumours |
| CO₂ | 10,600 | Water | ~0.1 mm | Skin and penile lesions |

### 1. Stones
- **Ho:YAG** is the gold standard (standard for stones and enucleation). It breaks all stone types. Used in URS (ureteroscopy), RIRS (retrograde intrarenal surgery), PCNL (percutaneous nephrolithotomy) and for bladder stones. It is water-absorbed, pulsed and **photothermal**: long pulses heat the stone until it decomposes.
- **Moses effect:** the start of each pulse makes a vapour channel; the rest crosses it to the stone. So keep the fibre close and perpendicular.
- Fragmenting: 0.6–1.0 J × 6–10 Hz. Dusting: 0.2–0.3 J × 40–80 Hz.
- **Retropulsion** rises with pulse energy (not frequency) and falls with longer pulses or pulse modulation.
- **TFL:** higher water absorption and low peak power give finer dust and less retropulsion. It beat low-power holmium in RCTs (randomised controlled trials) and equalled 120 W pulse-modulated holmium.
- **Heat** depends on power, not laser type. Keep ≤10 W in the ureter; use good flow and an access sheath.

### 2. BPH (benign prostatic hyperplasia)
- **HoLEP (holmium laser enucleation of prostate):**
  - Endoscopic equivalent of open prostatectomy, **size-independent**. Enucleate at about 2 J × 40–50 Hz, then morcellate.
  - Only endoscopic procedure superior to TURP (transurethral resection of prostate). Less bleeding, shorter catheter time.
- **PVP (photoselective vaporisation, KTP/LBO 120–180 W):**
  - Haemoglobin absorption gives bloodless vaporisation, so anticoagulants can continue.
  - **GOLIATH RCT:** non-inferior to TURP. Avoid >80 cc (retreatment).
- **Thulium:** vaporisation, vaporesection, ThuLEP (equivalent to HoLEP). Diode vaporisation also used.
- Nd:YAG coagulation was abandoned (1 cm penetration, sloughing).
- AUA (American Urological Association): HoLEP, ThuLEP or PVP for **high bleeding risk**.

### 3. Incisions
- Laser urethrotomy has no advantage over cold knife.
- Holmium incision of bladder neck contracture, ureteric stricture, endopyelotomy, ureterocele and calyceal diverticulum neck.

### 4. Urothelial tumours
- **NMIBC (non-muscle-invasive bladder cancer):** laser **en bloc resection** (Ho, Tm, KTP) gives detrusor in the specimen and no obturator jerk. Office ablation of small low-grade recurrences.
- **UTUC (upper tract urothelial carcinoma):** biopsy, then ablate by ureteroscopy:
  - Ho:YAG: <0.5 mm, haemostatic.
  - Nd:YAG: 5–6 mm coagulation, so stricture risk.
  - Thulium: <0.2 mm.
- **PDT (photodynamic therapy):** a photosensitiser plus laser light makes singlet oxygen.

### 5. Penile and genital lesions
- PeIN (penile intraepithelial neoplasia) and small Ta/T1: **Nd:YAG preferred**, with base biopsies.
- CO₂ is too superficial for T1 (recurrence up to 50%). CO₂ or Nd:YAG for genital warts.

### Safety
- Wavelength-specific eyewear for all, including the patient. Door signage, covered windows, trained staff and a laser safety officer.
- Check fibres; never fire inside the scope. Guard against fire and thermal injury.
- **Heat follows power (total power), not laser type (applies to all):** ≤10 W in the ureter, ≤25 W in the kidney; use low power, pauses, high flow, cool irrigation and an **access sheath**; damage (urothelial injury) begins above about 43 °C.

### Recent advances: newer generation laser technologies
- Pulse modulation (Moses) and high-frequency holmium. TFL and thin fibres. Suction sheaths. AI (artificial intelligence) settings.

#### Background
- Newer lasers aim at **less retropulsion, finer dust, faster work** (Aims: less retropulsion, finer dust, faster work).

#### 1. High-power holmium (60–120 W; other sources: 100–120 W machines) with pulse modulation
- High frequency (100–120 W machines allow it) allows **dusting** (low energy, high frequency: 0.2–0.3 J × 40–80 Hz) and "popcorning"; long-pulse modes reduce retropulsion.

#### 2. Pulse modulation (Moses technology and similar)
- **Moses effect:** the start (first part) of a pulse boils a **vapour channel**; vapour absorbs less light, so the rest reaches the stone (target).
- **Moses technology** splits each pulse in two: the first opens the channel, the second hits the target (strikes the stone).
- Less retropulsion; may shorten **HoLEP (holmium laser enucleation of the prostate)**.

#### 3. TFL (thulium fibre laser), 1940 nm
- **Diode-pumped silica fibre** laser, at (near) the **water absorption peak** (higher absorption than holmium: about **4× holmium absorption**); penetration about 0.2 mm (other sources: **0.08 mm**).
- **Low peak power, long pulses, very high frequency (above 2000 Hz)** → finer (fine) dust, **minimal retropulsion**.
- **Thin fibres** (down to about 150 µm): more (better) irrigation and scope deflection.
- **Stones:** finer dust, minimal retropulsion. **RCTs (randomised controlled trials):** TFL beat **low-power (30 W) holmium** (stone-free rate, time); it **equalled 120 W pulse-modulated holmium** in stone-free rate (**equal** to 120 W pulse-modulated holmium).
- **Prostate:** ThuFLEP (thulium fibre laser enucleation).
- Compact and quiet.

#### 4. Thulium:YAG (2013 nm)
- Originally continuous wave (0.2 mm penetration, carbonisation a drawback): ThuVARP (vaporesection), ThuVEP (vapoenucleation), **ThuLEP (laser enucleation**, size-independent like HoLEP).
- **Pulsed Tm:YAG (thulium:YAG):** newer pulsed solid-state version of the continuous-wave thulium laser for photothermal lithotripsy and enucleation; it also does lithotripsy.

#### Comparison
| | Ho:YAG | TFL |
|---|---|---|
| Wavelength | 2120 nm | 1940 nm |
| Water absorption | High | About 4× higher |
| Penetration | 0.31 mm | 0.08 mm |
| Peak power | High | Low |
| Retropulsion | More | Minimal (Less) |
| Fragments | Small | Smaller |
| Fibre | ≥200 µm | ≥150 µm |

#### Principles of settings
- **Retropulsion rises with pulse energy**, not frequency; it falls with longer pulses.
- Keep the fibre close and perpendicular to the stone.
- TFL settings ≤40 Hz often enough.

#### Others
- **Also know:** KTP/LBO (potassium titanyl phosphate/lithium triborate) 532 nm vaporisation (180 W GreenLight (532 nm) vaporisation); diode lasers; suction sheaths; burst wave lithotripsy (investigational).

**Diagram to draw:** water and haemoglobin absorption curves marking 532, 1064, 1940 and 2120 nm (for newer lasers: water absorption curve marking 1940 and 2120 nm).

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Chromophore sets penetration: TFL shallowest, Ho 0.4 mm, Nd:YAG deepest.
> - Holmium lithotripsy is photothermal (Moses); TFL gives less retropulsion.
> - HoLEP size-independent, superior to TURP; PVP for anticoagulated men (GOLIATH).
> - Nd:YAG for PeIN; eyewear for everyone.
> - TFL 1940 nm: water peak, low peak power, minimal retropulsion.
> - Moses: two-part pulse through a vapour channel.
> - TFL beats low-power, equals high-power pulse-modulated holmium.
> - Heat depends on power: ≤10 W in the ureter, ≤25 W in the kidney.
>
> **Key points to remember (stone angle):**
> - TFL (1940 nm): fine dust, minimal retropulsion, thin fibres; settings ≤40 Hz often enough.
> - Pulse-modulated (Moses) high-power holmium: vapour channel, less retropulsion.
> - Safety: ≤10 W in the ureter, ≤25 W in the kidney; access sheath; damage above 43 °C.

## Flexible endourological instruments, digital and current ureteroscopes
**Asked as:**
- Briefly outline flexible endourological instrument and its role in management of urological disease — *Practice Set 1 Paper IV Q1 (LAQ)*
- Digital flexible ureteroscopes — *Summer 2019 Paper IV Q3c (SAQ)*
- Current ureter scopes. — *Summer 2022 Paper IV Q3d (SAQ)*

**Type:** LAQ (25 marks); also asked as SAQ (10 marks)

(flexible ureteroscopy in detail: see Practice Set 1 Paper 2, Q3b; digital scopes also in 2019-Summer Paper 4, Q3c)

*Summer 2022 Q3d taken as "current ureteroscopes"; see also 2019-Summer Paper 4, Q3c.*

### Introduction
- A flexible endoscope has a **deflectable tip** and carries its image by a fibre bundle or a tip-mounted chip. It follows the natural curves of the urethra, ureter and collecting system.
- Physical basis: light travels along a curved glass fibre by **total internal reflection** (Tyndall).
- Marshall reported the first flexible URS (ureteroscopy) in 1964. It became therapeutic only when a **working channel** and **active tip deflection** were added in the early 1980s.
- **Fibreoptic flexible URS (ureteroscopy):** coherent glass-fibre bundle carries the image; fibres break → black spots; honeycomb (moiré) pattern.
- **Digital scopes** (first 2006): a sensor at the tip (**"chip-on-tip"**) converts light to an electrical signal, so the image does not degrade.

### Classification of current ureteroscopes
| Type | Key features |
|---|---|
| **Rigid rod-lens** | Larger calibre; largely historical |
| **Semirigid** | **Fibreoptic** image bundle; tip **≤7 Fr**; channel >3 Fr; often **two channels**; in-line or offset eyepiece |
| **Flexible fibreoptic** | Tip 4.5–7.5 Fr (smallest); image degrades as fibres break (black dots) |
| **Flexible digital** | "Chip-on-tip" CMOS (complementary metal-oxide semiconductor) or CCD (charge-coupled device) sensor plus LED (light-emitting diode); no camera head |
| **Single-use flexible** | Digital; LithoVue (2016) first; now several makers |
| **Ureteral resectoscope** | 11.5 Fr; largely replaced by laser |

### Semirigid ureteroscope
- Uses **coherent fibre bundles** for the image (random fibres carry only light).
- **Two channels** are preferred: irrigation through one while an instrument is in the other, or a laser through one to break a stone held in a basket.
- **Offset eyepiece** gives a straight channel for rigid probes (pneumatic, biopsy).
- **Rule:** semirigid below the iliac vessels, flexible above.

### Components
| Component | Key features |
|---|---|
| Optics | **Fibreoptic:** a *coherent* image bundle carries the picture; a *random* light bundle carries illumination; cladding improves reflection. **Digital:** CCD (charge-coupled device) or CMOS (complementary metal oxide semiconductor) chip at the tip with LED (light-emitting diode) light; no camera head or light cable |
| Deflection | Lever → control wires through movable metal rings → wire fixed at the tip (deflection mechanism: lever pulls wires). "Intuitive" = tip moves with the lever. The reticle (notch) marks the plane of deflection |
| Working channel | Single channel shared by irrigation and instruments, so instruments reduce flow and deflection |
| Shaft | Tapered tip; passive (secondary) deflection segment behind the tip helps lower-pole access |

- Flexible ureteroscope parts: optics, deflection mechanism (lever pulls wires), working channel.

### Sensor technology (digital)
| | CCD (charge-coupled device) | CMOS (complementary metal-oxide semiconductor) |
|---|---|---|
| Heat and power | More | Less (runs cooler) |
| Cost, speed | Higher, slower | Cheaper, faster |
| Status | Phased out | Current standard |

- **LED (light-emitting diode)** at the tip; no camera head or light cable → better ergonomics.

### Types
| Instrument | Specifications | Main use |
|---|---|---|
| Flexible cystoscope | 14–17 Fr; 37–40 cm; deflection 120–220°; channel 6.4–7.5 Fr; field about 120° | Office diagnosis and minor therapy |
| Flexible ureteroscope | Tip 4.5–7.5 Fr; deflection ≥270° both ways; channel **3.6 Fr**; dual-channel scope (2 × 3.3 Fr) exists | Kidney and upper ureter |
| Flexible nephroscope (or flexible cystoscope used through the tract) | Larger calibre; too wide for the UPJ (ureteropelvic junction) | Clearing calyces during PCNL (percutaneous nephrolithotomy) |

### Deflection and channel (flexible ureteroscope)
- Active deflection **≥270° both directions** (another source: **>270° both ways** in one plane); enhanced primary deflection eases lower-pole access / reaches the lower pole (ureteroinfundibular angle up to about 175°).
- Working channel **3.6 Fr**; dual-channel scopes exist.

### Fibreoptic vs digital (digital versus fibreoptic)
| Feature | Fibreoptic | Digital |
|---|---|---|
| Image | Honeycomb pattern; black dots as fibres break; degrades | Sharper, larger (magnified), stable image; no focusing or white balance |
| Size / Tip | Smallest tips, tapered | Larger (about 8.5 Fr), flatter; more access-sheath use |
| Outcome / Operative time | Reference; longer | Shorter operating time, equal (similar) stone-free rate (SFR) |
| Durability | Fragile; over-rotation damages fibres; repair about every 15 cases | **Not better** in later comparisons; not clearly better in trials |
| Processor | Platform-agnostic | Proprietary |

- Surprise finding: in vitro, illumination was better with the fibreoptic cystoscope.
- Digital: sharper image, **shorter operating time, same stone-free rate**. But larger tip, so **more access sheaths and sheath complications**.
- Larger tip → more access-sheath use. Keep a fibreoptic scope for narrow UPJ (pelviureteric junction) or infundibulum.

### Single-use scopes (single-use vs reusable)
- **LithoVue (2016):** first single-use digital ureteroscope; CMOS, LED, tip 7.7 Fr, shaft 9.5 Fr, 3.6 Fr channel, 270° each way. Single-use cystoscopes followed (2020).
- Rationale (reason): reusable ureteroscopes may need repair after only **3–5 cases**; reprocessing and repair add cost.
- Single-use advantages: maximal deflection each case, sterile, no reprocessing or repair; cost **lower below about 99 cases/year**.
- Best value in low-volume centres and for **large lower-pole stones**.
- Reusable: cheaper in high-volume centres; needs reprocessing (peracetic acid, glutaraldehyde, ethylene oxide).
- Cost advantage depends on case volume; environmental impact is similar to reusables. Most centres use a **hybrid fleet**.

### Accessories
- **Guidewires:** 0.035/0.038 inch PTFE (polytetrafluoroethylene)-coated safety wire; hydrophilic wires to pass impacted stones (exchange, as too slippery for safety); nitinol-core and hybrid wires.
- **Access sheath** (11–16 Fr): repeated passes, lower intrarenal pressure; small risk of ureteric injury.
- **Laser fibres** 200–365 µm; **nitinol tipless baskets** ≤3 Fr; three-prong graspers; 3 Fr cup biopsy forceps; 3 Fr balloon dilating to 12 Fr; fulguration electrodes; pressure irrigation pumps.
- Flexible EHL (electrohydraulic lithotripsy) probes work; flexible pneumatic probes restrict deflection.

### Role in urological disease
| Area | Role |
|---|---|
| **Lower tract** | Haematuria work-up; bladder cancer surveillance, office biopsy and fulguration; NBI (narrow-band imaging) and blue light; stent removal or exchange; bladder stones, foreign bodies, strictures; botulinum toxin injection; diversions through a catheterisable channel; difficult ureteric access via a suprapubic tract |
| **Stones** | RIRS (retrograde intrarenal surgery): renal stones <20 mm, lower pole <10 mm; >2 cm when PCNL is unsafe |
| **UTUC (upper tract urothelial carcinoma)** | Diagnostic URS and biopsy (predicts grade in about 9 of 10); laser ablation of low-grade tumours; prefer to percutaneous route (seeding) |
| **Obstruction** | Endopyelotomy without crossing vessels; endoureterotomy for strictures <1.5 cm |
| **Diagnosis** | Positive cytology, filling defects, lateralising haematuria (haemangioma fulgurated), recurrent one-sided infection; foreign body retrieval |
| **PCNL** | Antegrade or retrograde flexible endoscopy raises stone-free rate; ECIRS (endoscopic combined intrarenal surgery) |

### Care and complications
- **Scope damage:** deflection wear; laser fibre scraping and perforating the channel. Pass fibres with the scope straight; **fire laser only when the fibre tip is seen touching the stone** (fire only with the fibre tip seen on the stone); leave the bladder empty to avoid buckling.
- **Sterilisation:** sterilise with ethylene oxide, glutaraldehyde or peracetic acid.
- **Patient:** ureteric injury, stricture, sepsis from high pressure. Use saline irrigation and antibiotic prophylaxis for all upper tract procedures.

### Recent advances
- **Intrarenal pressure sensor** in the scope (LithoVue Elite): high pressure causes fever, SIRS (systemic inflammatory response syndrome), sepsis. Temperature sensing next.
- **AI (artificial intelligence):** stone recognition, laser setting adjustment, navigation.
- **Robotic flexible URS (ureteroscopy):** ergonomics, less radiation to surgeon.
- **Also know:** suction (access) sheaths, further miniaturisation.

**Diagram to draw:** cross-section of a flexible ureteroscope: chip, LED, working channel and deflection wires (scope tip cross-section showing CMOS chip, LEDs, 3.6 Fr channel and 270° deflection).

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Working channel plus active deflection made flexible scopes therapeutic.
> - fURS: 3.6 Fr channel, ≥270° (>270°) deflection; cystoscope 14–17 Fr.
> - Chip-on-tip CMOS digital: better image, larger tip, durability not better; shorter operation.
> - Single-use cheaper below about 99 cases/year; LithoVue first single-use (2016).
> - Semirigid: two channels; below the iliac vessels.
> - Pressure sensors and AI are the newest developments.
> - Golden rule: never fire unless the fibre tip is seen on the stone.

## Natural orifice endoscopic surgery.
**Type:** LAQ (25 marks)  
**Source:** 2022-Summer Paper 4, Q1

*Taken as NOTES (natural orifice transluminal endoscopic surgery) and its relatives (hybrid NOTES, LESS [laparoendoscopic single-site surgery]).*

### Definition and concept
- **NOTES:** entry into the peritoneal or retroperitoneal cavity through a deliberate incision in a hollow viscus reached through a natural orifice (mouth, vagina, bladder, rectum). The aim is **"scarless" surgery**: no abdominal wall wound, so no wound pain, infection or hernia.
- It differs from **endoluminal** surgery (e.g. ureteroscopy), which stays inside the lumen.
- **Pure NOTES:** all instruments through the orifice. **Hybrid NOTES:** natural orifice plus one or more small abdominal ports. Hybrid is what is actually used in humans.
- Chapter 142 places NOTES at the end of a spectrum: transperitoneal → retroperitoneal → hand-assisted → LESS → SP (single-port) robotic → NOTES. **Each step to fewer incisions buys cosmesis and less pain at the cost of working space and triangulation.**

### History (milestones)
| Year | Event |
|---|---|
| 2002 | Transvaginal laparoscopic nephrectomy in pigs (Gettman, Cadeddu) |
| 2004 | Transgastric peritoneoscopy in pigs (Kalloo) |
| 2006 | NOSCAR (Natural Orifice Surgery Consortium for Assessment and Research) white paper listed barriers |
| 2008–2010 | Hybrid, then pure, transvaginal nephrectomy in humans |

### Routes of access
| Route | Features | Urological use |
|---|---|---|
| **Transvaginal** (posterior colpotomy) | Commonest; easy closure under vision; large specimens pass; women only | Nephrectomy, donor nephrectomy, specimen extraction |
| **Transgastric** | Upper abdomen reachable; gastrotomy closure and leak are the main risk | Experimental (peritoneoscopy, renal surgery in animals) |
| **Transvesical** | Sterile urine; bladder dome port; small calibre; closure difficult | Peritoneoscopy (animal, Lima), transvesical access to pelvis |
| **Transurethral** | Urologist's familiar route | Experimental transurethral prostatectomy in cadavers |
| **Transcolonic/transanal** | Faecal contamination risk | Rarely urological |

### Hybrid NOTES and natural orifice extraction
- Standard laparoscopy does the dissection; **the vagina is the access for a camera or instrument and the extraction route** (Chapter 142). This is the only NOTES form with real clinical urological use.
- **NOSE (natural orifice specimen extraction):** kidney removed through the vagina after laparoscopic nephrectomy, avoiding the extraction incision, which is the largest and most painful wound.
- **NOTES-assisted donor nephrectomy:** laparoscopic or robotic donor nephrectomy with **transvaginal kidney extraction**. Reported benefits: less pain, faster recovery, no extraction scar; warm ischaemia acceptable.

### Steps of transvaginal (hybrid) nephrectomy
1. Lithotomy then modified flank; bowel and vaginal preparation; antibiotics.
2. Umbilical port (camera or instrument) plus one or two small ports.
3. **Posterior colpotomy** under laparoscopic vision; long port or flexible scope through the vagina.
4. Colon reflection, hilar dissection, ureter and vessels stapled.
5. Kidney in a bag, delivered through the colpotomy.
6. Colpotomy closed with absorbable sutures under vision.

### LESS: the close relative
- All ports through **one skin incision** (umbilicus or below the waistline) using several low-profile trocars or a multichannel port (TriPort; da Vinci SP 25–27-mm entry guide).
- Problems: **no triangulation**, instrument clashing, in-line view. Solutions: articulating or bent instruments, flexible-tip scopes, instruments of different lengths.
- Evidence (Chapter 142): randomised trial versus laparoscopy showed the same time, blood loss and complications, with less analgesia. Donor meta-analysis: lower pain scores. Oncological outcome unchanged.
- Drawback (Chapter 20): **more incisional and umbilical hernias** because of the larger single incision.
- **SP robot (FDA [Food and Drug Administration] approval 2018)** has revived single-site surgery.

### vNOTES (vaginal NOTES)
- A gynaecological development: a single-port device in the vagina gives access to the peritoneum.
- **HALON RCT (randomised controlled trial):** vNOTES hysterectomy was non-inferior to laparoscopic hysterectomy, with more same-day discharge and less pain.
- Urogynaecological uses: vNOTES apical prolapse repair (uterosacral suspension), adnexal surgery.

### Challenges (NOSCAR list)
- **Safe access** and **secure closure** of the viscerotomy (leak, fistula); peritoneal **infection**.
- **Spatial orientation**: retroflexed, off-axis view.
- **Platform**: flexible scopes lack stiffness, traction and triangulation.
- Control of **haemorrhage** and other complications.
- Training and cost; dyspareunia and fertility concerns after colpotomy; transvaginal route unavailable in men.
- Chapter 142: adoption is limited by **no clear therapeutic benefit**, safety concerns and very selective patients; **pure NOTES has very few urological cases.**

### Recent advances
- **Flexible robotic platforms** (Chapter 39): MASTER (NOTES, haptics), ViaCath.
- **Transvesical single-port** (LESS or da Vinci SP) surgery for diverticulectomy and foreign bodies (Chapter 124).
- Magnetic anchoring and guidance systems to restore triangulation.

**Also know:** LESSCAR consortium standardised the term LESS; "embryonic NOTES" (umbilicus as the natural scar); hidden-incision endoscopic surgery (HIdES) in children.

**Diagram to draw:** sagittal female pelvis showing posterior colpotomy with a transvaginal port and kidney extraction, plus umbilical port.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - NOTES = transluminal via natural orifice; hybrid transvaginal is the only routinely used urological form.
> - Transvaginal extraction removes the most painful wound.
> - LESS: one incision, lost triangulation, more hernias.
> - Barriers: closure, infection, orientation, platform; no proven benefit yet.

## Tissue engineering in urology, including recent advances
**Asked as:**
- Tissue engineering in urology. — *Summer 2022 Paper IV Q2 (LAQ)*
- Discuss recent advances in tissue engineering with respect to urology — *Practice Set 6 Paper IV Q2 (LAQ)*

**Type:** LAQ (25 marks)  
(also answered in 2023-Winter Paper 1, Q1 and 2022-Summer Paper 4, Q2; this answer [Practice Set 6] stresses recent advances)

### Definition and rationale
- **Tissue engineering:** applying (applies) engineering and life sciences to build biological substitutes that restore, maintain or improve tissue function (Langer and Vacanti, 1993).
- **Why in urology:** bowel used for bladder and diversion (bowel in the urinary tract) causes mucus, stones, metabolic acidosis, infection and malignancy (cancer); grafts for urethra (for long urethral defects) are limited in supply (scarce). The aim is **to replace "wrong tissue" with "right tissue"**.
- **Regenerative medicine** is the wider term: cells, scaffolds, growth factors and gene therapy.

### The tissue-engineering triad (basic principle)
```
Cells  +  Scaffold  +  Signals (growth factors, mechanical cycling in a bioreactor)
           -> construct -> implanted -> vascularised and remodelled by the host
```
```
Biopsy → cells expanded in culture → seeded on scaffold (+ signals)
       → conditioned in bioreactor → implanted → host vascularises and remodels
```

### Cell sources
| Source | Features / advance and limitation |
|---|---|
| **Autologous differentiated cells** (urothelial and smooth muscle cells from a bladder biopsy) | Standard; no rejection; expanded in culture; unusable if the bladder is malignant or diseased |
| **Adult stem cells:** bone marrow or adipose mesenchymal, muscle-derived, **urine-derived** | Easy, non-invasive harvest; paracrine effect (act mainly by paracrine growth factors) |
| **Embryonic stem cells** | Pluripotent; ethics, teratoma risk |
| **iPSCs** (induced pluripotent stem cells) | Adult cells reprogrammed; patient-specific, no ethical issue; tumour risk |
- Gene-edited and gene-modified cells are being explored. Cell sheet engineering builds layers without a scaffold.

### Scaffolds (biomaterials)
- **Requirements:** biocompatible; biodegradable at the rate of tissue ingrowth (degrade as tissue forms); porous for cell attachment and vessel ingrowth; mechanically suitable/suited (compliance for bladder; compliant bladder, non-collapsing tube).

| Type | Examples | Comment |
|---|---|---|
| **Natural** | Collagen, alginate, silk fibroin | Good cell recognition, weaker |
| **Acellular tissue matrices** | Bladder acellular matrix, SIS (small intestinal submucosa), decellularised dermis | Retain (keep) native architecture and growth factors |
| **Synthetic polymers** | PGA (polyglycolic acid), PLA (polylactic acid), PLGA (their copolymer) | Controlled degradation and shape; no bioactivity |
| **Newer** | Electrospun nanofibres, hydrogels (mimicking extracellular matrix); **"smart" scaffolds** releasing growth factors such as VEGF (vascular endothelial growth factor); silk fibroin | Mimic extracellular matrix |

- **Unseeded (acellular)** grafts (matrices) depend on host cell migration (host cells migrating in) and work (suit) only for short or onlay defects (about 0.5 cm for tubular urethra [tubes] in animal work). Longer or tubular gaps need **seeded** scaffolds, or they fibrose and contract.

### Bioreactors
- Perfuse nutrients through thick constructs and apply **cyclic stretch and filling**, so engineered bladder muscle is conditioned before implantation.
- **Also know:** bioreactors apply cyclic stretch to train smooth muscle.

### Solving vascularity (the main barrier)
- Oxygen diffuses only about 100–200 µm, so thick constructs die centrally.
- Strategies: omental wrap (Atala), angiogenic factors, co-seeding endothelial cells, printed vascular channels.

### Clinical applications (where we stand)
**1. Bladder**
- **Atala et al. (2006):** first seeded biodegradable bladder construct (autologous urothelial and muscle cells on collagen–PGA, wrapped in omentum) for augmentation in **7 myelomeningocele patients**; compliance improved.
- **Industry (Tengion) multi-institution phase II trial** in spina bifida: **no clinically or statistically significant improvement in compliance or capacity** (Chapter 48).
- Chapter 48 conclusion: a bladder with specialised urothelium and contractile muscle has **not** been achieved; it remains experimental.
- Chapter 111: an engineered bladder gives **storage only**; innervation for voluntary emptying cannot (yet) be recreated.

**2. Urinary diversion: Neo-Urinary Conduit (Tengion, NCT01087687)** (Chapter 135)
- Seeded biodegradable conduit to avoid bowel. Small enrolment; conduits survived about 250 days on average; **half developed stenosis or stricture.**
- Lessons: stiff, non-collapsible material; no cells formed around the connective-tissue sleeve; **no blood supply**.

**3. Urethra**
- Acellular bladder matrix onlay for hypospadias and stricture: success in short onlay repairs (short strictures).
- **Raya-Rivera et al. (2011, Lancet):** tubular PGA–PLGA scaffolds seeded with autologous cells in **5 boys** with posterior urethral defects; functional urethras at up to 6 years.
- Tissue-engineered oral mucosa (MukoCell) for urethroplasty, avoiding donor-site morbidity.

**4. Other organs**
- **Sphincter, detrusor:** autologous muscle-derived cells for SUI (stress urinary incontinence); also for underactive detrusor (Chapter 111: open-label improvement; open-label studies improved voiding efficiency). Biodegradable scaffold slings (Chapter 48).
- **Vagina:** engineered vaginas in MRKH (Mayer–Rokitansky–Küster–Hauser) syndrome (Raya-Rivera, 2014); off-the-shelf SIS grafts (Chapter 59).
- **Penis:** engineered corporal tissue, animal stage. ED (erectile dysfunction) stem cell therapy is investigational (Chapter 72).
- **Kidney:** decellularised–recellularised scaffolds (animal); renal assist devices; kidney organoids.
- **Testis:** spermatogonial stem cell banking for prepubertal cancer patients (future, Chapter 4).

| Organ | Status |
|---|---|
| **Bladder** | **Atala (2006):** seeded collagen–PGA construct with omental wrap in **7 myelomeningocele patients**, compliance improved. Industry phase II trial: **no significant gain in compliance or capacity**. Still experimental (Chapter 48) |
| **Conduit** | Neo-Urinary Conduit trial (NCT01087687): survived about 250 days on average; **half developed stenosis or stricture**; stiff material, no blood supply (Chapter 135) |
| **Urethra** | Acellular matrix onlay for short strictures; **Raya-Rivera (2011):** seeded tubular PGA–PLGA urethras in **5 boys**, functional up to 6 years; tissue-engineered oral mucosa avoids donor-site morbidity |
| **Sphincter, detrusor** | Autologous muscle-derived cells for SUI (stress urinary incontinence); for underactive detrusor, open-label studies improved voiding efficiency (Chapter 111) |
| **Vagina** | Engineered vaginas in MRKH (Mayer–Rokitansky–Küster–Hauser) syndrome (Raya-Rivera, 2014); off-the-shelf SIS grafts (Chapter 59) |
| **Penis, testis** | Corporal tissue (animal); stem cells for ED (erectile dysfunction) investigational; spermatogonial stem cell banking |

### 3D bioprinting
- Layer-by-layer deposition (laid layer by layer) of **"bio-ink"** (cells in hydrogel) with or without polymer, driven by patient CT (computed tomography) or MRI (magnetic resonance imaging) data.
- Advantages: exact patient-specific shape, multiple (several) cell types placed precisely (placing several cell types precisely), built-in channels for vessels.
- Current urological use: 3D-printed (non-living) kidney and prostate models for planning and teaching (already aid planning and teaching); bioprinted urethra and bladder constructs are still at the animal stage.

### Organoids
- 3D self-organising mini-organs grown from stem cells or tumour tissue.
- **Kidney organoids** from iPSCs: models of development and disease (model disease), drug toxicity testing.
- **Tumour organoids** (bladder, prostate): personalised drug testing (allow personalised drug testing) and biology (WNT/β-catenin work, Chapter 129).

### Whole-organ decellularisation
- Kidneys are stripped of cells by detergent perfusion, leaving the matrix and vascular tree, then recellularised with the patient's cells; still at the animal stage. Renal assist devices are a parallel route.

### Challenges
- **Vascularisation** of thick constructs (diffusion limit), **innervation** and contractility.
- Fibrosis and contracture; scale-up, cost, culture time; regulatory approval (regulation).
- Safety of stem cells (tumour formation; stem cell tumour risk). Disappointing controlled trials after promising pilots (promising pilots followed by disappointing controlled trials).

### Recent advances
- Robotic bladder auto-transplantation and vascularised composite bladder allograft (Chapter 135; robotic retrieval of a **vascularised composite bladder allograft**): a transplant, not engineered, route to "right tissue", needing immunosuppression.
- Smart scaffolds releasing growth factors; urine-derived stem cells; gene-edited cells.

### Future
- Vascularised bioprinted tissue, off-the-shelf cell-free smart scaffolds, combined gene and cell therapy.

**Diagram to draw:** biopsy → cell culture → seeding on scaffold → bioreactor → implant with omental wrap.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Triad: cells, scaffold, signals; vascularity (100–200 µm diffusion) is the main barrier.
> - Atala 2006 (7 patients) promising; phase II trial: no gain in compliance or capacity.
> - Neo-urinary conduit (conduit trial): half stenosed; no blood supply.
> - Raya-Rivera 2011: seeded tubular urethras in 5 boys.
> - New: iPSCs, urine-derived cells, bioprinting, organoids.

## Laparoendoscopic single-site (single-port) surgery: LESS
**Asked as:**
- Laproendoscopic single site surgery: LESS — *Winter 2023 Paper IV Q1 (LAQ)*
- Single port lap. Surgeries in urology — *Practice Set 2 Paper IV Q3c (SAQ)*

**Type:** LAQ (25 marks); also asked as SAQ (10 marks)  
(also answered as a long answer in 2023-Winter Paper 4, Q1)

*Read as laparoendoscopic single-site surgery (LESS), i.e. single-port (single-site) laparoscopic and robotic surgery in urology. NOTES (natural orifice transluminal endoscopic surgery) is covered in 2022-Summer Paper 4, Q1.*

### Definition and concept
- **LESS:** laparoscopic or robotic surgery in which all instruments and the camera enter (pass) through **one skin incision**, usually hidden in the umbilicus (umbilical) or below the waistline.
- Aim: further reduce access trauma, giving **better cosmesis, less pain and shorter stay**. Chapter 142 places it on a spectrum: multiport → hand-assisted → LESS → SP (single-port) robot → NOTES. Each step to fewer incisions buys cosmesis at the price of working space and triangulation.
- First urological single-port nephrectomies were reported in 2007; the first single-port robotic urological surgery followed in 2009.

### Nomenclature (LESSCAR)
- Early names were many: SILS (single-incision laparoscopic surgery), SPA (single-port access), OPUS (one-port umbilical surgery), TUES (transumbilical endoscopic surgery), e-NOTES (embryonic NOTES).
- **LESSCAR (Laparoendoscopic Single-Site Surgery Consortium for Assessment and Research, 2008)** agreed the single umbrella term **LESS** and set standards for reporting, training and research.
- The term was agreed by LESSCAR (LESS Consortium for Assessment and Research, 2008).
- Sub-types (variants): transumbilical or extraumbilical; **pure LESS**; **"LESS + 1"** or needlescopic-assisted (an extra 2–5-mm port); **R-LESS** (robotic LESS); purpose-built **SP (single-port) robot**.

### Access: ports and devices
| Option | Description |
|---|---|
| Several low-profile trocars | Placed close together through one fascial incision (the future extraction incision) |
| **Multichannel ports** | TriPort 15 (two 5-mm + one 15-mm); TriPort+ (three 5-mm + one 10-mm); SILS port; GelPOINT (gel cap, trocars placed anywhere on it) |
| **da Vinci SP entry guide** | One 25–27-mm cannula carrying three articulating (wristed) 6-mm instruments + one flexible articulating camera |

- Position: modified flank (transperitoneal) or full flank (retroperitoneal SP, 2.5–3-cm incision near the 12th rib tip, balloon dilatation).

### The triangulation problem
- In standard laparoscopy ports are spread so instruments meet the target at an angle (the "diamond"). In LESS all shafts are **parallel and in-line** with the camera (instruments lie parallel to the camera).
- Results: **instrument clashing**, **"crossing swords"**, poor depth perception, poor retraction and hard suturing.
- Solutions:
  - **Articulating instruments** (tip bends inside the abdomen) and **pre-bent or curved instruments**: they cross at the fascia, so the right hand works on the left side (**chiral manipulation**).
  - **Flexible-tip or 5-mm low-profile scopes**, offset optics, extra-long instruments and instruments of **different lengths** to avoid handle clash.
  - A 2-mm needlescopic instrument or extra 5-mm port (an extra needlescopic port) without hesitation.
  - **Robotic platforms** restore wristed movement.

### Robotic single-site surgery
| Platform | Features |
|---|---|
| da Vinci Single-Site (Si/Xi) | Curved crossing cannulas; semi-rigid **non-wristed** instruments; software swaps hand control |
| **da Vinci SP (FDA [Food and Drug Administration] approval 2018)** | Purpose-built; one arm, no external collisions; instruments with elbow and wrist; 3D articulating camera; on-screen navigator shows instrument positions |

- SP needs **10–25 cm between (from) cannula and (to) target** to deploy the joints; a GelPort lets instruments "float" outside the skin. Assistance through an **"SP + 1" port** or ROSI (remote-operated suction-irrigation). Limited by cost and availability.

### Indications
- **Kidney:** simple and **donor nephrectomy**, radical nephrectomy, partial nephrectomy, **pyeloplasty**, nephroureterectomy, cyst decortication, cryoablation.
- **Adrenalectomy**; varicocelectomy; orchidopexy and pyeloplasty in children (paediatric).
- **Pelvis:** SP radical prostatectomy (transperitoneal, extraperitoneal); **SP transvesical simple prostatectomy** and bladder diverticulectomy.
- **Best case:** thin patient (patients), small or benign lesion (lesions), experienced surgeon.
- **Relative contraindications (avoid in):** obesity, large or hilar tumours, complex vascular anatomy, dense adhesions, unfit for pneumoperitoneum.

### Outcomes versus conventional laparoscopy (Chapter 142)
- **Randomised trial (RCT, randomised controlled trial):** same operative time, blood loss, complications and stay; **less analgesia**.
- **Donor nephrectomy meta-analysis:** same stay, recovery, transfusion and conversion (otherwise equal); **lower pain scores**.
- **Renal mass:** same perioperative outcome and survival.
- **SP robotic partial nephrectomy vs multiport:** shorter stay, less opioid; same margins and complications; **longer warm ischaemia** in one review.
- An **extra port is often needed**; conversion is mostly to laparoscopy, rarely open.
- **Net effect:** gains are confined (limited) to **pain, cosmesis and stay**; oncological and functional outcomes are unchanged, but the operation is technically harder and longer.

### Complications
- All laparoscopic complications (usual laparoscopic injuries: access injury, vascular and bowel injury, CO₂ effects).
- **Incisional and umbilical hernia** is higher (larger fascial defect): about 5% with da Vinci Xi single-site, lower with SP.
- Wound infection; conversion; longer operation; surgeon ergonomic strain. Prevent by careful fascial closure and case selection.

### Recent advances
- **da Vinci SP** with same-day discharge pathways; **SP retroperitoneal** renal surgery.
- **Transvesical SP** prostatectomy; hidden incision endoscopic surgery (HIdES) in children.
- New single-port platforms; magnetic anchoring and guidance systems / instruments (restore triangulation).

**Diagram to draw:** umbilical multichannel port with articulating (crossed) instruments crossing at the fascia (chiral) versus a triangulated multiport diamond (layout).

> **Key points to remember:**
> - LESS = one incision; LESSCAR (2008) standardised the term.
> - Core problem: lost triangulation; solved by articulating or curved instruments and robots.
> - Same outcomes as laparoscopy with less pain and better cosmesis; more hernias.
> - da Vinci SP (FDA 2018): one 25–27-mm cannula, wristed instruments.

## Basic energy modalities in urological surgery, including bipolar energy
**Asked as:**
- Describe Basic Energy Modalities in Urological surgery? — *Winter 2025 Paper IV Q2 (LAQ)*
- Bipolar energy — *Practice Set 1 Paper IV Q3b (SAQ)* (also covered in 2025-Winter Paper 4, Q2)

**Type:** LAQ (25 marks); also asked as SAQ (10 marks)

### Introduction
- Every modality delivers energy that tissue absorbs and releases as **heat, mechanical stress or cavitation**. Frequency sets penetration; absorption sets the effect.
- Tissue effects of heat: about 60 °C coagulates protein; 100 °C vaporises cell water; higher temperatures carbonise.

### Classification
| Energy | Examples |
|---|---|
| Electrical | Monopolar, bipolar, vessel sealers, RFA (radiofrequency ablation), IRE (irreversible electroporation), EMDA (electromotive drug administration) |
| Electromagnetic | Lasers, MWA (microwave ablation) |
| Acoustic | Ultrasonic dissector, SWL (shock wave lithotripsy), HIFU (high-intensity focused ultrasound), histotripsy |
| Direct contact | Cryoablation, water vapour, waterjet (Aquablation) |

### 1. Electrosurgery
- **Physics:** alternating current at about **500 kHz** (radiofrequency). Mains current (50 Hz) depolarises nerve and muscle; at 500 kHz ions only oscillate and produce heat.
- **Ohm's law** (V = I × R): tissue is the resistance. **Current density** decides the effect: a small tip concentrates current and heats; a large pad disperses it.
- **Monopolar:** generator → active electrode → patient → dispersive pad → generator. Partial pad contact causes burns.
- **Bipolar:** current passes only between two jaws (**only between two electrodes on the same instrument**); less power, less spread, no pad (no dispersive pad; the patient is not in the circuit).

#### Bipolar energy in detail
- Radiofrequency current (about 500 kHz). Less power and less lateral thermal spread (1–6 mm vs 5–7 mm monopolar). In endoscopy, **saline** irrigation can be used.

| | Monopolar | Bipolar |
|---|---|---|
| Circuit | Loop → patient → pad | Active and return poles on the instrument |
| Irrigant | Glycine 1.5%, sorbitol–mannitol | Normal saline |
| TUR (transurethral resection) syndrome | Yes | Theoretically eliminated |
| Capacitive coupling, pad burns | Yes | No |

- **Endoscopic uses:**
  - **Bipolar TURP (transurethral resection of prostate):**
    - *Plasma system:* energy vaporises saline around the loop into plasma (orange glow of sodium); cuts at lower temperature while sealing vessels; platinum–iridium loop.
    - *TURis (transurethral resection in saline):* the sheath acts as return electrode; chapter notes report strictures and fistulae.
    - *True bipolar:* dual loops.
    - Outcomes: equal IPSS (International Prostate Symptom Score) and Qmax; less transfusion and clot retention; shorter catheter time and stay. NNT (number needed to treat) about 50 to prevent one TUR syndrome.
  - **Bipolar TUVP (transurethral vaporisation):** PlasmaButton; AUA (American Urological Association) option for <30 and 30–80 cc.
  - **Bipolar enucleation:** plasmakinetic energy can perform AEEP (anatomic endoscopic enucleation of prostate) satisfactorily; comparative data limited.
  - **TURBT (transurethral resection of bladder tumour):** fewer obturator jerks and less bleeding in reviews; one RCT (randomised controlled trial) showed only less thermal artefact.
- **Laparoscopic and open uses:** bipolar forceps for pinpoint haemostasis; vessel sealers (below); uses: prostatic pedicles, cystectomy.
- **Bipolar safety:** safer near pacemakers; touching tines complete the circuit without tissue. Obturator reflex reduced, not abolished.
- **Electrocautery** (heated wire) differs: no current enters the patient.

| Waveform | Character | Effect |
|---|---|---|
| Cut | Continuous, low voltage; 50–80 W | Rapid boiling, clean incision, little char |
| Coagulation | Interrupted (about 6% duty cycle), high voltage; 30–50 W | Desiccation; spread up to 3 mm |
| Fulguration (spray) | High voltage arcs across air | Non-contact surface haemostasis |
| Blend | Cut with interruptions | Cutting plus haemostasis |

- **Vessel sealers (advanced vessel sealers: LigaSure, EnSeal, Caiman):** bipolar current plus jaw compression fuses collagen and elastin; a feedback generator stops when impedance rises (impedance feedback stops output). Seal vessels up to 7 mm (**≤7 mm**) in one application (one application only), with little spread (spread 1–3 mm).
- **Endoscopic:** monopolar TURP (transurethral resection of prostate) needs non-conducting **1.5% glycine**, risking hyponatraemia (TUR syndrome); **bipolar** uses saline, with comparable efficacy and better safety.
- **Obturator reflex:** lateral-wall TURBT (transurethral resection of bladder tumour) stimulates the nerve → adduction → perforation. Prevent with paralysis, obturator block, partial filling, short bursts, bipolar.
- **Safety:** pad burns; insulation failure; **direct coupling** (touching metal); **capacitive coupling** (current induced through intact insulation); fire with oxygen; inadvertent activation. Use AEM (active electrode monitoring), lowest power, bipolar near pacemakers.

### 2. Ultrasonic energy
- **Ultrasonic dissector:** blade vibrates at about 55.5 kHz; friction and cavitation denature protein. No current through the patient; little lateral spread, but the blade stays hot.
- Intracorporeal ultrasonic lithotripter: vibrating probe, suction of fragments.

### 3. Lasers
- **Principle:** pumped gain medium in a mirrored cavity → stimulated emission → **monochromatic, coherent, collimated** beam.
- **Effect** depends on wavelength (chromophore: water, haemoglobin) and pulse duration.

| Laser | nm | Penetration (mm) | Use |
|---|---|---|---|
| KTP/LBO (potassium-titanyl-phosphate/lithium triborate) | 532 | 0.8 | Prostate photovaporisation (haemoglobin) |
| Nd:YAG (neodymium:yttrium-aluminium-garnet) | 1064 | 10 | Deep coagulation |
| TFL (thulium fibre laser) | 1940 | 0.08 | Stones, enucleation |
| Tm:YAG (thulium:YAG) | 2000 | 0.2 | Vaporesection, enucleation |
| Ho:YAG (holmium:YAG) | 2120 | 0.31 | Stones, HoLEP (holmium laser enucleation of prostate), strictures |

- **Mechanisms:** photothermal (long pulses), photomechanical (short pulses, shock waves), photochemical (PDT, photodynamic therapy).
- **Penetration predicts behaviour:** deep (Nd:YAG) coagulates widely with delayed sloughing; shallow (thulium) vaporises precisely with char.
- Safety: eye protection for all; irrigant heating; fibre damage.

### 4. Ablative energies
| Modality | Mechanism | Key points |
|---|---|---|
| **RFA** | 375–900 kHz current; ionic agitation | Target 60 °C; char at 150 °C; heat sink |
| **MWA** | 915 MHz or 2.45 GHz; dielectric hysteresis | Hotter, larger zones; less heat sink |
| **Cryoablation** | Argon expansion (Joule–Thomson) | Below −40 °C; **two freeze–thaw cycles**; ice ball visible |
| **HIFU** | Focused ultrasound absorbed → necrosis | 1500–10,000 W/cm²; rice-grain lesions; transrectal prostate |
| **IRE** | High-voltage microsecond pulses → permanent membrane pores | Non-thermal; spares vessels and ducts; focal prostate |

- Cryo mechanism: extracellular ice → cell dehydration → intracellular ice → membrane rupture on thawing; ischaemia and apoptosis follow.
- IRE mechanism: electric pulses open pores in cell membranes that do not reseal, so cells die by apoptosis without heat; collagen scaffolds survive. It needs general anaesthesia with muscle paralysis and ECG (electrocardiogram)-synchronised pulses.
- **Also know:** histotripsy (cavitation liquefaction, investigational); water vapour (Rezum, 103 °C, convective, no char); Aquablation (non-thermal saline jet); chemohyperthermia (42 °C) and EMDA for mitomycin.

### 5. Lithotripsy energies
- **SWL:** electrohydraulic, electromagnetic or piezoelectric sources. Stones break by **spallation, shear and cavitation**. Slower rates (about 1 Hz) work better and injure less.
- **Intracorporeal:** pneumatic (ballistic), ultrasonic, combined, electrohydraulic, laser. **Also know:** burst wave lithotripsy, ultrasonic propulsion.

**Diagram to draw:** monopolar vs bipolar circuit, with cut and coagulation waveforms.

**Diagram to draw:** monopolar loop-to-pad circuit beside bipolar loop with local return.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Electrosurgery at about 500 kHz; current density decides heating.
> - Cut continuous low voltage; coagulation interrupted high voltage.
> - Capacitive coupling and insulation failure cause unseen burns.
> - Cryo below −40 °C, two cycles; RFA 60 °C; MWA less heat sink.
> - Bipolar: no pad, saline irrigation, no TUR syndrome.
> - Bipolar TURP equals monopolar efficacy with fewer complications.
> - Vessel sealers: vessels ≤7 mm, spread 1–3 mm.

# Section 1 · Basic Principles — Short answer questions (SAQ)

## Urethral stents and metallic stents in urology
**Asked as:**
- Urethral stents — *Summer 2015 Paper IV Q3d (SAQ)*
- Metallic stents in urology — *Practice Set 3 Paper IV Q3c (SAQ)*
- Urethral stents. — *Practice Set 4 Paper IV Q3f (earlier version) (SAQ)*

**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)

(urethral stents also answered in 2015-Summer Paper 4, Q3d; ureteric stents in 2015-Summer Paper 3, Q1)

### Definition and introduction
- A urethral stent is an endoscopically placed tube that keeps the **prostatic or bulbar urethra** open, mainly in men unfit for surgery.
- Polymer stents (polyurethane, silicone) fail early under **extrinsic compression** and need frequent changes. Metal stents **resist compression** and stay longer, but suffer **encrustation, tissue ingrowth and migration**.

### Classification (urethral stents)
| Type | Examples | Features |
|---|---|---|
| **Temporary** | **Memokath** (nitinol), **Allium** | Not epithelialised (designed to avoid epithelial incorporation); removable; left 6–12 months |
| **Permanent, epithelialising** | **UroLume** | Urothelium grows over it; **withdrawn** |
| Biodegradable | Experimental | Dissolve |

### Classification of metallic stents (by site)
| Site | Type | Example | Features |
|---|---|---|---|
| Ureter | Full-length coiled metal, double pigtail | **Resonance** (2006) | Tightly coiled alloy; changed every 12 months |
| Ureter | Segmental self-expanding mesh, bare or covered | Wallstent-type; covered nitinol (Allium) | Placed across malignant stricture; bare mesh allows ingrowth |
| Ureter | Thermo-expandable nitinol coil | Memokath 051 | Removable |
| Prostate | Temporary thermo-expandable nitinol coil | **Memokath** | Not epithelialised; removable |
| Prostate, bulbar urethra | Covered self-expanding, removable | Allium | Left 6–12 months |
| Bulbar urethra | Permanent epithelialising mesh | **UroLume** | Withdrawn |

### Ureteric metal stents
- **Indications:** malignant extrinsic obstruction; retroperitoneal fibrosis (first Resonance use); benign stricture in a patient unfit for repair; ureteroenteric stricture with limited life expectancy; failed tandem polymer stents.
- **Outcomes:** full-length metal stent: up to 3 years of drainage, pooled success about 72.5%, migration about 1%, but about one-third failed in one malignant series. Ureteroenteric strictures: patency >80% at 6–22 months.
- **Problems:** encrustation, difficult exchange, ingrowth, migration.

### Urethral and prostatic stents
#### Indications
1. BPH (benign prostatic hyperplasia) retention in men unfit for TURP (transurethral resection of prostate) / unfit for surgery.
2. Recurrent short bulbar stricture in men unfit for urethroplasty.
3. **DSD (detrusor–sphincter dyssynergia)**: formerly (once) an alternative to sphincterotomy; now **abandoned** for complications.

#### Technique
- Local or regional anaesthesia with endoscopic or fluoroscopic guidance. Memokath expands when warmed (with warm water) and softens when cooled (with cold water) for removal. A bulbar stent must **not cross the external sphincter**, or incontinence follows.

#### Complications
- **Migration** (temporary stents), **encrustation**, **hyperplastic ingrowth** with restenosis (UroLume), incontinence, pain and painful erections.

#### Results (outcomes)
- Prostatic stents free most unfit men from catheters short term, but many are later removed. Memokath variable. Removable bulbar stents succeed only in small series. UroLume worked early (good early results, then ingrowth) but failed long term from **hyperplastic ingrowth** and restenosis, so withdrawn, and many men still carry one.
- No stent removes **spongiofibrosis**, so none cures a stricture.

### Guidelines
- **AUA (American Urological Association)**: **no (urethral) stent if urethroplasty is considered** (planned); stents make reconstruction harder.

### Recent advances
- **Optilume** paclitaxel balloon (FDA (Food and Drug Administration) approval 2023 for anterior stricture): **ROBUST III**, after failed endoscopy, about 75% vs 27% stricture-free. Optilume BPH (EVEREST-1).
- **iTind** (temporary nitinol prostatic device): nitinol device left 5–7 days; AUA 2023 option for 25–75 mL glands without median lobe.
- **Also know:** biodegradable and drug-eluting stents (experimental).

**Also know:** a soft silicone "urethral stent" (splint) after hypospadias repair or urethroplasty is a different device; in complicated hypospadias repairs it reduces meatal stenosis and fistula.

**Diagram to draw:** sagittal urethra with prostatic stent and bulbar stent distal to sphincter; prostatic Memokath ending above the external sphincter.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Temporary (Memokath, Allium) vs permanent (UroLume, withdrawn).
> - Metal resists extrinsic compression; weakness is ingrowth and encrustation.
> - Resonance: coiled, full-length, changed every 12 months.
> - Memokath: thermo-expandable nitinol, removable.
> - Migration, encrustation, ingrowth; never cross the sphincter.
> - AUA: no stent if urethroplasty planned; Optilume and iTind are newer.

## Describe evidence based medicine.
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2020-Summer Paper 4, Q3f

### Definition
- **EBM (evidence-based medicine)**: "the conscientious, explicit and judicious use of current best evidence in making decisions about the care of individual patients" (**Sackett, 1996**).
- It integrates best research evidence, **clinical expertise** and **patient values**.

### Steps ("5 As")
1. **Ask** a focused question in **PICO** form (Population, Intervention, Comparison, Outcome).
2. **Acquire**: PubMed, Cochrane Library, guidelines.
3. **Appraise**: validity (randomisation, blinding), effect size (ARR (absolute risk reduction), **NNT (number needed to treat) = 1/ARR**), precision (95% CI (confidence interval)), applicability.
4. **Apply** with expertise and patient preference.
5. **Assess** (audit) the outcome.

### Hierarchy: levels of evidence (Oxford CEBM (Centre for Evidence-Based Medicine), as adapted by EAU (European Association of Urology))
| Level | Type of evidence |
|---|---|
| **1a** | Systematic review/meta-analysis of RCTs (randomised controlled trials) |
| **1b** | At least one RCT |
| 2a | Well-designed controlled study without randomisation |
| 2b | Quasi-experimental (cohort) study |
| 3 | Non-experimental: case-control, correlation, case reports |
| 4 | Expert committee reports or opinion |

- **Grades**: A (consistent level 1), B (level 2–3), C (level 4 or consensus); D (some systems) = no recommendation possible.
- **GRADE** (Grading of Recommendations Assessment, Development and Evaluation): certainty high to very low; RCTs start high, observational studies low; **downgrade** for bias, inconsistency, indirectness, imprecision, publication bias; **upgrade** for large effect or dose–response. Recommendations **strong or weak** (now used by EAU). AUA (American Urological Association): evidence strength A/B/C; strong, moderate or conditional recommendations.

### Pitfalls in appraisal
- **Bias**: selection, confounding (comorbidity, frailty: needs risk adjustment), **lead-time**, **length-time**, stage migration (Will Rogers), attribution.
- **Surrogate end points** must meet the **Prentice criteria** (prostate cancer: only metastasis-free survival does).
- **Also know:** Clavien–Dindo; validated PROs (patient-reported outcomes); CONSORT, PRISMA, STROBE checklists; Donabedian model.

### Limitations
- Few surgical RCTs; trial patients differ from real ones; publication bias; "cookbook medicine" if patient values are ignored.

**Diagram to draw:** evidence pyramid and the three overlapping circles of EBM.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Sackett 1996: best evidence + clinical expertise + patient values.
> - 5 As: Ask (PICO), Acquire, Appraise, Apply, Assess.
> - Level 1a = systematic review of RCTs; GRADE: strong or weak.
> - NNT = 1/ARR; beware lead-time, length-time and confounding bias.

## Operating room safety : the role of surgeon.
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2021-Summer Paper 4, Q3a

### Introduction
- About half of hospital adverse events involve surgery; half of these are preventable. The surgeon is **team leader** and ultimately responsible.
- **Swiss cheese model (Reason):** harm occurs when holes in several barriers line up, so safety needs layered defences.

### WHO (World Health Organization) Surgical Safety Checklist (2008, 19 items)
| Phase | Key checks |
|---|---|
| **Sign in** (before induction) | Identity, site, procedure, consent; site marked; allergy; airway; blood loss >500 mL |
| **Time out** (before incision) | Team introductions; confirm patient, site, procedure; surgeon states critical steps, duration, blood loss; antibiotic within 60 min; imaging displayed |
| **Sign out** (before leaving theatre) | Procedure recorded; counts correct; specimen labelled; equipment problems; recovery concerns |

- Pilot in 8 hospitals: deaths fell 47%, major complications 36%.

### Surgeon's responsibilities
1. **Universal Protocol** (Joint Commission, 2004): verification, **site marking** by the operating surgeon, patient awake, correct side only, time-out. Wrong-site surgery is a never event.
2. **Lead the time-out:** whole team engaged; repeat when procedure or surgeon changes.
3. **Retained surgical items** (sponges commonest): a count discrepancy raises risk about 100-fold; do not close until resolved; X-ray or RF (radiofrequency) tags.
4. **Fire safety:** the surgeon owns the **ignition source** (diathermy, light cables, laser). Let alcohol prep dry; FiO₂ (inspired oxygen fraction) ≤30% near the airway.
5. **Sharps:** double gloves, blunt needles, neutral zone (no hand-to-hand passing).
6. **Distractions:** "sterile cockpit" during critical steps (e.g. hilar clamping).
7. **Communication** (failure underlies up to 70% of events): Use **TeamSTEPPS** (Team Strategies and Tools to Enhance Performance and Patient Safety) tools: brief, debrief, **SBAR** (situation, background, assessment, recommendation), closed-loop check-back, **CUS** ("concerned, uncomfortable, safety issue"), two-challenge rule. Flatten hierarchy so juniors speak up.
8. **Self-monitoring:** **I'M SAFE** (illness, medication, stress, alcohol, fatigue, eating).

- **Also know:** positioning and padding; VTE (venous thromboembolism) prophylaxis; simulation.

**Diagram to draw:** Swiss cheese model with aligned holes.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - WHO checklist: sign in, time out, sign out.
> - Universal Protocol: verify, mark, time-out.
> - Count discrepancy: ~100-fold risk of a retained item.
> - Fire triangle: surgeon owns ignition.

## Urological applications in nanotechnology.
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2022-Summer Paper 4, Q3a

*Taken as "applications of nanotechnology in urology".*

### Definition
- **Nanotechnology:** design and use of materials of about **1–100 nm**. At this scale, surface area and optical, magnetic and thermal properties change.
- **Why useful:** particles pass through leaky tumour vessels (**enhanced permeability and retention**), carry drugs or tracers, and can be targeted with ligands such as PSMA (prostate-specific membrane antigen) antibodies.

### Applications
| Area | Application |
|---|---|
| **Imaging and staging** | **USPIO (ultrasmall superparamagnetic iron oxide) lymphotropic MRI (magnetic resonance imaging):** normal nodes take up particles and lose T2 signal; metastatic nodes do not (Chapter 6; investigational in penile cancer, Chapter 84). Gold-nanoparticle CT (computed tomography) contrast, quantum dots |
| **Sentinel node** | Technetium-99m **nanocolloid** for dynamic sentinel node biopsy in penile cancer (Chapter 84) |
| **Diagnosis** | Nanowire and nanoparticle biosensors detecting PSA (prostate-specific antigen) and urinary markers at very low levels |
| **Drug delivery** | Nanoparticle (albumin-bound) paclitaxel in urothelial and prostate cancer (Chapter 164: tolerated, no pathological complete response); **NanoPac** intratumoral paclitaxel (Chapter 163); liposomal drugs; mucoadhesive intravesical carriers; siRNA (small interfering RNA) delivery |
| **Focal ablation** | **Gold nanoshells (AuroLase):** collect in tumour vessels and are heated by near-infrared laser; about three-quarters success on 12-month biopsy (Chapter 163). Magnetic nanoparticle hyperthermia |
| **Irreversible electroporation** | Electric pulses create **nanoscale membrane pores** ("NanoKnife"); spares matrix, vessels and ducts; investigational (Chapter 143) |
| **Devices** | Silver and other nanocoatings on catheters and stents against biofilm and encrustation |
| **Tissue engineering** | Electrospun nanofibre scaffolds that mimic extracellular matrix |
| **Stone disease** | "Calcifying nanoparticles" (nanobacteria) linked to Randall plaque, not proven causal (Chapter 95); nano-CT of stones and tissue |

### Limitations
- Toxicity and long-term tissue accumulation; uptake by liver and spleen; manufacturing cost; regulatory hurdles. Most uses remain experimental.

**Diagram to draw:** nanoparticle with drug core, polymer shell and surface PSMA ligand entering a leaky tumour vessel.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - 1–100 nm; enhanced permeability and retention allows tumour targeting.
> - USPIO MRI: benign nodes lose signal; metastatic nodes do not.
> - AuroLase gold nanoshells: photothermal focal therapy.

## Different types of trocars in laparoscopy.
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2022-Summer Paper 4, Q3c

### Definition
- A **trocar** is a **cannula (port) plus an obturator**; 3–20 mm diameter, 5–20 cm long (Chapter 18).
- Parts: obturator tip, cannula, **valve** (keeps gas in), insufflation side-arm, retention mechanism.

### Classification
| Basis | Types |
|---|---|
| **Obturator tip** | Bladed (cutting) with spring safety shield; **bladeless (blunt dilating)**; **optical (visual)**; blunt (Hasson) |
| **Material** | Reusable metal; disposable plastic |
| **Valve** | Flap or trumpet valve (needs reducer); **multiseal** (5–12 mm, no reducer); **valveless (AirSeal)** |
| **Retention** | Smooth (suture-anchored); threaded sleeve; expandable arms; **balloon (Blunt Tip)** |
| **Special** | Hasson open cannula; radially expanding; hand ports; single-site multichannel ports; robotic 8-mm metal ports |

### Main types explained
- **Bladed trocars:** cut through tissue; more epigastric injury and port-site hernia. Now of historical interest.
- **Bladeless (dilating) trocars:** spread muscle fibres. About **fivefold less inferior epigastric injury**; trocar-site bleeding 3% vs 9%. Switching cut port hernia from 1.83% to 0.19%. **Fascial closure only for midline ports >10 mm** or stretched sites.
- **Optical trocars:** clear tip with a 0° scope inside; each layer is seen during entry.
- **Hasson cannula:** blunt obturator and cone, fixed with fascial stay sutures after open entry; safer with adhesions; leaks more gas.
- **Radially expanding trocar:** a sleeve over the Veress needle is dilated to 12 mm; less port-site bleeding and pain (Chapter 39).
- **Balloon (Blunt Tip) trocar:** internal balloon and external foam cuff seal the wall; prevents leak and **subcutaneous emphysema** in retroperitoneoscopy.
- **AirSeal valveless port:** stable pneumoperitoneum at 8–12 mmHg despite suction.
- **Single-site ports:** TriPort; da Vinci SP (single-port) 25–27-mm guide. **Hand ports:** GelPort.

### Safety points
- **Never use a metal trocar with a plastic retaining ring**: capacitive coupling burns viscera out of view.
- Insert under vision; ports 8–10 cm apart.

**Also know:** Veress needle (14-gauge, spring-loaded blunt stylet) is an entry needle, not a trocar.

**Diagram to draw:** trocar with obturator tip types (bladed, bladeless, optical) and valve.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Trocar = cannula + obturator.
> - Bladeless: fewer epigastric injuries and hernias.
> - Balloon trocar prevents retroperitoneal emphysema.

## General complications related to laparoscopy.
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2022-Summer Paper 4, Q3e
(adapted from 2019-Summer Paper 1, Q2)

### Incidence
- Historically about 4%, mortality 0.03–0.08%. Contemporary series 13–22% because procedures are complex. **Vascular, then adjacent-organ injuries are commonest.**
- Risk factors: complex procedures, operating time >4 h, creatinine >1.5 mg/dL, learning curve.

### Complications by phase
| Phase | Complication | Key points |
|---|---|---|
| **Access (Veress, first trocar)** | Preperitoneal insufflation; vessel or bowel puncture; **major vascular injury (0.11–2%)** | Confirm Veress tests; open access with adhesions. Major vessel: leave trocar in, laparotomy |
| **Insufflation** | **Gas embolism**; subcutaneous emphysema; pneumothorax (1.6–4%); pneumomediastinum, pneumopericardium; hypercarbia | Embolism: end-tidal CO₂ (carbon dioxide) rises then falls, mill-wheel murmur → stop gas, left lateral head-down, 100% oxygen, aspirate central line |
| **Secondary trocars** | Epigastric bleeding; crossing swords, rollover | Transilluminate; bladeless trocars; insert under vision |
| **Dissection** | Thermal or mechanical bowel injury; vascular injury; bladder or ureter injury | Four electrosurgical mechanisms below |
| **Positioning** | Nerve injury (about 2.8%: brachial plexus, femoral, sciatic); **rhabdomyolysis** (about 1% retroperitoneal) | Padding, kidney rest <1 h, cases <5 h |
| **Exit** | Bowel entrapment, late port bleeding | Inspect each site at 5 mmHg |
| **Postoperative** | Port-site hernia (0.3–5.4%), wound infection, shoulder-tip pain, DVT (deep vein thrombosis) | Close fascia of large ports |

### Electrosurgical bowel injury: four mechanisms
1. **Inappropriate activation** out of view.
2. **Direct coupling** with another metal instrument.
3. **Capacitive coupling** (metal cannula in a plastic retainer).
4. **Insulation failure.**
- Monopolar injury presents **late (up to 18 days)**; resect with margin.

### Missed bowel injury (Bishoff)
