- Not classic peritonitis: **pain at one trocar site, leucopenia, low fever, distension, diarrhoea** at 24–48 h.
- **CT (computed tomography) with oral contrast**; free gas is normal for up to 7–9 days.

### Physiological complications
- Hypercarbia and acidosis; oliguria from renal vein compression; reduced venous return; raised intracranial pressure in head-down tilt.

**Diagram to draw:** abdominal wall showing capacitive coupling through a metal cannula in a plastic anchor.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Vascular injury is commonest; leave the trocar in place.
> - Gas embolism: left lateral, head down.
> - Missed bowel injury: trocar-site pain, leucopenia.

## Thalium laser in urology
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 1 Paper 4, Q3c
(also covered in 2024-Winter Paper 4, Q3c)

*Assumed meaning: thulium laser ("thalium" is a spelling error).*

### Types and physics
| | Tm:YAG (thulium:yttrium-aluminium-garnet) | TFL (thulium fibre laser) |
|---|---|---|
| Wavelength | 2013 nm | **1940 nm** (water absorption peak) |
| Source | Diode-pumped solid crystal | Diode-pumped thulium-doped silica fibre |
| Output | Continuous wave (now also pulsed) | Pulsed or continuous |
| Water absorption | High | About 4× holmium |

- Optical penetration is shallowest of urological lasers: about 0.2 mm (chapter values for TFL: 0.08–0.2 mm), against 0.31–0.4 mm for Ho:YAG (holmium:YAG, 2120 nm).
- Shallow absorption gives precise vaporisation and clean incision, but **carbonisation**.

### Stone lithotripsy (TFL)
- Frequency up to about 2000 Hz (holmium about 120 Hz); pulse energy as low as 0.025 J.
- **Low peak power, long pulse** → photothermal "dusting", minimal retropulsion.
- Fibres 50–150 µm → better irrigation and scope deflection. Small, quiet unit; no dedicated high-voltage outlet.
- Settings: start 0.6 J × 6 Hz; dusting 0.2 J × 40 Hz; avoid >40 Hz (pulses miss the stone). "Paint, do not drill."
- RCTs (randomised controlled trials): better than 30 W holmium; equal stone-free rate to 120 W pulse-modulated holmium with less retropulsion.

### Prostate
- **Techniques:** ThuVARP (vaporesection), ThuVAP (vaporisation), ThuVEP (vapoenucleation), **ThuLEP** (enucleation, 2010; mostly blunt dissection), ThuFLEP (fibre laser enucleation).
- Settings: 70–90 W enucleation, 30 W coagulation; morcellation as for HoLEP (holmium laser enucleation).
- Outcomes: ThuLEP equals HoLEP; size-independent; safe on anticoagulants (AUA [American Urological Association]). Retrograde ejaculation about four-fifths.

### Other uses
- **Also know:** en bloc bladder tumour resection; UTUC (upper tract urothelial carcinoma) ablation; incision of strictures.

### Safety
- Heat follows power: ≤10 W in the ureter, high flow, access sheath. Eyewear for all.
- Concerns: efficacy on all stone types; thermal injury at high power.

**Diagram to draw:** water absorption curve marking 1940, 2013 and 2120 nm.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - TFL 1940 nm, Tm:YAG 2013 nm; water absorption about 4× holmium.
> - TFL: low peak power, fine dust, thin fibres.
> - ThuLEP is size-independent and equal to HoLEP.

## Morcellation
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 1 Paper 4, Q3d

### Definition
- Cutting a large specimen into small pieces so it leaves through a port, small incision or endoscope.
- **Manual:** ring forceps or Kelly clamp inside a bag. **Power (electromechanical):** rotating cylindrical blade (laparoscopy) or endoscopic tissue morcellator (prostate).

### 1. Endoscopic morcellation after enucleation
- Made **HoLEP (holmium laser enucleation of prostate)** practical; also ThuLEP (thulium laser enucleation).
- **Steps:**
  1. Achieve good haemostasis first.
  2. Pass a long nephroscope with a 5 mm channel.
  3. Run two inflow lines fully open to keep the bladder distended.
  4. Engage lobes with suction; cut only in mid-bladder, away from the wall.
  5. Collect fragments in a trap for histology.
- Devices: Piranha (rotating blades), VersaCut (oscillating).
- **Complications:** bladder mucosal injury (stop, release suction, disengage; mostly catheter only); intraperitoneal perforation needs laparotomy; **retained adenoma** (from malfunction or poor vision) needs repeat morcellation.

### 2. Laparoscopic morcellation (kidney, spleen)
- **LapSac** (nylon with polyurethane lining) is impermeable to tumour cells and bacteria.
- **Rules:** purpose-built bag; drapes; change gloves and instruments after; no deep forceps passes; keep pneumoperitoneum and watch the bag.

| | Intact extraction | Morcellation |
|---|---|---|
| Incision | Larger | Small, better cosmesis |
| Pain, time, stay | Similar | Similar |
| Pathology | Full staging | Type and grade kept; size, capsule, vein invasion, and depth in UTUC (upper tract urothelial carcinoma) lost |
| Seeding | — | Rare port-site recurrence |

- Splenectomy morcellation risks **splenosis** if the bag ruptures.

### 3. FDA 2014 warning (gynaecology)
- In 2014 the FDA (Food and Drug Administration) warned against laparoscopic power morcellation in hysterectomy or myomectomy for fibroids, because unsuspected uterine sarcoma (about 1 in 350) was spread and upstaged.
- A boxed warning followed; contained (in-bag) morcellation is advised.
- Lesson for urology: avoid uncontained power morcellation of possible malignancy.

### Complications summary
- Bag rupture, seeding, lost staging, visceral or vascular injury, retained tissue.

**Diagram to draw:** enucleated lobes in a distended bladder with morcellator suction.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Morcellator made HoLEP practical; distend bladder, haemostasis first.
> - Morcellation loses stage information; histology still obtained.
> - FDA 2014: uncontained power morcellation spreads occult sarcoma.

## KTP\*\*\* laser in urology
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 1 Paper 4, Q3e
(lasers in BPH also in 2017-Winter Paper 2, Q3a)

*Assumed meaning: KTP (potassium titanyl phosphate) laser, the "GreenLight" laser; the asterisks are ignored.*

### Physics
- A 1064 nm Nd:YAG (neodymium:yttrium-aluminium-garnet) beam passes through a **KTP or LBO (lithium triborate) crystal**. This doubles frequency and halves wavelength to **532 nm** (green).
- Green light is absorbed by **haemoglobin**, not water. Optical penetration 0.8 mm; tissue effect 1–3 mm.
- Effect: **photoselective vaporisation** with a thin coagulation rim, so vaporisation is bloodless.

### Platforms
| Generation | Power |
|---|---|
| KTP | 80 W |
| LBO HPS (High Performance System) | 120 W |
| LBO XPS | **180 W**; 750 µm MoXy fibre (metal tip, fluid cooling) |

- Side-firing fibre emits at 70°; 23–24 Fr cystoscope; saline irrigation.

### PVP (photoselective vaporisation of prostate) technique
- Identify ureteric orifices; open the bladder neck first.
- Sweep 15–30° in 1–2 seconds at ≤2 mm working distance. Large bubbles mean efficient vaporisation.
- Pulsatile bleeding: coagulation mode (about 30 W). Energy >5 kJ/cc predicts complete vaporisation.

### Indications
- AUA (American Urological Association): prostates <30 cc and 30–80 cc; men at **higher bleeding risk** (anticoagulation continued). Not >80 cc (high retreatment).

### GOLIATH trial
- RCT (randomised controlled trial), 29 European centres, about 270 men, prostate ≤100 cc: **180 W PVP vs TURP** (transurethral resection of prostate).
- PVP **non-inferior** for IPSS (International Prostate Symptom Score), Qmax and complications at 6 months, 1 and 2 years.
- PVP: shorter catheter time and stay, fewer early reinterventions. TURP: faster. Retrograde ejaculation (about two-thirds) and 2-year reoperation equal.

### Complications
- Dysuria and storage symptoms (highest of transurethral techniques); UTI (urinary tract infection) and epididymitis from coagulum; capsular perforation 0.2–1%; retrograde ejaculation.
- No TUR (transurethral resection) syndrome; transfusion very rare.
- **No tissue** for histology.

### Other uses
- **Also know:** laparoscopic non-contact cutting (largely supplanted); KTP enucleation possible.

**Diagram to draw:** side-firing fibre at 70° vaporising lateral lobe.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - 532 nm: frequency-doubled Nd:YAG; haemoglobin chromophore.
> - GOLIATH: 180 W PVP non-inferior to TURP.
> - Best for anticoagulated men; not >80 cc.

## Stem cell therapy
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 1 Paper 4, Q3f
(tissue engineering also in 2023-Winter Paper 1, Q1)

### Definition
- Stem cells **self-renew** and **differentiate** into specialised cells. Therapy uses them to repair or replace damaged tissue.

### Types
| Type | Source | Comment |
|---|---|---|
| Embryonic | Blastocyst | Pluripotent; ethics, teratoma |
| Adult mesenchymal | Bone marrow, adipose (ADSC, adipose-derived stem cells), umbilical cord | Easy harvest; mainly paracrine |
| Muscle-derived (MDSC) | Skeletal muscle biopsy | Used for sphincter and detrusor |
| iPSCs (induced pluripotent stem cells) | Reprogrammed adult cells | Patient-specific; tumour risk |
| Urine-derived, spermatogonial | Urine; testis | Research |

### Mechanisms
- Differentiation into muscle, nerve or endothelium.
- **Paracrine action:** growth factors, anti-inflammatory, anti-fibrotic and neurotrophic signals.
- Homing to injured tissue; seeding of scaffolds.

### Applications
- **SUI (stress urinary incontinence):**
  - Autologous muscle-derived cells: thigh muscle biopsy → culture → transurethral or periurethral injection into the sphincter.
  - Aim: regenerate the striated sphincter, not just bulk it.
  - Early studies improved; placebo-controlled trials have not shown clear superiority, so it remains investigational.
  - After childbirth injury, stem cell homing chemokines (CXCL12, CCL7) rise.
- **ED (erectile dysfunction):** intracavernosal bone-marrow, adipose or cord cells; animal and small uncontrolled human series only. SMSNA (Sexual Medicine Society of North America): experimental, IRB (institutional review board) protocols only.
- **Underactive detrusor:** muscle-derived cells injected into the bladder wall; two open-label studies improved voiding; research.
- **Tissue engineering:** cell-seeded scaffolds for bladder (Atala, 2006) and urethra (Raya-Rivera, 2011); adipose stem cell-seeded grafts for Peyronie disease (experimental).
- **Fertility:** spermatogonial stem cell banking in prepubertal cancer patients.
- **Kidney:** iPSC kidney organoids (research).

### Challenges
- Poor cell survival and retention; tumour formation (embryonic, iPSC); standardisation, cost and regulation.
- Unregulated "stem cell clinics" offer unproven treatments outside trials, a growing ethical concern.
- **Also know:** cancer stem cells (a different concept) may drive tumour recurrence and resistance.

**Diagram to draw:** muscle biopsy → culture → periurethral injection into sphincter.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Adult stem cells act mainly by paracrine signals.
> - SUI muscle-derived cells and ED stem cells are investigational.
> - Embryonic and iPSCs carry tumour risk.

## Holmium laser
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 2 Paper 4, Q3e
(holmium enucleation in detail in 2023-Winter Paper 4, Q2; stone energy sources in Practice Set 1 Paper 4, Q2)

### Physics
- Ho:YAG (holmium:yttrium-aluminium-garnet), a solid-state, flashlamp-pumped, **pulsed** laser at **2120 nm** (often quoted as 2100 nm).
- Chromophore **water**; penetration about **0.4 mm**. It cuts and vaporises; heat spreads 2–3 mm and seals small vessels.
- Flexible quartz fibres; works in saline.

### Mechanism on stones
- Long pulses (>250 µs) act **photothermally**, not by shock waves: the stone melts or decomposes.
- **Moses effect:** the start of each pulse makes a vapour channel; the rest of the pulse crosses it to the stone. So keep the fibre close and perpendicular.
- Breaks **all stone types**, including cystine and calcium oxalate monohydrate.

### Settings
| Mode | Settings | Effect |
|---|---|---|
| Fragmenting | 0.6–1.0 J × 6–10 Hz | Pieces for basketing; more retropulsion |
| Dusting | 0.2–0.3 J × 40–80 Hz | Fine dust; "popcorning" in a calyx |
| HoLEP enucleation | 2.0 J × 40–50 Hz | Cutting along the capsule |
- Retropulsion rises with pulse energy, falls with longer pulses or pulse modulation.
- Heat tracks power: ≤10 W in the ureter; good outflow.

### Uses
- **Stones:** URS (ureteroscopy), RIRS (retrograde intrarenal surgery), PCNL (percutaneous nephrolithotomy), bladder stones.
- **BPH (benign prostatic hyperplasia):** HoLEP (holmium laser enucleation of the prostate): size-independent, the only endoscopic procedure superior to TURP (transurethral resection of the prostate), safe on anticoagulants.
- **Incisions:** strictures, bladder neck contracture, endopyelotomy, ureterocele.
- **Tumours:** UTUC (upper tract urothelial carcinoma) ablation; en bloc bladder tumour resection.

### Safety
- Eyewear for all; thermal urothelial injury; steep HoLEP learning curve.

### Recent advances
- High-power (120 W) pulse-modulated machines. TFL (thulium fibre laser, 1940 nm) beat low-power holmium but equalled high-power holmium in RCTs (randomised controlled trials).

**Diagram to draw:** vapour bubble (Moses channel) between fibre tip and stone.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - 2120 nm; water chromophore; pulsed; 0.4 mm penetration.
> - Photothermal; Moses effect: fibre close and perpendicular.
> - Fragment 0.6–1.0 J × 6–10 Hz; dust 0.2–0.3 J × 40–80 Hz; ≤10 W in ureter.
> - HoLEP: size-independent, superior to TURP.

## Hemostasis in lap surgery
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 2 Paper 4, Q3f
(haemostasis in laparoscopic partial nephrectomy in 2019-Summer Paper 3, Q1; energy sources in 2015-Summer Paper 2, Q3d)

### Principles
- Devices seal by **denaturing protein**. Plan from imaging; keep a haemorrhage tray ready.

### Effect of pneumoperitoneum
- At 12–15 mmHg CO₂ **tamponades venous bleeding**, hiding it.
- Raise to 20–25 mmHg briefly for venous bleeding. Before exit, **inspect at low pressure (5–10 mmHg)** to unmask hidden bleeders and port-site bleeding.

### Energy devices
| Device | Lateral spread | Note |
|---|---|---|
| Monopolar | 5–7 mm | Capacitive coupling, insulation failure |
| Bipolar | 1–6 mm | Current only between jaws |
| Vessel sealer (LigaSure) | 1–3 mm | Seals vessels **≤7 mm**; one application |
| Ultrasonic (Harmonic) | ≈1 mm | Blade >200 °C; cools slowly; no current |
| Argon beam | Superficial | Raw surfaces; gas embolism |

### Clips
- Titanium clips: no diathermy near them (slip).
- **Hem-o-lok** (polymer, locking): circumferential dissection, see the tip, feel the snap, at least two clips on the patient side with a 2-mm cuff.
- **FDA (Food and Drug Administration) warning:** after donor renal artery stump haemorrhage, the manufacturer contraindicated Hem-o-lok on the **living-donor renal artery**. A later series found it safe with correct technique, but use a stapler there.
- Lapra-Ty absorbable clips act as knots.

### Staplers
- Endo GIA through a 12-mm port; **grey 2.0 mm or white 2.5 mm** for renal vessels; blue or green for thicker tissue.
- **Never fire across clips** (misfire). Reinforce a bleeding staple line with clips.

### Sutures and haemostatic agents
- Intracorporeal or barbed sutures. Adjuncts: fibrin sealant, gelatin–thrombin matrix, oxidised cellulose.

### Managing a venous injury
- Raise pressure → suction → pledget tamponade → sealant → extra port for suturing or Satinsky → hand-assist → convert. Keep the patient well filled (gas embolism).
- Arterial: minor, tamponade; renal artery, staple; aortic, convert.

**Diagram to draw:** Hem-o-lok placement with two patient-side clips and a 2-mm cuff.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Pneumoperitoneum hides venous bleeding: inspect at low pressure before exit.
> - LigaSure seals ≤7 mm; spread monopolar 5–7 mm, ultrasonic ≈1 mm.
> - Hem-o-lok contraindicated on the donor renal artery.
> - Never staple across clips; grey or white loads for vessels.

## Advances in lap surgeries
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 3 Paper 4, Q3a
(related: LESS in 2023-Winter Paper 4, Q1; energy sources in 2015-Summer Paper 2, Q3d)

### Introduction
- Since the first laparoscopic nephrectomy (Clayman, 1991), advances aim at **less access trauma, better vision, safer haemostasis, gentler physiology and faster training**.

### 1. Access and insufflation
- **Non-bladed dilating and optical trocars**: port-site hernia fell from 1.83% to 0.19%, wall bleeding from 0.83% to 0.16%.
- **Balloon dilators** for retroperitoneal and extraperitoneal space.
- **AirSeal** valveless system: stable pneumoperitoneum at **8–12 mmHg** despite suction, smoke evacuation, less CO₂ absorption.
- Lower pressure (12 rather than 15 mmHg): less pain and oliguria.

### 2. Fewer and smaller incisions
| Technique | Gain | Limitation |
|---|---|---|
| **LESS** (laparoendoscopic single-site surgery) | Cosmesis, less analgesia | No triangulation, clashing |
| **NOTES** (natural orifice transluminal endoscopic surgery) | No abdominal scar (hybrid transvaginal extraction) | No proven benefit |
| Mini-laparoscopy (2–3 mm instruments) | Less pain, cosmesis | Instrument flexibility |
| Hand-assisted | Tactile feedback, fast bleeding control | Larger wound |

### 3. Vision
- Chip-on-tip HD (high-definition) and **3D** laparoscopes; deflectable tips.
- **ICG (indocyanine green) near-infrared fluorescence**: vessel mapping for selective clamping in partial nephrectomy, ureteric identification, sentinel nodes.
- Laparoscopic ultrasound; 3D-printed planning models.

### 4. Haemostasis and suturing
- **Advanced bipolar sealers** (LigaSure: vessels ≤7 mm, 1–3 mm spread) and **ultrasonic shears** (about 1 mm spread, but very hot jaws).
- **Hem-o-lok** clips, **barbed sutures** (shorter anastomosis), Lapra-Ty clips, powered articulating staplers.

### 5. Perioperative care
- **ERAS (enhanced recovery after surgery)**: stay about 30% shorter, complications halved; no routine bowel preparation.

### 6. Training
- Box trainers, VR (virtual reality) simulators, EAU **E-BLUS** (European Basic Laparoscopic Urological Skills) certification, 5-day mini-residencies.

### 7. Robot-assisted laparoscopy
- Wristed 3D platforms (da Vinci Xi, SP 2018; Hugo, Versius, SSI Mantra) solved laparoscopic suturing (**see Q3b**).
- **Also know:** augmented reality, artificial intelligence, telesurgery.

**Diagram to draw:** LESS multichannel port in the umbilicus with articulating instruments.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Blunt trocars cut port-site hernia to 0.19%.
> - AirSeal allows 8–12 mmHg working pressure.
> - ICG fluorescence guides selective clamping.
> - LESS: cosmesis at the cost of triangulation.

## Artificial intelligence in urology
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 4 Paper 4, Q3d

### Definitions
- **AI (artificial intelligence):** computer systems doing tasks that need human intelligence: recognising patterns, predicting, deciding.
- **ML (machine learning):** algorithms learn from data rather than fixed rules. **Supervised** (labelled data, e.g. tumour or not), **unsupervised** (finds clusters), reinforcement.
- **Deep learning:** multilayer **ANNs (artificial neural networks)**; **CNNs (convolutional neural networks)** are the commonest for images.
- **NLP (natural language processing)** and **LLMs (large language models)** handle text; **radiomics** extracts quantitative image features.

### Development steps
```
Data collection and labelling → training → internal validation
 → external validation → regulatory approval → use and monitoring
```

### Applications
| Area | Examples |
|---|---|
| Imaging | CNNs detect stones on CT (computed tomography) with very high sensitivity; ANNs detect renal tumours; prostate MRI (magnetic resonance imaging) lesion detection; deep-learning reconstruction allows ultra-low-dose CT (about 1 mSv) |
| Pathology | Automated cancer detection and Gleason grading on digital slides; Paige Prostate was the first FDA (Food and Drug Administration)-authorised AI pathology tool (2021) |
| Endoscopy | Real-time bladder-tumour detection at cystoscopy; future ureteroscopes setting laser energy, irrigation, pressure and temperature |
| Stones | Predict composition and stone-free outcome |
| Prognosis | Radiomics, pathomics and genomics combined for RCC (renal cell carcinoma) prognosis; AI on biopsy slides predicting benefit of ADT (androgen deprivation therapy) with radiotherapy |
| Robotic surgery | Video and kinematic data for skill assessment, outcome prediction, real-time guidance; future semi-autonomy |
| Paediatrics | Deep learning on ultrasound predicts CKD (chronic kidney disease) progression in valves; VUR (vesicoureteral reflux) infection-risk models |
| Others | Urodynamics interpretation; LLM chatbots for patient information |

### Advantages
- Fast big-data processing; consistency; decision support.

### Limitations and ethics
- Needs large, high-quality labelled data; **bias** and poor generalisability (overfitting); "black box" decisions; few prospective or external validations.
- Privacy and data sharing; medico-legal liability; LLM "hallucinations"; regulation.
- AI assists, not replaces, the urologist or radiologist.

**Diagram to draw:** neural network: input layer (image pixels), hidden layers, output (stone or no stone).

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Deep learning is part of ML, which is part of AI; CNNs for images.
> - CNNs: very high sensitivity for CT stones.
> - Paige Prostate: first FDA-authorised AI pathology tool (2021).
> - Limits: data, bias, black box, validation, liability.

## 3D laparoscopy
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 5 Paper 4, Q3b

### Introduction
- Standard laparoscopy gives a **two-dimensional (2D)** image. Depth is judged from monocular cues (shadow, size, motion parallax, touch), which slows suturing.
- **Three-dimensional (3D) laparoscopy** restores **stereoscopic depth perception**, as in robotic surgery (Chapter 18: two-lens binocular systems give depth perception).

### Principle
- Two lenses or two chip-on-tip sensors, a few millimetres apart, capture two images from slightly different angles, like the two eyes.
- The monitor shows both images; **polarised glasses** send one to each eye. The brain fuses them, and the **binocular disparity** is perceived as depth.
- **Also know:** active-shutter glasses; head-mounted displays; glasses-free (autostereoscopic) screens; 3D scopes at 0° and 30°, and with a deflectable tip; 3D 4K systems.

### Advantages
- Better depth judgement and hand–eye coordination.
- **Shorter time for complex tasks**, especially intracorporeal suturing and knotting, with fewer errors.
- **Shorter learning curve**; novices gain more than experts.
- Gives robotic-type vision at much lower cost.

### Disadvantages
- Visual strain, headache, dizziness or nausea in some users; glasses dim the image.
- Every team member needs glasses and a correct viewing distance and angle.
- Higher cost than 2D; 30° rotation needs care.
- No wristed instruments, tremor filtering or motion scaling: these come only with the robot.

### Urological uses
- Reconstructive steps: **pyeloplasty**, vesicourethral anastomosis in laparoscopic radical prostatectomy, **partial nephrectomy** renorrhaphy, ureteric reimplantation, donor nephrectomy.

### Evidence
- Randomised trials and meta-analyses in laboratory tasks and laparoscopic surgery show shorter operating and suturing times with 3D. Differences in complications and oncological outcomes are not established.

| Feature | 2D laparoscopy | 3D laparoscopy | Robot |
|---|---|---|---|
| Depth perception | No | Yes | Yes (magnified) |
| Wristed instruments | No | No | Yes |
| Cost | Low | Moderate | High |

**Diagram to draw:** two lenses at the scope tip, two images, polarised glasses, fused 3D image in the brain.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Two lenses, two images, polarised glasses: binocular disparity gives depth.
> - Main gain: faster, more accurate suturing; shorter learning curve.
> - Drawbacks: eye strain, headache, cost, glasses for all.
> - Robotic vision at low cost, but without wristed instruments.

## Telemedicine in urology
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 5 Paper 4, Q3c

### Definition
- **Telemedicine** is delivery of health care at a distance using information and communication technology (video, phone, messaging, data transfer).
- **COVID-19 (coronavirus disease 2019)** distancing accelerated its adoption. Chapter 2: urologists' telehealth use rose sharply in 2019–2020.

### Types
| Type | Method | Urological example |
|---|---|---|
| Synchronous | Real-time video or phone consultation | Stone, PSA (prostate-specific antigen) and post-operative follow-up |
| Asynchronous (store-and-forward) | Reports, images, bladder diaries sent for later review | Scan reports, uroflow traces |
| Remote monitoring | Apps and home devices | Bladder diary apps, home uroflowmetry |
| Provider-to-provider | E-consults, telementoring, tele-tumour boards | Haematuria triage (Chapter 8) |
| Telesurgery | Remote robotic surgery | Experimental; limited by latency |

### Uses in urology
- **History-driven care:** LUTS (lower urinary tract symptoms) review, results, drug titration.
- ED (erectile dysfunction) evaluation by selected telemedicine (Chapter 72); men's health in large, sparse regions (Chapter 68).
- After mid-urethral sling, telemedicine follow-up is safe, and app-based follow-up gave higher satisfaction than office visits (Chapter 116).
- Internet-based pelvic floor training equalled a postal programme in an RCT (randomised controlled trial) (Chapter 114).

### Benefits (Chapter 2)
- Access for rural and underserved patients; less travel; shorter waits; relatives join remotely; sustained satisfaction.

### Limitations (Chapter 2)
- **No examination or in-office tests**: vital signs, cystoscopy, PVR (post-void residual).
- Needs connectivity and a secure, simple platform; hard for elderly or non-technical patients.
- No assistant to pre-gather results; more no-shows.
- **Medico-legal:** US licensure is state-based, and pandemic payment waivers have ended.

### Indian framework
- **Telemedicine Practice Guidelines (2020)**: only a registered medical practitioner may consult; identity of patient and doctor confirmed; consent and records kept; narcotic and Schedule X drugs may not be prescribed.
- Government platform **eSanjeevani**; patient data covered by the Digital Personal Data Protection Act 2023.

**Diagram to draw:** patient (home or district hospital) linked by video and data to the urologist, with remote monitoring feeding in.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Telemedicine trades examination for access.
> - Best for follow-up, results and history-driven visits.
> - Limits: no examination, connectivity, elderly, medico-legal.
> - India: Telemedicine Practice Guidelines 2020; eSanjeevani.

## Simulators in urology
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 5 Paper 4, Q3d

### Rationale
- Fewer training cases and complex technology mean skills must be learnt **outside the patient**.
- Chapter 1: simulation is used to **acquire and maintain skills and to warm up** before operating.

### Types
| Type | Examples | Strengths and limits |
|---|---|---|
| **Bench / box trainer** | Peg transfer, cutting, suturing; chicken oesophagus anastomosis; 3D (three-dimensional)-printed perfused hydrogel kidney | Cheap, proven skill gains; limited realism |
| **VR (virtual reality)** | LapSim, LapMentor, RobotiX Mentor, SimNow (robotic prostatectomy); endourology trainers for cystoscopy, ureteroscopy, PCNL (percutaneous nephrolithotomy), TURP (transurethral resection of the prostate) | Records metrics (precision, economy of motion), builds curricula, sets expert benchmarks; costly; poor tissue feel |
| **Live animal** | Pig | Real bleeding; ethics, cost |
| **Cadaver** | Fresh-frozen | Real human anatomy; scarce |
| **Team (non-technical)** | High-fidelity theatre crises | Communication and teamwork |

### Validity (needed before a simulator is trusted)
- **Face:** looks real. **Content:** teaches the right skills. **Construct:** experts score better than novices. **Concurrent:** agrees with an established test. **Predictive:** simulator score forecasts operating performance.

### Evidence (Chapter 18, Chapter 12)
- VR training **beats no training or box training** for novices and improves live performance and operating time.
- Robotic simulator scores correlate with live RARP (robot-assisted radical prostatectomy) performance.
- **FRS (Fundamentals of Robotic Surgery):** a multi-institutional RCT (randomised controlled trial) showed better speed and accuracy.
- Ureteroscopy team simulation taught non-technical skills better than lectures (randomised trial).

### Structured curricula and assessment
- **EAU (European Association of Urology) E-BLUS** (European Basic Laparoscopic Urological Skills): online theory, then dry-model tasks, then examination.
- **Proficiency-based progression:** move on only after reaching expert benchmarks.
- Scoring: **GEARS (Global Evaluative Assessment of Robotic Skills)** and **OSATS (Objective Structured Assessment of Technical Skills)**.

### Limitations
- Cost, poor tissue feel, limited proof of better patient outcomes.

**Diagram to draw:** pyramid: box trainer at the base, then VR, then animal or cadaver, then supervised surgery at the top.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Bench, VR, animal, cadaver and team simulation.
> - Validity: face, content, construct, concurrent, predictive.
> - VR beats box or no training for novices.
> - E-BLUS and FRS; proficiency-based progression; GEARS.

## Virtual and augmented reality in urology
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 5 Paper 4, Q3e

### Definitions
- **Virtual reality (VR):** the user is immersed in a fully computer-generated environment, usually through a head-mounted display.
- **Augmented reality (AR):** digital images are laid over the real view, for example a 3D (three-dimensional) tumour model on the robotic camera image.
- **Mixed reality:** virtual holograms are anchored in the room and can be handled.

### Workflow
```
CT (computed tomography) / MRI (magnetic resonance imaging)
   -> segmentation of organ, tumour, vessels, collecting system
   -> 3D virtual model -> viewed in VR, 3D-printed, or overlaid (AR)
   -> registration to the live surgical view
```

### Applications
| Use | Examples |
|---|---|
| **Pre-operative planning** | Partial nephrectomy: tumour depth, arterial branches, selective clamping. **PCNL (percutaneous nephrolithotomy)**: calyx and tract choice (Chapter 98: immersive VR and 3D printing show early promise). Prostatectomy: MRI lesion model to plan nerve-sparing |
| **Intra-operative navigation** | AR overlay in the robot console to locate an endophytic tumour, hilar vessels, or a prostate lesion near the capsule |
| **Education** | VR anatomy, 360° operation videos; simulation, see Q3d |
| **Patient counselling** | Patients understand their anatomy and planned operation; better-informed consent |
| **Paediatrics** | VR distraction reduces anxiety before surgery and procedures such as catheterisation and VCUG (voiding cystourethrogram) (Chapters 35 and 38) |

### Limitations
- **Registration error:** organs move and deform with breathing, pneumoperitoneum and dissection, so a rigid overlay drifts; manual alignment is still common.
- Segmentation time and cost.
- Chapter 161: promising but **resource-intensive and hard to validate**.
- Chapter 98: 3D reformatting added nothing over orthogonal CT measurements for predicting stone-free rate.
- Little outcome evidence; cybersickness with headsets.

### Future
- **AI (artificial intelligence)** for automatic segmentation and real-time tracking.
- **Digital surgery:** reconstruction of the patient's own anatomy allows rehearsal before the operation (Chapter 147).

**Diagram to draw:** CT kidney, segmented 3D model, and overlay of the model on the robotic view of a renal tumour.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - VR immerses; AR overlays on reality.
> - Main uses: planning partial nephrectomy, PCNL and prostatectomy; navigation; teaching; counselling.
> - Biggest problem: registration on moving, deforming organs.
> - Evidence for outcomes still limited.

## 3D printing in urology
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 6 Paper 4, Q3a

### Definition
- **3D (three-dimensional) printing** is additive manufacturing: a solid object is built layer by layer from a digital model.
- In urology it turns a patient's CT (computed tomography) or MRI (magnetic resonance imaging) into a physical replica of kidney, tumour, vessels or prostate.

### Workflow
```
Thin-slice CT/MRI (DICOM files) → segmentation of kidney, tumour, vessels, calyces
   → STL (standard tessellation language) mesh → printer → post-processing
```
- Takes hours to days; segmentation needs expertise.

### Printing technologies
| Method | Principle | Use |
|---|---|---|
| FDM (fused deposition modelling) | Melted plastic filament extruded | Cheap, rigid models |
| SLA (stereolithography) | Laser cures liquid resin | Fine detail, transparent models |
| Material jetting (PolyJet) | Jetted photopolymers, multi-colour | Tumour, vessels and calyces in different colours |
| SLS (selective laser sintering) | Laser fuses powder | Durable parts, devices |
| **Bioprinting** | Living cells in hydrogel "bio-ink" | Tissue engineering (see Practice Set 6 Paper 4, Q2) |

### Applications
1. **Partial nephrectomy planning:** complex hilar or endophytic tumours. The model shows the tumour against vessels and collecting system, so the surgeon plans the approach and rehearses steps. Promising.
2. **PCNL (percutaneous nephrolithotomy) planning:** staghorn stones, anomalous kidneys; choice of calyx. Early promise; 3D reformatting did not predict stone-free rate better.
3. **Prostate:** models with MRI lesions for margin and nerve-sparing planning and focal therapy.
4. **Patient education and consent:** patients understand their anatomy and the operation better.
5. **Training and simulation:** silicone or hydrogel phantoms for PCNL puncture, partial nephrectomy, pyeloplasty, anastomosis.
6. **Patient-specific devices:** prototype stents, catheters and surgical guides.

### Limitations
- Cost, printing time and segmentation errors.
- Materials do not reproduce tissue feel or bleeding.
- Evidence mostly small series; no proven outcome benefit.

### Future
- Virtual and augmented reality models on the robotic console, point-of-care printing, drug-eluting printed stents.

**Diagram to draw:** CT → segmentation → STL → printer → colour kidney model with tumour, artery and calyces.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Additive manufacturing from CT/MRI via segmentation and an STL file.
> - Main uses: partial nephrectomy and PCNL planning, consent, simulation.
> - Limits: cost, time, unrealistic tissue, weak outcome evidence.

## Rapid Revision Sheet – Section 1 · Basic Principles

*All the key points from this section in one place. Read this in the last 30 minutes before the exam.*

#### Robotics in urology (including robotic vs standard laparoscopy) (LAQ)

- da Vinci 2000; Xi 2014; SP 2018; patents expired 2019; 7 degrees of freedom.
- Yaxley and Coughlin: RARP equal function to open at 12 weeks and 24 months; RAZOR non-inferior; iROC more days out of hospital.
- RAPN trifecta: negative margin, no major complication, warm ischaemia ≤25 min.
- Trendelenburg causes oedema and raised IOP; avoid shoulder braces.
- Robot: 3D, 7 DOF, tremor filtration, but no haptics and high cost.
- Robotic partial nephrectomy: shorter warm ischaemia, fewer conversions.
- Robot made prostatectomy routine; no benefit for simple extirpation.
- Surgeon matters more than platform.

#### LASERs in urology: applications and newer generation laser technologies (new LASER for stone treatment) (LAQ)

- Chromophore sets penetration: TFL shallowest, Ho 0.4 mm, Nd:YAG deepest.
- Holmium lithotripsy is photothermal (Moses); TFL gives less retropulsion.
- HoLEP size-independent, superior to TURP; PVP for anticoagulated men (GOLIATH).
- Nd:YAG for PeIN; eyewear for everyone.
- TFL 1940 nm: water peak, low peak power, minimal retropulsion.
- Moses: two-part pulse through a vapour channel.
- TFL beats low-power, equals high-power pulse-modulated holmium.
- Heat depends on power: ≤10 W in the ureter, ≤25 W in the kidney.
- TFL (1940 nm): fine dust, minimal retropulsion, thin fibres; settings ≤40 Hz often enough.
- Pulse-modulated (Moses) high-power holmium: vapour channel, less retropulsion.
- Safety: ≤10 W in the ureter, ≤25 W in the kidney; access sheath; damage above 43 °C.

#### Flexible endourological instruments, digital and current ureteroscopes (LAQ)

- Working channel plus active deflection made flexible scopes therapeutic.
- fURS: 3.6 Fr channel, ≥270° (>270°) deflection; cystoscope 14–17 Fr.
- Chip-on-tip CMOS digital: better image, larger tip, durability not better; shorter operation.
- Single-use cheaper below about 99 cases/year; LithoVue first single-use (2016).
- Semirigid: two channels; below the iliac vessels.
- Pressure sensors and AI are the newest developments.
- Golden rule: never fire unless the fibre tip is seen on the stone.

#### Natural orifice endoscopic surgery. (LAQ)

- NOTES = transluminal via natural orifice; hybrid transvaginal is the only routinely used urological form.
- Transvaginal extraction removes the most painful wound.
- LESS: one incision, lost triangulation, more hernias.
- Barriers: closure, infection, orientation, platform; no proven benefit yet.

#### Tissue engineering in urology, including recent advances (LAQ)

- Triad: cells, scaffold, signals; vascularity (100–200 µm diffusion) is the main barrier.
- Atala 2006 (7 patients) promising; phase II trial: no gain in compliance or capacity.
- Neo-urinary conduit (conduit trial): half stenosed; no blood supply.
- Raya-Rivera 2011: seeded tubular urethras in 5 boys.
- New: iPSCs, urine-derived cells, bioprinting, organoids.

#### Laparoendoscopic single-site (single-port) surgery: LESS (LAQ)

- LESS = one incision; LESSCAR (2008) standardised the term.
- Core problem: lost triangulation; solved by articulating or curved instruments and robots.
- Same outcomes as laparoscopy with less pain and better cosmesis; more hernias.
- da Vinci SP (FDA 2018): one 25–27-mm cannula, wristed instruments.

#### Basic energy modalities in urological surgery, including bipolar energy (LAQ)

- Electrosurgery at about 500 kHz; current density decides heating.
- Cut continuous low voltage; coagulation interrupted high voltage.
- Capacitive coupling and insulation failure cause unseen burns.
- Cryo below −40 °C, two cycles; RFA 60 °C; MWA less heat sink.
- Bipolar: no pad, saline irrigation, no TUR syndrome.
- Bipolar TURP equals monopolar efficacy with fewer complications.
- Vessel sealers: vessels ≤7 mm, spread 1–3 mm.

#### Urethral stents and metallic stents in urology (SAQ)

- Temporary (Memokath, Allium) vs permanent (UroLume, withdrawn).
- Metal resists extrinsic compression; weakness is ingrowth and encrustation.
- Resonance: coiled, full-length, changed every 12 months.
- Memokath: thermo-expandable nitinol, removable.
- Migration, encrustation, ingrowth; never cross the sphincter.
- AUA: no stent if urethroplasty planned; Optilume and iTind are newer.

#### Describe evidence based medicine. (SAQ)

- Sackett 1996: best evidence + clinical expertise + patient values.
- 5 As: Ask (PICO), Acquire, Appraise, Apply, Assess.
- Level 1a = systematic review of RCTs; GRADE: strong or weak.
- NNT = 1/ARR; beware lead-time, length-time and confounding bias.

#### Operating room safety : the role of surgeon. (SAQ)

- WHO checklist: sign in, time out, sign out.
- Universal Protocol: verify, mark, time-out.
- Count discrepancy: ~100-fold risk of a retained item.
- Fire triangle: surgeon owns ignition.

#### Urological applications in nanotechnology. (SAQ)

- 1–100 nm; enhanced permeability and retention allows tumour targeting.
- USPIO MRI: benign nodes lose signal; metastatic nodes do not.
- AuroLase gold nanoshells: photothermal focal therapy.

#### Different types of trocars in laparoscopy. (SAQ)

- Trocar = cannula + obturator.
- Bladeless: fewer epigastric injuries and hernias.
- Balloon trocar prevents retroperitoneal emphysema.

#### General complications related to laparoscopy. (SAQ)

- Vascular injury is commonest; leave the trocar in place.
- Gas embolism: left lateral, head down.
- Missed bowel injury: trocar-site pain, leucopenia.

#### Thalium laser in urology (SAQ)

- TFL 1940 nm, Tm:YAG 2013 nm; water absorption about 4× holmium.
- TFL: low peak power, fine dust, thin fibres.
- ThuLEP is size-independent and equal to HoLEP.

#### Morcellation (SAQ)

- Morcellator made HoLEP practical; distend bladder, haemostasis first.
- Morcellation loses stage information; histology still obtained.
- FDA 2014: uncontained power morcellation spreads occult sarcoma.

#### KTP\*\*\* laser in urology (SAQ)

- 532 nm: frequency-doubled Nd:YAG; haemoglobin chromophore.
- GOLIATH: 180 W PVP non-inferior to TURP.
- Best for anticoagulated men; not >80 cc.

#### Stem cell therapy (SAQ)

- Adult stem cells act mainly by paracrine signals.
- SUI muscle-derived cells and ED stem cells are investigational.
- Embryonic and iPSCs carry tumour risk.

#### Holmium laser (SAQ)

- 2120 nm; water chromophore; pulsed; 0.4 mm penetration.
- Photothermal; Moses effect: fibre close and perpendicular.
- Fragment 0.6–1.0 J × 6–10 Hz; dust 0.2–0.3 J × 40–80 Hz; ≤10 W in ureter.
- HoLEP: size-independent, superior to TURP.

#### Hemostasis in lap surgery (SAQ)

- Pneumoperitoneum hides venous bleeding: inspect at low pressure before exit.
- LigaSure seals ≤7 mm; spread monopolar 5–7 mm, ultrasonic ≈1 mm.
- Hem-o-lok contraindicated on the donor renal artery.
- Never staple across clips; grey or white loads for vessels.

#### Advances in lap surgeries (SAQ)

- Blunt trocars cut port-site hernia to 0.19%.
- AirSeal allows 8–12 mmHg working pressure.
- ICG fluorescence guides selective clamping.
- LESS: cosmesis at the cost of triangulation.

#### Artificial intelligence in urology (SAQ)

- Deep learning is part of ML, which is part of AI; CNNs for images.
- CNNs: very high sensitivity for CT stones.
- Paige Prostate: first FDA-authorised AI pathology tool (2021).
- Limits: data, bias, black box, validation, liability.

#### 3D laparoscopy (SAQ)

- Two lenses, two images, polarised glasses: binocular disparity gives depth.
- Main gain: faster, more accurate suturing; shorter learning curve.
- Drawbacks: eye strain, headache, cost, glasses for all.
- Robotic vision at low cost, but without wristed instruments.

#### Telemedicine in urology (SAQ)

- Telemedicine trades examination for access.
- Best for follow-up, results and history-driven visits.
- Limits: no examination, connectivity, elderly, medico-legal.
- India: Telemedicine Practice Guidelines 2020; eSanjeevani.

#### Simulators in urology (SAQ)

- Bench, VR, animal, cadaver and team simulation.
- Validity: face, content, construct, concurrent, predictive.
- VR beats box or no training for novices.
- E-BLUS and FRS; proficiency-based progression; GEARS.

#### Virtual and augmented reality in urology (SAQ)

- VR immerses; AR overlays on reality.
- Main uses: planning partial nephrectomy, PCNL and prostatectomy; navigation; teaching; counselling.
- Biggest problem: registration on moving, deforming organs.
- Evidence for outcomes still limited.

#### 3D printing in urology (SAQ)

- Additive manufacturing from CT/MRI via segmentation and an STL file.
- Main uses: partial nephrectomy and PCNL planning, consent, simulation.
- Limits: cost, time, unrealistic tissue, weak outcome evidence.

# Section 2 · Oncology, DSD, Gender & Ageing — Long answer questions (LAQ)

## Rationale of using cancer vaccines in Urology. Explain their mechanism of action. Enumerate various vaccines for renal, prostate and bladder malignancies . Write a note on Sipuleucel-T.
**Type:** LAQ (25 marks)  
**Source:** 2024-Winter Paper 4, Q2

### Introduction
- A **cancer vaccine** is a therapeutic agent that trains the patient's own T cells to recognise **tumour antigens**. It treats existing cancer; it does not prevent it.
- **BCG (bacille Calmette–Guérin)**, a tuberculosis vaccine, was among the first immune treatments for any cancer. It is non-specific. Cancer vaccines aim for a **specific** anticancer response.

### Rationale in urology
- **Immune editing (three Es):** Elimination → Equilibrium → **Escape**. A clinical cancer has escaped by losing antigens, losing MHC (major histocompatibility complex), or recruiting Tregs (regulatory T cells). Vaccines try to restart the response.
- **Kidney:** rare spontaneous regression of metastases after nephrectomy; responses to IL-2 (interleukin-2) and interferon.
- **Prostate:** slow growth gives time to build a response; **organ-specific antigens** (PSA [prostate-specific antigen], PAP [prostatic acid phosphatase]).
- **Bladder:** BCG success; high mutation load gives many neoantigens.
- Low toxicity; possible lasting memory.

### Mechanism of action
- Two components: **antigen** (overexpressed in tumour) + **adjuvant** (a danger signal, e.g. GM-CSF [granulocyte-macrophage colony-stimulating factor], which activates DCs [dendritic cells]).

```
Intradermal antigen + adjuvant
  → immature DC takes up antigen; adjuvant matures it
  → DC migrates to lymph node
  → peptide on MHC I/II = signal 1; B7 costimulation = signal 2
  → CD4 Th1 help (IL-2, interferon-γ) + CD8 cytotoxic T cells
  → T cells traffic to tumour → apoptosis of antigen-bearing cells
  → memory T cells
```

- Without signal 2 the T cell becomes tolerant, hence the adjuvant.
- **Prime–boost:** first dose primes, later doses boost.
- **Delayed effect:** survival may improve without tumour shrinkage or PSA fall.
- **Resistance:** PD-L1 (programmed death ligand-1) on tumour, Tregs, MDSCs (myeloid-derived suppressor cells), MHC loss. This is the basis for combining vaccines with checkpoint inhibitors.

### Types (platforms)
| Platform | Example |
|---|---|
| Whole-cell (autologous or allogeneic) | Reniale (RCC [renal cell carcinoma]); GVAX (prostate) |
| Peptide/protein | IMA901 |
| Dendritic-cell (ex vivo loaded) | Sipuleucel-T; AGS-003 (rocapuldencel-T) |
| Viral vector (poxvirus) | PROSTVAC-VF; TroVax (MVA-5T4) |
| Heat-shock protein–peptide | Vitespen (HSPPC-96) |
| DNA/mRNA, personalised neoantigen | Phase I–II trials |

### Vaccines by organ
**Renal cell carcinoma**
- **IMA901:** 10 tumour-associated peptides + GM-CSF, low-dose cyclophosphamide to deplete Tregs; HLA-A*02+ (human leucocyte antigen) metastatic RCC with sunitinib. **No OS (overall survival) benefit.**
- **AGS-003 (rocapuldencel-T):** autologous DCs loaded with tumour RNA, with sunitinib. **Terminated for lack of efficacy.**
- **TroVax** with standard first-line therapy: no OS benefit. **Vitespen** (adjuvant): no overall benefit.
- **Reniale** (adjuvant autologous tumour-cell vaccine): claimed benefit, not adopted.
- Checkpoint inhibitors, not vaccines, are today's RCC immunotherapy.

**Prostate cancer**
- **Sipuleucel-T:** the **only approved** vaccine (below).
- **PROSTVAC-VF:** vaccinia prime, fowlpox boost, encoding PSA + TRICOM (three costimulatory molecules). Phase III **closed for futility**.
- **GVAX:** allogeneic whole-cell lines secreting GM-CSF. Phase III trials **failed**.
- Also: DCVAC/PCa (phase III negative); DNA vaccines against PAP (trials).

**Bladder cancer**
- **BCG:** intravesical live attenuated mycobacteria; standard for high-risk NMIBC (non-muscle-invasive bladder cancer).
- **Gene and viral therapy:** **nadofaragene firadenovec** (adenovirus carrying IFN-α2b gene; FDA [Food and Drug Administration] approved December 2022 for BCG-unresponsive CIS [carcinoma in situ]); **cretostimogene** (oncolytic adenovirus with GM-CSF gene).
- **N-803:** IL-15 superagonist given with BCG.
- Peptide and neoantigen vaccines: experimental only.

### Why most vaccines failed
- Tolerance to self-antigens; immunosuppressive tumour environment; dysfunctional patient DCs; bulky late disease; progression endpoints miss delayed effects.
- **Pattern: only sipuleucel-T succeeded in phase III.**

### Note on Sipuleucel-T
- **Nature:** autologous cellular immunotherapy: the patient's **CD54+ APCs (antigen-presenting cells)** activated with **PA2024**, a fusion protein of **PAP + GM-CSF**.
- **Manufacture and dosing:**
  1. Leukapheresis collects PBMCs (peripheral blood mononuclear cells).
  2. Cells incubated about 36 h with PA2024 at a central facility.
  3. Infused intravenously about 3 days after collection.
  4. **Three doses at 2-week intervals** (one month course).
- **Mechanism:** primes PAP-specific CD4 and CD8 T cells; later infusions boost.
- **IMPACT trial (Kantoff, NEJM 2010):** 512 men, 2:1 versus placebo, asymptomatic or minimally symptomatic mCRPC (metastatic castration-resistant prostate cancer).
  - **OS 25.8 vs 21.7 months (HR [hazard ratio] 0.78)**, gain 4.1 months, despite placebo crossover.
  - **No change in time to progression or PSA.**
- **FDA approval April 2010:** first therapeutic vaccine for a solid tumour.
- **Indication:** asymptomatic or minimally symptomatic mCRPC, good performance status, **no visceral metastases, no opioid need**; usually before chemotherapy.
- **Toxicity:** mild infusion reactions (fever, chills, fatigue); premedicate with paracetamol and antihistamine.
- **Limitations:** cost, logistics, no response marker; not available in India.

**Diagram to draw:** sipuleucel-T flow: leukapheresis → PA2024 loading → reinfusion → T-cell priming.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Vaccine = antigen + adjuvant; DC gives signal 1 (MHC–peptide) and signal 2 (costimulation).
> - IMA901, AGS-003, PROSTVAC, GVAX all failed in phase III.
> - Sipuleucel-T: PAP–GM-CSF; IMPACT OS 25.8 vs 21.7 months; no PSA or progression effect.
> - BCG is the original urological immunotherapy.

## Discuss in brief, present concepts in various GU cancers
**Type:** LAQ (25 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 4 Paper 4, Q1

*Read as: the current key concepts in diagnosis and management of each GU (genitourinary) cancer: kidney, upper tract, bladder, prostate, testis and penis.*

### Common themes
- **De-escalation:** surveillance and organ sparing for low-risk disease.
- **Precision:** molecular classification, germline testing and better imaging (mpMRI (multiparametric MRI, magnetic resonance imaging), PSMA (prostate-specific membrane antigen) PET (positron emission tomography)).
- **Perioperative systemic therapy:** immune checkpoint inhibitors (ICIs), ADCs (antibody–drug conjugates) and targeted drugs.
- **Robotic surgery** as the default approach.

### 1. Kidney: RCC (renal cell carcinoma)
- **WHO (World Health Organization) 2022:** new molecularly defined types (FH (fumarate hydratase)-deficient, SDH (succinate dehydrogenase)-deficient, TFE3-rearranged RCC).
- **Mechanism:** VHL (von Hippel–Lindau) loss lets HIF (hypoxia-inducible factor) accumulate and drive VEGF (vascular endothelial growth factor). This explains why VEGF TKIs (tyrosine kinase inhibitors) and the HIF-2α inhibitor **belzutifan** work.
- **Small renal mass:** active surveillance in the elderly or unfit; renal mass biopsy; **partial nephrectomy** for cT1 to avoid CKD (chronic kidney disease); thermal ablation for small tumours.
- **Adjuvant pembrolizumab** after nephrectomy for high-risk RCC (**KEYNOTE-564**: better disease-free and overall survival).
- **Metastatic:** IMDC (International Metastatic RCC Database Consortium) risk groups. First line is an **ICI combination**: nivolumab plus ipilimumab, or an ICI plus TKI (pembrolizumab–axitinib, nivolumab–cabozantinib, pembrolizumab–lenvatinib). **CARMENA (2018):** sunitinib alone was non-inferior to cytoreductive nephrectomy plus sunitinib in intermediate/poor risk, so cytoreduction is now selective. SBRT (stereotactic body radiotherapy) for oligometastases.

### 2. Upper tract urothelial carcinoma (UTUC)
- **Risk stratification (EAU (European Association of Urology)):** low risk (unifocal, <2 cm, low-grade, non-invasive on CT (computed tomography) urography) goes to **kidney-sparing** ureteroscopic ablation or segmental ureterectomy; UGN-101 (mitomycin gel) is an option. High risk goes to **RNU (radical nephroureterectomy) with bladder cuff** and template lymph node dissection.
- **Single postoperative intravesical mitomycin** after RNU cuts bladder recurrence (**ODMIT-C**: 28% to 16% at 1 year).
- **POUT:** adjuvant platinum chemotherapy improved disease-free survival in pT2–T4 or N+ disease.
- **Also know:** screen for **Lynch syndrome**; aristolochic acid (Balkan) nephropathy.

### 3. Bladder
- **NMIBC (non-muscle-invasive bladder cancer):** EAU risk groups; en bloc TURBT (transurethral resection of bladder tumour); blue-light or narrow-band cystoscopy; re-TURBT for T1; BCG (bacillus Calmette–Guérin) induction plus maintenance. **BCG-unresponsive** disease: radical cystectomy is standard; bladder-sparing options are pembrolizumab, nadofaragene firadenovec (gene therapy) and N-803 with BCG.
- **MIBC (muscle-invasive):** VI-RADS (Vesical Imaging Reporting and Data System) on MRI; **cisplatin-based neoadjuvant chemotherapy** then cystectomy; **trimodality** bladder preservation in selected patients; adjuvant **nivolumab** (**CheckMate 274**: disease-free survival 20.8 vs 10.8 months); perioperative durvalumab with chemotherapy (**NIAGARA, 2024**) improved event-free and overall survival.
- **Metastatic:** **enfortumab vedotin plus pembrolizumab** (**EV-302**) replaced platinum first line; avelumab maintenance; erdafitinib for FGFR3 (fibroblast growth factor receptor 3) alterations.

### 4. Prostate
- **Diagnosis:** **MRI before biopsy** (**PRECISION**: more significant and fewer insignificant cancers); **transperineal biopsy** (less sepsis); **PSMA PET** staging (**proPSMA**: more accurate than CT plus bone scan); ISUP (International Society of Urological Pathology) grade groups; germline testing (BRCA2).
- **Localised:** **active surveillance** for low risk and selected favourable intermediate risk (**ProtecT, 15 years**: cancer mortality low and similar with surveillance, surgery or radiotherapy); robotic prostatectomy; **hypofractionated** radiotherapy; focal therapy in selected men.
- **Advanced:** mHSPC (metastatic hormone-sensitive) gets ADT (androgen deprivation therapy) plus an **ARPI** (androgen receptor pathway inhibitor); **triplet** with docetaxel (**PEACE-1, ARASENS**); prostate radiotherapy for low volume. EMBARK: enzalutamide for high-risk biochemical recurrence. mCRPC (metastatic castration-resistant): **PARP (poly-ADP ribose polymerase) inhibitors** for HRR (homologous recombination repair) mutations; **¹⁷⁷Lu-PSMA-617** (**VISION**: overall survival HR (hazard ratio) 0.62).

### 5. Testis
- **Stage I:** **surveillance** preferred for most seminomas and NSGCTs (non-seminomatous germ cell tumours); alternatives are one cycle of carboplatin (seminoma) or one BEP (bleomycin, etoposide, cisplatin) for high-risk NSGCT.
- **miR-371a-3p:** new serum marker for seminoma and NSGCT.
- **Low-volume stage II seminoma:** **primary RPLND (retroperitoneal lymph node dissection)** (SEMS, PRIMETEST) spares chemotherapy toxicity; robotic, nerve-sparing templates preserve ejaculation.
- **Also know:** IGCCCG (International Germ Cell Cancer Collaborative Group) 2021 update; survivorship care.

### 6. Penis
- **WHO 2022:** **HPV (human papillomavirus)-associated** vs **HPV-independent** squamous cell carcinoma; p16 staining; HPV vaccination for prevention.
- **Organ sparing:** topical treatment or laser for PeIN (penile intraepithelial neoplasia), glans resurfacing, wide excision, glansectomy; a negative margin of a few millimetres is enough.
- **Nodes:** for cN0 pT1b or higher, **DSNB (dynamic sentinel node biopsy)** or modified inguinal dissection. Bulky nodes: neoadjuvant TIP (paclitaxel, ifosfamide, cisplatin), then consolidative dissection.

**Diagram to draw:** six-row table: cancer, key diagnostic advance, localised treatment, advanced treatment.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - RCC: adjuvant pembrolizumab (KEYNOTE-564); first-line ICI combinations; CARMENA.
> - UTUC: single post-RNU instillation; POUT adjuvant chemotherapy.
> - Bladder: EV-302 enfortumab–pembrolizumab; CheckMate 274; NIAGARA.
> - Prostate: MRI first, PSMA PET, ARPI triplets, Lu-PSMA (VISION); testis: miR-371, RPLND for stage II seminoma.

# Section 2 · Oncology, DSD, Gender & Ageing — Short answer questions (SAQ)

## Prepucioplasty.
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2017-Summer Paper 4, Q3b

*Read as: preputioplasty, a foreskin-preserving operation for phimosis.*

### Definition
- An operation that widens the tight preputial ring but keeps the foreskin. It is an alternative to circumcision.

### Background
- **Physiological phimosis** at birth is due to glans–preputial adhesions. Smegma and erections separate them. About 90% retract by 3 years; fewer than 1% are phimotic at 17 years.
- **Secondary phimosis** follows scarring (forced retraction, balanitis) or BXO (balanitis xerotica obliterans).

### Indications
- Persistent symptomatic phimosis: recurrent balanoposthitis, UTI (urinary tract infection), paraphimosis, painful erection.
- Failed **topical steroid** (betamethasone 0.05% twice daily for about 4 weeks). About 1 in 6 fail.
- Wish to keep the foreskin (cultural reasons, cosmesis).

### Contraindications
- **BXO** with scarring: circumcision is preferred. Triradiate incision with triamcinolone has been reported in selected boys.
- Physiological non-retractility needs only reassurance. Treat active infection first.

### Techniques
| Technique | Method |
|---|---|
| Dorsal slit, transverse closure | Longitudinal incision through the ring, closed transversely (Heineke–Mikulicz) |
| Triple incision | Three longitudinal cuts over the ring, each closed transversely |
| Y–V plasty | V-flap of outer skin advanced into a Y-cut |

- General anaesthesia or penile block; confirm full retraction; absorbable sutures.

### Postoperative care
- **Daily retraction from the first week**, continued for weeks, prevents re-stenosis. A topical steroid may help.

### Outcomes and complications
- Most achieve a retractile foreskin. It is quicker and less painful than circumcision.
- Complications: re-phimosis (needs circumcision), bleeding, infection, poor cosmesis, paraphimosis.
- **Also know:** preputial reconstruction in hypospadias repair is also called preputioplasty; dehiscence is its risk.

**Diagram to draw:** dorsal incision through the ring, closed transversely.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Steroid first; about 1 in 6 fail.
> - Cut lengthwise, close crosswise; daily retraction after.
> - BXO: circumcision preferred.

## Enlist the steps in female to male gender reassignment surgery.
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2024-Winter Paper 4, Q3b

### Preconditions
- Gender dysphoria diagnosed per **WPATH (World Professional Association for Transgender Health)** by an MDT (multidisciplinary team).
- **Testosterone:** chapter cites **at least 12 months** before genital surgery (clitoral growth plateaus at about 2 years). WPATH SOC8 (Standards of Care 8, 2022) relaxed this to about 6 months.
- **Urological screening:** uroflowmetry, PVR (post-void residual), renal ultrasound; treat LUTD (lower urinary tract dysfunction) first.

### Stage 1: removal of female structures
1. **Subcutaneous mastectomy** (usually first).
2. **Hysterectomy + bilateral salpingo-oophorectomy**, preferably laparoscopic; remove whole cervix ± upper third of vagina.

### Stage 2: genitoperineal transformation
3. **Vaginectomy** (needed if the urethra is lengthened):
   - Hydrodissection; circumferential introital incision.
   - Rectovaginal plane bluntly; urethrovaginal plane sharply, **no diathermy near the sphincter**.
   - Paravaginal haemostasis; levators closed in midline.
4. **Urethral lengthening, fixed (perineal) part:** vestibular mucosa and labia minora tubularised over 18 Fr; covered with bulbospongiosus.
5. **Phallus construction**, either:
   - **Metoidioplasty:** release clitoral chordee and urethral plate; lengthen urethra to the glans (preputial or labial flap ± buccal graft). Gives a 3–8 cm phallus with erection and sensation.
   - **Phalloplasty:** **RFFF (radial forearm free flap)** or ALT (anterolateral thigh) flap with **tube-in-tube urethra**; microvascular anastomosis; **clitoral nerve coapted** to a flap nerve.
6. **Urethral anastomosis** of phallic to fixed part; neomeatus at the tip.
7. **Scrotoplasty:** labia majora flaps rotated 180° in front (Ghent type). Monsplasty and perineal closure.
8. **SPC (suprapubic catheter)** + urethral catheter; urethral catheter out at 14–21 days.

### Stage 3: prostheses
9. **Testicular prostheses** (17 mL) at 6 months or more.
10. **Erectile prosthesis** after 1 year, once protective sensation returns; single cylinder fixed to pubic bone (dedicated devices, e.g. ZSI 475 FTM [female-to-male]).

### Complications
- **Fistula** (phallic base), **stricture** (fixed–phallic anastomosis), hair growth, dribbling, prosthesis infection or erosion, vaginectomy bleeding or rectal/bladder injury.

**Diagram to draw:** three parts of the neourethra (native, fixed, phallic).

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Mastectomy → hysterectomy/oophorectomy → vaginectomy + urethroplasty + phallus + scrotum → prostheses.
> - No urethral lengthening without vaginectomy (fistula risk).
> - Commonest stricture: fixed–phallic anastomosis.

## Penile Inversion Vaginoplasty Technique
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2025-Winter Paper 4, Q3d
(also answered in 2022-Summer Paper 2, Q3b)

### Introduction
- Commonest gender-affirming genital operation for transgender women: a sensate, functional vulva and vagina.
- **Principle:** homologous penile and scrotal tissues on their own blood supply.

### Preparation
- **WPATH (World Professional Association for Transgender Health) SOC8 (Standards of Care 8):** one assessment, **6 months of hormones**.
- **Permanent hair removal** of scrotal graft skin; BMI (body mass index) usually ≤35 kg/m²; stop smoking; fertility preservation.
- **Choose depth:** full-depth vs **vulvoplasty (zero-depth)**: vulva without a canal, chosen for high surgical risk, prior prostatectomy, or no wish for penetrative intercourse.

### Tissue transfer
| Male structure | Becomes |
|---|---|
| Glans on dorsal neurovascular bundle | **Clitoris** |
| Preputial collar | Clitoral hood, labia minora |
| Spatulated urethra | Vestibule |
| Inverted penile skin | **Neovaginal lining** |
| Scrotal full-thickness graft | Proximal lining |
| Lateral scrotal skin | Labia majora |

### Steps
```
Lithotomy; tranexamic acid 1 g; catheter
→ harvest, defat, tubularise scrotal graft
→ bilateral orchidectomy
→ deglove penis, keep dartos with skin (blood supply)
→ dorsal neurovascular bundle + glans raised;
   excise ALL erectile tissue
→ clitoroplasty; shorten, spatulate urethra
→ canal dissection between prostate and rectum;
   keep Denonvilliers fascia intact; rectal checks
→ invert skin tube + graft; labiaplasty
→ pack canal
```
- **Why excise all erectile tissue:** residual tissue engorges and narrows the canal.
- **Peritoneal flaps (robotic)** add depth; **intestinal vaginoplasty** when skin is inadequate or for revision.

### Postoperative care
- Pack and catheter out about day 5; **lifelong dilation** (several times daily at first, tapering); pelvic floor physiotherapy; continue oestrogen; most achieve orgasm.

### Complications
- **Wound dehiscence** (commonest, 12–33%).
- **Rectovaginal fistula** (0.8% primary, 6.3% revision); urethrovaginal fistula (0.8–3.9%).
- Urethral stricture, meatal malposition (spraying); **vaginal stenosis** (poor dilation); prolapse 4–10%.

**Diagram to draw:** sagittal view of neovagina between prostate and rectum.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Glans → clitoris; penile skin → canal; scrotal skin → graft.
> - Excise all erectile tissue; preserve Denonvilliers fascia.
> - Lifelong dilation; rectovaginal fistula is worst.

## Antioxidants in urological malignancy
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 1 Paper 4, Q3a
(prevention trials also in 2017-Summer Paper 4, Q3a)

### Rationale
- ROS (reactive oxygen species) cause **oxidative DNA damage**; polyunsaturated fat and excess iron increase it. Antioxidants scavenge ROS, so diet studies suggested protection.
- Randomised trials of single high-dose supplements have been **null or harmful**.

### Prostate cancer
| Agent | Evidence | Result |
|---|---|---|
| **SELECT** (Selenium and Vitamin E Cancer Prevention Trial) | 35,533 men; selenium 200 µg, vitamin E 400 IU, both, or placebo | Stopped early (median 5.46 years) for futility. No benefit. Vitamin E alone trended to more cancer (P = 0.06); extended follow-up showed a significant **increase (about 17%)** |
| ATBC (Alpha-Tocopherol, Beta-Carotene) | Low-dose vitamin E in heavy smokers | Secondary analysis: prostate cancer ↓32%, deaths ↓41%; beta-carotene ↑ lung cancer |
| Selenised yeast | Skin-cancer trial | Secondary fall in prostate cancer prompted SELECT |
| Lycopene (cooked tomato) | Meta-analysis: modestly lower risk, mainly advanced cancer | WCRF (World Cancer Research Fund) downgraded "probable" (2007) to "no conclusion" (2018); supplement trials mostly null |
| Green tea catechins | HGPIN (high-grade prostatic intraepithelial neoplasia)/ASAP trials | Large fall in progression to cancer (meta-analysis); dose impractical |
| Vitamin C, high-dose antioxidants | High doses act as pro-oxidants | Supplements above median → more advanced cancer |

- **Also know:** vitamin D (VITAL, no effect); coffee (modestly lower advanced risk); pomegranate, soy and curcumin unproven.

### Bladder cancer
- Supplemental beta-carotene, vitamins C and E and folate do not lower risk; SELECT showed no effect.
- Fruit and vegetable benefit disappears after adjusting for smoking. Smoking cessation is the real prevention.

### Kidney cancer
- No antioxidant trial has shown benefit.

### Why the trials failed
- Single nutrient at high dose; wrong chemical form; non-smoking populations; PSA-era indolent cancers; U-shaped dose effects.

### Current position
- Antioxidant supplements are **not recommended** for cancer prevention. Advise a balanced diet, no smoking and normal weight.

**Diagram to draw:** ROS → DNA damage → cancer, with antioxidants blocking the first arrow.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - SELECT: no benefit; vitamin E alone increased prostate cancer.
> - ATBC benefit limited to smokers on low dose.
> - Lycopene downgraded to "no conclusion" (2018).

## Gene therapy in urology
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 4 Paper 4, Q3f
(intravesical gene therapy also answered in 2022-Summer Paper 2, Q3a)

### Definition
- Delivery of nucleic acid (DNA or RNA) into cells to treat disease: add, replace or silence a gene.

### Vectors
| Vector | Features |
|---|---|
| **Adenovirus** | Efficient, non-integrating, transient expression; replication-incompetent or oncolytic |
| Retrovirus, lentivirus | Integrate: long expression, risk of insertional mutagenesis |
| AAV (adeno-associated virus) | Low immunogenicity, long expression |
| Non-viral | Naked plasmid, liposomes, nanoparticles: safer, less efficient |
| Gene silencing | siRNA (small interfering RNA), antisense; CRISPR (clustered regularly interspaced short palindromic repeats) editing experimental |

### Strategies in urological cancer
1. **Immunogene (cytokine) therapy:** **nadofaragene firadenovec**: replication-incompetent adenovirus type 5 carrying the **interferon-α2b gene** with **Syn3** surfactant, which crosses the bladder's protective barrier. The urothelium keeps secreting interferon, overcoming the 1–2-hour dwell of protein interferon. 75 mL every 3 months; CIS (carcinoma in situ) complete response **53% at 3 months, 24% at 12 months**. **FDA (Food and Drug Administration) approved December 2022** for BCG (bacille Calmette–Guérin)-unresponsive NMIBC (non-muscle-invasive bladder cancer).
2. **Oncolytic virus:** **cretostimogene (CG0070)**: replication-competent adenovirus; E2F promoter limits replication to RB (retinoblastoma) pathway-defective tumour cells; **GM-CSF (granulocyte-macrophage colony-stimulating factor)** transgene adds immunity.
3. **Suicide gene:** HSV-tk (herpes simplex virus thymidine kinase) turns ganciclovir or valacyclovir into a toxin; prostate cancer with radiotherapy (trials).
4. **Toxin gene under a tumour promoter:** BC-819 (diphtheria toxin A, H19 promoter).
5. **Gene-modified cells:** CAR-T (chimeric antigen receptor T cells) against PSMA (prostate-specific membrane antigen): early trials, toxic. Viral-vector vaccine PROSTVAC failed.

### Benign conditions
- **OAB (overactive bladder) and ED (erectile dysfunction):** URO-902 (hMaxi-K) plasmid for the BK potassium channel relaxes smooth muscle; phase 1 only.
- **Primary hyperoxaluria type 1:** lumasiran (RNA interference against glycolate oxidase) lowers oxalate.
- Underactive detrusor: experimental.

### Challenges
- Delivery barriers, vector immunity, short expression, cost, biosafety.

**Diagram to draw:** adenovirus → urothelial cell → interferon secretion → tumour apoptosis.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Nadofaragene: non-replicating adenovirus + interferon-α2b + Syn3; FDA 2022.
> - CIS CR 53% at 3 months, 24% at 12 months.
> - Cretostimogene: oncolytic adenovirus + GM-CSF.
> - Lumasiran: RNA interference for primary hyperoxaluria type 1.

## Molecular targeted therapy in urological cancers
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 6 Paper 4, Q3c

### Concept
- **Targeted therapy** blocks a molecule the cancer depends on (receptor, kinase, transcription factor, DNA-repair enzyme, surface antigen), not all dividing cells.
- Classes: small-molecule TKIs (tyrosine kinase inhibitors, "-nib"), monoclonal antibodies ("-mab"), mTOR (mammalian target of rapamycin) inhibitors, HIF-2α (hypoxia-inducible factor-2α) inhibitor, PARP (poly-ADP-ribose polymerase) inhibitors, ADCs (antibody–drug conjugates), radioligands.

### RCC (renal cell carcinoma)
- **Basis:** VHL (von Hippel–Lindau) loss → HIF accumulates even in normoxia → constant VEGF (vascular endothelial growth factor), PDGF (platelet-derived growth factor) and other targets.

| Target | Drugs | Key evidence |
|---|---|---|
| VEGF receptors | Sunitinib, pazopanib, axitinib, cabozantinib (also MET, AXL), lenvatinib | COMPARZ: pazopanib noninferior to sunitinib; METEOR: cabozantinib beat everolimus |
| VEGF ligand | Bevacizumab | Modest PFS (progression-free survival) gain |
| mTOR | Temsirolimus (IV, poor risk), everolimus (oral) | Temsirolimus improved OS (overall survival) over interferon |
| HIF-2α | **Belzutifan** | VHL disease tumours; LITESPARK-005 beat everolimus after immunotherapy and TKI |

- First line now: TKI **plus** checkpoint inhibitor. Everolimus also treats tuberous sclerosis angiomyolipoma.

### Urothelial carcinoma
- **FGFR3 (fibroblast growth factor receptor 3)** alterations, typical of luminal tumours: **erdafitinib** (oral) after prior therapy.
- **ADCs:** **enfortumab vedotin** (Nectin-4) improved OS after platinum and immunotherapy; sacituzumab govitecan (Trop-2); HER2 (human epidermal growth factor receptor 2)-directed ADCs emerging.
- **EV-302 (2024):** enfortumab vedotin plus pembrolizumab, first line, roughly doubled OS versus platinum chemotherapy and is now standard (chapter notes predate it).

### Prostate cancer (mCRPC, metastatic castration-resistant)
- **PARP inhibitors** exploit **synthetic lethality** in HRR (homologous recombination repair)-deficient tumours, especially BRCA2. **PROfound:** olaparib beat a second hormonal agent (imaging PFS 7.4 vs 3.6 months in BRCA/ATM).
- **PSMA (prostate-specific membrane antigen) radioligand:** **177Lu-PSMA-617; VISION**: OS 15.3 vs 11.3 months.

### Toxicity
- VEGF TKIs: hypertension, hand–foot syndrome, hypothyroidism. mTOR: mucositis, hyperglycaemia. Erdafitinib: hyperphosphataemia. Enfortumab: neuropathy, rash. PARP inhibitors: anaemia.

### Limitations
- Resistance develops; cures rare; biomarker testing needed.

**Diagram to draw:** VHL–HIF–VEGF pathway with drug sites (TKI at receptor, bevacizumab at VEGF, mTOR and HIF-2α inhibitors).

> **Key points to remember:**
> - RCC: VEGF TKIs, mTOR inhibitors, belzutifan (HIF-2α).
> - Bladder: erdafitinib (FGFR3), enfortumab vedotin (Nectin-4).
> - Prostate: PARP inhibitors (BRCA2), 177Lu-PSMA (VISION).

## Rapid Revision Sheet – Section 2 · Oncology, DSD, Gender & Ageing

*All the key points from this section in one place. Read this in the last 30 minutes before the exam.*

#### Rationale of using cancer vaccines in Urology. Explain their mechanism of action. Enumerate various vaccines for renal, prostate and bladder malignancies . Write a note on Sipuleucel-T. (LAQ)

- Vaccine = antigen + adjuvant; DC gives signal 1 (MHC–peptide) and signal 2 (costimulation).
- IMA901, AGS-003, PROSTVAC, GVAX all failed in phase III.
- Sipuleucel-T: PAP–GM-CSF; IMPACT OS 25.8 vs 21.7 months; no PSA or progression effect.
- BCG is the original urological immunotherapy.

#### Discuss in brief, present concepts in various GU cancers (LAQ)

- RCC: adjuvant pembrolizumab (KEYNOTE-564); first-line ICI combinations; CARMENA.
- UTUC: single post-RNU instillation; POUT adjuvant chemotherapy.
- Bladder: EV-302 enfortumab–pembrolizumab; CheckMate 274; NIAGARA.
- Prostate: MRI first, PSMA PET, ARPI triplets, Lu-PSMA (VISION); testis: miR-371, RPLND for stage II seminoma.

#### Prepucioplasty. (SAQ)

- Steroid first; about 1 in 6 fail.
- Cut lengthwise, close crosswise; daily retraction after.
- BXO: circumcision preferred.

#### Enlist the steps in female to male gender reassignment surgery. (SAQ)

- Mastectomy → hysterectomy/oophorectomy → vaginectomy + urethroplasty + phallus + scrotum → prostheses.
- No urethral lengthening without vaginectomy (fistula risk).
- Commonest stricture: fixed–phallic anastomosis.

#### Penile Inversion Vaginoplasty Technique (SAQ)

- Glans → clitoris; penile skin → canal; scrotal skin → graft.
- Excise all erectile tissue; preserve Denonvilliers fascia.
- Lifelong dilation; rectovaginal fistula is worst.

#### Antioxidants in urological malignancy (SAQ)

- SELECT: no benefit; vitamin E alone increased prostate cancer.
- ATBC benefit limited to smokers on low dose.
- Lycopene downgraded to "no conclusion" (2018).

#### Gene therapy in urology (SAQ)

- Nadofaragene: non-replicating adenovirus + interferon-α2b + Syn3; FDA 2022.
- CIS CR 53% at 3 months, 24% at 12 months.
- Cretostimogene: oncolytic adenovirus + GM-CSF.
- Lumasiran: RNA interference for primary hyperoxaluria type 1.

#### Molecular targeted therapy in urological cancers (SAQ)

- RCC: VEGF TKIs, mTOR inhibitors, belzutifan (HIF-2α).
- Bladder: erdafitinib (FGFR3), enfortumab vedotin (Nectin-4).
- Prostate: PARP inhibitors (BRCA2), 177Lu-PSMA (VISION).

# Section 3 · Infections & Inflammation — Long answer questions (LAQ)

## Interstitial cystitis / painful bladder syndrome: recent trends in evaluation, diagnosis and management
**Asked as:**
- Discuss the recent trends in the evaluation. Diagnosis and management of interstitial cystitis. — *Winter 2024 Paper IV Q1 (LAQ)*
- Painful bladder syndrome management. — *Summer 2021 Paper IV Q3d (SAQ)*

**Type:** LAQ (25 marks); also asked as SAQ (10 marks)  
(also answered in 2017-Winter Paper 2, Q3b and 2021-Summer Paper 4, Q3d)

*Read as: recent trends in the evaluation, diagnosis and management of IC/BPS (interstitial cystitis/bladder pain syndrome).*

### Definition: how it has changed
- **NIDDK (National Institute of Diabetes and Digestive and Kidney Diseases, 1987):** research criteria; missed about 60% of patients, so the definition was broadened.
- **AUA (American Urological Association), current:** an unpleasant sensation (pain, pressure, discomfort) perceived to be related to the bladder, with LUTS (lower urinary tract symptoms) for **more than 6 weeks** (**>6 weeks**), without infection or other identifiable cause.
- **ESSIC (European Society for the Study of Interstitial Cystitis, 2008; uses >6 months):** chronic (>6 months) bladder pain with at least one other urinary symptom, after confusable diseases are excluded.
- **ICD-11 (International Classification of Diseases, 11th revision):** "chronic primary bladder pain syndrome".
- **Trend: splitting the syndrome.** "IC" is reserved for **Hunner lesion (HL) disease**, a true inflammatory disease (true inflammation); BPS without HL is a symptom complex. Both sit within UCPPS (urological chronic pelvic pain syndrome).
- **Phenotypes:** Hunner lesion (HL) disease versus non-HL; also pelvic-floor-dominant and widespread (centralised) pain.

### Pathophysiology (current view)
- Multifactorial: leaky GAG (glycosaminoglycan) layer; mast-cell activation; neurogenic inflammation; **central sensitisation**; stress-axis dysfunction; small-fibre neuropathy. No infection proven.
- HL disease shows dense lymphoplasmacytic infiltrate; non-HL bladders are often histologically normal.
- **Associated:** IBS (irritable bowel syndrome, >30%), fibromyalgia, vulvodynia, endometriosis; tender pelvic floor in about 80%.

### Recent trends in evaluation (AUA 2022) — before treatment
- **Diagnosis of exclusion plus localisation:** tests aim to find the pain generator.
- **History:** pain worse on filling, relieved by voiding; "painful urgency" without urge incontinence, unlike OAB (overactive bladder). **Ask about ketamine.**
- **Examination:** **six-site pelvic-floor palpation** (anterior and posterior levator ani, obturator internus, each side); vulval swab test; DRE (digital rectal examination) in men.
- **Basic tests:** PVR (post-void residual), urinalysis, culture; cytology if risk factors; frequency–volume chart.
- **Scores:** O'Leary–Sant ICSI/ICPI (Interstitial Cystitis Symptom/Problem Index), GUPI (Genitourinary Pain Index), pain VAS (visual analogue scale); **score pain and urinary symptoms separately.**
- **Body map:** widespread pain = 3 or more of 12 regions besides the pelvis.
- **Office cystoscopy if over 50 or HL suspected** (AUA 2022 change): HL is a stellate red area with radiating vessels, found only by looking.
- **Local anaesthetic challenge** (lidocaine ± bupivacaine) localises pain to the bladder and selects intravesical therapy.
- **Dropped:** potassium sensitivity test (neither sensitive nor specific); routine hydrodistension (glomerulations non-specific); routine urodynamics.
- **Biopsy** only to exclude CIS (carcinoma in situ).

**ESSIC classification** (cystoscopy with hydrodistension + biopsy, e.g. "3C"):

| Cystoscopy | Biopsy |
|---|---|
| X not done; 1 normal; 2 glomerulations; 3 Hunner lesion | X not done; A normal; B inconclusive; C positive (inflammation, mastocytosis, fibrosis) |

**Exclude:** UTI (urinary tract infection) including tuberculosis, CIS (carcinoma in situ), stones, radiation or cyclophosphamide cystitis, **ketamine cystitis**, OAB (overactive bladder), diverticulum, endometriosis.

### Phenotype-based diagnosis (key trend)
| Phenotype | Identified by | Treatment |
|---|---|---|
| Hunner lesion | Cystoscopy | Fulguration and/or triamcinolone; ciclosporin A if refractory |
| Anaesthetic-responsive bladder pain | Local anaesthetic challenge | Intravesical therapy |
| Pelvic-floor tenderness | Pelvic-floor examination | Myofascial physiotherapy |
