| Widespread (centralised) pain | Body map, associated pain conditions | Systemic: amitriptyline, gabapentinoids, CBT (cognitive behavioural therapy), pain team |

- **MAPP (Multi-Disciplinary Approach to the Study of Chronic Pelvic Pain) reanalysis, 2023:** hydroxyzine/PPS (pentosan polysulfate) helped localised pain; amitriptyline helped widespread pain.

### Recent trends in management (AUA 2022): principles
- **Tiers abandoned:** **Old AUA (2011/2014): six tiers**, from self-care to surgery. **AUA 2022 dropped the tiers:** the six-tier ladder is replaced by grouped options, phenotype-guided, shared decisions; multimodal treatment (care) allowed early; major surgery stays last. Stop treatments that fail.

### Treatment options
| Group | Options |
|---|---|
| Behavioural | Education; avoid trigger foods (coffee, citrus, spicy food, alcohol); stress management; flare plan / flare plans |
| Physical | **Myofascial pelvic-floor therapy / physiotherapy** if tender (Standard); **avoid Kegel exercises** |
| Oral | **Amitriptyline** 10 mg at night, up to about (~) 50 mg; hydroxyzine; cimetidine; **PPS (pentosan polysulfate) 100 mg three times daily** (**pigmentary maculopathy**: baseline and periodic eye checks; retinal checks) |
| Intravesical | **DMSO (dimethyl sulfoxide) 50%, 50 mL, 15–20 min**; heparin; alkalinised lidocaine (flares); also hyaluronic acid, chondroitin |
| Procedures | **HL: fulguration and/or triamcinolone injection** (75–90% improve); short (1–3 min) low-pressure (60–80 cmH₂O) hydrodistension; **onabotulinumtoxinA 100 U into the trigone** (must accept self-catheterisation; risk of retention); SNM (sacral neuromodulation) |
| Refractory HL | **Oral ciclosporin A** (watch kidney function and blood pressure) |
| Last resort (<10%) | Cystoplasty (augmentation) or diversion ± cystectomy; pain may persist |

- **Not offered / do not offer:** long-term antibiotics, intravesical BCG (bacille Calmette–Guérin), long-term oral steroids.
- Pain management: gabapentinoids; opioids only via pain specialists.
- Monitor with symptom scores; stop ineffective treatments. Most hydrodistension responses last under 2 months.
- Treat associated pain conditions (IBS (irritable bowel syndrome), fibromyalgia) with other specialists.

### Recent advances
- Urinary biomarkers and functional MRI (magnetic resonance imaging) for phenotyping (research); phenotype-stratified trials.
- **Also know:** tanezumab (anti-NGF [nerve growth factor]) not adopted; liposomal intravesical botulinum toxin no better than placebo; pudendal neuromodulation.

**Diagram to draw:** cystoscopic Hunner lesion with radiating vessels, and the four-phenotype treatment chart.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - AUA definition: bladder pain with LUTS >6 weeks; ESSIC >6 months.
> - AUA 2022: no tiers; phenotype-based; cystoscopy if over 50 or HL suspected; anaesthetic challenge, not potassium test.
> - HL: fulguration or triamcinolone (fulguration/triamcinolone); ciclosporin if refractory.
> - PPS causes maculopathy; antibiotics and BCG not offered.

# Section 3 · Infections & Inflammation — Short answer questions (SAQ)

## Chronic non bacterial prostatitis.
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2018-Summer Paper 4, Q3d

### Definition and classification
- Now called **CP/CPPS (chronic prostatitis/chronic pelvic pain syndrome)**, NIH (National Institutes of Health) category III: pelvic pain for **≥3 of the last 6 months** without uropathogenic infection. 90–95% of prostatitis.

| NIH category | Description |
|---|---|
| I | Acute bacterial prostatitis |
| II | Chronic bacterial prostatitis (recurrent same-organism UTI) |
| **IIIA** | CP/CPPS, **inflammatory** (leucocytes in EPS (expressed prostatic secretion), VB3 (post-massage urine) or semen) |
| **IIIB** | CP/CPPS, **non-inflammatory** |
| IV | Asymptomatic inflammatory |

- IIIA vs IIIB has no treatment value.

### Aetiology
- Interplay of past infection, neurogenic inflammation, **pelvic floor muscle dysfunction**, autoimmunity, psychological stress and **central sensitisation**. The prostate may not be the pain source.

### Clinical features
- Perineal (commonest), testicular, suprapubic or penile pain; ejaculatory pain; LUTS (lower urinary tract symptoms); sexual dysfunction.

### Evaluation (diagnosis of exclusion)
- **NIH-CPSI (Chronic Prostatitis Symptom Index):** pain, urinary and quality-of-life domains (0–43).
- DRE (digital rectal examination): prostate tender in fewer than half; palpate the pelvic floor for **trigger points** (tender in ~half).
- Urinalysis and culture; **two-glass test** (pre- and post-massage). Cytology if irritative symptoms or haematuria. PVR (post-void residual), flow.
- Selected: cystoscopy, urodynamics.
- **UPOINT phenotyping:** **U**rinary, **P**sychosocial, **O**rgan-specific, **I**nfection, **N**eurological/systemic, **T**enderness of muscles.

### Management: multimodal, phenotype-directed (monotherapy trials fail)
| Domain | Therapy |
|---|---|
| Urinary | **α-blockers** in recent-onset, α-blocker-naive men (<1 year); antimuscarinics |
| Psychosocial | CBT (cognitive behavioural therapy), stress reduction |
| Organ (prostate) | **Quercetin**, **pollen extract** (small RCTs, randomised controlled trials), 5-ARI (5α-reductase inhibitor) if BPH (benign prostatic hyperplasia) |
| Infection | Quinolone or tetracycline for ≥6 weeks if treatment-naive and <1 year (EAU, European Association of Urology), or positive VB3; no repeated courses |
| Neurological | Pregabalin, amitriptyline, duloxetine |
| Tenderness | **Pelvic floor physiotherapy**, myofascial trigger-point release, biofeedback |

- **Others:** **acupuncture** (beat sham in a large RCT), PTNS (percutaneous tibial nerve stimulation), shockwave therapy, aerobic exercise; NSAIDs (non-steroidal anti-inflammatory drugs) only in combination.
- **Not recommended:** TURP (transurethral resection of the prostate), radical prostatectomy.

**Diagram to draw:** UPOINT snowflake with a therapy for each domain.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - NIH III: pain ≥3 of 6 months, no infection; IIIA inflammatory, IIIB not.
> - NIH-CPSI to score; UPOINT to phenotype.
> - Multimodal therapy; α-blocker plus antibiotic only early.

## Nitrofurantoin.
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2020-Summer Paper 4, Q3e

### Introduction
- A synthetic **nitrofuran**; **first-line for acute uncomplicated cystitis** (EAU (European Association of Urology)) because it concentrates in urine and spares bowel and vaginal flora.

### Mechanism
- Reduced by bacterial flavoproteins (nitroreductases) to **reactive intermediates** that damage ribosomal proteins, DNA and several enzyme systems.
- **Bacteriostatic**, bactericidal at high urinary concentration.
- Multiple targets keep **acquired resistance low**.

### Pharmacokinetics
- Well absorbed (better with food); **rapidly excreted in urine**. Serum and tissue levels are sub-therapeutic.
- Hence **useless for pyelonephritis, prostatitis, febrile or bloodstream infection**.
- Macrocrystals: slower absorption, less nausea.

### Spectrum
| Susceptible | Resistant |
|---|---|
| ***E. coli*** (most), ***Enterococcus faecalis***, *Staphylococcus saprophyticus*, *S. aureus* (not MRSA (methicillin-resistant *S. aureus*)), many *Klebsiella*, *Citrobacter* | ***Proteus*** (intrinsic), *Pseudomonas*, *Serratia*, *Providencia*, *Morganella*, *Acinetobacter* |

- Active against many ESBL (extended-spectrum β-lactamase)-producing *E. coli*.

### Uses and doses
| Indication | Regimen |
|---|---|
| Uncomplicated cystitis (women) | **100 mg twice daily × 5 days** (modified release) |
| Recurrent UTI (urinary tract infection) prophylaxis, adults | 50–100 mg at night, or post-coital |
| Children: treatment / prophylaxis | 3–5 mg/kg/day in 2 doses / 1–2 mg/kg at night (VUR (vesicoureteral reflux), after 3 months of age) |
| Pregnancy | Second and third trimesters; first trimester only if no alternative; **avoid near term** |

### Adverse effects
- **GI (gastrointestinal) upset** (commonest).
- **Pulmonary**: acute hypersensitivity (fever, cough, dyspnoea); **chronic interstitial fibrosis** with long-term use.
- **Peripheral neuropathy** (worse with renal impairment, diabetes, anaemia, B-vitamin deficiency).
- **Haemolysis in G6PD (glucose-6-phosphate dehydrogenase) deficiency**, including the neonate (hence avoid at term and <3 months of age).
- Hepatotoxicity (cholestatic or chronic hepatitis), rare.

### Precautions
- **Renal impairment**: inadequate urine levels and more toxicity. Historically avoided if CrCl (creatinine clearance) <60; Beers Criteria (2015) allow short courses down to **CrCl 30 mL/min**; avoid long-term use in the elderly.
- Interactions: probenecid (blocks excretion), magnesium antacids, quinolones (antagonism).

**Diagram to draw:** spectrum table (susceptible versus resistant).

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Urine-only drug: cystitis yes, pyelonephritis or prostatitis no.
> - 100 mg twice daily × 5 days; prophylaxis 50–100 mg at night (children 1–2 mg/kg).
> - Proteus intrinsically resistant; resistance in E. coli low.
> - Toxicity: pulmonary fibrosis, neuropathy, G6PD haemolysis; CrCl ≥30 (Beers 2015).

## Rapid Revision Sheet – Section 3 · Infections & Inflammation

*All the key points from this section in one place. Read this in the last 30 minutes before the exam.*

#### Interstitial cystitis / painful bladder syndrome: recent trends in evaluation, diagnosis and management (LAQ)

- AUA definition: bladder pain with LUTS >6 weeks; ESSIC >6 months.
- AUA 2022: no tiers; phenotype-based; cystoscopy if over 50 or HL suspected; anaesthetic challenge, not potassium test.
- HL: fulguration or triamcinolone (fulguration/triamcinolone); ciclosporin if refractory.
- PPS causes maculopathy; antibiotics and BCG not offered.

#### Chronic non bacterial prostatitis. (SAQ)

- NIH III: pain ≥3 of 6 months, no infection; IIIA inflammatory, IIIB not.
- NIH-CPSI to score; UPOINT to phenotype.
- Multimodal therapy; α-blocker plus antibiotic only early.

#### Nitrofurantoin. (SAQ)

- Urine-only drug: cystitis yes, pyelonephritis or prostatitis no.
- 100 mg twice daily × 5 days; prophylaxis 50–100 mg at night (children 1–2 mg/kg).
- Proteus intrinsically resistant; resistance in E. coli low.
- Toxicity: pulmonary fibrosis, neuropathy, G6PD haemolysis; CrCl ≥30 (Beers 2015).

# Section 4 · Paediatric Urology 1 — Long answer questions (LAQ)

## Hypospadias: present concepts in management, and hypospadias fistula
**Asked as:**
- Present concept in management of hypospadias — *Practice Set 6 Paper IV Q1 (LAQ)*
- Present concept in management of hypospadias — *Practice Set 6 Paper IV Q1 (earlier version) (LAQ)*
- Etiology and management of hypospadias fistula — *Summer 2015 Paper IV Q3a (SAQ)*
- Etiology and management of hypospadias fistula. — *Practice Set 6 Paper IV Q3d (earlier version) (SAQ)*

**Type:** LAQ (25 marks); also asked as SAQ (10 marks)  
(embryology and proximal repair also in 2017-Winter Paper 3, Q1; Snodgrass repair in 2023-Winter Paper 3, Q3c)

*Read as "present (current) concepts in the management of hypospadias".*

### Introduction
- Hypospadias is an arrest of the androgen-dependent "closing zipper" of urethral fold fusion (weeks 8–16). Triad: ventral ectopic meatus, ventral curvature, dorsal hooded prepuce.
- 1 in 150–300 male births; distal 70–85%, proximal 10–25%.
- **Goals:** a straight erection, a urethra to the tip (standing voiding, semen delivery) and acceptable appearance, with the fewest operations and a result lasting into adulthood.

### Concept 1: severity is judged in theatre, not by the meatus
- A "distal" meatus may hide absent spongiosum and severe curvature, so classify **after degloving**.

| GMS (glans–meatus–shaft) score | Glans | Meatus | Shaft curvature |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Good size, deep groove | Glanular | None |
| 2 | Adequate, grooved | Coronal | <30° |
| 3 | Small; narrow or flat plate | Mid or distal shaft | 30–60° |
| 4 | Very small; flat plate | Proximal shaft, penoscrotal | >60° |

- Total 3–12; a higher score predicts more complications.
- **POST (plate objective scoring tool):** a low ratio of neomeatus length to glans fusion length means a poor plate, which favours an inlay graft over plain TIP (tubularised incised plate).

### Concept 2: evaluation
- **DSD (disorder of sex development) work-up** if hypospadias plus undescended testis (especially non-palpable), perineal meatus or ambiguity: karyotype, ultrasound for Müllerian remnants, testosterone, LH (luteinising hormone), FSH (follicle-stimulating hormone), AMH (anti-Müllerian hormone).
- No routine VCUG (voiding cystourethrogram) or renal ultrasound. Associations: hernia or hydrocele 9–16%, cryptorchidism about 7% (10% in proximal), enlarged utricle.
- **Aetiology:** causes converge on deficient androgen action (placental insufficiency, assisted reproduction, genes, endocrine disruptors).

### Concept 3: timing and preparation
- Repair at **6–18 months** (AAP (American Academy of Pediatrics): 6–12 months), before genital awareness at about 18 months. One short anaesthetic appears low-risk.
- A straight penis that voids standing may gain little from surgery: counsel honestly.
- **Preoperative androgen** if glans width <14–15 mm: testosterone 2 mg/kg IM (intramuscular) at 5 and 2 weeks before surgery (about 4 mm glans gain). Evidence mixed; two RCTs (randomised controlled trials) showed fewer complications.

### Concept 4: curvature decides the operation
```
Deglove + excise dysplastic dartos + artificial erection
  ├─ straight       → urethroplasty
  ├─ residual <30°  → dorsal midline plication (12 o'clock nerve-free zone)
  └─ >30°           → divide plate → still >30° → ventral corporotomy ± graft → staged repair
```
- Recurrence 28% after plication versus 9% after grafting. Under-correction causes repeated surgery, so correct fully first time.

### Concept 5: choice of technique
| Situation | Current choice |
|---|---|
| Glanular meatus, no curvature | Advancement: MAGPI (meatal advancement and glanuloplasty), MIV (M inverted V) glansplasty; selectively |
| Distal or midshaft, good plate | **TIP (Snodgrass)**: >90% of distal, about 80% of midshaft repairs; Thiersch–Duplay; GAP (glans approximation procedure) for a deep groove |
| Narrow or shallow plate | TIP with **dorsal inlay graft**; onlay island flap; Mathieu (SLAM modification) |
| Proximal, curvature >30° | **Two-stage**: STAG (staged tubularised autograft) or Bracka graft; Byars flaps; distal-first (Ulaanbaatar) |
| Proximal, curvature corrected, good tissue | One-stage onlay or TPIF (transverse preputial island flap); proximal TIP abandoned |

- **Why stage:** a urethroplasty laid over a fresh corporal graft fails more often; staging gives a supple, vascular bed. Stage 2 after ≥6 months.

### Concept 6: protect blood supply
- Magnification, tension-free closure, offset suture lines, **vascularised barrier layer** (dartos, tunica vaginalis), rapidly absorbed fine sutures, no hair-bearing skin, haemostasis.
- Postoperative: stent 3–21 days by complexity, compressive dressing, oxybutynin; antibiotic prophylaxis debated.

### Concept 7: standardised complications and outcomes
- Definitions: glans dehiscence (fusion <2 mm); meatal stenosis (symptoms plus <8 Fr before puberty, <12 Fr after); recurrent curvature >30°.
- TIP meta-analysis (Pfistermuller 2015): complications 10.6%, fistula 5.7%. Proximal complications 23–68%; STAG about 23% (lowest); Byars 43–81%.
- A long, rigid, spongiosum-free neourethra raises resistance (Poiseuille's law), causing fistula and diverticulum. Fistula: see 2015-Summer Paper 4, Q3a (another source: see Practice Set 6 Paper 4, Q3d).
- **Also know:** BXO (balanitis xerotica obliterans), excise and stage with buccal graft; redo repairs use buccal (lip inside glans) mucosa, Cecil scrotal parasitisation, tissue expansion.

### Concept 8: follow-up and patient-reported outcomes
- About three-quarters of complications appear by 3 years; stricture and curvature appear late. Follow proximal cases **through puberty**.
- Tools: HOSE (Hypospadias Objective Scoring Evaluation, surgeon), PPS (Penile Perception Score), HOPE (Hypospadias Objective Penile Evaluation, photo panels). Decision aids reduce parental regret.

### Recent advances
- Registries, AI (artificial intelligence) video review, genomic risk profiling, a quality-of-life measure, engineered tissue.

**Diagram to draw:** curvature algorithm and two-stage graft repair.

### Hypospadias fistula: aetiology and management
(also answered in 2015-Summer Paper 4, Q3a)

#### Definition and incidence
- A UCF (urethrocutaneous fistula) is a urethral leak anywhere below the meatus: the **commonest complication** of hypospadias repair, just under 10% at short-term follow-up.
- After TIP (tubularised incised plate): 5.7% (**Pfistermuller 2015**); after proximal repair 3–45%. Usually at the original meatus or coronal margin.

#### Aetiology
- **Distal obstruction** (meatal stenosis, stricture): raises voiding pressure and turbulence; the **key correctable cause**.
- **Ischaemia**, oedema, infection, haematoma, tension.
- **Technical:** overlapping suture lines, poorly inverted epithelium, slowly absorbed sutures, **no barrier layer**.
- **Patient:** proximal meatus, glans <14 mm, redo surgery. A long, rigid neourethra raises resistance (Poiseuille's law).

#### Prevention
- **Vascularised barrier** (dartos; **tunica vaginalis** in proximal or redo), offset suture lines, stent in complex repairs.

#### Evaluation
- Split stream / multiple streams, usually within 2 years.
- Under anaesthesia: **bougie calibration** for distal narrowing, **urethroscopy** if irregular, and a **stress test** (compress proximally and distally, inject saline or methylene blue) for hidden tracts.

#### Management
- Rarely closes spontaneously. **Wait 6–12 months**; **correct distal obstruction at the same sitting**.

| Fistula | Repair |
|---|---|
| Small, shaft | Excise the epithelialised tract, inverting subepithelial closure with fine absorbable suture, multiple offset layers, **dartos barrier** |
| Large or thin tissue | Trap-door or island skin flap plus second layer |
| Coronal margin (glans dehiscence) | Implies distal narrowing or poor tissue: **redo urethroplasty and glansplasty** (redo urethroplasty and glans) |
| With diverticulum | Excise, or reuse the redundant wall as a flap |

- Recurrence about **20%**; reduce with interposed vascularised layers (dartos, tunica vaginalis, Cecil modification when tissue is scarce).
- Catheter: none for simple repairs, 7–14 days for complex.
- **Also know:** preputial fistula does not involve the urethra; congenital urethral fistula is closed the same way; follow through puberty.

```
UCF → wait 6–12 months → calibrate, urethroscopy, stress test
 ├─ distal obstruction → correct at same sitting
 ├─ small → excise + layered closure + barrier flap
 └─ large/coronal → flap repair or redo urethroplasty
```

**Diagram to draw (fistula):** neourethra, dartos barrier and skin with offset suture lines.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Classify after degloving (GMS); curvature, not meatus, decides one versus two stages.
> - TIP for distal; staged graft repair (STAG/Bracka) for severe proximal.
> - Hypospadias plus undescended testis: DSD work-up.
> - Follow through puberty; use patient-reported outcomes.
> - Fistula: commonest complication, just under 10%; TIP 5.7%.
> - Exclude distal obstruction; wait 6–12 months.
> - Layered closure with barrier flap; recurrence about 20%.

## VUR: etiopathogenesis, recent trends in management and STING technique
**Asked as:**
- Describe etiopathogenesis of VUR and recent trends in management — *Practice Set 2 Paper IV Q2 (LAQ)*
- STING technique for VUR. — *Summer 2020 Paper IV Q3c (SAQ)*
- STING technique for VUR. — *Practice Set 5 Paper IV Q3e (earlier version) (SAQ)*

**Type:** LAQ (25 marks); also asked as SAQ (10 marks)  
(VUR also answered in Practice Set 1 Paper 1, Q3f and 2020-Summer Paper 1, Q2; detailed management of bilateral VUR with surgical techniques: see Practice Set 2 Paper 3, Q2)
(STING also answered in 2020-Summer Paper 4, Q3c)

### Definition and epidemiology
- VUR (vesicoureteral reflux) is retrograde flow of urine from the bladder into the upper tract.
- Found in about **30% of children with UTI (urinary tract infection)** and about 17% imaged for hydronephrosis. The younger the child, the likelier VUR.
- Infant boys predominate (higher voiding pressures, more UTI if uncircumcised); overall girls twice as often. Less common in Black children.

### Normal antireflux mechanism
- Balance of three factors: **ureteric peristalsis**, **UVJ (ureterovesical junction) anatomy** and **bladder dynamics**.
- **Passive flap valve:** the intramural ureter is compressed against detrusor backing as the bladder fills. This needs adequate tunnel length and fixation.
- **Paquin:** tunnel length to diameter about **5:1** normally versus 1.4:1 in refluxing units. Orifice shape also matters.
- Waldeyer's sheath anchors the intramural ureter to the deep trigone.

### Etiopathogenesis
**1. Embryology (Mackie and Stephens)**
| | Early budding | Late budding |
|---|---|---|
| Orifice | High and lateral | Distal and medial |
| Result | Short tunnel, **VUR** | Ectopic, often obstructed ureter |
| Kidney | Abnormal bud–metanephros contact: **dysplasia** | — |
- This single fault explains why high-grade VUR and dysplasia occur together.

**2. Primary versus secondary**
| Primary | Secondary |
|---|---|
| Congenitally deficient UVJ: short tunnel, weak detrusor backing, lateral orifice | Normal UVJ overwhelmed by pressure: PUV (posterior urethral valves), neurogenic bladder (VUR in up to 30% of spina bifida), dysfunctional voiding; or UVJ anomaly (ureterocele, duplication, paraureteral diverticulum) |
- They overlap: "primary" VUR often has a treatable secondary component.

**3. Bladder and bowel dysfunction (BBD):** high voiding pressures and incomplete emptying distort the UVJ. Detrusor overactivity is the commonest urodynamic finding. Infant boys have high voiding pressures that settle with maturation.

**4. Genetics:** sibling prevalence about 30%, highest under 3 years; identical twins almost fully concordant; polygenic.

**5. Infection:** cystitis lowers the reflux threshold; *E. coli* endotoxin causes ureteric atony.

### Pathogenesis of renal damage
| | Reflux-associated dysplasia | Acquired scar |
|---|---|---|
| Cause | Abnormal bud (congenital) | Infected urine plus VUR |
| Typical | Boy, grade IV–V, antenatal hydronephrosis | Girl, recurrent febrile UTI, BBD |
| Preventable | No | Yes |
- **Intrarenal reflux** through flat compound papillae at the poles needs only 2 mmHg in neonates, so scars are polar.
- **Sterile reflux rarely scars** unless pressures are high (Ransley and Risdon). Most scars follow early infections ("big bang", now debated).
- Sequelae: hypertension in **34–38%** of scarred children; CKD (chronic kidney disease); poor growth; pre-eclampsia.

### International grading (1981)
| Grade | Appearance on VCUG (voiding cystourethrogram) |
|---|---|
| I | Ureter only |
| II | Up to calyces, no dilatation |
| III | Mild–moderate dilatation, minimal forniceal blunting |
| IV | Moderate dilatation and tortuosity |
| V | Gross dilatation and tortuosity; papillary impressions lost |
- Natural history: grade II resolves in 63–85%, grade III about 50%; grade IV–V and bilateral III rarely.

### Recent trends in management
1. **Treat infection risk, not reflux:** risk stratification by sex, age, grade, laterality, BBD, scars and presentation (Hidas model). Breakthrough UTI on prophylaxis at 2 years: 8.6% low, 26% intermediate, 62.5% high risk.
2. **Fewer VCUGs:** ultrasound first (AAP [American Academy of Pediatrics] 2011, NICE [National Institute for Health and Care Excellence]); or "top-down" DMSA (dimercaptosuccinic acid) first. Contrast-enhanced voiding urosonography avoids radiation.
3. **Objective measures:** UDR (ureteral diameter ratio) predicts resolution and breakthrough UTI better than grade; machine-learning models.
4. **Selective CAP (continuous antibiotic prophylaxis):** **RIVUR** halved recurrent UTI (23.6% to 12.9%) but did not reduce scarring. NNT (number needed to treat) is 5 in high-risk and 18 in low-risk children. The Swedish Reflux Trial showed benefit in girls with dilating VUR.
5. **BBD first:** timed voiding, constipation treatment, anticholinergics. Topical steroid for phimosis in boys.
6. **Endoscopic injection:** Dx/HA (dextranomer/hyaluronic acid); HIT and double HIT (hydrodistension implantation technique) give about 90% resolution. Vantris works better but can obstruct late. (Details: STING technique below.)
7. **RALUR-EV (robot-assisted extravesical reimplantation):** less pain and shorter stay; results approaching the open benchmark of **95.9%**.
8. **Less follow-up imaging:** VCUG may be omitted after surgery for low-grade VUR if ultrasound is normal.

### STING technique for VUR

#### Definition and principle
- **STING (subureteric Teflon injection)**: endoscopic injection of a **bulking agent** beneath the ureteric orifice in VUR (vesicoureteral reflux). The mound supports the intramural ureter, restoring the **flap-valve**.
- Matouschek, 1981; popularised by **O'Donnell and Puri, 1986**.

#### Bulking agents
| Agent | Particle | Problem |
|---|---|---|
| Teflon (PTFE (polytetrafluoroethylene) paste) | 90% <40 µm, permanent | **Migration**; never FDA (Food and Drug Administration) approved |
| **Dx/HA (dextranomer/hyaluronic acid, Deflux)** | **80–250 µm**; gel resorbed | Volume loss about 23%; FDA approved 2001; standard agent |
| Polyacrylate (Vantris) | 300 µm, permanent | Late ureteric obstruction |

#### Technique
1. Antibiotic at induction; offset-lens cystoscope; rigid 3.7–5 Fr needle; bladder partly filled.
2. **STING**: needle enters **2–3 mm below the orifice at 6 o'clock**, advanced **4–5 mm** submucosally.
3. Inject slowly; a mound should appear within **0.1–0.2 mL**, else reposition.
4. Aim for a **"volcano" mound** with a slit-like orifice. Do not cauterise bleeding. Day case.

| Technique | Needle entry | Result |
|---|---|---|
| STING | Below orifice | Baseline |
| **HIT** (hydrodistension implantation technique) | Inside hydrodistended orifice | Better than STING, all grades |
| **Double HIT** | Two intraureteric sites | About 90% resolution |

#### Indications
- Persistent primary VUR (grades II–IV) with breakthrough febrile UTI (urinary tract infection), new scars, poor adherence or parental preference; residual VUR after reimplantation.
- **Relative contraindications**: grade V, **UVJ (ureterovesical junction) obstruction**, untreated BBD (bladder and bowel dysfunction).

#### Results
- Low-grade VUR about 90% after one injection. **Elder 2006 meta-analysis**: grade I–II about 79%, III 72%, IV 63%, V 51%.
- **Swedish Reflux Trial**: 20% recurrence at 2 years.

#### Complications
- Failure or recurrence (repeat 1–2 injections, then reimplant); new contralateral VUR; **early or late obstruction**; calcified mound mimics a stone. **Also know:** Macroplastique.

#### Follow-up
- Antibiotics 3 months; ultrasound at 2–6 weeks; ultrasound ± VCUG (voiding cystourethrogram) at 3 months.

**Diagram to draw:** normal long oblique tunnel versus short lateral refluxing tunnel, with an early-budding Y-diagram; needle sites: STING below orifice, HIT and double HIT inside the tunnel, with volcano mound.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Paquin 5:1 normal versus 1.4:1 refluxing; early budding causes VUR and dysplasia.
> - Sterile reflux rarely scars; 34–38% of scarred children become hypertensive.
> - RIVUR: CAP halves UTI, not scars.
> - Trend: risk-stratified care, fewer VCUGs, BBD first, endoscopic or robotic correction.
> - O'Donnell and Puri 1986; needle 2–3 mm below orifice at 6 o'clock.
> - Dx/HA 80–250 µm; HIT better than STING; double HIT about 90%.
> - Watch late obstruction; calcified mound mimics stone.

# Section 5 · Paediatric Urology 2 — Short answer questions (SAQ)

## Vesicostomy.
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2016-Summer Paper 4, Q3f

### Definition
- **Cutaneous vesicostomy**: the bladder dome is brought to the lower abdominal skin as an incontinent stoma draining into the nappy. It "vents" the bladder, protecting the upper tracts; usually a **temporary** bridge to reconstruction.

### Indications
- **PUV (posterior urethral valves)**: infant too small for a resectoscope; upper tract deterioration despite drainage or ablation.
- **Prune-belly syndrome**: acute renal failure, urosepsis, urethral atresia. A larger stoma is needed because stenosis is common.
- **Neurogenic bladder**: high pressure despite CIC (clean intermittent catheterisation) and drugs, or CIC impossible; worsening hydronephrosis; febrile UTI (urinary tract infection) with high-grade reflux.
- **Also know:** urethral atresia; MMIHS (megacystis-microcolon-intestinal hypoperistalsis syndrome); urogenital sinus anomalies.

### Blocksom technique (Duckett modification)
1. Transverse 2 cm incision midway between umbilicus and pubis. (In prune-belly, excise any urachal diverticulum.)
2. Incise the rectus sheath; separate the recti.
3. Fill the bladder; mobilise the **dome** up to the urachus, sweeping peritoneum off.
4. Bring the **dome** (not the anterior wall) to the wound. This reduces prolapse of the posterior wall.
5. Anchor detrusor to rectus fascia; stoma should take about 24 Fr.
6. Open the dome; suture mucosa to skin. No catheter needed.

### Advantages
- Simple; no catheter or appliance.
- The bladder still fills and empties through the stoma at low leak pressure, so compliance is **not** lost; it remains usable for reconstruction or transplantation.

### Complications
- **Stomal stenosis**, mucosal **prolapse**, peristomal dermatitis, stones, UTI; leakage harder to manage as the child grows.

### Closure
- When the cause is treated (valve ablation, urethral growth) or at definitive reconstruction; check bladder function first (cystogram or urodynamics).
- Excise the stoma, free the bladder from fascia, close in two layers, catheter drainage for a few days.

### Alternatives
| Option | Use |
|---|---|
| Cutaneous ureterostomy or pyelostomy | Rare; persistent upper tract obstruction |
| Ileovesicostomy | Unable to catheterise; 10–15 cm ileum |
| Appendicovesicostomy (Mitrofanoff, 1980) | Continent catheterisable channel |

**Diagram to draw:** sagittal view of bladder dome sutured to fascia and skin.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Blocksom: dome-based stoma midway between umbilicus and pubis.
> - Bladder keeps cycling; compliance preserved.
> - Stenosis and prolapse are the main complications.

## Management of Defecation Disorders resulting into LUTS
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2025-Winter Paper 4, Q3f

*Assumed meaning: bladder and bowel dysfunction, where constipation or defecation disorders cause LUTS (lower urinary tract symptoms).*

### Why the bowel causes LUTS
- **Shared space:** a loaded rectum compresses the bladder, reducing capacity and obstructing outflow.
- **Shared nerves:** bladder and bowel afferents converge on the same spinal segments; cross-organ sensitisation causes urgency and detrusor overactivity.
- **Shared muscle:** a pelvic floor that contracts during defecation (**dyssynergic defecation**) also contracts during voiding (**dysfunctional voiding**) → high PVR (post-void residual), UTI (urinary tract infection), reflux.
- Result: urgency, daytime incontinence, enuresis, retention, recurrent UTI.

### Evaluation
- Stool frequency, pain, **Bristol chart**; 3-day bladder and bowel diary.
- **Rome IV (child):** at least 2, weekly for 1 month: ≤2 defecations/week; faecal incontinence weekly; retentive posturing; painful or hard stools; large rectal mass; large-diameter stools.
- **Rome IV (adult), for 3 months:** ≥2 of straining, hard stools, incomplete evacuation, blockage, manual manoeuvres (each in >25% of defecations), <3 stools/week.
- Examine abdomen, spine and perineum; exclude organic causes (Hirschsprung disease, hypothyroidism, neurogenic).
- **Also know:** rectal diameter ≥30 mm on ultrasound; no routine X-ray; manometry if refractory.

### Management
**Rule: treat the bowel first (EAU, European Association of Urology/ESPU, European Society for Paediatric Urology).**
1. **Disimpaction:** oral PEG (polyethylene glycol) 3350, 1–1.5 g/kg/day for 3–6 days; enemas if needed.
2. **Maintenance:** PEG 0.4 g/kg/day (range 0.2–0.8), at least 2 months; lactulose alternative; stimulants (senna) for expulsion.
3. **Urotherapy:** toileting after meals (gastrocolic reflex); footstool posture; normal fluid and fibre.
4. **Biofeedback, pelvic floor physiotherapy** for dyssynergia.
5. **Refractory:** **transanal irrigation** (water via rectal cone; a quarter to a third stop); **ACE (antegrade continence enema, Malone)**; botulinum toxin to the sphincter; sacral or tibial neuromodulation.
- **Adults:** laxatives, prucalopride or linaclotide, biofeedback; rectocele repair; irrigation in neurogenic bowel.
- **Caution:** anticholinergics worsen constipation; LUTS return.

**Diagram to draw:** pelvic cross-section showing loaded rectum compressing bladder.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Treat the bowel first.
> - Rome IV: ≥2 criteria for 1 month (child).
> - PEG disimpaction 1–1.5 g/kg/day for 3–6 days.

# Section 6 · Female Urology — Short answer questions (SAQ)

## Acute urinary retention in females.
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2017-Summer Paper 4, Q3f

### Introduction
- AUR (acute urinary retention) is sudden inability to void. It is uncommon in women and is often non-obstructive, so search for the cause.

### Causes
| Group | Examples |
|---|---|
| Obstructive | Large cystocele or prolapse; tight sling after incontinence surgery; urethral diverticulum, stricture; pelvic mass, **retroverted gravid uterus**, haematocolpos; cancer |
| Functional | **Fowler syndrome**; dysfunctional voiding; primary bladder neck obstruction (rare) |
| Neurogenic | MS (multiple sclerosis; may present first with retention), cauda equina, transverse myelitis, diabetes |
| Inflammatory | Cystitis, vulvovaginitis, **genital herpes**, Bartholin abscess |
| Drugs | Anticholinergics, opioids, tricyclics, calcium-channel blockers |
| Postoperative/postpartum; psychogenic | Epidural, pain; diagnosis of exclusion |

### Fowler syndrome
- Young women (20–35) with painless retention and bladder capacity over 1 litre.
- **Urethral sphincter EMG (electromyography):** complex repetitive discharges, so the sphincter fails to relax. Urethral closure pressure >100 cm H₂O.

### Evaluation
- **History:** drugs, surgery, delivery, neurological and bowel symptoms, genital lesions.
- **Examination:** pelvic (prolapse, mass); neurological (perineal sensation, anal tone).
- **Tests:** drained volume; urine culture; creatinine; pregnancy test; ultrasound.
- MRI (magnetic resonance imaging) spine if neurological signs; cystoscopy.
- **Later:** video-urodynamics (female obstruction, e.g. Qmax ≤12 mL/s with Pdet ≥20 cm H₂O); sphincter EMG.

### Management
- **Immediate:** urethral catheter; suprapubic if urethral access fails.
- **Treat the cause:** stop drugs; treat infection or herpes; pessary or prolapse repair; reposition the uterus; sling division; diverticulectomy.
- **TWOC (trial without catheter)** after correction.
- **Persistent retention:**
  - **CISC (clean intermittent self-catheterisation)** rather than an indwelling catheter.
  - **SNM (sacral neuromodulation):** about 70% success in Fowler syndrome.
  - Bethanechol not recommended. Bladder neck incision only with caution (incontinence).

**Diagram to draw:** flowchart: AUR → catheterise → find cause → treat → TWOC → CISC or SNM.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Look for prolapse, sling obstruction, pelvic mass, neurology and drugs.
> - Fowler: abnormal sphincter EMG; SNM about 70%.
> - CISC for persistent retention.

## Rapid Revision Sheet – Section 6 · Female Urology

*All the key points from this section in one place. Read this in the last 30 minutes before the exam.*

#### Acute urinary retention in females. (SAQ)

- Look for prolapse, sling obstruction, pelvic mass, neurology and drugs.
- Fowler: abnormal sphincter EMG; SNM about 70%.
- CISC for persistent retention.

# Section 7 · Voiding Dysfunction — Long answer questions (LAQ)

## Neuromodulation for voiding dysfunction, including sacral neuromodulation
**Asked as:**
- Discuss neuromodulation for voiding dysfunction. — *Summer 2019 Paper IV Q1 (LAQ)*
- Sacral neuromodulation — *Summer 2015 Paper IV Q3c (SAQ)*
- Sacral neuromodulation. — *Summer 2016 Paper IV Q3c (SAQ)*
- Sacral Neuromodulation — *Winter 2017 Paper IV Q3b (SAQ)*

**Type:** LAQ (25 marks); also asked as SAQ (10 marks)

**See 2015-Summer Paper 3, Q3a (identical question)** (for Summer 2015 Paper IV Q3c; recent advances to add are listed under Recent advances below). **See 2017-Winter Paper 3, Q3a (full answer)** (for Winter 2017 Paper IV Q3b).

### Definition and history
- **Neuromodulation:** electrical stimulation of a peripheral nerve that alters the activity of central pathways controlling the bladder. It acts on the nervous system, not directly on muscle.
- Direct muscle stimulation failed (painful, local). Stimulating **afferents** needs less current and affects the whole organ.
- **Tanagho and Schmidt** (about 1985): SNM (sacral neuromodulation). PTNS (percutaneous tibial nerve stimulation): FDA (Food and Drug Administration) approval 2000.
- **SNM (sacral neuromodulation)** (Tanagho and Schmidt): continuous low-amplitude **S3** stimulation through a tined lead and IPG (implantable pulse generator).
- **Rule:** exclude anatomical causes first (obstruction, stones, cancer, fistula). Stimulation cannot correct anatomy or restore muscle.

### Mechanism
- Sacral roots are mixed nerves. Current excites **afferents → spinal cord and forebrain** (areas of awareness and attention) → altered PMC (pontine micturition centre) output. It acts on **afferent** nerves, not motor nerves.
- **OAB (overactive bladder):** heightened central control damps PMC firing → fewer involuntary contractions.
- **Non-obstructive retention:** restores awareness of filling (filling sensation) and pelvic floor tone → PMC switches on → **EUS (external urethral sphincter) relaxes**. Hence SNM treats opposite disorders (treats **both**).
- **Tibial:** nerve carries **S2–S3** fibres; inhibition of OAB-type (C-fibre) reflexes is **opioid-mediated** (naloxone blocks it); long-term cortical plasticity.

### Modalities
| Modality | Target and method | Main use |
|---|---|---|
| **SNM** | Tined lead in **S3** foramen + implanted generator | Refractory OAB, NOUR (non-obstructive urinary retention) |
| **PTNS** | 34-gauge needle above medial malleolus | Refractory OAB |
| TTNS (transcutaneous tibial) | Surface patch, home use | OAB (less effective) |
| Implantable tibial | Subcutaneous device over tibial nerve | OAB, avoids clinic visits |
| Pudendal | Tined lead in Alcock's canal | IDO (idiopathic detrusor overactivity), NDO (neurogenic DO), SNM failure |
| DGNS (dorsal genital nerve stimulation) | Dorsal penile/clitoral nerve | Experimental; NDO |
| IVES (intravesical electrical stimulation) | Intravesical cathode | Underactive bladder in children |
| **SARS (sacral anterior root stimulator)** | Anterior roots + dorsal rhizotomy | Complete SCI (spinal cord injury) |

### A. Sacral neuromodulation
- **Third line** for OAB (AUA (American Urological Association) and EAU (European Association of Urology) agree). **FDA approved:** UUI (urgency urinary incontinence) 1997; urgency-frequency and NOUR 1999. **Not approved:** bladder pain, pelvic pain, neurogenic bladder.

#### Indications (third line)
| FDA (Food and Drug Administration) approved | Not approved |
|---|---|
| Refractory UUI (urgency urinary incontinence) 1997; urgency-frequency 1999 | Bladder pain, pelvic pain |
| **Non-obstructive retention** (e.g. Fowler's syndrome) 1999; faecal incontinence 2011 | Neurogenic bladder |

- **Fowler's syndrome**: young women in retention with abnormal sphincter EMG (electromyography); responds well. Retention needs some detrusor function.

#### Technique
- **Test phase** (bladder diary): **PNE (percutaneous nerve evaluation)**: office, temporary lead, 5–7 days; or **staged implant**: permanent tined lead under fluoroscopy, test up to 4 weeks, then generator in the buttock (preferred for NOUR).

| Test phase | PNE (percutaneous nerve evaluation) | Staged tined lead |
|---|---|---|
| Lead | Temporary, office | Permanent, theatre |
| Duration | 5–7 days | Up to 4 weeks |
| Use | OAB | More reliable; retention |

- **Landmark:** Prone; needle into S3 about **9 cm above the coccyx tip, 1–2 cm lateral**. **S3 response: perineal "bellows" + great-toe flexion** (S2: calf/foot rotation; S4: bellows only).

| Root | Pelvic response | Foot response |
|---|---|---|
| S2 | — | Calf and foot rotation |
| **S3** | **Bellows**, perineal sensation | **Great-toe flexion** |
| S4 | Bellows only | None |

- **Implant if ≥50% improvement** in a diary parameter (retention: ≥50% fewer catheterisations); IPG in the buttock.
- **Also know:** 10–16 Hz at sensory threshold; bilateral stimulation no better.

#### Outcomes
- **Evidence:** **InSite**: success 61% vs 42% with medical therapy (over medical therapy at 6 months). **ROSETTA**: onabotulinumtoxinA 200 U reduced UUI slightly more (−3.9 vs −3.3 episodes/day) but caused more UTI (urinary tract infection) (24% vs 10%) and CIC (clean intermittent catheterisation) (self-catheterisation). NOUR: most stop catheterising.

#### Complications and contraindications
- **Problems:** pain, lead migration, loss of effect, infection; **revision >30% at 5 years** (over 30%); costly.
- Contraindications: failed test, inability to use the programmer, sacral anomaly.

#### Recent advances
- **Advances:** rechargeable (≥15 years) devices (**Rechargeable** stimulators: FDA (Food and Drug Administration) 2019); non-rechargeable devices lasting ≥10 years (2021); full-body (**Whole-body**) **MRI (magnetic resonance imaging)-conditional** systems at 1.5 T and 3 T.
- **Implantable tibial nerve stimulators** (BlueWind, eCoin); PTNS (percutaneous tibial nerve stimulation) as an alternative.

### B. Tibial nerve stimulation
- **PTNS technique:** 34-gauge needle **3–4 cm (three fingerbreadths) above the medial malleolus**, behind the tibia; ground pad on same leg; 20 Hz, 0–10 mA. Confirm by **toe flexion/fanning** or sole tingling. **30 min weekly × 12 weeks**, then maintenance.
- **SUmiT** (n = 220, validated sham): response **55% vs 21%**. Extension: 77% relapse-free at 3 years with tapering maintenance.
- **OrBIT:** PTNS comparable to extended-release tolterodine, with fewer side effects.
- **TTNS:** home self-treatment; skin impedance limits amplitude, so less effective.
- **Implantable devices** remove clinic visits: **BlueWind** (miniature, external control; benefit sustained at 3 years); **eCoin** (coin-sized, battery-powered, office insertion under local anaesthesia).

### C. Other targets
- **Pudendal nerve stimulation:** tined lead via posterior approach. Better than SNM in a blinded crossover; **salvage after failed SNM**. Off-label.
- **DGNS:** purely sensory; acute DO suppression in SCI; **conditional (on-demand)** stimulation possible. Electrode fixation is difficult.
- **IVES (Katona):** intravesical cathode in saline activates Aδ afferents → detrusor contraction. Needs some afferent and muscle function; daily sessions; the sham-controlled trial was negative.
- **Brindley SARS:** stimulates anterior S2–S4 roots → voiding, defecation, erection. **Dorsal rhizotomy** abolishes reflex incontinence and DSD (detrusor–sphincter dyssynergia). Sphincter relaxes faster than detrusor between bursts → **post-stimulus voiding**. Needs intact efferents.

### Comparison (third-line OAB)
| | PTNS | OnabotulinumtoxinA 100 U | SNM |
|---|---|---|---|
| Invasiveness | Lowest | Moderate | Implant |
| Drawback | 12 weekly visits | UTI, retention/CIC | Revision, cost |
| Retention | No role | Worsens | **Treats** |

- **Also know:** intravaginal stimulation (SUI, stress urinary incontinence); sacral dermatome stimulation; bilateral SNM no better; closed-loop SNM.

**Diagram to draw:** S3 lead with buttock generator (lead in S3 foramen, IPG in the buttock), and PTNS needle above the medial malleolus.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Afferent modulation explains SNM benefit in both OAB and retention.
> - S3: bellows + (plus) great-toe flexion; implant if ≥50% improvement (if test improves ≥50%).
> - SUmiT: PTNS 55% vs sham 21%; 12 weekly sessions.
> - ROSETTA: Botox 200 U slightly better for UUI, more UTI and CIC.

> **Key points to remember (recent advances angle):**
> - Rechargeable and long-life non-rechargeable stimulators, lasting at least 10 years.
> - Full-body MRI (magnetic resonance imaging)-conditional devices at 1.5 T and 3 T.
> - Implantable tibial nerve stimulators (BlueWind, eCoin) and transcutaneous tibial stimulation as less invasive alternatives.
> - Standardised tined-lead placement improves outcomes and lowers revision.

# Section 7 · Voiding Dysfunction — Short answer questions (SAQ)

## Video Urodynamics
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2016-Summer Paper 4, Q3d

### Definition
- **VUDS (videourodynamics)**: multichannel urodynamics (pressure–flow, with or without EMG (electromyography)) recorded at the same time as fluoroscopy, using **contrast as the filling fluid**.

### Set-up
- Vesical and rectal catheters (Pdet (detrusor pressure) = Pves − Pabd), flowmeter, EMG; C-arm (most flexible) or a tilting table. Position for the question: **SUI (stress urinary incontinence) standing**; voiding in the usual position (women sitting, men standing).
- **Screen only at key moments** (provocation, pressure rise, voiding) to limit radiation.

### What fluoroscopy adds
| Situation | VUDS finding |
|---|---|
| Neurogenic bladder | **Gold standard** (EAU); DESD (detrusor–external sphincter dyssynergia) and DISD (detrusor–internal sphincter dyssynergia); trabeculation, diverticula |
| **VUR** (vesicoureteric reflux) | Volume, pressure and grade at onset; reflux "pops off" pressure, so compliance looks falsely safe |
| **Bladder neck** | Only way to see it; **PBNO** (primary bladder neck obstruction) in young men and women |
| Female obstruction | **Nitti criteria**: radiographic narrowing between bladder neck and meatus during voiding |
| Dysfunctional voiding | "Spinning-top" urethra at the external sphincter |
| SUI | Leakage with ALPP (abdominal leak point pressure); open bladder neck at rest suggests ISD (intrinsic sphincter deficiency) |
| Male obstruction | Localises prostatic obstruction, bladder neck contracture or stricture |

**PBNO types**: I high pressure, low flow; II normal pressure, low flow with bladder-neck narrowing; III delayed bladder-neck opening.

### Indications
- Known or suspected NLUTD (neurogenic lower urinary tract dysfunction); unexplained retention in women; young men with LUTS (lower urinary tract symptoms); complex incontinence; prior radical pelvic surgery or radiation; diversion; renal transplant.
- **Children**: PUV (posterior urethral valves), spinal dysraphism; contrast-enhanced ultrasound VUDS avoids radiation.

### Urodynamic risk factors seen
- Impaired compliance; DESD; DISD; high-pressure DO (detrusor overactivity); DLPP (detrusor leak point pressure) >40 cm H₂O; poor emptying with high storage pressure.

### Limitations
- Radiation, cost, space, availability, contrast allergy. AUA (American Urological Association) guideline: lowest dose that answers the question.

**Diagram to draw:** VUDS trace (Pves, Pabd, Pdet, flow, EMG) beside a voiding cystogram image.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - VUDS = urodynamics + fluoroscopy with contrast.
> - Only test for bladder neck dysfunction and DISD.
> - Reflux pop-off makes compliance look falsely good.
> - DLPP >40 cm H₂O endangers upper tracts.

## Botulinum toxin in urology
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2017-Winter Paper 4, Q3a

**See 2017-Winter Paper 3, Q3b (full answer).**

> **Key points to remember (recent advances angle):**
> - Paediatric NDO (neurogenic detrusor overactivity): FDA approved onabotulinumtoxinA for children aged ≥5 years (2021); used from about 2 years of age.
> - Other formulations: abobotulinumtoxinA and incobotulinumtoxinA under study for detrusor overactivity; doses are not interchangeable between products.
> - Injection-free delivery under research: intravesical liposome-encapsulated toxin and other instillation vehicles.
> - Standard adult intradetrusor doses for comparison: onabotulinumtoxinA 100 U (idiopathic OAB (overactive bladder)), 200 U (NDO).

## Detrussor underactivity.
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2021-Summer Paper 4, Q3b
(also answered in 2019-Summer Paper 2, Q3d)

### Definition
- **DUA (detrusor underactivity), ICS (International Continence Society) 2002:** a contraction of reduced strength and/or duration, causing prolonged or incomplete emptying. **Acontractile detrusor:** no contraction.
- **UAB (underactive bladder):** the symptom complex (slow stream, hesitancy, straining, incomplete emptying). DUA is common in elderly men and women having urodynamics.

### Pathophysiology and causes
- Failure may be **myogenic** (myocyte loss, fibrosis), **efferent** (poor nerve activation) or **afferent** (reduced sensation cannot sustain the voiding reflex).

| Type | Examples |
|---|---|
| Neurogenic | Cauda equina, disc prolapse, spinal injury, diabetic neuropathy, radical pelvic surgery |
| Myogenic | Long-standing BOO (bladder outlet obstruction), diabetes, ischaemia, ageing |
| Drugs | Anticholinergics, opioids |
| Functional | Fowler syndrome, dysfunctional voiding |

### Evaluation
- Bladder diary, flow rate, **PVR (post-void residual)**, sacral neurological examination, spinal MRI if deficit, drug review.
- **Pressure-flow study is diagnostic:** low detrusor pressure with low flow.
- **BCI (bladder contractility index) = Pdet@Qmax (detrusor pressure at maximum flow) + 5 Qmax:** <100 weak, 100–150 normal, >150 strong. Also PIP (projected isovolumetric pressure), Watts factor, stop test.
- Ambulatory urodynamics if no contraction is seen.

### Management
- **Goals:** protect kidneys; prevent UTI (urinary tract infection) and stones; relieve symptoms. No drug reliably improves contraction.

| Aim | Options |
|---|---|
| Drainage | Timed and double voiding; **CISC (clean intermittent self-catheterisation), the mainstay**; suprapubic catheter if CISC impossible |
| Lower outlet resistance | α-blockers; pelvic-floor biofeedback; outlet surgery (TURP, transurethral resection of the prostate) in selected obstructed men |
| Improve contraction | **SNM (sacral neuromodulation)** for non-obstructive retention (Fowler); **sacral anterior root stimulator** with rhizotomy in complete spinal injury |
| Not recommended | Bethanechol, distigmine (weak evidence; bronchospasm, rarely cardiac arrest); Credé or Valsalva voiding (reflux risk) |
| Experimental | Intravesical prostaglandins, intravesical electrotherapy, TAC-302, latissimus dorsi myoplasty, stem cells |

- SNM and parasympathomimetics need some intact detrusor and sensory function; a fibrosed detrusor responds to nothing.

**Diagram to draw:** pressure-flow plot with BCI lines at 100 and 150.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - BCI = Pdet@Qmax + 5Qmax; <100 weak.
> - CISC is the mainstay; parasympathomimetics not recommended.
> - SNM for Fowler syndrome.

- Full-body MRI (magnetic resonance imaging)-conditional devices at 1.5 T and 3 T.

- CISC is the mainstay; parasympathomimetics not recommended.

# Section 9 · Andrology & Sexual Medicine — Long answer questions (LAQ)

## Define Azoospermia and describe types of testicular biopsies and surgical sperm retrival.
**Type:** LAQ (25 marks)  
**Source:** 2021-Summer Paper 4, Q2
(also answered in 2019-Summer Paper 1, Q3d and 2019-Summer Paper 2, Q3c)

### Definition
- **Azoospermia:** no sperm in the **centrifuged pellet** of the ejaculate on **at least two** semen analyses. Found in about 1% of men and 10–15% of infertile men.
- Not **cryptozoospermia** (rare sperm in pellet) or **aspermia** (no ejaculate).

### Classification
| Type | Causes | Clues |
|---|---|---|
| Pre-testicular | Kallmann syndrome, pituitary disease, **exogenous testosterone** | Low FSH (follicle-stimulating hormone), LH (luteinising hormone) |
| Testicular: **NOA (non-obstructive azoospermia)** | Klinefelter, Y microdeletion, cryptorchidism, chemotherapy, mumps | Small testes, high FSH |
| Post-testicular: **OA (obstructive azoospermia)** | CBAVD (congenital bilateral absence of the vas deferens), vasectomy, epididymal or ejaculatory duct obstruction | Normal testes and FSH |
| Ejaculatory | Retrograde ejaculation, anejaculation | Low volume; sperm in post-ejaculate urine |

### Evaluation (brief)
- Absent vasa → CFTR (cystic fibrosis transmembrane conductance regulator) testing of both partners. Volume <1 mL → post-ejaculate urine, then TRUS (transrectal ultrasound).
- **FSH ≤7.6 IU/L and testis long axis >4.6 cm → obstruction likely;** FSH >7.6 and axis ≤4.6 cm → spermatogenic failure.
- **Karyotype and Y microdeletion** in NOA. **AZF (azoospermia factor) a or b complete deletion: no sperm, do not operate.**

### Testicular biopsy
**Indications**
- Azoospermia with **normal testes, palpable vasa and normal FSH**: separates OA from NOA (AUA/ASRM, American Urological Association/American Society for Reproductive Medicine).
- Since ICSI (intracytoplasmic sperm injection) needs one sperm, biopsy is now **diagnostic and therapeutic**: plan it as TESE (testicular sperm extraction) and **cryopreserve** sperm.
- **Not needed:** CBAVD with normal FSH (spermatogenesis always present).

**Types**
| Type | Method | Use | Limits |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Open biopsy** | Small incision in the albuginea, pea-sized tissue | **Gold standard** for histology and retrieval | Open procedure |
| **Percutaneous core biopsy** | 14 G biopsy gun under cord block | Fresh retrieval in OA | Blind; may injure epididymis or testicular artery; avoid in scarred scrotum |
| **FNA (fine-needle aspiration)**, i.e. TESA (testicular sperm aspiration) | 23 G needle, suction, several passes | OA retrieval; backup to epididymal methods | Few tubules, poor architecture; low yield in NOA |
| **Microdissection biopsy** (micro-TESE) | Operating microscope | NOA | Long operation |

- **Also know:** multi-site FNA "mapping" in NOA.

**Open biopsy technique**
1. Local anaesthesia with cord block (or general/spinal); choose the larger testis.
2. 2 cm transverse incision; open tunica vaginalis.
3. 3–4 mm albugineal incision avoiding surface vessels (loupes).
4. Excise extruding tubules with iris scissors, **no forceps**.
5. Fix in **Bouin's, Zenker's or glutaraldehyde, never formalin** (distorts histology).
6. **Touch or wet prep** at once for sperm; negative → contralateral biopsy.
7. Close albuginea and tunica vaginalis separately; no antibiotics.

**Histology patterns**
| Pattern | Meaning |
|---|---|
| Normal | Suggests obstruction |
| Hypospermatogenesis | All stages, fewer cells |
| Maturation arrest | Stops at a germ-cell stage |
| SCO (Sertoli cell–only) | No germ cells |
| Tubular sclerosis/hyalinisation | End-stage (e.g. Klinefelter) |

- **Complications:** haematoma (commonest); **inadvertent epididymal biopsy** (most serious: causes obstruction); infection rare.

### Surgical sperm retrieval
**Indications:** CBAVD; unreconstructable obstruction; declined or failed vasectomy reversal; backup at reconstruction; NOA; anejaculation.

| Technique | Method | Best for | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|
| **PESA** (percutaneous epididymal sperm aspiration) | Fine needle into caput | OA | Few sperm; may obstruct epididymis |
| **MESA** (microsurgical epididymal sperm aspiration) | Single tubule opened at 16–25×; micropipette by **capillary action**, never suction | **OA (best)** | Many motile sperm, multiple frozen vials |
| **TESA** | 23 G needle, suction | OA | Least invasive; immotile sperm |
| **Conventional TESE** | Open biopsy, one or more sites | OA, NOA | Blind sampling |
| **Micro-TESE** (Schlegel 1999) | Testis opened in an avascular plane; **larger, opaque tubules** excised under the microscope | **NOA (best)** | Most sperm, least tissue |

**Key principles**
- **MESA:** start distally and move **proximally** until motile sperm appear; the most motile sperm are often in the efferent ductules.
- **Micro-TESE:** spermatogenesis in NOA is **focal**. Thin white tubules are Sertoli cell–only; plump ones hold sperm. Embryologist examines tissue in theatre; map positive sites; negative → other testis.

**Micro-TESE retrieval by diagnosis**
| Condition | Retrieval |
|---|---|
| Post-orchidopexy | 74% |
| AZFc deletion | 70% |
| Klinefelter | 68% |
| Post-chemotherapy | 53% |
| Maturation arrest | 40% |
| SCO | 30% |
| **AZFa/AZFb deletion** | **0%** |

- Overall sperm found in **about half** of NOA.
- **Before retrieval in NOA:** stop testosterone; repair clinical varicocele; off-label clomiphene, anastrozole or hCG (human chorionic gonadotropin).

### Use of retrieved sperm
- **ICSI is essential** (poor motility). Frozen sperm equal fresh, except in spinal cord injury.
- NOA: more miscarriage (sperm DNA damage); genetic counselling. No sperm: donor sperm or adoption.
- **Also know:** post-mortem retrieval needs prior consent; microfluidic sperm sorting.

**Diagram to draw:** micro-TESE: bivalved testis with plump spermatogenic tubules beside thin Sertoli cell–only tubules.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Azoospermia: no sperm in centrifuged pellet on two samples.
> - Open biopsy is the gold standard; fix in Bouin's, never formalin.
> - OA: MESA; NOA: micro-TESE (sperm in about half).
> - AZFa/b deletion: 0% retrieval, do not operate.

# Section 9 · Andrology & Sexual Medicine — Short answer questions (SAQ)

## Male menopause (andropause, late-onset hypogonadism)
**Asked as:**
- Male menopause. — *Summer 2017 Paper IV Q3e (SAQ)*
- Andropause — *Winter 2017 Paper IV Q3e (SAQ)*
- Male menopause — *Summer 2019 Paper IV Q3f (SAQ)*

**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)

*Read as: late-onset hypogonadism (testosterone deficiency of ageing men, "andropause"). Same topic as 2017-Summer Paper 4, Q3e and 2017-Winter Paper 4, Q3e; full answer given here.*

### Definition
- **Symptoms plus consistently low serum testosterone** in an ageing man.
- "Menopause" (or "Andropause") is a misnomer: the fall is gradual and partial. Preferred terms: PADAM (partial androgen deficiency of the ageing male), LOH (late-onset hypogonadism).

### Pathophysiology
- Testosterone falls slowly from middle age.
- Testosterone in the 70s is about one-third lower than in young men. (Another source: mean testosterone in the seventh decade is about **one-third lower** than in young men.)
- **Testis:** fewer Leydig cells, lower sperm output; FSH (follicle-stimulating hormone) rises about threefold.
- **Centre:** blunted feedback, irregular GnRH (gonadotrophin-releasing hormone) pulses.
- **SHBG (sex hormone-binding globulin) rises with age**, so free testosterone (bioavailable [free + albumin-bound] testosterone) falls more.
- Worse with obesity, diabetes and **opioids**.

### Clinical features
- **Sexual (most specific):** low libido, ED (erectile dysfunction), fewer morning erections.
- Fatigue, low muscle mass, osteoporosis, anaemia, low mood.
- **ADAM (Androgen Deficiency in the Aging Male)** questionnaire: sensitive, not specific.

### Diagnosis
- **Morning (7–11 am) total testosterone, twice (repeated).** AUA (American Urological Association): **<300 ng/dL** (≈10.4 nmol/L). EAU (European Association of Urology): about **12 nmol/L**; calculated free testosterone if borderline. Calculated bioavailable testosterone when SHBG is abnormal.
- **LH (luteinising hormone):** high in primary, low or normal in secondary hypogonadism. Check **prolactin** if LH is low (Low LH → **prolactin**); pituitary MRI (magnetic resonance imaging) if testosterone <150 ng/dL or prolactin high.
- Baseline: PSA (prostate-specific antigen), DRE (digital rectal examination), haematocrit.

### Treatment
- Lifestyle first: weight loss, exercise, treat comorbidities.
- **TRT (testosterone replacement therapy)** for symptoms + confirmed low level: gels, injections (enanthate every 2–3 weeks; undecanoate every 10–14 weeks), oral undecanoate. Stop if no benefit at 3–6 months (another source: at about 3 months).
- **Contraindications:**
  - Prostate or breast cancer.
  - **Wish for fertility** (TRT stops sperm production [suppresses spermatogenesis]; use hCG [human chorionic gonadotrophin] or clomiphene).
  - Haematocrit >50% (raised haematocrit).
  - Untreated severe sleep apnoea; recent MI (myocardial infarction) or stroke.

### Monitoring
- At 3–6 months, then yearly: testosterone, **haematocrit** (stop/hold if >54%), PSA.

### Safety evidence
- **TRAVERSE (2023):** 5,246 men aged 45–80 at high cardiovascular risk. TRT was **non-inferior for major cardiac events**; more atrial fibrillation and pulmonary embolism.
- **Testosterone Trials**: modest sexual benefit, **no cognitive benefit**.

**Diagram to draw:** hypothalamic–pituitary–testis axis with ageing defects.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Symptoms + low morning testosterone twice (AUA <300 ng/dL; EAU 12 nmol/L).
> - SHBG rises with age; LH separates primary from secondary; prolactin if LH low.
> - TRT harms fertility; watch haematocrit; TRAVERSE: cardiac non-inferiority.

## Klinefelter's syndrome.
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2018-Summer Paper 4, Q3b

### Definition and genetics
- **47,XXY**: commonest sex chromosome aneuploidy and **commonest genetic cause of male infertility**; 1 in 500–1000 male births.
- Non-mosaic in **80–90%**; the rest mosaic **46,XY/47,XXY** (milder, more sperm) or 48,XXXY variants. Extra X from non-disjunction (maternal age).
- Underdiagnosed: most never diagnosed; increasingly detected on NIPT (non-invasive prenatal testing).

### Pathophysiology
- Seminiferous tubules **hyalinise** progressively from puberty; germ cells are lost. Leydig cells fail, so testosterone falls. Puberty starts on time, then stalls.

### Clinical features
| Age | Features |
|---|---|
| Infant | Usually normal; micropenis, cryptorchidism, hypospadias |
| Adolescent/adult | **Small firm testes (<3.5 cm, 1–4 mL)**, gynaecomastia, tall stature with long legs (only ~30%), sparse hair, female fat distribution |
| Neurodevelopment | Language, learning and attention difficulties |

### Hormones and tests
- **High FSH (follicle-stimulating hormone) and LH (luteinising hormone), low or low-normal testosterone, high oestradiol** (raised oestradiol:testosterone ratio). Inhibin B low.
- Semen: **azoospermia** in most (~8% have ejaculated sperm).
- **Karyotype** confirms (AUA/ASRM, American Urological Association/American Society for Reproductive Medicine, guideline: karyotype all men with non-obstructive azoospermia or severe oligozoospermia).

### Management
- **Fertility:** **micro-TESE** (microdissection testicular sperm extraction) + ICSI (intracytoplasmic sperm injection). Sperm found in **~40–50%** (68% in one series); offspring are usually chromosomally normal.
- Spermatogenesis declines with age, so retrieve early (late adolescence or young adulthood). Some give clomiphene, anastrozole or hCG (human chorionic gonadotropin) beforehand.
- **TRT (testosterone replacement therapy):** for hypogonadism (bone, muscle, mood, libido). It **suppresses spermatogenesis**, so offer sperm retrieval or banking **before** starting.
- Gynaecomastia: reduction mammoplasty if persistent. Speech, educational and psychological support.

### Associated risks
- Osteoporosis, metabolic syndrome, diabetes, VTE (venous thromboembolism), autoimmune disease.
- **Mediastinal (extragonadal) germ cell tumours**; male breast cancer (~1% lifetime).

**Diagram to draw:** hypothalamic–pituitary–testis axis showing high FSH and LH, low testosterone.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - 47,XXY; non-mosaic 80–90%; 1 in 500–1000.
> - Small firm testes, high FSH and LH, azoospermia.
> - Micro-TESE ~50% success; retrieve sperm before testosterone.
> - Risks: mediastinal germ cell tumour, breast cancer, osteoporosis.

## Semen analysis nomenclature.
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2018-Summer Paper 4, Q3e

### Collection (WHO, World Health Organization, 2021, 6th edition)
- Masturbation into a clean wide-mouthed non-toxic container (special condom if needed); complete sample.
- Abstinence **2–7 days** (evidence: 1–2 days best). Keep at 20–37 °C; analyse within **1 hour**, after liquefaction (~30 min).
- At least **two samples, 2–3 weeks apart**, because of variability.

### Lower reference limits (5th centile, fertile men)
| Parameter | Lower limit |
|---|---|
| Volume | **1.4 mL** |
| Concentration | **16 million/mL** |
| Total count | **39 million/ejaculate** |
| Total motility | **42%** |
| Progressive motility | **30%** |
| Normal forms (strict) | **4%** |
| Vitality | **54%** |
| Peroxidase-positive leucocytes | <1 million/mL |

- These are reference values, not fertility cut-offs. The 6th edition grades motility as progressive, non-progressive or immotile.

### Nomenclature
| Term | Meaning |
|---|---|
| Normozoospermia | All parameters at or above reference |
| Oligozoospermia | Total count (or concentration) below reference |
| Asthenozoospermia | Progressive motility below reference |
| Teratozoospermia | Normal forms below reference |
| OAT (oligoasthenoteratozoospermia) | All three abnormal |
| Azoospermia | No sperm, **including centrifuged pellet** |
| Cryptozoospermia | Sperm absent in fresh smear, present only after centrifugation |
| Aspermia | No ejaculate (dry orgasm) |
| Hypospermia | Volume below reference |
| Necrozoospermia | Low vitality (many dead sperm) |
| Leucocytospermia | Peroxidase-positive white cells above 1 million/mL |
| Haemospermia | Blood in ejaculate |
| Globozoospermia | Round-headed sperm without acrosome |

### Notes
- **Low volume or aspermia:** post-ejaculatory urine for retrograde ejaculation; TRUS (transrectal ultrasound) for EDO (ejaculatory duct obstruction).
- **Vitality:** eosin–nigrosin, or **HOS (hypo-osmotic swelling)** test, which keeps sperm usable for ICSI (intracytoplasmic sperm injection).
- Only azoospermia is definitive; values overlap between fertile and infertile men.
- **Also know:** Kruger strict morphology; DNA fragmentation; antisperm antibodies (MAR, mixed antiglobulin reaction); semen pH no longer recommended.

**Diagram to draw:** normal sperm (head, midpiece, tail) beside a WHO reference box.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - WHO 2021: 1.4 mL, 16 million/mL, 39 million, 42%/30%, 4%, 54%.
> - Cryptozoospermia = sperm only in pellet; aspermia = no ejaculate.
> - Two samples 2–3 weeks apart; abstinence 2–7 days.

## Penile implants.
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2023-Winter Paper 4, Q3c
(also answered in 2018-Summer Paper 2, Q3d and 2020-Summer Paper 2, Q2)

### Indications
- ED (erectile dysfunction) failing, contraindicated for, or declining PDE5Is (phosphodiesterase type 5 inhibitors), intracavernosal injections or vacuum devices.
- Post-prostatectomy, diabetes, pelvic radiotherapy; **Peyronie disease with ED**; corporal fibrosis (**ischaemic priapism >36 h**, implant removal).
- **Contraindications:** active infection (urinary, skin, systemic), uncontrolled diabetes, unrealistic expectations, reversible ED.

### Types
| Type | Examples | Features |
|---|---|---|
| **Malleable (semirigid) rods** | Tactra (nitinol), Genesis (silver) | Simple, cheap, rarely fail; always firm; erode more in insensate men |
| 2-piece inflatable | Ambicor (discontinued) | No reservoir; poor flaccidity |
| **3-piece inflatable** | AMS 700 (CX, LGX), Titan | Cylinders, scrotal pump, reservoir; **best rigidity and flaccidity** |

### Preparation and consent
- Sterile urine; **blood glucose <200 mg/dL on the day**; chlorhexidine–alcohol skin preparation; vancomycin or cephalosporin plus gentamicin.
- **Consent:** the erect penis may be shorter; sensation and ejaculation may reduce; infection or failure may need reoperation.

### Surgical steps (3-piece, penoscrotal)
- Catheter → upper scrotal incision → corporotomy between stay sutures → dilate proximally and distally (instruments dorsolateral) → **goalpost test** for crossover → measure → cylinders equal to or 1 cm shorter.
- Pump in a dependent dartos pouch; reservoir in the **space of Retzius**, or **ectopic submuscular** after pelvic surgery (lockout valve prevents autoinflation).
- Infrapubic approach: reservoir placed under vision; dorsal nerve at risk. Activation at 4–6 weeks.

### Complications
- **Infection 1–3%** (biofilm): **antibiotic-coated devices halve it** (InhibiZone rifampin–minocycline; hydrophilic Titan dipped). **No-touch technique** <0.5%. Treat by removing **all** parts; **Mulcahy salvage** succeeds in **84%**.
- **Mechanical failure:** repair about **15% at 5 years, 30% at 10**.
- Crural perforation, crossover, urethral injury; erosion; glans necrosis; reservoir herniation.

### Outcomes
- **Satisfaction 80–90%** (patient and partner), highest of any ED treatment.

**Diagram to draw:** 3-piece inflatable implant: cylinders, scrotal pump, reservoir in the space of Retzius.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - 3-piece inflatable: best rigidity and flaccidity.
> - Glucose <200 mg/dL; coated device; no-touch.
> - Infected implant: remove all; Mulcahy salvage 84%.
> - Repair 15% at 5 years; satisfaction 80–90%.

## Rapid Revision Sheet – Section 9 · Andrology & Sexual Medicine

#### Define Azoospermia and describe types of testicular biopsies and surgical sperm retrival. (LAQ)

- Azoospermia: no sperm in centrifuged pellet on two samples.
- Open biopsy is the gold standard; fix in Bouin's, never formalin.
- OA: MESA; NOA: micro-TESE (sperm in about half).
- AZFa/b deletion: 0% retrieval, do not operate.

- Symptoms + low morning testosterone twice (AUA <300 ng/dL; EAU 12 nmol/L).
- SHBG rises with age; LH separates primary from secondary; prolactin if LH low.
- TRT harms fertility; watch haematocrit; TRAVERSE: cardiac non-inferiority.

#### Klinefelter's syndrome. (SAQ)

- 47,XXY; non-mosaic 80–90%; 1 in 500–1000.
- Small firm testes, high FSH and LH, azoospermia.
- Micro-TESE ~50% success; retrieve sperm before testosterone.
- Risks: mediastinal germ cell tumour, breast cancer, osteoporosis.

#### Semen analysis nomenclature. (SAQ)

- WHO 2021: 1.4 mL, 16 million/mL, 39 million, 42%/30%, 4%, 54%.
- Cryptozoospermia = sperm only in pellet; aspermia = no ejaculate.
- Two samples 2–3 weeks apart; abstinence 2–7 days.

#### Penile implants. (SAQ)

- 3-piece inflatable: best rigidity and flaccidity.
- Glucose <200 mg/dL; coated device; no-touch.
- Infected implant: remove all; Mulcahy salvage 84%.
- Repair 15% at 5 years; satisfaction 80–90%.

# Section 10 · Testis, Penis & Urethra — Short answer questions (SAQ)

## Testis sparing surgeries for CA testis
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2016-Summer Paper 4, Q3b

### Definition and rationale
- **Partial orchidectomy**: inguinal excision of the tumour, preserving the rest of the testis. The standard remains **radical inguinal orchidectomy**; sparing aims to keep androgen production and avoid lifelong testosterone.

### Indications
**Suspected germ cell tumour (GCT), all of:**
- Synchronous bilateral tumours or tumour in a **solitary testis**.
- Tumour **≤2 cm** (up to 2–3 cm, under 30% of testicular volume), preferably polar.
- Adequate preoperative testosterone.
- Not with a normal contralateral testis.

**Suspected benign or indeterminate lesions:**
- Small impalpable lesion (<10 mm) with normal markers: most benign (cyst, infarct, Leydig nodule), but 20–50% are small seminomas. Lesions <5 mm are rarely malignant.
- **Prepubertal**: teratoma and epidermoid cyst with normal AFP (alpha-fetoprotein): partial orchidectomy is the standard.

### Preoperative work-up
- Ultrasound of both testes; AFP, beta-hCG (human chorionic gonadotropin), LDH (lactate dehydrogenase); testosterone, LH (luteinising hormone); **sperm banking**; consent for radical orchidectomy.

### Technique
1. Inguinal incision; early cord control with a soft clamp.
2. Deliver the testis; isolate with towels.
3. **Intraoperative ultrasound** localises the lesion.
4. Cold ischaemia optional if resection <30 minutes.
5. Incise the tunica parallel to the segmental vessels; excise the mass with a rim of normal tubules.
6. **Frozen section** (benign versus malignant) and biopsies of adjacent parenchyma for GCNIS (germ cell neoplasia in situ).
7. Close the tunica; fix the testis in the scrotum.

### Management of GCNIS
- **Radiotherapy about 18–20 Gy** to the remnant: prevents invasive GCT, keeps Leydig function, but causes sterility; up to 40% later need testosterone.
- German Testicular Cancer Study Group: no local recurrences with radiotherapy; most men who omitted it recurred.
- Radiotherapy may be deferred for paternity under close follow-up. Local recurrence → completion orchidectomy.

### Complications and follow-up
- Haematoma, atrophy, hypogonadism, local recurrence. Follow with self-examination, ultrasound, markers and testosterone.

**Diagram to draw:** testis with polar tumour excised with a rim of normal tissue.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Solitary or bilateral tumour, ≤2 cm, normal testosterone.
> - Frozen section plus adjacent biopsies for GCNIS.
> - GCNIS: 18–20 Gy keeps Leydig function but sterilises.

## Videoendoscopic inguinal lymphadenectomy
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2019-Summer Paper 4, Q3d

### Definition
- **VEIL (video-endoscopic inguinal lymphadenectomy):** ILND (inguinal lymph node dissection) through thigh ports, beneath Scarpa's fascia; laparoscopic (Tobias-Machado) or robotic (**RVEIL**).
- **Aim:** open-surgery template without the groin incision that causes flap necrosis.

### Indications
- Penile SCC (squamous cell carcinoma), **impalpable nodes**, intermediate/high risk (pT1b+, G3, LVI (lymphovascular invasion)).
- **Mobile, non-bulky palpable nodes.**
- **Not for** bulky, fixed (cN3) or skin-involving nodes → chemotherapy, then open surgery.
- Also vulval cancer, melanoma.

### Boundaries (femoral triangle)
- Superior: **inguinal ligament**; lateral: **sartorius**; medial: **adductor longus**; floor: fascia lata; apex: muscles crossing.
- Mark an inverted triangle on the skin **before insufflation**, because gas distorts landmarks.

### Technique
```
Position: supine frog-leg / low lithotomy
→ 2 cm camera incision below the femoral triangle apex
→ finger dissection beneath Scarpa's fascia
→ two lateral and medial working ports (+ assistant port)
→ CO2 at 15 mmHg (higher briefly to create space)
→ develop space up to the inguinal ligament
→ remove nodal packet off fascia lata, apex to ligament
→ preserve saphenous vein if possible; clip tributaries
→ frozen section → deep dissection medial to femoral vein if positive
→ bag retrieval, suction drain in dependent part
```
- **Template:** superficial ± deep; Daseler's five zones; Cloquet's node is the highest deep node.
- **Never dissect lateral to the femoral artery** (femoral nerve).

### Outcomes vs open
| | Open ILND | VEIL/RVEIL |
|---|---|---|
| Wound infection, necrosis, dehiscence | Higher | **Lower** |
| Hospital stay, drain time | Longer | Shorter |
| Operative time | Shorter | About 2 h (slightly longer per side) |
| Nodal yield, recurrence | Reference | **Similar** |
| Evidence | Retrospective | Retrospective, selection-biased |

- Commonest complication: **lymphocele**, then lymphoedema and cellulitis.

### Robotic VEIL
- 3D vision, wristed instruments, ICG (indocyanine green) for lymphatics; adequate dissection on open re-exploration (phase I). No VEIL vs RVEIL comparison.

- **Also know:** DSNB (dynamic sentinel node biopsy) first for impalpable nodes (EAU-ASCO, European Association of Urology–American Society of Clinical Oncology); modified (Catalona) template; complete nodal management within 3 months.

**Diagram to draw:** femoral triangle with port sites below the apex.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Same template as open: ligament, sartorius, adductor longus.
> - Fewer wound complications; equal nodal yield.
> - Not for bulky, fixed or skin-involving nodes.

## Surgical management of primary scrotal cancer.
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2021-Summer Paper 4, Q3c

### Introduction
- Rare. **SCC (squamous cell carcinoma)** is commonest. **Percivall Pott (1775)** linked it to chimney soot: the first occupational cancer.
- **Risks:** soot, tar, mineral oils, arsenic, PUVA (psoralen + ultraviolet A), HPV (human papillomavirus), chronic inflammation.
- **Other types:** EMPD (extramammary Paget disease), BCC (basal cell carcinoma), melanoma, sarcoma.
- Lymph drains to **superficial inguinal nodes**, crossing the midline: both groins at risk.

### Evaluation
- Nodule or ulcer on the anterolateral scrotum, often late; **biopsy** confirms.
- Examine both groins; FNAC (fine-needle aspiration cytology) of palpable nodes; CT (computed tomography) for staging.

### Staging (Lowe modification of Ray and Whitmore)
| Stage | Extent |
|---|---|
| A1 | Confined to scrotum |
| A2 | Invades testis, cord, penis, pubis or perineum |
| B | Inguinal nodes only |
| C | Pelvic nodes, no distant spread |
| D | Distant metastases |

### Management of the primary
1. **Wide local excision with a 2 cm margin**, full thickness including dartos; frozen sections for margins.
2. Testes and cord preserved unless invaded. Hemi- or total scrotectomy for large tumours.
3. **Reconstruction:** scrotal laxity usually allows primary closure. Defect >50%: local or thigh flaps, split-skin graft, or thigh pouches for the testes.
4. Stage A2: en bloc removal of involved testis, penis, perineum or pubis.

### Management of nodes (extrapolated from penile cancer)
- **Palpable, cytology positive:** ILND (inguinal lymph node dissection) ± pelvic dissection. Nodal status is the main prognostic factor.
- **Impalpable:** surveillance; dynamic sentinel node biopsy or modified ILND for high-grade/invasive tumours. Routine prophylactic dissection is not advised.
- **Bulky/fixed nodes:** neoadjuvant cisplatin-based chemotherapy, then surgery; RT (radiotherapy) for palliation.

### Other histologies
- **EMPD:** wide excision (up to 3 cm) guided by **mapping biopsies**; screen for associated GU (genitourinary) and other cancers.
- **Also know:** BCC (excision or Mohs); melanoma (excision by Breslow depth ± sentinel node); sarcoma (wide excision).

**Diagram to draw:** elliptical wide excision with 2 cm margin and inguinal node drainage.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Pott 1775: chimney sweeps, first occupational cancer.
> - Wide local excision, 2 cm margin; testes spared unless invaded.
> - Nodes: managed as in penile cancer; nodes decide prognosis.

## Muscle assisted urethroplasty
**Asked as:**
- Muscle assisted urethroplasty — *Winter 2023 Paper IV Q3b (SAQ)*
- Muscle assisted urethroplasty — *Practice Set 5 Paper IV Q3a (earlier version) (SAQ)*

**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
(also answered in 2023-Winter Paper 4, Q3b)

*Read as muscle-flap-assisted graft urethroplasty: a vascularised muscle flap used as bed or cover for a graft. "Muscle-sparing" bulbar urethroplasty is noted at the end.*

### Rationale
