- A free graft (e.g. **BMG [buccal mucosal graft]**) has no blood supply. It survives by **imbibition** (about 48 h, nutrients from the bed), then **inosculation** (new microcirculation). So **"a graft lives on its bed"**.
- **Poor beds:** radiation, multiple failed repairs, fistula, pelvic-fracture scar, infection. Here a **muscle flap brings blood supply**, fills dead space and separates suture lines.

### Muscle flaps used
| Flap | Supply | Use |
|---|---|---|
| **Gracilis** (muscle-only, axial, multiple pedicles) | Medial circumflex femoral branch, proximal thigh | Irradiated posterior urethra, rectourethral fistula, complex perineal repair |
| **Bulbospongiosus** | Perineal branches | Covers a ventral graft; support in bulbar repair |
| Rectus abdominis (VRAM, vertical rectus abdominis myocutaneous) | Inferior epigastric | Large pelvic or perineal defects |

### Technique (gracilis-assisted BMG)
1. Lithotomy; perineal exposure; excise scar or fistula.
2. Close rectal side; urethra opened.
3. Gracilis harvested through a medial thigh incision; pedicle preserved; tunnelled to perineum.
4. Muscle interposed between rectum and urethra, or used as the graft bed.
5. **BMG quilted onto the muscle** as onlay; urethra closed over a catheter.
6. Catheter drainage; urethrogram before removal.

### Results (Chapter 86)
- Gracilis with or without BMG for rectourethral fistula: **non-irradiated, all closed**, no later strictures. **Irradiated:** most closed in one stage, but more strictures and permanent bowel diversion.
- Muscle interposition is integral; BMG is invaluable for urethral closure.

### Complications
- Thigh wound problems; graft contraction, restricture, fistula recurrence.

### "Muscle-sparing" bulbar urethroplasty (Barbagli)
- The **bulbospongiosus muscle, central perineal tendon and perineal nerves** are left intact; dorsal BMG onlay through a lateral approach.
- Aim: less post-void dribbling and ejaculatory dysfunction.

**Diagram to draw:** gracilis flap tunnelled to the perineum, lying between rectum and urethra with BMG on it.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Graft survival: imbibition then inosculation.
> - Muscle flap rescues a poor (irradiated) bed.
> - Gracilis is axial, robust, easy to tunnel.
> - Muscle-sparing: preserves bulbospongiosus and nerves.

## Robotic RPLND
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2025-Winter Paper 4, Q3e

**See 2025-Winter Paper 3, Q3f (full answer).**

> **Key points to remember (recent advances angle):**
> - Expanding indication: primary RPLND for small-volume stage II seminoma to avoid chemotherapy and radiotherapy.
> - Robotic nerve sparing preserves antegrade ejaculation in ≥90% after primary surgery.
> - Serum miR-371a-3p (microRNA) may select stage I patients with occult nodes for RPLND (retroperitoneal lymph node dissection).

## Gracilis myocutaneous flap in Uro.
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 3 Paper 4, Q3d
(gracilis-assisted urethroplasty also in 2023-Winter Paper 4, Q3b)

### Definition
- A pedicled **axial** flap of gracilis muscle, with or without skin, rotated from the medial thigh to bring **vascularised bulk** to the perineum, groin or vagina.

### Anatomy
| Feature | Detail |
|---|---|
| Origin–insertion | Inferior pubic ramus and body of pubis → medial proximal tibia (pes anserinus) |
| Blood supply | **Mathes–Nahai type II**: dominant pedicle from the **medial circumflex femoral artery** (profunda femoris), entering about 8–10 cm below the pubic tubercle; minor distal pedicles from the superficial femoral artery |
| Nerve | Anterior branch of the **obturator nerve** |
| Skin paddle | Reliable over the proximal two-thirds only |
| Function | Expendable (other adductors compensate) |

### Why it is used
- Brings blood supply into **irradiated or scarred** fields, fills dead space and **separates suture lines**. Near the perineum, easy to tunnel.

### Uses in urology
1. **Rectourethral fistula** (perineal): non-irradiated all closed; irradiated mostly closed, but more strictures and permanent colostomy.
2. **Complex or irradiated VVF (vesicovaginal fistula)** with tissue loss.
3. **Urethroplasty** in a poor bed: muscle as bed for BMG (buccal mucosal graft).
4. Extensive perineal or scrotal loss after **Fournier gangrene**.
5. **Groin cover** of femoral vessels after inguinal node dissection with skin excision.
6. Vaginal reconstruction after exenteration.

### Technique
1. Lithotomy; line from pubic tubercle to medial femoral condyle; gracilis lies just behind it.
2. Medial thigh incision; skin paddle over the proximal muscle if needed.
3. Divide the distal tendon; ligate minor pedicles; **preserve the dominant pedicle**.
4. Tunnel subcutaneously to the perineum; rotate without twisting.
5. Interpose and fix; drain the donor site.

### Complications
- Donor seroma, dehiscence, **distal skin-paddle necrosis**, thigh numbness; flap loss rare.

- **Also know:** alternatives: Martius, omentum, VRAM (vertical rectus abdominis myocutaneous), Singapore flap.

**Diagram to draw:** gracilis tunnelled on its proximal pedicle between urethra and rectum.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Type II flap: medial circumflex femoral pedicle 8–10 cm below the pubic tubercle.
> - Skin paddle unreliable over the distal third.
> - Main uses: rectourethral fistula, complex VVF, Fournier, groin cover.

## Rapid Revision Sheet – Section 10 · Testis, Penis & Urethra

*All the key points from this section in one place. Read this in the last 30 minutes before the exam.*

#### Testis sparing surgeries for CA testis (SAQ)

- Solitary or bilateral tumour, ≤2 cm, normal testosterone.
- Frozen section plus adjacent biopsies for GCNIS.
- GCNIS: 18–20 Gy keeps Leydig function but sterilises.

#### Videoendoscopic inguinal lymphadenectomy (SAQ)

- Same template as open: ligament, sartorius, adductor longus.
- Fewer wound complications; equal nodal yield.
- Not for bulky, fixed or skin-involving nodes.

#### Surgical management of primary scrotal cancer. (SAQ)

- Pott 1775: chimney sweeps, first occupational cancer.
- Wide local excision, 2 cm margin; testes spared unless invaded.
- Nodes: managed as in penile cancer; nodes decide prognosis.

#### Muscle assisted urethroplasty (SAQ)

- Graft survival: imbibition then inosculation.
- Muscle flap rescues a poor (irradiated) bed.
- Gracilis is axial, robust, easy to tunnel.
- Muscle-sparing: preserves bulbospongiosus and nerves.

#### Robotic RPLND (SAQ)

- Expanding indication: primary RPLND for small-volume stage II seminoma to avoid chemotherapy and radiotherapy.
- Robotic nerve sparing preserves antegrade ejaculation in ≥90% after primary surgery.
- Serum miR-371a-3p (microRNA) may select stage I patients with occult nodes for RPLND (retroperitoneal lymph node dissection).

#### Gracilis myocutaneous flap in Uro. (SAQ)

- Type II flap: medial circumflex femoral pedicle 8–10 cm below the pubic tubercle.
- Skin paddle unreliable over the distal third.
- Main uses: rectourethral fistula, complex VVF, Fournier, groin cover.

# Section 11 · Stones, Obstruction & Trauma — Long answer questions (LAQ)

## Urolithiasis: etiopathogenesis, medical management of (recurrent) stone disease and recent advances in RIRS instrumentation
**Asked as:**
- Describe etiopathogenesis of stone formation and medical management of urolithiasis. — *Summer 2020 Paper IV Q2 (LAQ)*
- Describe medical management of recurrent stone disease and discuss recent advances in instrumentations of RIRS. — *Summer 2016 Paper IV Q2 (LAQ)*

**Type:** LAQ (25 marks)

### Introduction
- Urolithiasis is a **chronic metabolic disease** (chronic metabolic disorder) punctuated by stone episodes; recurrence is up to 50% within 5 years (another source: about half recur within 5–10 years). Removal is surgical; recurrence is metabolic. Recurrent formers need a **metabolic evaluation** and targeted therapy.
- **Composition**: calcium oxalate about 60%, mixed with apatite about 20%, uric acid and struvite about 7% each, cystine 1–3%.
- RIRS (retrograde intrarenal surgery) is flexible ureteroscopy inside the kidney.

### Aetiology: risk factors
- **Epidemiological**: male sex (gap narrowing), hot climate and occupation (low urine volume), diet (animal protein, salt), family history.
- **Systemic**: **metabolic syndrome**, obesity, diabetes (insulin resistance lowers urine pH), gout, primary hyperparathyroidism, RTA (renal tubular acidosis), bowel disease or bypass, sarcoidosis.
- **Anatomical**: PUJ (pelviureteric junction) obstruction, horseshoe kidney, calyceal diverticulum, medullary sponge kidney (stasis plus metabolic defects).
- **Drugs**: direct (indinavir, triamterene); indirect (acetazolamide, topiramate, vitamin C and D excess).

### Physicochemistry: state of saturation
| State | Concentration product (CP) | What happens |
|---|---|---|
| Undersaturated | < Ksp (solubility product) | No crystals; stones may dissolve |
| **Metastable** | Between Ksp and Kf (formation product) | No spontaneous nucleation; growth on existing crystals; inhibitors work. **Zone of therapy** |
| Unstable | > Kf | Spontaneous nucleation, growth, aggregation |

- Crystals form in metastable urine by local high CP, stasis, or **heterogeneous nucleation** on cells, debris or other crystals (needs less energy).

### Steps of stone formation
1. **Supersaturation** (low volume, high calcium, oxalate, uric acid; low citrate).
2. **Nucleation**, then **growth** and **aggregation** (aggregation can occlude a tubule within the 5–7 minute nephron transit).
3. **Retention**: fixed-particle theory. **Randall plaque** (calcium apatite) starts in the basement membrane of the thin loop of Henle, spreads through the interstitium, erodes the papillary urothelium and anchors calcium oxalate growth (idiopathic formers). **Ductal plugs** (crystals in collecting ducts) suggest a secondary cause (enteric hyperoxaluria, distal RTA, hyperparathyroidism, cystinuria).

### Inhibitors, promoters, matrix
- **Inhibitors**: **citrate** (complexes calcium), **magnesium** (complexes oxalate), pyrophosphate, glycosaminoglycans, nephrocalcin, **Tamm-Horsfall protein**, osteopontin, bikunin. There is **no inhibitor of uric acid**.
- **Matrix** (about 2.5% of stone weight; matrix substance A): role uncertain.

### Pathogenesis by abnormality
| Abnormality | Mechanism |
|---|---|
| **Hypercalciuria** (commonest) | Absorptive (gut), renal leak (high PTH (parathyroid hormone)), resorptive (primary hyperparathyroidism) |
| Hyperoxaluria | Primary (hepatic enzyme defect, PH1 AGXT), **enteric** (fat binds calcium, freeing oxalate), dietary |
| Hyperuricosuria | Urate promotes calcium oxalate nucleation |
| **Hypocitraturia** | Distal RTA, diarrhoea, hypokalaemia, animal protein (intracellular acidosis) |
| **Uric acid** | **Low urine pH** (<5.5; pKa 5.5) more than excess uric acid |
| **Cystine** | Autosomal recessive defect in renal cystine reabsorption |
| **Struvite** | **Urease** (Proteus) splits urea → ammonia, pH >7.2 → magnesium ammonium phosphate + carbonate apatite; biofilm |

### Medical management
**1. Acute colic and MET (medical expulsive therapy)**
- **NSAIDs (non-steroidal anti-inflammatory drugs)** first line; opioids only if refractory.
- **α-blocker** (tamsulosin; silodosin faster) for **distal ureteric stones 5–10 mm**: relaxes ureteric muscle. **SUSPEND (2015)** was negative because it included all sizes and sites. Trial of passage 4–6 weeks.

**2. Dissolution**: uric acid stones (radiolucent) dissolve with oral potassium citrate (EAU (European Association of Urology): pH 7.0–7.2 for dissolution).

**3. Prevention: general measures (every former)**
- **Fluid** for urine output **≥2.5 L/day**. **Borghi 1996**: urine >2 L cut 5-year recurrence (12.1% vs 27% recurrence at 5 years).
- **Normal calcium**, low sodium (≤2.3 g/day), less animal protein, more fruit and vegetables (DASH (Dietary Approaches to Stop Hypertension)-type diet). **Borghi 2002**: this diet halved recurrence compared with a low-calcium diet. Restricting calcium raises oxalate absorption.
- Citrus juices (lemonade, orange) are adjuncts only.

**4. Metabolic evaluation that guides therapy** (high-risk and recurrent formers) (see 2015-Summer Paper 2, Q2)
- **Stone analysis** (infrared spectroscopy or X-ray diffraction); serum chemistry: serum calcium, uric acid, creatinine, electrolytes, PTH (parathyroid hormone) if calcium is high-normal.
- One or **two 24-h urines** on usual diet, at least 4 weeks after an episode: volume, calcium, oxalate, citrate, uric acid, pH, sodium, cystine.
- Targets: volume >2.5 L, calcium <250 mg (women) or <300 mg (men), oxalate <40 mg, citrate >320 mg, uric acid <600 mg.
- Repeat within 6 months of therapy, then yearly.

**5. Selective drug therapy**
| Abnormality | Drug and dose (treatment) | Rationale (mechanism and notes) |
|---|---|---|
| Hypercalciuria | **Thiazide**: hydrochlorothiazide 25 mg twice daily, chlorthalidone 25–50 mg, indapamide 2.5 mg | Distal calcium reabsorption plus volume contraction. Add potassium citrate: hypokalaemia causes hypocitraturia (hypokalaemia → hypocitraturia) |
| Primary hyperparathyroidism | Parathyroidectomy | Cures resorptive hypercalciuria |
| Hypocitraturia, distal RTA (renal tubular acidosis), chronic diarrhoea | **Potassium citrate 30–60 mEq/day** | Alkali load reduces tubular (proximal) citrate reabsorption; avoid urine pH ≥7 |
| Hyperuricosuria / hyperuricosuric calcium oxalate | Allopurinol 300 mg/day | Watch hypersensitivity; reduce dose in renal impairment |
| Uric acid stones | Potassium citrate to **pH 6.0–6.5**; allopurinol only for hyperuricosuria or failed alkalinisation | A pH disease: pKa 5.5; dissolution possible |
| Enteric hyperoxaluria | Low fat and oxalate; **calcium with meals**; liquid potassium citrate; magnesium; cholestyramine | Calcium binds oxalate in the gut (binds gut oxalate) |
| Primary hyperoxaluria type 1 | Pyridoxine; **lumasiran** (RNA (ribonucleic acid) interference against glycolate oxidase) | ILLUMINATE-A: 84% vs 0% reached near-normal urine oxalate |
| Cystinuria | Urine ≥3 L/day; low sodium and protein; potassium citrate to pH **7.0–7.5**; then **tiopronin** (or D-penicillamine) | Aim cystine <250 mg/L; mixed disulfide: thiols form soluble mixed disulfides |
| Struvite | Complete clearance; culture-specific antibiotics; AHA (acetohydroxamic acid) 250 mg three times daily if surgery impossible | Urease inhibitor; risk of thromboembolism |

- **Also know:** recurrent calcium stones with a normal work-up: thiazide or potassium citrate (AUA (American Urological Association)); citrus juices are adjuncts.
- **Trials**: Ettinger 1988 (chlorthalidone) and Barcelo 1993 (potassium citrate) reduced new stones. **NOSTONE (2023)**: hydrochlorothiazide 12.5–50 mg did not reduce recurrence, which questions the older trials.
- **Follow-up**: repeat 24-h urine within 6 months, then yearly; serum potassium 1–2 weeks after starting a thiazide or citrate; imaging for new stones.

### Recent advances in RIRS instrumentation
| Advance | Principle | Benefit or limitation |
|---|---|---|
| **Digital flexible ureteroscopes** | CMOS (complementary metal-oxide semiconductor) chip at the tip; LED (light-emitting diode) light | Sharper image that does not degrade; tip larger (~8.5 Fr); no more durable |
| **Single-use scopes** (LithoVue, 2016) | 7.7 Fr tip, ≥270° deflection, new each case | No repair or reprocessing; cheaper below ~99 cases a year; for lower pole and difficult cases |
| **Thulium fibre laser** (about 1940 nm) | Long pulse, very high frequency | Fine dust, minimal retropulsion; faster than Ho:YAG (holmium:yttrium-aluminium-garnet, 2120 nm) at low settings |
| **Pulse-modulated (Moses) Ho:YAG** | Pulse shaped through a vapour bubble | Less retropulsion, faster dusting |
| Access sheaths (12/14 Fr) | Outflow channel | Lower intrarenal pressure and temperature; severe ureteric injury in ~1 in 8 |
| **Suction sheaths and FANS** (flexible and navigable suction access sheath) | Vacuum at the sheath; flexible tip follows the scope into calyces | Aspirates dust; lower pressure; better stone-free rates in early studies |
| **Intrarenal pressure monitoring** | Sensor in the wire or scope (LithoVue Elite) | High pressure causes backflow, SIRS (systemic inflammatory response syndrome) and sepsis; safe limits not yet defined |
| **Robotic flexible ureteroscopy** (Avicenna Roboflex) | Seated console controls the scope | Ergonomics, less radiation to surgeon |

- **Laser safety**: tissue damage above 43 °C, so keep power ≤10 W in the ureter and ≤25 W in the kidney, with continuous cool irrigation.
- **Also know:** dusting versus fragment-and-basket; nitinol tipless baskets; anti-retropulsion devices; Monarch robotic platform for PCNL (percutaneous nephrolithotomy) access.

**Diagram to draw:** saturation zones (Ksp, Kf) and Randall plaque on a papilla.

**Diagram to draw:** 24-h urine → abnormality → drug flowchart.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Metastable zone between Ksp and Kf is where inhibitors and therapy act.
> - Randall plaque = interstitial apatite anchor; ductal plugs suggest secondary cause.
> - Urine ≥2.5 L/day; Borghi 1996: 12% vs 27% recurrence; MET for distal 5–10 mm stones.
> - Thiazide plus potassium citrate for hypercalciuria; NOSTONE 2023 negative.
> - Uric acid pH 6.0–6.5; cystine pH 7.0–7.5 and <250 mg/L.
> - RIRS: single-use digital scopes, thulium laser, suction sheaths, pressure sensing.

## Energy sources for breaking stones in urology
**Asked as:**
- Describe energy sources for breaking stones in Urology — *Practice Set 1 Paper IV Q2 (LAQ)*
- Describe energy sources for braking stones in uro — *Practice Set 5 Paper IV Q1 (LAQ)*

**Type:** LAQ (25 marks)  
(also covered in 2025-Winter Paper 4, Q2 and Q3b; also answered in Practice Set 1 Paper 4, Q2)

*Read as "energy sources for breaking stones in urology".*

### Introduction
- Stones are **brittle**: they crack under tension and shear. Tissue is **elastic** and survives the same stress. Every lithotripter exploits this difference.
- Energy reaches the stone **extracorporeally** (SWL, shock wave lithotripsy) or **intracorporeally** through an endoscope.

### Classification
| Group | Sources |
|---|---|
| Extracorporeal | SWL: electrohydraulic, electromagnetic, piezoelectric; BWL (burst wave lithotripsy, investigational) |
| Intracorporeal mechanical | Ballistic (pneumatic), ultrasonic, combined ultrasonic–pneumatic |
| Intracorporeal electrical | EHL (electrohydraulic lithotripsy) |
| Intracorporeal light | Lasers: Ho:YAG (holmium:yttrium-aluminium-garnet), TFL (thulium fibre laser), pulsed Tm:YAG (thulium:YAG); historic pulsed dye and FREDDY |

### 1. Shock wave lithotripsy
- **Shock wave:** peak positive pressure 30–100 MPa, then a tensile tail of about −10 MPa. It is generated at F1, focused, and delivered to the stone at F2.

| Generator | Mechanism | Strength | Weakness |
|---|---|---|---|
| Electrohydraulic (original HM3, 1980) | Underwater spark at F1 of an ellipsoid reflector | Very effective | Electrode life <40,000 shocks; focus drifts |
| Electromagnetic (modern standard) | Coil pushes a metal membrane; lens or reflector focuses | Consistent; high energy at F2; long life | — |
| Piezoelectric | Many ceramic elements on a spherical dish | Long life; little pain | Small focal zone, low energy |

- **Fracture mechanisms:** shear and tear forces; **spallation** at the distal face; quasistatic squeezing; **cavitation** (bubble collapse erodes the surface); dynamic squeezing (most powerful). A focal zone wider than the stone works best.
- **Technique:** 60–90 shocks/min beats 120/min, because bubble clouds shield the stone and injure the kidney; voltage ramping; bubble-free gel coupling.
- Poor results: calcium oxalate monohydrate, brushite, cystine, density >1000 HU (Hounsfield units), skin-to-stone distance >10 cm.

### 2. Ballistic (pneumatic) lithotripsy
- Compressed air drives a projectile against a metal probe, a **jackhammer** (about 12 Hz).
- Breaks all stones cheaply with little ureteric injury. Drawbacks: **retropulsion**; rigid probes only, as flexible probes block deflection.

### 3. Ultrasonic lithotripsy
- A piezoceramic transducer vibrates a hollow probe at ultrasonic frequency; contact drills the stone.
- **Suction** through the hollow probe removes fragments at once. Best for soft stones in PCNL (percutaneous nephrolithotomy); weak on hard stones; needs irrigation for cooling.

### 4. Combined devices
- Ultrasonic plus ballistic in one probe (for example Trilogy, ShockPulse-SE): ballistic cracks hard stones while ultrasound and suction clear fragments. Faster PCNL.

### 5. Electrohydraulic lithotripsy
- A spark at the electrode tip forms a **vapour bubble**; expansion and collapse send pressure waves in all directions.
- Cheap; 3–5 Fr flexible probes. Poorly focused, so it injures the ureter and the scope; weak on cystine. AUA (American Urological Association) advises against it as first-line ureteric lithotripsy; still used for bladder stones.

### 6. Lasers
- **Pulse duration decides mechanism.** Short pulses (<10 µs: pulsed dye, FREDDY) are photomechanical, with cavitation shocks. Long pulses (>250 µs: holmium, thulium) are **photothermal**: the stone is heated until it melts, decomposes or flashes its water to steam.

| | Ho:YAG | TFL |
|---|---|---|
| Wavelength | **2120 nm** (often quoted 2100) | 1940 nm (water absorption peak) |
| Frequency | Up to about 120 Hz | Up to about 2000 Hz |
| Peak power | High | Low → less retropulsion |
| Fibre | ≥200 µm | 50–150 µm (more flow and deflection) |
| Product | Fragments and dust | Finer dust |

- **Holmium:** standard since the 1990s; breaks **every** composition; flexible quartz fibres. Fragmenting 0.6–1.0 J × 6–10 Hz; dusting 0.2–0.3 J × 40–80 Hz; "popcorning" in a calyx.
- **Moses effect:** the start of the pulse boils a vapour channel through which the rest reaches the stone; pulse modulation reduces retropulsion.
- **TFL RCTs (randomised controlled trials):** superior to low-power (30 W) holmium; equal stone-free rate to 120 W pulse-modulated holmium.
- **Retropulsion** rises with pulse energy, not frequency, and falls with longer pulses.

### Safety
- Laser heat follows total power: ≤10 W in the ureter, pauses, high flow, access sheath. Eyewear for all.
- Never fire without seeing the fibre tip on the stone.
- SWL: haematoma, arrhythmia, steinstrasse. Contraindications: pregnancy, coagulopathy or anticoagulants, untreated infection, aneurysm, distal obstruction.

### Choice by site
| Site | Usual energy |
|---|---|
| Ureter | Laser (semirigid or flexible); pneumatic distally; SWL |
| Kidney | SWL; laser via flexible URS (ureteroscopy); PCNL with ultrasonic, combined or laser |
| Bladder | Laser, pneumatic, EHL, mechanical |

### Recent advances
- **BWL:** handheld ultrasound bursts (5–100 cycles) break stones without anaesthesia. **Ultrasonic propulsion** moves fragments.
- Suction sheaths; AI (artificial intelligence)-adjusted laser settings.

**Diagram to draw:** SWL electrohydraulic ellipsoid with F1 and F2, stone at F2.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - SWL: electromagnetic is the modern standard; slow rate (60–90/min) is better.
> - Holmium 2120 nm, photothermal, breaks every composition.
> - TFL 1940 nm: low peak power, finer dust, less retropulsion.
> - EHL is poorly focused, so it is not first-line in the ureter.

# Section 11 · Stones, Obstruction & Trauma — Short answer questions (SAQ)

## Tubeless PCNL
**Asked as:**
- Tubeless PCNL — *Summer 2016 Paper IV Q3e (SAQ)*
- Tubeless PCNL — *Practice Set 6 Paper IV Q3e (earlier version) (SAQ)*

**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
(also answered in 2016-Summer Paper 4, Q3e and 2025-Winter Paper 2, Q3e)

### Definition
- PCNL (percutaneous nephrolithotomy) that ends **without a nephrostomy tube**.
- **Tubeless**: drainage by a ureteral stent (double-J, or externalised stent or string). **Totally tubeless**: no stent and no tube.
- **Wickham (1984)** did the first tubeless PCNL; **Bellman (1997)** popularised it.

### Rationale
- A large nephrostomy tube causes pain, more analgesia, longer stay and (higher) cost. Most tracts stop bleeding without tamponade.

### Selection
| Suitable | Keep a nephrostomy |
|---|---|
| Complete clearance, single tract | Pyonephrosis or infected stone |
| Minimal bleeding | Significant bleeding |
| Intact collecting system | Significant perforation of the collecting system (significant collecting-system injury) |
| Supracostal access, bilateral cases (shown safe) | Residual stone needing a second look |

### Technique (exit strategy)
1. Confirm clearance by rigid and **flexible nephroscopy** and fluoroscopy.
2. Antegrade double-J stent, or externalised stent through the urethra or a string.
3. Withdraw the sheath under vision; the **safety wire is removed last**.
4. Watch the tract; apply pressure (rib as backstop in thin patients). Fibrin glue in the tract did not reduce bleeding in randomised trials; some use haemostatic sealants or tract cautery.
5. Urethral catheter overnight.

### Evidence
- Meta-analyses of randomised trials: shorter stay and operative time.
- Less pain than large tubes (**>16 Fr**), but **no benefit against (over) small 8–9 Fr tubes**.
- No difference in fever, infection, transfusion or stone-free rate.
- Stent discomfort: worse WiSQoL (Wisconsin Stone Quality of Life) scores than nephrostomy in two trials. Externalised stents are removed (come out) before discharge without cystoscopy.
- Children: insufficient evidence.

### Disadvantages and complications
- Loss of tract for a second look.
- Stent symptoms and a further procedure for removal.
- Urine leak or urinoma.
- Bleeding without tamponade: re-insert a balloon or Kaye catheter, then **angioembolisation**.

### Current position
- Choose tubeless, small tube (≤10 Fr) or stent case by case; both stents and small tubes are acceptable (AUA, American Urological Association). Pairs well with mini-PCNL.

**Diagram to draw:** kidney with sealed tract and double-J stent.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Tubeless = stent only; totally tubeless = neither.
> - Benefit is mainly over large (>16 Fr) tubes.
> - Keep a tube for bleeding, perforation, infection or second look.

## Miniperc: miniaturised PCNL, its nomenclature and newer PCNL techniques
**Asked as:**
- Miniperc — *Winter 2017 Paper IV Q3d (SAQ)*
- Newer PCNL techniques and nomenclature of miniaturised PCNL — *Practice Set 4 Paper IV Q3a (SAQ)* (mini-PCNL also answered in 2022-Summer Paper 1, Q1; prone versus supine in 2024-Winter Paper 1, Q3f)
- Miniperc — *Practice Set 6 Paper IV Q3f (earlier version) (SAQ)* (also answered in 2017-Winter Paper 4, Q3d and 2022-Summer Paper 1, Q1)

**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)

### Definition and why miniaturise
- PCNL (percutaneous nephrolithotomy) through a **smaller tract** than standard, aiming to reduce bleeding and renal trauma. First "mini-perc" by **Jackman (1998)**, 13 Fr, in children.
- In PCNL, bleeding rises with tract size. Smaller tracts mean less bleeding and pain, at the cost of longer operating time and poorer vision.

### Nomenclature (by tract or outer sheath size)
| Name (designation) | Tract (size) | Features |
|---|---|---|
| Standard | 24–34 Fr | Ultrasonic or pneumatic lithotripsy; graspers |
| **Mini** | 15–22 Fr | First "mini-perc": Jackman 1998, 13 Fr; irrigation washes fragments out ("vacuum-cleaner" effect) |
| **Super-mini** | About 10–14 Fr | Sheath with **active suction** of fragments |
| **Ultra-mini** | 11–14 Fr | 3 Fr inline telescope in 11–13 Fr sheath |
| **Micro-perc** | <10 Fr | Single-step "all-seeing needle" with optics; laser only, fragments pass spontaneously |

- **Problem:** the nomenclature is contested: names overlap and cut-offs are contested (paediatric chapter: mini <20–24 Fr), so many authors report the **actual sheath size in Fr**.
- The **working channel**, not sheath size, decides which instruments pass (decides what passes): the smallest systems take only a laser fibre.

### Instruments and technique
- Mini nephroscopes (e.g. 12 Fr telescope, 15–18 Fr sheath, 6 Fr channel); laser (holmium/thulium) or small pneumatic probes.
- **Fragment removal**: irrigation outflow around the scope washes fragments out (**"vacuum-cleaner" effect**), or **active suction sheaths** (super-mini PCNL).
- Ultrasound or fluoroscopic access; supine or prone; ECIRS (endoscopic combined intrarenal surgery) possible.
- Exit: often **tubeless** or small nephrostomy (≤10 Fr: less pain).

### Newer techniques
1. **Supine PCNL and ECIRS (endoscopic combined intrarenal surgery):** Galdakao-modified supine; simultaneous retrograde flexible ureteroscopy watches puncture and dilatation; fewer tracts.
2. **Ultrasound-guided access:** less radiation; shows pleura and viscera.
3. **Single-step or balloon dilatation.**
4. **Suction sheaths:** lower pressure, faster clearance.
5. **Lasers:** high-power and pulse-modulated holmium, TFL (thulium fibre laser) for dusting through small tracts.
6. **Flexible nephroscopy** after bulk removal: higher stone-free rate.
7. **Tubeless or small (≤10 Fr) nephrostomy:** less pain.

- **Also know:** robotic or artificial intelligence-assisted puncture under study.

### Advantages over standard PCNL
- Less haemoglobin drop, **fewer transfusions**, shorter stay; less parenchymal damage; useful in children.
- Children: a 24–30 Fr tract in a child equals about a 72 Fr tract in an adult, so miniaturisation matters most here.

### Disadvantages
- **Longer operative time**, poorer visualisation (vision), slower fragment clearance.
- **Intrarenal pressure**: high-pressure irrigation through a small (tight) sheath, especially when the scope nearly fills it, can raise pelvic pressure → (causes) pyelovenous backflow, **fever and sepsis**. Poor outflow through small sheaths raises intrarenal pressure. Keep outflow free, use low-pressure irrigation, or suction sheaths.

### Evidence
- Mini vs standard (meta-analysis): **non-inferior stone-free rate**, less bleeding (fewer transfusions), shorter stay, longer surgery (operation); same complications (adverse events).
- Stones >2 cm: equal stone-free rate and fever; mini bleeds less than **30 Fr** tracts, but **every advantage disappears versus (against) 24 Fr** (against a **24 Fr** tract these advantages disappear).
- Mini-PCNL versus RIRS (retrograde intrarenal surgery), large matched study: similar stone-free and retreatment rates; RIRS shorter stay.

### Indications and contraindications
- Renal stones **1–2 cm**, **lower pole stones** >1 cm (alternative to RIRS (retrograde intrarenal surgery)), failed SWL (shock wave lithotripsy) or RIRS, **paediatric stones**, calyceal diverticular stones; selected larger stones or extra staghorn tracts.
- **Contraindications**: as for PCNL (untreated infection, uncorrected coagulopathy, pregnancy).

**Diagram to draw:** tract sizes compared (standard, mini, ultra-mini, micro) — scale bar of tract sizes from micro to standard.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Standard 24–34, mini 15–22 Fr; ultra-mini 11–14 Fr; micro <10 Fr; Jackman 1998: first mini-perc (13 Fr).
> - Fewer transfusions and shorter stay, but longer operation (time).
> - Working channel decides instruments; suction lowers pressure.
> - Watch intrarenal pressure; advantage lost against 24 Fr tracts (equal to 24 Fr).

## Struvite stones.
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2018-Summer Paper 4, Q3a

### Definition and epidemiology
- **Infection stones:** magnesium ammonium phosphate hexahydrate (MgNH₄PO₄·6H₂O), usually mixed with **carbonate apatite**.
- 5–15% of stones (falling); female:male ~2:1; highest in India and Pakistan. Risk: neurogenic bladder, spinal injury, diversion, catheters, obstruction, diabetes, elderly.

### Pathogenesis
```
Urease-producing bacteria
  Urea + H2O -> 2NH3 + CO2
  NH3 -> NH4+ (urine pH > 7.2)
  CO2 -> carbonate; phosphate -> PO4(3-)
  + Mg -> struvite;  + Ca -> carbonate apatite
```
- Sterile urine has no urease, so infection is essential. Ammonia damages the GAG (glycosaminoglycan) layer; bacteria live inside the stone in a **biofilm**, so antibiotics cannot sterilise a retained stone.
- **Organisms:** **Proteus mirabilis** (commonest), Klebsiella, Pseudomonas, Providencia, Staphylococcus, **Ureaplasma** (easily missed). E. coli is rarely urease-positive.

### Clinical features
- Recurrent UTI (urinary tract infection), fever, flank pain, haematuria; often few symptoms. Grow fast into **staghorn** calculi. Untreated: pyonephrosis, XGP (xanthogranulomatous pyelonephritis), sepsis, renal loss.

### Evaluation
- Urine pH >7; culture (urine and stone cultures often differ). NCCT (non-contrast computed tomography): burden and anatomy; DMSA (dimercaptosuccinic acid) or renogram for split function. Stone analysis; 24-h urine (many have hypercalciuria or hypocitraturia).

### Management
- **Complete stone clearance** is the goal: each fragment is an infected nidus.
- Culture-specific antibiotics before surgery; drain an obstructed infected kidney first.
- **PCNL (percutaneous nephrolithotomy): first line** for staghorns (AUA, American Urological Association). Add flexible nephroscopy, ECIRS (endoscopic combined intrarenal surgery) or second look. SWL (shock wave lithotripsy) or RIRS (retrograde intrarenal surgery) for small stones only.
- Anatrophic nephrolithotomy rarely; nephrectomy for a poorly functioning kidney.
- **Prevention:** surveillance cultures, suppressive antibiotics, fluids, correct stasis or metabolic defects.
- **AHA (acetohydroxamic acid)** 250 mg three times daily (urease inhibitor), only when surgery is exhausted. Side effects in up to 30% (haemolytic anaemia, DVT (deep vein thrombosis)); avoid if creatinine >2.5 mg/dL or pregnancy.
- **Also know:** hemiacidrin irrigation (historical); matrix stones (radiolucent, Proteus).

**Diagram to draw:** urease cascade; staghorn with PCNL tract.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Urease → ammonia → pH >7.2 → struvite + carbonate apatite.
> - Proteus mirabilis commonest; Ureaplasma easily missed.
> - Complete clearance with PCNL; AHA only when surgery is not possible.

## Role of open and laparoscopic stone surgeries in modern era of endourology.
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2021-Summer Paper 4, Q3f

### Introduction
- SWL (shock wave lithotripsy), URS (ureteroscopy) and PCNL (percutaneous nephrolithotomy) treat almost all stones. Open surgery is now a small minority in centres with full endourology, but remains in resource-limited settings.
- **AUA (American Urological Association)/Endourological Society 2016:** consider laparoscopic, robotic or open surgery **only when SWL, URS and PCNL fail or are expected to fail**; not first line.

### Indications
| Category | Examples |
|---|---|
| Endourology failed or unlikely | Very complex staghorn needing many PCNL sessions; failed multiple procedures |
| Anatomical problem treated together | **PUJ (pelviureteric junction) obstruction + stone:** pyeloplasty with pyelolithotomy; anterior calyceal diverticulum; infundibular stenosis |
| Difficult access | Pelvic or ectopic kidney (laparoscopy-assisted PCNL); morbid obesity; skeletal deformity |
| Poor renal function | **Nephrectomy** for a non-functioning kidney; partial nephrectomy for a non-functioning pole |
| Large impacted ureteric stone | Laparoscopic ureterolithotomy after failed URS/SWL |
| Concomitant surgery | Other open or laparoscopic procedure |
| Bladder | Very large stone; with open prostatectomy, diverticulectomy or augmentation; small bladder; urethral stricture |

### Procedures
- **Pyelolithotomy:** simple, extended (Gil-Vernet, sinus dissection) or coagulum.
- **Anatrophic nephrolithotomy (Smith and Boyce):** for complex staghorn. Clamp renal artery; cool with ice slush; give mannitol; incise along **Brödel's avascular line**; remove stone; repair infundibula; close.
- Radial nephrotomy, ureterolithotomy, cystolithotomy.

### Laparoscopic and robotic surgery
- Transperitoneal or retroperitoneal pyelolithotomy and ureterolithotomy; robotic anatrophic nephrolithotomy reported.
- Best for large pelvic stone in an extrarenal pelvis: high single-stage clearance, less bleeding than PCNL. Drawbacks: longer operation, calyceal stones need a flexible scope.

| Feature | Open | Laparoscopic/robotic |
|---|---|---|
| Morbidity | Large incision, pain, long stay | Less pain, shorter stay |
| Kidney | Ischaemic loss of function (anatrophic) | Similar repair, less wound morbidity |
| Later surgery | Adhesions make redo difficult | Fewer |

### Complications
- Bleeding, urinary leak, infection, loss of renal function, residual fragments.

**Diagram to draw:** Brödel's line incision for anatrophic nephrolithotomy.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Open/laparoscopic only when endourology fails or is unsuitable.
> - Laparoscopic pyeloplasty + pyelolithotomy for PUJ obstruction with stone.
> - Anatrophic nephrolithotomy: Brödel's line, cold ischaemia.

## Diet in stone disease
**Asked as:**
- Diet in stone disease. — *Winter 2023 Paper IV Q3f (SAQ)*
- Diet in stone disease. — *Practice Set 5 Paper IV Q3f (earlier version) (SAQ)*

**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
(also answered in 2023-Winter Paper 4, Q3f)

### Principle
- Diet lowers urinary **supersaturation** by **dilution**, by reducing promoters (calcium, oxalate, uric acid, sodium) and by raising inhibitors (citrate).
- General advice for all; recurrent formers tailored to **24-hour urine**.

### General measures
| Measure | Target | Mechanism and evidence |
|---|---|---|
| **Fluid** | Urine **≥2.5 L/day** (about 3 L intake), spread through the day | Most important single factor. **Borghi 1996 RCT (randomised controlled trial):** recurrence 12.1% vs 27% at 5 years |
| **Normal calcium** | 1000–1200 mg/day, with meals | Binds oxalate in the gut. **Restriction increases stones**. **Borghi 2002:** normal-calcium, low-salt, low-protein diet halved stone events vs low-calcium diet |
| **Low sodium** | Salt about 4–5 g/day | Sodium increases urinary calcium, lowers citrate |
| **Limit animal protein** | 0.8–1.0 g/kg/day | Acid and purine load: hypercalciuria, hypocitraturia, hyperuricosuria |
| **Fruit and vegetables** (DASH [Dietary Approaches to Stop Hypertension] diet) | Daily | Alkali and citrate; DASH linked to fewer stones |
| **Weight** | BMI (body mass index) 18–25 | Obesity: low urine pH, more oxalate and uric acid |

- **Citrus:** lemonade (most evidence) and orange juice raise citrate; orange juice adds alkali. **Volume matters more than drink type.**
- Avoid sugar-sweetened drinks and **high-dose vitamin C** (converted to oxalate).

### Stone-specific diet
| Stone or abnormality | Diet |
|---|---|
| **Hyperoxaluria** | Avoid large portions of **spinach, beets, chocolate, nuts, tea**; calcium with meals |
| **Enteric hyperoxaluria** | Strict low oxalate and **low fat**; **calcium up to 1 g/day with meals**; fluid; magnesium |
| **Uric acid** | Less animal protein (purines give about 30% of urinary uric acid); fruit and vegetables; weight loss |
| **Cystine** | Fluid **3–5 L/day** including night; sodium **≤2300 mg/day**; less animal protein (not in children) |
| **Struvite** | Infection control; diet has little role |

### Adherence
- Poor: only half reach 2.5 L at 6 months; apps and smart bottles help.

**Diagram to draw:** "stone-prevention plate": water, fruit and vegetables, normal dairy, little meat and salt.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Urine ≥2.5 L/day (Borghi 1996).
> - Never restrict calcium (Borghi 2002).
> - Low salt, less animal protein, more fruit and vegetables.
> - Cystine: ≥3 L urine; enteric: low fat, calcium with meals.

## Newer methods of stone analysis and their significance in management of calculus disease
**Asked as:**
- Newer methods of stone analysis and their significance in management of calculus disease. — *Winter 2024 Paper IV Q3a (SAQ)*
- Newer methods of stone analysis and their significance in management of calculus disease. — *Practice Set 6 Paper IV Q3a (earlier version) (SAQ)*

**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)

(also answered in 2024-Winter Paper 4, Q3a)

### Why analyse stones
- AUA (American Urological Association) and EAU (European Association of Urology): analyse a stone at least once in every former; **repeat if stones recur on treatment** (evolving metabolic condition).
- Composition points to the cause: **uric acid** suggests metabolic disease, **cystine** a genetic one, **struvite** infection. Cystine, struvite, 2,8-DHA (2,8-dihydroxyadenine) and xanthine are diagnostic in themselves.

### Why wet chemical analysis is obsolete
- Detects ions, not **crystal phases**, so it cannot separate COM (calcium oxalate monohydrate, whewellite) from COD (dihydrate, weddellite), or apatite from brushite.
- High error rates; misses rare stones (2,8-DHA [2,8-dihydroxyadenine], drugs); needs a large sample.

### Newer methods
| Method | Principle | Comment |
|---|---|---|
| **FTIR (Fourier-transform infrared) spectroscopy** | Each compound absorbs a characteristic infrared spectrum | Commonest reference method; small (tiny) sample; identifies (detects) proteins and drugs |
| **XRD (X-ray diffraction)** | Crystal lattice diffracts X-rays in a unique pattern | Gold standard for crystals; misses amorphous material |
| **Polarising microscopy** | Crystal optics (birefringence) | Cheap; experienced observer (needs experience) |
| **Morpho-constitutional analysis (Daudon)** | Stereomicroscopy of surface and section, plus FTIR | Subtypes point to cause (e.g. type Ia COM low urine volume, Ic primary hyperoxaluria / Ic suggests primary hyperoxaluria) |
| **SEM (scanning electron microscopy) ± EDX (energy-dispersive X-ray)** | Surface structure, elements | Research |
| **Micro-CT (micro-computed tomography)** | 3-D non-destructive structure | Research |
| **Raman spectroscopy** | Scattered light spectrum | Research |

### In vivo prediction
- **DECT (dual-energy computed tomography):** two energies (e.g. 80 and 140 kVp) separate **uric acid from non–uric acid stones** with near-perfect accuracy.
- **Hounsfield units:** >1000 HU predicts SWL (shock wave lithotripsy) failure; SWL works best below about 900–1000 HU; cystine and brushite are dense.
- Endoscopic recognition (lost when stones are dusted). Dusting loses the specimen: collect fragments.

### Significance in management
- **Uric acid:** oral dissolution by urinary alkalinisation (potassium citrate).
- **Struvite:** complete clearance, culture, (culture-directed) antibiotics.
- **Cystine:** hydration, alkalinisation, tiopronin; avoid SWL.
- **COM, brushite, cystine:** hard, SWL-resistant; prefer endoscopy.
- **Brushite:** often converts (can convert) from calcium oxalate after SWL or infection; avoid over-alkalinisation.
- **2,8-DHA:** APRT (adenine phosphoribosyltransferase) deficiency, treat with allopurinol or febuxostat. **Drug stones** (indinavir, triamterene): stop the drug.

**Diagram to draw:** FTIR spectrum outline and COM (dumbbell) versus COD (envelope) crystal shapes.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - FTIR and XRD are reference methods; wet chemistry is obsolete.
> - DECT separates uric acid stones in vivo.
> - Composition guides dissolution, SWL choice and prevention.

## ECIRS (endoscopic combined intrarenal surgery)
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 6 Paper 4, Q3b

### Definition
- **ECIRS:** PCNL (percutaneous nephrolithotomy) and retrograde flexible ureteroscopy performed **at the same time, in one position**, by two surgeons.
- Term and technique popularised by **Scoffone (2008)** in the **GMSV (Galdakao-modified supine Valdivia) position**. Combined access dates back to the 1980s (Lawson's retrograde needle placement, 1983).

### Rationale
- Each route covers the other's blind spots: the retrograde scope guides and watches the puncture, and reaches calyces the rigid nephroscope cannot.
- Result: one tract, better clearance.

### Positioning
| Position | Features |
|---|---|
| **GMSV** (preferred) | Supine; flank on a bolster at the table edge; ipsilateral leg extended, contralateral leg in lithotomy |
| Bart's modified supine | Variant of supine |
| Prone split-leg | Easier posterior calyces; needs a split-leg table |

- **Supine advantages:** airway access, gravity drainage of fragments, no turning, simultaneous retrograde access.

### Technique
1. Cystoscopy; UAS (ureteral access sheath) placed; flexible ureteroscope maps the stones and chooses the target calyx.
2. Ultrasound or fluoroscopic puncture **watched endoscopically** (double bull's-eye technique).
3. Wire grasped and brought out: through-and-through access.
4. Tract dilated under vision (balloon) and sheath placed.
5. Antegrade lithotripsy and suction; the retrograde scope **relocates stones** from inaccessible calyces into the nephroscope's view, or lasers them.
6. Final check with both scopes; stent or tubeless exit.
- Mini-ECIRS uses a mini-PCNL tract.

### Indications
- Staghorn and complex multi-calyceal stones, combined renal and ureteric stones, anomalous kidneys, failed previous treatment, urinary diversion, obese or cardiopulmonary patients (supine).

### Advantages (meta-analysis)
- Single position, **fewer tracts, less bleeding**, fewer ancillary procedures, **higher stone-free rate**; less fluoroscopy; the UAS keeps intrarenal pressure low.

### Disadvantages
- Two surgeons and two sets of equipment; cost; longer set-up; ureteric injury from the sheath; less working space in supine.

**Diagram to draw:** GMSV position with nephroscope from the flank and flexible ureteroscope from below in the same calyx.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Simultaneous PCNL plus flexible ureteroscopy in one position (Scoffone, GMSV).
> - Retrograde scope guides the puncture and relocates stones.
> - Fewer tracts, less bleeding, higher stone-free rate.

## Rapid Revision Sheet – Section 11 · Stones, Obstruction & Trauma

*All the key points from this section in one place. Read this in the last 30 minutes before the exam.*

#### Urolithiasis: etiopathogenesis, medical management of (recurrent) stone disease and recent advances in RIRS instrumentation (LAQ)

- Metastable zone between Ksp and Kf is where inhibitors and therapy act.
- Randall plaque = interstitial apatite anchor; ductal plugs suggest secondary cause.
- Urine ≥2.5 L/day; Borghi 1996: 12% vs 27% recurrence; MET for distal 5–10 mm stones.
- Thiazide plus potassium citrate for hypercalciuria; NOSTONE 2023 negative.
- Uric acid pH 6.0–6.5; cystine pH 7.0–7.5 and <250 mg/L.
- RIRS: single-use digital scopes, thulium laser, suction sheaths, pressure sensing.

#### Energy sources for breaking stones in urology (LAQ)

- SWL: electromagnetic is the modern standard; slow rate (60–90/min) is better.
- Holmium 2120 nm, photothermal, breaks every composition.
- TFL 1940 nm: low peak power, finer dust, less retropulsion.
- EHL is poorly focused, so it is not first-line in the ureter.

#### Tubeless PCNL (SAQ)

- Tubeless = stent only; totally tubeless = neither.
- Benefit is mainly over large (>16 Fr) tubes.
- Keep a tube for bleeding, perforation, infection or second look.

#### Miniperc: miniaturised PCNL, its nomenclature and newer PCNL techniques (SAQ)

- Standard 24–34, mini 15–22 Fr; ultra-mini 11–14 Fr; micro <10 Fr; Jackman 1998: first mini-perc (13 Fr).
- Fewer transfusions and shorter stay, but longer operation (time).
- Working channel decides instruments; suction lowers pressure.
- Watch intrarenal pressure; advantage lost against 24 Fr tracts (equal to 24 Fr).

#### Struvite stones. (SAQ)

- Urease → ammonia → pH >7.2 → struvite + carbonate apatite.
- Proteus mirabilis commonest; Ureaplasma easily missed.
- Complete clearance with PCNL; AHA only when surgery is not possible.

#### Role of open and laparoscopic stone surgeries in modern era of endourology. (SAQ)

- Open/laparoscopic only when endourology fails or is unsuitable.
- Laparoscopic pyeloplasty + pyelolithotomy for PUJ obstruction with stone.
- Anatrophic nephrolithotomy: Brödel's line, cold ischaemia.

#### Diet in stone disease (SAQ)

- Urine ≥2.5 L/day (Borghi 1996).
- Never restrict calcium (Borghi 2002).
- Low salt, less animal protein, more fruit and vegetables.
- Cystine: ≥3 L urine; enteric: low fat, calcium with meals.

#### Newer methods of stone analysis and their significance in management of calculus disease (SAQ)

- FTIR and XRD are reference methods; wet chemistry is obsolete.
- DECT separates uric acid stones in vivo.
- Composition guides dissolution, SWL choice and prevention.

#### ECIRS (endoscopic combined intrarenal surgery) (SAQ)

- Simultaneous PCNL plus flexible ureteroscopy in one position (Scoffone, GMSV).
- Retrograde scope guides the puncture and relocates stones.
- Fewer tracts, less bleeding, higher stone-free rate.

# Section 12 · Bladder Cancer & Diversion — Long answer questions (LAQ)

## Continent urinary diversion: orthotopic neobladder (Studer) versus non-orthotopic diversion
**Asked as:**
- Orthotopic versus Non Orthotopic bladder. — *Summer 2016 Paper IV Q1 (LAQ)*
- Describe principles of continent urinary diversions. Current status of orthotopic neobladder. — *Practice Set 3 Paper IV Q2 (LAQ)*
- Neobladder. — *Summer 2020 Paper IV Q3a (SAQ)*
- Studer Neobladder — *Summer 2015 Paper IV Q3e (SAQ)*
- Studer Neobladder. — *Practice Set 4 Paper IV Q3c (earlier version) (SAQ)*
- Neobladder. — *Practice Set 4 Paper IV Q3d (earlier version) (SAQ)*

**Type:** LAQ (25 marks); also asked as SAQ (10 marks)

(neobladder also answered in 2020-Summer Paper 4, Q3a; Studer also answered in 2015-Summer Paper 4, Q3e; selection and general points: see Practice Set 4 Paper 2, Q3e)
**See Practice Set 4 Paper 2, Q3e (full answer).** Studer technique: see Q3c above.

*Read as: orthotopic neobladder versus non-orthotopic (heterotopic) urinary diversion after radical cystectomy.*

### Introduction, definition and types
- A continent diversion stores urine in a bowel reservoir and empties it **at will**: through the native urethra (**orthotopic neobladder**) or by CIC (clean intermittent catheterisation) of a stoma (**continent cutaneous pouch**).
- **Neobladder definition**: an **orthotopic** reservoir of detubularised bowel (usually ileum) joined to the native urethra after radical cystectomy, allowing **volitional voiding** without a stoma.
- Evolution: ileal conduit (Bricker, 1950) → continent cutaneous (Kock, 1982) → orthotopic (Camey, 1979; Hautmann, 1988; Studer, 1989).
- **Ileal conduit** (Bricker, 1950) remains the commonest diversion worldwide; **ureterosigmoidostomy** is the oldest continent diversion.
- AUA (American Urological Association)/SUO (Society of Urologic Oncology) and EAU (European Association of Urology): counsel every patient on all options. **80–90% of men and ~75% of women** are medically eligible for a neobladder, but far fewer receive one.

| Group | Examples | How urine is held and emptied |
|---|---|---|
| Orthotopic | Studer, Hautmann W, T pouch, Padua, Mainz III | Native rhabdosphincter; voids by relaxing and straining |
| Incontinent cutaneous | Ileal conduit; transverse colon, sigmoid or jejunal conduit | Stoma and appliance |
| Continent cutaneous | Indiana pouch; right colon pouch with appendix (Mitrofanoff) or Yang–Monti channel; Kock (historical) | Self-catheterisation through a valved channel |
| Rectal | Ureterosigmoidostomy, Mainz II (sigma-rectum pouch) | Anal sphincter; urine mixed with stool |

### Basic principles of a continent reservoir
1. **Reliable outlet.** Orthotopic: intact rhabdosphincter (pudendal nerve) and a healthy urethra. Cutaneous: a stable catheterisable efferent channel with a dependable continence mechanism.
2. **Low pressure throughout filling.** Complete **detubularisation** and cross-folding abolish (abolishes) coordinated peristaltic contractions. Folding into a **sphere** gives the greatest (largest) volume for its (a given) surface area (Laplace: wall tension = pressure × radius). Result: a low-pressure reservoir.
3. **Adequate volume: ≥500 mL when mature.** Bowel stretches with time (ileum more than colon); 44 cm of double-folded ileum holds <200 mL at first (initially) and 500–600 mL within a year (by 1 year).
4. **Unobstructed ureterointestinal anastomosis** to protect the kidneys.
5. **Enough renal function** to clear the acid load reabsorbed by bowel mucosa.
- Continence is a **pressure balance**: low reservoir pressure against adequate outlet resistance.
- **Studer**: **orthotopic low-pressure ileal reservoir** (**Studer, 1989**): detubularised ileal **orthotopic** reservoir with a long **isoperistaltic afferent limb**, joined to the urethra. **Detubularisation** stops coordinated contractions, so pressure stays low; a **sphere** (Laplace) gives most volume for surface area. The **isoperistaltic afferent limb** limits reflux (damps reflux during Valsalva voiding) and accepts **short ureters**. No staples in the reservoir. Continence depends on the **rhabdosphincter**.

### Choice of bowel segment
| Segment | Points |
|---|---|
| **Ileum** (default) | Most compliant, lowest pressure; mesentery reaches the urethra; mucosa atrophies, so absorption falls |
| Ileum + caecum (Mainz) | Near-ileal compliance; loses ileocaecal valve |
| Colon (right, sigmoid) | Less distensible, pressure waves → more night leakage; when ileum is short or diseased |
| Stomach | Secretes acid: helps in renal impairment; haematuria–dysuria; rarely used |

### Reflux versus obstruction
- Voiding by Valsalva raises pressure equally throughout, so reflux into a low-pressure pouch is benign, while **every antireflux valve can obstruct** (Le Duc tunnel stricture 7–29%; late Kock nipple stenosis).
- Direct refluxing anastomosis (Wallace, Leadbetter): stricture about 3–6%. **USC (University of Southern California) RCT (randomised controlled trial)**, 484 patients: Studer vs T pouch, no difference in renal function or infection at 3 years; T pouch needed more secondary procedures (a USC randomised trial found the antireflux T pouch no better: gave no renal benefit and more reoperations).
- Studer: reflux on cystogram, yet kidneys preserved in about (~) 95%.

### Continent cutaneous mechanisms
| Mechanism | Example |
|---|---|
| Flap valve: appendix tunnelled in taenia | **Mitrofanoff** (Penn, right colon pouch); Yang–Monti ileal tube if no appendix |
| Imbricated ileocaecal valve + tapered ileum | **Indiana** pouch |
| Intussuscepted nipple | Kock (historical) |
- Used when the urethra is unusable (continence wanted but urethra unusable); umbilical stoma preferred.

### Patient selection
| | Orthotopic only | All continent diversions |
|---|---|---|
| Absolute contraindications | Tumour at the urethral margin (frozen section); significant urethral stricture | Severe renal (eGFR <40 mL/min) or hepatic dysfunction (liver failure); physical or mental inability to self-catheterise |
| Relative | Poor external sphincter; bladder-neck or anterior vaginal wall tumour (women); prostatic stromal invasion (men); irradiated sphincter (irradiated pelvis) | Frailty, poor cognition, social problems, prior radiation or bowel resection |

- **Renal function**: creatinine <1.7–2.2 mg/dL or eGFR (estimated glomerular filtration rate) **>35–40 mL/min** (below about 35–40 mL/min = severe renal impairment; another source: <40 mL/min), because the kidney must excrete acid reabsorbed from stored urine. Formal tests: urine pH ≤5.8 after ammonium chloride, osmolality ≥600 mOsm/kg after water deprivation.
- **Urethral recurrence**: in men the strongest predictor is prostatic stromal invasion (prostatic urethral tumour raises urethral recurrence risk); in women, bladder-neck or anterior vaginal wall tumour. **Intraoperative frozen section** of the urethral margin decides. CIS (carcinoma in situ) or multifocality alone do not exclude a neobladder.
- **Age and obesity are not contraindications** (age and obesity alone are not bars; age alone is not a bar); comorbidity, cognition and support decide. A conduit suits the frail or unsupported patient.
- Continent cutaneous pouch: continence wanted but urethra unusable.
- Mark a conduit stoma site in everyone: up to ~10% change plan intraoperatively.

### Comparison
| Feature | Orthotopic neobladder | Ileal conduit | Continent cutaneous | Ureterosigmoidostomy |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Appliance | None | Bag | None; small stoma | None |
| Body image | Best | Worst | Good | Good |
| Complexity | High | Lowest | Highest | Moderate |
| Continence | Day 80–90%; night incontinence 20–50% | Not applicable | High (Indiana, Mitrofanoff) | Depends on anal sphincter |
| Catheterisation | 10–20% of men, ~30% of women | None | Always | None |
| Bowel used | Long ileal segment (Studer 54–56 cm) | 10–15 cm ileum | Right colon + terminal ileum | None isolated |
| Typical late problems | Night leakage, retention, acidosis | Stomal stenosis (20–24%), parastomal hernia, renal deterioration | Stomal stenosis, stones, difficult catheterisation | Severe acidosis, pyelonephritis, cancer at the anastomosis |

### Neobladder types
| Design | Segment | Ureters |
|---|---|---|
| **Studer** | 40–44 cm ileum + afferent limb | Refluxing, isoperistaltic limb |
| **Hautmann W** | About 70 cm ileum | Refluxing; largest capacity |
| T pouch | 44 cm + T limb | Antireflux flap valve |
| Camey II; Padua | Ileum | Antireflux (Le Duc) |
| Mainz III; sigmoid | Ileocaecal; colon | Tunnelled |

| | Studer | Hautmann |
|---|---|---|
| Ileum | 54–56 cm, U + afferent limb | ~70 cm folded in a W |
| Initial capacity | Smaller (smaller early capacity) | Larger: earlier continence |
| Late | — (less late retention) | More retention, reabsorption |

### Studer technique (operative; brief)
- Brief: isolate ileum 15–25 cm from the ileocaecal valve; open the distal part on the antimesenteric border, sew into a U, fold into a sphere; end-to-side ureteric anastomoses to the afferent limb; anastomose the dependent point to the urethra.
1. **Isolate 54–56 cm of terminal ileum**, ending **15–25 cm** above the ICV (ileocaecal valve) (another source: **15–20 cm proximal to the ICV**); restore bowel continuity.
2. **Reservoir: distal 40–44 cm** (two 20–22-cm limbs) opened on the antimesenteric border; sew the two limbs (medial edges) into a **U** (back wall).
3. **Afferent limb:** leave the proximal **10–15 cm** tubular as the afferent limb (another source: **about 15 cm**; original 20 cm; 10–12 cm in later figures); close its end.
4. **Fold the U in the opposite direction** (across) and close the anterior wall into a **sphere**; join its most dependent point to the urethra.
5. **Ureters:** spatulated **end-to-side, refluxing** ureteroileal anastomoses to the afferent limb, interrupted 4-0, over stents (**stented**).
6. **Urethral anastomosis** at its most dependent point with pre-placed sutures; catheter and suprapubic tube.
- Capacity rises from <200 to **500–600 mL** in a year.
- **Intracorporeal robotic Studer** follows the same steps.

### Postoperative care, voiding training and follow-up
- Mucus irrigation; oral bicarbonate; cystogram before catheter removal.
- **Voiding training**: void **sitting** (sit), relax the pelvic floor, strain gently; **timed voiding** every 2 hours, extended to 3–4 hours as capacity grows (under 200 mL to 500–600 mL in a year). **Night alarm.**
- Annual metabolic panel, B12, upper-tract imaging; urethral wash cytology if CIS (carcinoma in situ).

### Current status of orthotopic neobladder
**1. Preferred where suitable:** closest to a native bladder. AUA/SUO (American Urological Association/Society of Urologic Oncology) and EAU (European Association of Urology): counsel every cystectomy patient on all options and refer to an experienced surgeon.

**2. Under-used.** 80–90% of men and about 75% of women are eligible, yet only about 9% of US patients receive any continent diversion, and this is falling; continent diversion in RAZOR 21%, iROC 11%. Causes: comorbid patients, surgeon training, intracorporeal robotics.

**3. Designs in use.** **Studer** (40–44 cm U plus isoperistaltic afferent limb) and **Hautmann W** (about 70 cm; refluxing since 1997) dominate; T pouch or serous-lined tunnel when antireflux is wanted.

**4. Oncological safety.**
- Men: urethral recurrence about 7% at 5 years; main risk is prostatic stromal involvement. The neobladder seems protective (meta-analysis HR (hazard ratio) 0.44).
- Women: risk lies with bladder-neck tumour; no skip lesions, so preserve the urethra. **Frozen section** of the urethral margin decides in both sexes.
- Local recurrence <10%, rarely disabling the pouch; nodal disease is not a bar. Selected **post-radiation** patients do well.

**5. Function (outcomes)**
| Outcome | Result |
|---|---|
| Day continence | 80–90% (most by 3–6 months); another source (Studer): 90–98%; about 90% |
| Night incontinence | 20–50% (night continence lower: up to half leak): no sphincter reflex, mucosal water secretion, nocturnal diuresis |
| Retention needing CIC | Men 4–10%; **women 20–30%** (pouch angulation): men leak, women retain |
| Self-catheterisation | **10–20% of men, ~30% of women** |

**6. Complications.** Early morbidity equals conduit. Late: hyperchloraemic metabolic acidosis, vitamin B12 deficiency, mucus, ureteroileal stricture, stones, pouch rupture, pouch–vaginal fistula (5–10%), hernia.

**7. Robotic intracorporeal neobladder:** comparable to open; long-term data lacking.

**8. Quality of life:** no diversion proven superior; body image favours neobladder.

- **Also know:** nerve sparing and uterine preservation (nerve and uterine sparing) aid continence; intracorporeal robotic Studer; lifelong follow-up (metabolic panel, B12, upper tracts, urethral wash cytology if CIS (carcinoma in situ)).

### Metabolic complications (bowel in the urinary tract)
| Segment | Disturbance |
|---|---|
| Ileum, colon | **Hyperchloraemic metabolic acidosis** (ammonium chloride absorbed); hypokalaemia, worse with colon |
| Jejunum | Hyponatraemic, hypochloraemic, hyperkalaemic acidosis |
| Stomach | Hypochloraemic, hypokalaemic alkalosis |

- Acidosis is severe in most ureterosigmoidostomies; most colonic (Mainz) pouches need alkali. Treat with sodium bicarbonate or citrate, and replace potassium as well.
- **Also know:** osteomalacia; vitamin B12 deficiency and bile-salt diarrhoea after ileal loss (**B12 and bile-salt loss**; liver B12 stores last 3–5 years); stones (ileal conduit 10–12%, continent caecal reservoirs ~20%); bacteriuria; drug reabsorption (methotrexate, phenytoin); ammoniagenic encephalopathy; growth delay in children.
- **Secondary cancer**: ureterosigmoidostomy 6–29% after 10–20 years (yearly endoscopy from 5 years); ileal conduit and ileal neobladder lowest.

### Surgical complications
- **Early (≤90 days)**: ileus, urine leak (long pouch suture lines; drain percutaneously), infection, mucus retention; not more than with a conduit.
- **Late**: ureteroileal stricture ~3–6% (Wallace or direct end-to-side) (Studer/neobladder sources: **<3%**); neobladder retention, urethral stricture, rare pouch rupture; ventral and parastomal hernia; **hyperchloraemic metabolic acidosis**, **vitamin B12** deficiency, stones, pyelonephritis (commonest late, Studer), urethral recurrence.

### Quality of life
- No randomised trial. Body image favours the neobladder, urinary function the conduit; no diversion is superior on HRQoL (health-related quality of life).

### Recent advances
- RARC (robot-assisted radical cystectomy): **RAZOR (2018)** non-inferior 2-year progression-free survival (open diversion in both arms); **iROC**: intracorporeal diversion gave more days alive and out of hospital at 90 days.
- Intracorporeal neobladders and nerve- or organ-sparing cystectomy are increasing.

**Diagram to draw:** Studer neobladder and ileal conduit side by side; Studer U-pouch with afferent limb, ureters and urethral anastomosis; ileal segment marked (afferent limb plus 40–44-cm U), then the folded spherical pouch with afferent limb.

> **Key points to remember (incl. SAQ angle):**
> - Three principles: reliable outlet, low pressure (detubularise and make a sphere), ≥500 mL; 500–600 mL by 1 year.
> - Detubularised ileal sphere: low pressure, large capacity; continence depends on the rhabdosphincter.
> - Neobladder needs eGFR (estimated glomerular filtration rate) >35–40 mL/min, a tumour-free urethral margin and ability to self-catheterise. Absolute bar: positive urethral margin, stricture, eGFR <40.
> - 54–56 cm ileum, 15–20 cm from ICV: 40–44-cm U (sphere) plus 10–15 cm afferent limb.
> - Refluxing end-to-side ureters; stricture <3%; kidneys preserved ~95%; refluxing ureteric anastomosis is acceptable (USC (University of Southern California) trial).
> - Obstruction, not reflux, destroys kidneys; USC RCT: Studer = T pouch.
> - Day continence 80–90% (about 90%); night incontinence 20–50% (up to half); women retain (20–30%).
> - Sitting Valsalva voiding, timed voiding, night alarm.
> - Eligible 80–90% men, 75% women; actual uptake about 9% in the US.
> - Ileum/colon: hyperchloraemic acidosis; jejunum: hyperkalaemic; stomach: alkalosis.
> - No diversion is proven best for quality of life; counsel all options.

## Intravesical therapy for urothelial (transitional cell) carcinoma of bladder, including combination intravesical therapy
**Asked as:**
- Describe in brief intravesical therapy for transitional cell carcinoma of Urinary bladder. — *Summer 2017 Paper IV Q1 (LAQ)*
- Combination Intravesical therapy — *Winter 2025 Paper IV Q3c (SAQ)*
- Combination Intravesical therapy — *Practice Set 6 Paper IV Q3c (earlier version) (SAQ)*

**Type:** LAQ (25 marks); also asked as SAQ (10 marks)  
(combination therapy also answered in 2025-Winter Paper 4, Q3c)

### Introduction
- NMIBC (non-muscle-invasive bladder cancer: Ta, T1, CIS [carcinoma in situ]) is 70–80% of bladder cancers. "Urothelial carcinoma" now replaces "transitional cell carcinoma".
- Two problems: **recurrence** (40–60%) and **progression** (about 6% in low-grade Ta to 17% in high-grade T1). Intravesical therapy follows complete TURBT (transurethral resection of bladder tumour).
- **Rule:** chemotherapy reduces recurrence only. BCG (bacille Calmette-Guérin) is the only agent that also reduces progression.

### Risk-adapted use (AUA [American Urological Association]/SUO)
| Risk | Criteria | Therapy |
|---|---|---|
| Low | LG (low-grade) solitary Ta ≤3 cm; PUNLMP | Single post-op instillation only |
| Intermediate | LG Ta recurrent, >3 cm or multifocal; LG T1; HG (high-grade) Ta ≤3 cm | 6-week chemotherapy or BCG; maintenance 1 year |
| High | HG T1; HG Ta large, multifocal or recurrent; any CIS; LVI (lymphovascular invasion); variant histology | BCG induction + maintenance 3 years |

- EAU (European Association of Urology) adds a "very high" group; EORTC (European Organisation for Research and Treatment of Cancer) and CUETO risk tables.

### 1. Single immediate instillation
- **Rationale:** kills floating cells before they implant. So it must be early: **within 6 hours** (no benefit at 24 hours).
- Agents: MMC (mitomycin C) 40 mg, epirubicin 50 mg, **gemcitabine 2 g** (standard in USA). **SWOG S0337:** 4-year recurrence 35% vs 47% with saline.
- **Withhold** after perforation or extensive resection. **Never give BCG** now (intravasation, sepsis).

### 2. Adjuvant chemotherapy
- **MMC** (alkylating), 40 mg in 20 mL weekly ×6. Optimise it (Au, 2001): empty bladder, fluid restriction, sodium bicarbonate, concentrated dose. Skin rash.
- **Gemcitabine** (nucleoside analogue): minimal absorption, well tolerated. **Anthracyclines** (epirubicin, doxorubicin) cause chemical cystitis.
- Intermediate risk: 6-week induction, then monthly maintenance for 1 year.
- **Gem/Doce:** gemcitabine 1 g, drain, then docetaxel 37.5 mg; weekly ×6, then monthly for 2 years. Used in BCG-unresponsive disease and during BCG shortage.

### 3. BCG immunotherapy
- **Mechanism:** BCG binds **fibronectin**. Cytokines follow (IFN-γ [interferon-gamma], IL [interleukin]-2, IL-12, TNF-α [tumour necrosis factor-alpha]). A **Th1 (T-helper 1)** response gives cell-mediated tumour kill; TRAIL release is important.
- **Technique:** 1 vial in 50 mL saline. Start **2–4 weeks** after TURBT (re-epithelialisation). Urinalysis first; retain 1–2 hours.
- **SWOG schedule (Lamm, 2000):** 6 weekly induction doses, then 3 weekly doses at 3 and 6 months, then every 6 months to 3 years. Median recurrence-free survival 76.8 vs 35.7 months.
- **EORTC 30962:** full dose; 1 year for intermediate, 3 years for high risk. **NIMBUS:** reduced frequency was inferior.
- CIS complete response up to 84%; BCG beats MMC only with maintenance.
- **Contraindications:** immediately after TURBT, gross haematuria, traumatic catheterisation (all risk intravasation); previous BCG sepsis; total incontinence; immunosuppression (now relative). Relative: active UTI (urinary tract infection), liver disease, previous TB (tuberculosis).

### BCG toxicity
| Grade | Features | Management |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | Cystitis, fever <38.5 °C, <48 h | Anticholinergics, analgesics, NSAIDs (non-steroidal anti-inflammatory drugs) |
| 2 | Severe or >48 h | Culture; isoniazid + rifampicin; resume at ½–⅓ dose |
| 3 | Sepsis, organ involvement | Stop BCG; isoniazid + rifampicin + ethambutol; steroids only with cover |

- BCG is resistant to **pyrazinamide**. BCG sepsis 0.5%.

### BCG failure
- **Adequate BCG:** ≥5 of 6 induction + ≥2 of 3 maintenance doses.
- **BCG-unresponsive:** persistent or recurrent CIS or HG Ta/T1 despite adequate BCG, or HG relapse within 6 months (Ta/T1) or 12 months (CIS) of the last dose.
- **Also know:** refractory, intolerant, exposed, delayed relapse.

### BCG-unresponsive options
- **Timely radical cystectomy** is the standard (EAU, AUA).
- If unfit or refusing:
  - **Pembrolizumab** (KEYNOTE-057): IV every 3 weeks; CIS complete response 41%.
  - **Nadofaragene firadenovec:** adenovirus with interferon-α2b gene, every 3 months; CIS complete response 53%.
  - **N-803:** IL-15 superagonist with BCG; CIS complete response 71%.
  - Gem/Doce; valrubicin; **TAR-200** (gemcitabine-releasing device); cretostimogene (trials).

### Device-assisted therapy
- **EMDA (electromotive drug administration):** current increases MMC tissue delivery 4–7-fold. Sequential BCG + EMDA-MMC improved outcomes in T1.
- **Chemohyperthermia:** RITE (radiofrequency, 42 °C); HIVEC (heated recirculating MMC).
- **Also know:** UGN-102 MMC gel; interferon with low-dose BCG.

### Combination intravesical therapy

#### Rationale
- Single agents fail: chemotherapy reduces **recurrence** only; BCG (bacille Calmette-Guérin) reduces **progression** but fails in about a third.
- Combinations add **different mechanisms**, overcome resistance, and help (are useful) during **BCG shortage** and in **BCG-unresponsive** NMIBC (non-muscle-invasive bladder cancer).

#### 1. Sequential chemotherapy (doublets)
| Regimen | Method | Results |
|---|---|---|
| **Gem/Doce** (gemcitabine–docetaxel) | Gemcitabine 1 g in 50 mL (60–90 min) → drain → docetaxel 37.5 mg (60–120 min); weekly ×6, then monthly for 2 years | High-grade recurrence-free survival about 65% (at 1 year), 52% (at 2 years); CIS (carcinoma in situ) complete response 60% at 1 year; **84% avoid cystectomy** |
| Gem/MMC (mitomycin C) | Gemcitabine 1 g → MMC 40 mg | Durable recurrence-free about 38% |

- **Why it works:** gemcitabine (antimetabolite) and docetaxel (microtubule stabiliser) act on different cell-cycle phases.
- Oral sodium bicarbonate and antiemetics aid tolerance. No level 1 evidence; Phase III BRIDGE (versus BCG in BCG-naive) is testing it (compares it with BCG).

#### 2. Chemotherapy with BCG
- Sequential MMC–BCG: **no benefit** over BCG alone.
- **Exception: BCG alternating with EMDA (electromotive drug administration)-MMC** in T1: lower recurrence and progression than BCG alone. A later trial was non-significant.
- Chemohyperthermia (RITE, radiofrequency-induced thermochemotherapeutic effect) potentiates BCG.

#### 3. BCG with immunomodulators
- **BCG + interferon-α (α2b):** no gain over BCG; allows reduced BCG dose (BCG dose reduction to one-third).
- **N-803 (nogapendekin alfa)** = (an) IL-15 (interleukin-15) superagonist activating CD8 T and NK (natural killer) cells. With BCG in BCG-unresponsive CIS (carcinoma in situ): complete response 71%, 45% at 12 months. FDA (Food and Drug Administration)-approved (in 2024; chapter: "under review").

#### 4. BCG with checkpoint inhibitors (BCG-naive high risk)
- **CREST** (subcutaneous sasanlimab + BCG) and **POTOMAC** (durvalumab + BCG): improved event-free/disease-free (or) survival over BCG alone.
- **ALBAN** (atezolizumab + BCG): negative. KEYNOTE-676 (pembrolizumab) ongoing.

#### 5. Device-assisted and novel combinations
- **Chemohyperthermia** (MMC at 42 °C: RITE, radiofrequency-induced thermochemotherapeutic effect; HIVEC, hyperthermic intravesical chemotherapy) increases uptake.
- **TAR-200:** gemcitabine-releasing intravesical device (± cetrelimab); high (very high early) complete response in BCG-unresponsive CIS (SunRISe-1).
- **Cretostimogene** (oncolytic adenovirus) + pembrolizumab: about two-thirds complete response at 12 months.

#### Toxicity of combinations
- Additive cystitis; immune-related events with checkpoint inhibitors.

**Diagram to draw:** BCG mechanism (fibronectin → cytokines → Th1 → kill) with SWOG schedule timeline; Gem/Doce sequence timeline (instil, dwell, drain, instil).

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Single instillation within 6 h; never BCG immediately (SWOG S0337 gemcitabine).
> - Only maintenance BCG reduces progression; SWOG 3-year schedule.
> - BCG-unresponsive: cystectomy; pembrolizumab, nadofaragene, N-803.
> - Gem/Doce: gemcitabine 1 g then docetaxel 37.5 mg; 84% cystectomy-free.
> - Only EMDA-MMC improves BCG sequentially.
> - N-803 + BCG: CIS complete response 71%.
> - BCG + checkpoint: CREST, POTOMAC positive; ALBAN negative.

## Discuss bladder preservation surgery for TCC of urinary bladder.
**Type:** LAQ (25 marks)  
**Source:** 2019-Summer Paper 4, Q2

*Read as: bladder-preserving treatment of muscle-invasive TCC (transitional cell carcinoma, urothelial carcinoma), including radical TURBT (transurethral resection of bladder tumour), partial cystectomy and trimodality therapy.*

### Introduction
- Standard for MIBC (muscle-invasive bladder cancer): cisplatin NAC (neoadjuvant chemotherapy) + RC (radical cystectomy) with PLND (pelvic lymph node dissection).
- RC is morbid and patients are often elderly. Preservation suits **(1) unfit patients** and **(2) fit, motivated patients with limited disease**.
- **Why single modalities fail:** they leave either the remaining urothelium or occult nodes untreated (cT2 understaged to pT3 in up to about 40%; occult nodes about 25%).

### Options
| Option | Status |
|---|---|
| Radical TURBT alone | Not a primary treatment |
| Partial cystectomy + PLND | Highly selected solitary tumours; urachal cancer |
| RT (radiotherapy) alone | Inferior; quasi-palliative |
| Chemotherapy alone | Not recommended |
| **TMT (trimodality therapy)** | **Best-studied, guideline-accepted** |

### A. Radical TURBT
- Rationale: about 10% are pT0 at cystectomy. Only for tumour limited to superficial muscle, with negative re-resection.
- About a third progress to MIBC on long follow-up; selected series report 5-year CSS (cancer-specific survival) about 82%.

### B. Partial cystectomy
**Ideal candidate:**
- Small (**<3 cm**), **solitary** tumour in a mobile part of bladder (dome).
- **No CIS (carcinoma in situ)**, no hydronephrosis.
- 1–2 cm margin achievable away from the ureteric orifice and trigone; adequate residual capacity.
- **Treatment of choice for urachal adenocarcinoma** (en bloc dome, urachus and umbilicus).

**Contraindications:** CIS, multifocal tumour, trigone/bladder neck tumour, small bladder, prostatic urethral involvement.

**Technique:** NAC if cisplatin-eligible → PLND → cystotomy away from tumour → full-thickness excision with perivesical fat, **frozen-section margins** → reimplant ureter if needed → watertight closure → irrigation (prevents seeding) → drain; cystogram before catheter removal.

**Outcomes:** 5-year OS (overall survival) 67–70%, intact bladder 65–74%; salvage RC up to a quarter; late recurrence → lifelong follow-up.

### C. Trimodality therapy
**Components:** (1) **maximal, visibly complete TURBT**; (2) **RT** to bladder ± pelvic nodes; (3) concurrent **radiosensitising chemotherapy**.

**Selection criteria:**
- cT2 (selected cT3–4a), small solitary tumour.
- **No hydronephrosis, no extensive CIS**, visibly complete TURBT.
- Good bladder function; motivated, compliant patient.
- Only 6–19% of MIBC meet strict criteria.

**Radiosensitising regimens:**
| Regimen | Evidence |
|---|---|
| **Mitomycin C + 5-FU (fluorouracil)** | **BC2001**: 2-year locoregional DFS (disease-free survival) **67% vs 48%** vs RT alone, no excess late toxicity |
| Cisplatin (± 5-FU or paclitaxel) | MGH (Massachusetts General Hospital)/RTOG (Radiation Therapy Oncology Group) protocols |
| Gemcitabine | Cisplatin-ineligible |
| Carbogen + nicotinamide (BCON) | Hypoxia modification |

**Radiation schedules:**
```
Split course (MGH/RTOG):  TURBT → chemoRT ~40 Gy → cystoscopy + biopsy
                           CR → consolidation to 60–66 Gy
                           Residual tumour → immediate cystectomy
Continuous (current):      TURBT → chemoRT 64 Gy/32 fr or 55 Gy/20 fr
                           → cystoscopy and imaging at 3–4 months
```

**Response assessment:** cystoscopy with biopsy of tumour site, cytology, CT (computed tomography)/MRI (magnetic resonance imaging). **CR (complete response)** → surveillance. Non-response → salvage RC.

**Salvage cystectomy:**
- Needed in **10–30%** for persistent or recurrent muscle-invasive disease; it is the designed safety net.
- NMIBC (non-muscle-invasive) recurrence → TURBT + intravesical BCG (bacillus Calmette–Guérin).

**Outcomes:**
- 5-year CSS 50–84%, OS 36–74%.
- MGH/RTOG: CR 69%; 5-year DSS (disease-specific survival) 71%; 80% of survivors keep the bladder.
- Propensity-matched RC vs TMT: equal 5-year metastasis-free survival (about 75%) and CSS (81% vs 84%).
- Low late toxicity; good quality of life.

### Comparison with radical cystectomy
| | Radical cystectomy | TMT |
|---|---|---|
| Staging | Pathological | Clinical only |
| Nodes | Removed | Irradiated or not treated |
| Function | Diversion; sexual dysfunction | Native bladder; bowel/bladder toxicity |
| Follow-up | Upper tract, urethra | **Intensive cystoscopy for 10 years** |
| Evidence | Standard | No completed RCT (SPARE closed for poor accrual) |

### Follow-up after preservation
- Cystoscopy every 3–4 months for 2 years, 6-monthly years 3–4, then yearly to 10 years; CT urogram and chest CT every 3–6 months for 2 years, then yearly to 5 years; cytology.

### Recent advances
- **Immunotherapy + chemoradiation:** PD-1/PD-L1 (programmed death-1 / ligand-1) inhibitors with TMT under trial (e.g. SWOG/NRG S1806 with atezolizumab; KEYNOTE-992 with pembrolizumab); results awaited.
- Hypofractionation 55 Gy/20 non-inferior (BC2001–BCON meta-analysis: same toxicity).
- Bladder sparing after complete response to NAC (± checkpoint inhibitor) with biomarker selection (DNA repair gene mutations, circulating tumour DNA); RETAIN: about half recurred.
- **Also know:** robotic partial cystectomy; molecular subtypes.

**Diagram to draw:** TMT flowchart from TURBT to chemoradiation, response check, surveillance or salvage cystectomy.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - TMT = complete TURBT + RT + radiosensitiser; select T2, no hydronephrosis, no CIS.
> - BC2001: MMC/5-FU + RT, 2-year locoregional DFS 67% vs 48%.
