> - Salvage cystectomy 10–30%; follow up the bladder for 10 years.
> - Partial cystectomy: solitary <3 cm, no CIS, frozen-section margins, always PLND.

## Role of neoadjuvant therapy and Lymphadenectomy for muscle invasive bladder cancer and upper tract urothelial cell cancer?
**Type:** LAQ (25 marks)  
**Source:** 2025-Winter Paper 4, Q1

### Introduction
- After RC (radical cystectomy) alone, nearly half of MIBC (muscle-invasive bladder cancer) patients develop metastases from occult micrometastases.
- Cure improves with **systemic therapy before surgery** and a **meticulous lymph node dissection** (accurate staging, possible therapeutic value).
- UTUC (upper tract urothelial carcinoma) behaves alike, but RNU (radical nephroureterectomy) removes a kidney, changing chemotherapy timing.

### A. Neoadjuvant therapy in MIBC
**Rationale**
- Treats micrometastases early; better tolerated before surgery, so more complete it.
- Downstages; **pT0 at cystectomy** gives about 90% 5-year recurrence-free survival.
- Drawback: delays surgery in non-responders.

**Selection:** cT2–T4a N0 M0, cisplatin-eligible. **Cisplatin-ineligible** if any one: GFR (glomerular filtration rate) <60 mL/min, ECOG (Eastern Cooperative Oncology Group) performance status ≥2, hearing loss grade ≥2, neuropathy grade ≥2, NYHA (New York Heart Association) class III–IV failure.

| Trial | Regimen | Result |
|---|---|---|
| **SWOG 8710** (Southwest Oncology Group) | MVAC (methotrexate, vinblastine, doxorubicin, cisplatin) ×3 then RC vs RC | pT0 38% vs 15%; 5-year OS (overall survival) 57% vs 43%, **P = 0.06** |
| **BA06 30894** (EORTC/MRC, European Organisation for Research and Treatment of Cancer/Medical Research Council) | CMV (cisplatin, methotrexate, vinblastine) ×3 | 16% relative reduction in mortality; 10-year OS 36% vs 30% |
| ABC (Advanced Bladder Cancer) meta-analysis | Cisplatin combinations | OS HR (hazard ratio) 0.86; **5% absolute benefit** at 5 years |
| **VESPER** | ddMVAC (dose-dense MVAC) ×6 vs GC (gemcitabine–cisplatin) ×4 | Organ-confined response 77% vs 63%; 3-year PFS (progression-free survival) 66% vs 56%; more toxicity |
| **NIAGARA** | Durvalumab + GC, then adjuvant durvalumab, vs GC | Better event-free survival (HR 0.68) and OS (HR 0.75) |

- Single-agent cisplatin, carboplatin and neoadjuvant radiotherapy give **no survival benefit**.
- **Guidelines (EAU, European Association of Urology; AUA, American Urological Association; NCCN, National Comprehensive Cancer Network):** cisplatin-based NAC (neoadjuvant chemotherapy) for all eligible patients; ddMVAC 3–6 cycles or GC 4 cycles. NIAGARA has since added perioperative durvalumab + GC (the chapter predates it and calls immunotherapy unproven).
- **Cisplatin-ineligible:** proceed to RC; neoadjuvant pembrolizumab (PURE-01) and atezolizumab (ABACUS) are investigational.
- **Adjuvant** (pT3–4 or N+): cisplatin chemotherapy if not given before; **nivolumab** (CheckMate 274: disease-free survival 20.8 vs 10.8 months).

### B. Lymphadenectomy in MIBC
- **Primary drainage:** obturator, internal and external iliac, presacral. **Secondary:** common iliac, para-aortic, paracaval.
- Node positivity: pT2 8–26%, pT3 33–50%, pT4 36–80%.

| Template | Extent |
|---|---|
| **Standard** | Bilateral external and internal iliac and obturator nodes; genitofemoral nerve laterally, node of Cloquet caudally, up to the common iliac bifurcation |
| **Extended** | Adds common iliac and presacral nodes, up to the aortic bifurcation |
| Super-extended | Up to the inferior mesenteric artery |

- **Evidence:** German phase III (limited vs extended): no survival benefit. **SWOG S1011** (most had NAC): median nodes 24 vs 39, **no difference** in disease-free survival (HR 1.10) or OS; more grade ≥3 events. So **standard bilateral PLND (pelvic lymph node dissection)** is the standard of care.
- **Node count:** fewer than 10 nodes doubled the risk of death (SWOG 8710 reanalysis). **Also know:** lymph node density, extranodal extension (prognostic).
- Unexpected nodes at RC: frozen section, proceed if resectable within the pelvis. Clinically node-positive: chemotherapy first, then RC in responders.

### C. Neoadjuvant and adjuvant therapy in UTUC
- **Why neoadjuvant:** CKD (chronic kidney disease) is common and GFR falls after RNU, losing cisplatin eligibility. Give platinum **while both kidneys work**.
- Biopsy under-stages, so select by high-risk features (high grade, invasion on imaging, hydronephrosis).
- **EA8141** (phase II): accelerated MVAC, or gemcitabine–carboplatin if CrCl (creatinine clearance) 30–49; complete response 14%, below ypT2N0 60%.
- **AUA/SUO (Society of Urologic Oncology):** neoadjuvant platinum is a standard option for high-risk UTUC, especially if postoperative CrCl will fall below 50.
- **Adjuvant: POUT** (phase III, pT2–T4 or N+ after RNU): gemcitabine–platinum vs surveillance; disease-free survival HR 0.45, 3-year 71% vs 46%. Weaker with carboplatin, favouring neoadjuvant cisplatin. Adjuvant nivolumab (CheckMate 274) also included UTUC.

### D. Lymphadenectomy in UTUC
- Node positivity 12–25%. Lymph drains **right to left and top to bottom**, so the template depends on side and level.

| Primary site | Right | Left |
|---|---|---|
| Renal pelvis, upper two-thirds ureter | Hilar, paracaval, retrocaval, **interaortocaval** | Hilar, para-aortic |
| Lower third ureter | Ipsilateral common, external, internal iliac, obturator, presacral | Same |

- Value: pN0 better than pNx; ≥8 nodes improved disease-specific survival above pT1. Indicated for high-grade or invasive disease; robotic yield equals open. Risk: chylous leak.

**Diagram to draw:** standard vs extended pelvic templates, and right vs left UTUC templates.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Cisplatin combinations only: ABC 5% absolute 5-year survival gain; SWOG 8710 P = 0.06.
> - VESPER: ddMVAC beat GC; NIAGARA: perioperative durvalumab improved event-free and overall survival.
> - SWOG S1011: extended PLND gives no survival benefit over standard.
> - UTUC: neoadjuvant before kidney loss; POUT disease-free survival HR 0.45; right-sided needs interaortocaval nodes.

# Section 12 · Bladder Cancer & Diversion — Short answer questions (SAQ)

## Optical coherence tomography in carcinoma bladder
**Asked as:**
- Optical coherence tomography in carcinoma bladder. — *Summer 2017 Paper IV Q3c (SAQ)*
- Optical coherence tomography in carcinoma bladder. — *Practice Set 5 Paper IV Q3c (earlier version) (SAQ)*

**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)

### Introduction
- **WLC (white-light cystoscopy)** is the gold standard but subjective: many biopsies are negative and small lesions are missed. WLC misses flat lesions and cannot show depth.
- **OCT (optical coherence tomography)** is an **"optical biopsy"** technique listed among enhanced cystoscopic methods for NMIBC (non-muscle-invasive bladder cancer): real-time cross-sectional imaging of the bladder wall. It works like ultrasound but uses light.

### Principle
- OCT is the **optical analogue of B-mode ultrasound**. **Near-infrared light** (about 1300 nm) is shone on tissue. It measures the echo delay of back-scattered **near-infrared light** instead of sound: using **low-coherence interferometry**: back-scattered light is measured against a reference beam.
- The delay of reflected light gives depth. Scanning across builds a cross-sectional image.
- **Resolution about 10–20 µm** (near histology). **Depth about 2–3 mm** (another source: only **1–2 mm depth**): urothelium, lamina propria and superficial muscle.
- First described in 1991 (ophthalmology).

### Technique
- A fibre-optic probe passes through the cystoscope's working channel (channel) and touches the mucosa.
- WLC or blue light finds the lesion; the probe is placed **in contact** with it and read at once.

### Appearance (image interpretation)
| Normal | Tumour |
|---|---|
| Thin, dark urothelium | Thickened urothelium |
| Bright lamina propria; clear boundary | Layers lost |
| Muscle visible below | Broken boundary = invasion (T1) |

| Tissue | OCT appearance |
|---|---|
| Normal | Three layers: thin low-scatter urothelium, bright lamina propria, darker muscularis |
| Inflammation | Layers preserved but thickened |
| Papillary tumour (Ta) | Thickened urothelium, papillary pattern; lamina propria boundary intact |
| Invasive (T1) | **Loss of the urothelium–lamina propria boundary** |

### Uses (clinical uses)
- Tell tumour from inflammation at suspicious areas / separate benign from malignant flat lesions; fewer unnecessary biopsies.
- **Staging Ta vs T1** (lamina propria invasion); estimate Ta versus T1 during TURBT; helps in diverticula and thin walls.
- Check margins and completeness of TURBT (transurethral resection of bladder tumour).
- **UTUC (upper tract urothelial carcinoma):** through the ureteroscope, OCT correlates well with final grade and stage.

### Accuracy (evidence) and limitations
- Small studies show **high sensitivity but lower (moderate) specificity**. Inflammation, scar, recent TURBT or BCG (bacille Calmette-Guérin; post-BCG change) give false positives (mimic tumour). Adding fluorescence cystoscopy (OCT to blue light) improves specificity.
- Small (tiny) field of view: needs WLC or PDD (photodynamic diagnosis) to guide it.
- Cannot judge deep muscle invasion (too shallow to stage muscle invasion); grading unreliable (no cellular detail, so cannot grade); cost; learning curve.
- Still investigational; not in routine guideline use (not recommended routinely by guidelines).

### Other enhanced cystoscopy (techniques)
| Technique | Principle |
|---|---|
| **PDD (blue light, HAL, hexaminolevulinate)** | Tumour porphyrins fluoresce red; finds (detects) about one-third more CIS (carcinoma in situ) |
| **NBI (narrow-band imaging)** | 415/540 nm light absorbed by haemoglobin; shows vessels; many false positives |
| **CLE (confocal laser endomicroscopy)** | Cellular-level (cell-level surface) images after fluorescein |
- **Also know:** Raman spectroscopy.

**Diagram to draw:** OCT image showing normal (three bladder) layers vs tumour with lost layers (loss of layering).

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Near-infrared interferometry ("ultrasound with light"); 10–20 µm resolution; 2–3 mm depth (another source: depth 1–2 mm).
> - Loss of layering suggests tumour.
> - Role: Ta vs T1; cannot assess deep muscle.
> - High sensitivity, moderate specificity, false positives from inflammation; investigational.

## Re-look (second-look) TUR bladder tumour
**Asked as:**
- Re-look TUR bladder tumour. — *Summer 2017 Paper IV Q3d (SAQ)*
- Re-look TUR bladder tumour. — *Practice Set 5 Paper IV Q3d (earlier version) (SAQ)*

**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)

*Read as second-look (repeat or restaging) TURBT.*

### Definition
- A **planned second (restaging) TURBT (transurethral resection of bladder tumour)** of the primary site, including detrusor muscle, a few weeks after the first. It completes resection and corrects staging. It is a quality indicator of NMIBC (non-muscle-invasive bladder cancer) care.

### Rationale
- **TURBT is a staging operation first.** Completeness and muscle in the specimen are the quality markers.
- **Incomplete resection** — residual tumour is common:
  - At the resection site in **≥40%** when re-resected within weeks; tumour at first check cystoscopy in up to 45%.
  - **HG (high-grade) Ta:** residual up to 50%, upstaging up to 15%.
  - **Understaging:** in **HG T1**, re-resection shows worse disease or upstaging to muscle invasion in **25–30%**. This rises to about half if the first specimen had **no muscle**.
  - Understaging is about twice as likely without muscle.

### Indications (AUA [American Urological Association] / EAU [European Association of Urology])
| Recommended | Consider | Not needed |
|---|---|---|
| Incomplete first (initial) resection | HG Ta, large or multifocal (AUA) (residual up to 50%, upstaging up to 15%) | Low-grade Ta |
| **All T1 tumours** | Outside resection with no slides for review | Primary CIS (carcinoma in situ) |
| **No detrusor muscle** in specimen (except low-grade Ta and primary CIS) | | Clear muscle invasion |
| | | Cystectomy already planned |

### Timing
- **2–6 weeks** after the first TURBT (EAU); **within 6 weeks** (AUA), before BCG (bacille Calmette–Guérin). This allows healing yet avoids delaying intravesical BCG.

### Technique
- Resect the **primary-site scar and base deep into detrusor**, plus any residual tumour; send the base / specimens separately. Use blue light or NBI (narrow-band imaging) if available; it helps.

### Benefits / evidence
- Corrects stage and **changes treatment in about one-third**.
- **Divrik RCT (randomised controlled trial, 2010)** in T1: re-TUR improved recurrence-free and progression-free survival (lower recurrence and progression).
- Better BCG response with minimal residual tumour / when BCG follows re-TUR.

### Result-based plan (decision after re-TUR)
- **Residual T1 at re-resection** means (predicts) progression in up to **80%**: consider early cystectomy.
```
No tumour (T0) / Ta       → BCG induction + maintenance
Residual HG T1            → early cystectomy (or BCG with close review)
Muscle invasion (≥T2)     → treat as muscle-invasive: neoadjuvant chemotherapy + cystectomy
```

### Recent advances
- **En bloc resection** gives muscle (more detrusor) more often, less residual tumour, and may reduce re-TUR. It is not yet a proven substitute.
- Expert pathology review changes management in about one-third.
- **Also know:** maximal TURBT before trimodality therapy.

**Diagram to draw:** flowchart: T1 or no muscle → re-TUR 2–6 weeks → T2: cystectomy pathway / residual T1: consider cystectomy / T0–Ta: BCG; and bladder wall showing first resection scar and deep second resection into detrusor.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Re-TUR indications: all T1, incomplete resection, no muscle; consider HG Ta.
> - Timing 2–6 weeks; residual ≥40%; HG T1 upstaged (upstaging) in 25–30% (about half if no muscle / without muscle).
> - Residual T1 → progression (progresses) in up to 80%.

## Secondary tumours after urinary diversion
**Asked as:**
- Secondary tumors after urinary diversion. — *Summer 2021 Paper IV Q3e (SAQ)*
- Secondary tumors after urinary diversion. — *Practice Set 4 Paper IV Q3e (earlier version) (SAQ)* (also answered in 2021-Summer Paper 4, Q3e)

**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)

### Definition
- New cancers arising in or next to bowel used for urinary diversion or bladder augmentation, usually after a **long latency** (10–20 years or more). Distinct from recurrent urothelial cancer.

### Risk by diversion (German registry, Kälble 2011)
| Diversion | Risk | Surveillance |
|---|---|---|
| **Ureterosigmoidostomy** | 2.6% (6–29% in long-term series) | Regular colonoscopy **from 5 years** |
| Augmentation cystoplasty | 1.6% | Endoscopy, often from 10 years |
| Colonic neobladder | 1.3% | — |
| Ileocaecal pouch | 0.14% | If symptomatic |
| Ileal neobladder | 0.05% | No routine |
| Ileal conduit | 0.02% | No routine |

- **Pattern:** risk is highest where urothelium meets colon and is bathed in faeces; purely ileal diversions are lowest.

### Pathogenesis
- **Urothelium–colonic junction** exposed to urine and faeces; tumours arise at the ureterocolic anastomosis.
- **N-nitrosamines** from bacterial action on urinary nitrates (probably enhancers, not sole cause); chronic inflammation; growth factors (EGF, epidermal growth factor); ornithine decarboxylase; abnormal mucin.
- Removing the urothelium prevents tumours experimentally.
- **Defunctioned anastomoses still form cancer:** when converting a ureterosigmoidostomy, excise the old anastomosis.

### Pathology
- Mostly **adenocarcinoma**; also adenomatous polyps, urothelial carcinoma, sarcoma.
- **Augmentation:** adenocarcinoma and aggressive urothelial carcinoma, often advanced at diagnosis; earliest at 4 years. **Gastrocystoplasty** carries relatively high risk. Exstrophy and immunosuppression add risk.
- **Nephrogenic adenoma:** benign; commonest tumour after cystoplasty.

### Presentation
- Haematuria, rectal bleeding, change in bowel habit, ureteric obstruction (hydronephrosis), recurrent infection, mass.

### Management
- Biopsy and staging.
- **Ureterosigmoidostomy:** resection of the segment with the anastomosis; conversion to conduit or continent diversion.
- **Augmentation:** partial or total cystectomy with new diversion.
- **Prevention:** prefer ileal segments; counsel patients; lifelong follow-up.

**Also know:** after cystectomy, recurrent urothelial cancer may arise in the upper tracts or a retained urethra (urethral recurrence about 7–9%); needs separate surveillance.

**Diagram to draw:** ureterosigmoid anastomosis with tumour at the urothelial–colonic junction.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Ureterosigmoidostomy: highest risk; colonoscopy from year 5.
> - Mostly adenocarcinoma at the anastomosis.
> - Excise defunctioned anastomoses.

## VI-RADS
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 4 Paper 4, Q3b

### Definition
- **VI-RADS (Vesical Imaging–Reporting and Data System):** a 5-point score for the likelihood of **muscle (detrusor) invasion** by bladder cancer on mpMRI (multiparametric magnetic resonance imaging). Panebianco et al., Eur Urol 2018.
- Standardises bladder MRI reporting, as PI-RADS (Prostate Imaging Reporting and Data System) does for the prostate.

### Technique
- Multiplanar **T2W (T2-weighted)**, **DWI (diffusion-weighted imaging)** with ADC (apparent diffusion coefficient) map, and **DCE (dynamic contrast-enhanced)** T1.
- Done **before TURBT (transurethral resection of bladder tumour)**: resection oedema mimics invasion. Distend the bladder adequately.

### Principle
- On T2W the detrusor is a **low-signal (dark) line**; urine, mucosa and submucosa are bright. The question is whether tumour **interrupts that dark line**.

### Scoring
| Score | Invasion | T2W | DCE | DWI |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Highly unlikely | <1 cm, ± stalk; dark line intact | No early muscle enhancement | Muscle continuous |
| 2 | Unlikely | >1 cm with stalk and thick inner layer, or sessile; line intact | Inner layer enhances only | As 1 |
| 3 | Equivocal | No category 2 features; line intact | No muscle interruption | As 1 |
| 4 | Likely | **Line interrupted** | Enhancement extends into muscle | High signal into muscle |
| 5 | Highly likely | **Into perivesical fat** | Through wall into fat | High signal through wall and fat |

- **Cut-off ≥3 = suspicious for MIBC (muscle-invasive bladder cancer)**; the "equivocal" category already counts as positive.
- Sensitivity and specificity roughly 80–90%; DWI is the main functional sequence.

### Clinical uses
- Local staging before TURBT; earlier referral for neoadjuvant chemotherapy and cystectomy.
- Patients who cannot have iodinated contrast (renal impairment, allergy).
- **MRI-first pathway** (UK BladderPath trial) shortened time to correct treatment for MIBC.
- Response after neoadjuvant chemotherapy or bladder preservation: under study.

### Limitations
- Needs an experienced radiologist; post-TURBT or BCG (bacille Calmette–Guérin) inflammation; small, bladder-neck or diverticular tumours; poor for CIS (carcinoma in situ) and nodes; does not replace histology.

**Diagram to draw:** bladder wall with tumour breaching the dark detrusor line (VI-RADS 4).

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Five points; tests the dark muscle line on T2W.
> - ≥3 suspicious for MIBC; 4 line broken, 5 fat.
> - Scan before TURBT.
> - Panebianco 2018; accuracy about 80–90%.

## Virtual cystoscopy
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 5 Paper 4, Q3a

### Definition and principle
- **Virtual cystoscopy (VC)** is a computer-generated, three-dimensional (3D) endoluminal view of the bladder. It is reconstructed from thin-section **CT (computed tomography)** or **MRI (magnetic resonance imaging)** data of a distended bladder.
- Software "flies through" the lumen using **surface or volume rendering**; multiplanar images also show wall thickness and extravesical spread.

### Technique
| Step | CT virtual cystoscopy | MR virtual cystoscopy |
|---|---|---|
| Distension | Air or CO2 through a catheter (negative contrast), or excretory-phase contrast (positive contrast, no catheter) | Urine-filled bladder; urine bright on T2 |
| Acquisition | Thin slices, supine and prone (moves dependent urine off the posterior wall) | 3D T2 or T1 contrast sequences |
| Drawback | Radiation; catheter for gas | Cost, time, motion artefact |

### Advantages
- Non-invasive; no anaesthesia.
- Sees where a scope cannot: through a **tight urethral stricture**, inside **diverticula**, beyond a large median lobe.
- With **CTU (CT urography)**, upper tracts and staging are imaged in one study (Chapter 5: high accuracy for bladder tumours if well distended).

### Limitations
- Detects polypoid tumours ≥5 mm well; misses **small and flat lesions, including CIS (carcinoma in situ)**.
- No colour or mucosal detail, **no biopsy and no treatment**.
- False positives: trabeculation, clot, debris, folds of an underdistended bladder, prostatic impression.

### Indications (selective)
- Unfit for, or refusing, cystoscopy; urethral stricture; diverticulum; bladder evaluation during CTU for haematuria.
- **Guideline position:** EAU (European Association of Urology) and AUA (American Urological Association) do not recommend VC in place of cystoscopy. Flexible cystoscopy remains the standard.

### Related advances
- **Also know:** MRI **VI-RADS (Vesical Imaging–Reporting and Data System)** scores muscle invasion (Chapter 6); blue-light and narrow-band cystoscopy improve CIS detection (Chapter 8); virtual ureteroscopy uses the same principle.

**Diagram to draw:** thin-slice CT of a gas-distended bladder, then a 3D endoluminal fly-through showing a polypoid tumour.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - 3D endoluminal reconstruction from thin-section CT or MRI.
> - Good for polypoid tumours ≥5 mm; misses flat lesions and CIS.
> - No biopsy; does not replace cystoscopy.
> - Useful beyond strictures and inside diverticula.

## Bladder transplantation
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 5 Paper 4, Q3f

### Rationale
- After cystectomy, or when the bladder cannot be reconstructed, the usual substitute is **bowel**. Bowel in the urinary tract causes mucus, metabolic acidosis, stones, infection and malignancy risk.
- Chapter 135: two frontiers aim to **remove bowel from urinary reconstruction**: tissue engineering and bladder transplantation.

### Tissue engineering (why it has not succeeded)
- **Atala et al. (2006):** first seeded biodegradable construct for neurogenic augmentation. A later multi-institution trial showed **no significant gain in compliance or capacity** (Chapter 48).
- Engineered conduit trial: **half developed stenosis or stricture**, because of stiff material, poor cell coverage and **no blood supply** (Chapter 135).
- Engineered bladder: storage only (Chapter 111). See 2022-Summer Paper 4, Q2.

### Bladder transplantation: development (Chapter 135)
- A **VCA (vascularised composite allograft)**: the donor bladder is transplanted with its own blood vessels.
- Stepwise preclinical work at USC (University of Southern California): pigs, perfused cadavers, then brain-dead human donors.

### Technique (robotic)
1. **Robotic retrieval** of the bladder on its vesical vascular pedicles from the donor.
2. **Back-table vascular reconstruction**.
3. **Robotic implantation**: vessels to recipient iliac vessels; ureters joined to the graft.
4. **ICG (indocyanine green) fluorescence** confirms perfusion. Vascularity and mucosal integrity held for over 12 hours.

### First clinical case
- Reported in **2025** from Los Angeles: a combined kidney and bladder transplant from a deceased donor, in a dialysis patient who had lost most of his bladder.

### Problems
- **Lifelong immunosuppression**: infection, malignancy, drug nephrotoxicity. This is the major limitation (Chapter 135), especially after cancer.
- **Denervated graft**: stores urine; voiding may need straining or CIC (clean intermittent catheterisation).
- Rejection monitoring, donor supply, unknown long-term function.

### Likely candidates
- Already immunosuppressed patients (kidney transplant recipients) with an absent or non-functioning bladder.

**Diagram to draw:** donor bladder on its vascular pedicles anastomosed to recipient iliac vessels, ureters entering the graft.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Goal: avoid bowel in the urinary tract.
> - Tissue-engineered bladders failed on blood supply and function.
> - Robotic VCA; first human case 2025.
> - Limits: immunosuppression and denervation.

## Artificial urinary bladder
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 6 Paper 4, Q3d

*Read as a man-made bladder substitute (prosthetic, engineered or transplanted). Bowel neobladder: see Practice Set 4 Paper 2, Q3e.*

### Need
- Bowel substitutes absorb urine and secrete mucus, causing acidosis, mucus, stones, infection, perforation and cancer. The aim is a substitute that **is bladder-like, not bowel**.

### Requirements of an ideal substitute
- Low-pressure storage of adequate volume; continence.
- Complete, voluntary emptying; no reflux or upper-tract damage.
- Biocompatible: no encrustation, infection, rejection or cancer risk; durable.

### Approaches
| Approach | Principle | Status |
|---|---|---|
| **Alloplastic (prosthetic) bladder** | Synthetic reservoir (silicone, polymers) joined to ureters and urethra | Failed: encrustation, infection, leak at the tissue–prosthesis junction, migration, fibrosis |
| **Tissue-engineered bladder** | Autologous urothelial and muscle cells seeded on a biodegradable scaffold, omental wrap | Atala (2006): 7 patients improved; phase II trial showed no significant gain in capacity or compliance; experimental |
| **Engineered conduit** | Seeded scaffold as urinary conduit | Neo-Urinary Conduit trial: half developed stenosis or stricture; stiff, no blood supply |
| **Bladder transplantation** | Robotic retrieval and transplant of a vascularised composite bladder allograft | Feasible in models and brain-dead donors; first human case reported in 2025; needs immunosuppression |

### Why progress is slow
- **Vascularity:** thick constructs die centrally.
- **Innervation and contraction:** an engineered bladder stores urine but cannot yet empty voluntarily.
- **Urine–material interface:** foreign material encrusts and becomes infected.
- Fibrosis and contracture.

### Alternatives in clinical use
- Augmentation (bowel, ureterocystoplasty, autoaugmentation); continent or orthotopic diversion; ileal conduit.
- **Also know:** neuroprosthetic emptying (sacral anterior root stimulator), artificial urinary sphincter, intraurethral valve-pump devices for underactive bladders.

### Future
- Bioprinted vascularised constructs, stem cells (urine-derived, iPSCs, induced pluripotent stem cells), smart scaffolds.

**Diagram to draw:** biopsy → cell culture → seeding on bladder-shaped scaffold → omental wrap → anastomosis to bladder base.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Prosthetic bladders failed (encrustation, infection, leak).
> - Atala 2006 promising; later trial no gain; still experimental.
> - Barriers: vascularity, innervation, urine–material interface.

## Rapid Revision Sheet – Section 12 · Bladder Cancer & Diversion

*All the key points from this section in one place. Read this in the last 30 minutes before the exam.*

#### Continent urinary diversion: orthotopic neobladder (Studer) versus non-orthotopic diversion (LAQ)

- Three principles: reliable outlet, low pressure (detubularise and make a sphere), ≥500 mL; 500–600 mL by 1 year.
- Detubularised ileal sphere: low pressure, large capacity; continence depends on the rhabdosphincter.
- Neobladder needs eGFR (estimated glomerular filtration rate) >35–40 mL/min, a tumour-free urethral margin and ability to self-catheterise. Absolute bar: positive urethral margin, stricture, eGFR <40.
- 54–56 cm ileum, 15–20 cm from ICV: 40–44-cm U (sphere) plus 10–15 cm afferent limb.
- Refluxing end-to-side ureters; stricture <3%; kidneys preserved ~95%; refluxing ureteric anastomosis is acceptable (USC (University of Southern California) trial).
- Obstruction, not reflux, destroys kidneys; USC RCT: Studer = T pouch.
- Day continence 80–90% (about 90%); night incontinence 20–50% (up to half); women retain (20–30%).
- Sitting Valsalva voiding, timed voiding, night alarm.
- Eligible 80–90% men, 75% women; actual uptake about 9% in the US.
- Ileum/colon: hyperchloraemic acidosis; jejunum: hyperkalaemic; stomach: alkalosis.
- No diversion is proven best for quality of life; counsel all options.

#### Intravesical therapy for urothelial (transitional cell) carcinoma of bladder, including combination intravesical therapy (LAQ)

- Single instillation within 6 h; never BCG immediately (SWOG S0337 gemcitabine).
- Only maintenance BCG reduces progression; SWOG 3-year schedule.
- BCG-unresponsive: cystectomy; pembrolizumab, nadofaragene, N-803.
- Gem/Doce: gemcitabine 1 g then docetaxel 37.5 mg; 84% cystectomy-free.
- Only EMDA-MMC improves BCG sequentially.
- N-803 + BCG: CIS complete response 71%.
- BCG + checkpoint: CREST, POTOMAC positive; ALBAN negative.

#### Discuss bladder preservation surgery for TCC of urinary bladder. (LAQ)

- TMT = complete TURBT + RT + radiosensitiser; select T2, no hydronephrosis, no CIS.
- BC2001: MMC/5-FU + RT, 2-year locoregional DFS 67% vs 48%.
- Salvage cystectomy 10–30%; follow up the bladder for 10 years.
- Partial cystectomy: solitary <3 cm, no CIS, frozen-section margins, always PLND.

#### Role of neoadjuvant therapy and Lymphadenectomy for muscle invasive bladder cancer and upper tract urothelial cell cancer? (LAQ)

- Cisplatin combinations only: ABC 5% absolute 5-year survival gain; SWOG 8710 P = 0.06.
- VESPER: ddMVAC beat GC; NIAGARA: perioperative durvalumab improved event-free and overall survival.
- SWOG S1011: extended PLND gives no survival benefit over standard.
- UTUC: neoadjuvant before kidney loss; POUT disease-free survival HR 0.45; right-sided needs interaortocaval nodes.

#### Optical coherence tomography in carcinoma bladder (SAQ)

- Near-infrared interferometry ("ultrasound with light"); 10–20 µm resolution; 2–3 mm depth (another source: depth 1–2 mm).
- Loss of layering suggests tumour.
- Role: Ta vs T1; cannot assess deep muscle.
- High sensitivity, moderate specificity, false positives from inflammation; investigational.

#### Re-look (second-look) TUR bladder tumour (SAQ)

- Re-TUR indications: all T1, incomplete resection, no muscle; consider HG Ta.
- Timing 2–6 weeks; residual ≥40%; HG T1 upstaged (upstaging) in 25–30% (about half if no muscle / without muscle).
- Residual T1 → progression (progresses) in up to 80%.

#### Secondary tumours after urinary diversion (SAQ)

- Ureterosigmoidostomy: highest risk; colonoscopy from year 5.
- Mostly adenocarcinoma at the anastomosis.
- Excise defunctioned anastomoses.

#### VI-RADS (SAQ)

- Five points; tests the dark muscle line on T2W.
- ≥3 suspicious for MIBC; 4 line broken, 5 fat.
- Scan before TURBT.
- Panebianco 2018; accuracy about 80–90%.

#### Virtual cystoscopy (SAQ)

- 3D endoluminal reconstruction from thin-section CT or MRI.
- Good for polypoid tumours ≥5 mm; misses flat lesions and CIS.
- No biopsy; does not replace cystoscopy.
- Useful beyond strictures and inside diverticula.

#### Bladder transplantation (SAQ)

- Goal: avoid bowel in the urinary tract.
- Tissue-engineered bladders failed on blood supply and function.
- Robotic VCA; first human case 2025.
- Limits: immunosuppression and denervation.

#### Artificial urinary bladder (SAQ)

- Prosthetic bladders failed (encrustation, infection, leak).
- Atala 2006 promising; later trial no gain; still experimental.
- Barriers: vascularity, innervation, urine–material interface.

# Section 13 · Kidney & UTUC — Long answer questions (LAQ)

## Radiology for solid renal masses.
**Type:** LAQ (25 marks)  
**Source:** 2018-Summer Paper 4, Q1

### Introduction
- Most renal masses are now found incidentally. Imaging is the **strongest predictor of malignancy**; size and **enhancement** are the two key variables.
- About 20% of solid enhancing cT1 masses are benign (oncocytoma, fat-poor AML (angiomyolipoma)).
- **Goals:** solid or cystic? enhancing? fat? subtype? stage and surgical anatomy.

### A. CT (computed tomography): the standard test
**Renal mass protocol** (dedicated, thin slices):

| Phase | Timing | Use |
|---|---|---|
| Non-contrast | – | Baseline HU (Hounsfield units), fat, calcification, haemorrhage |
| Corticomedullary | 30–70 s | Hypervascular masses; arteries; clear cell vs other subtypes |
| Nephrographic | 90–120 s | **Most sensitive** for detection (tumour enhances less than parenchyma); renal vein and IVC (inferior vena cava) thrombus |
| Excretory | 7–10 min | Collecting system relation |

**Enhancement thresholds** (rise from unenhanced value):

| Rise | Meaning |
|---|---|
| ≥20 HU | Enhancing: solid tumour |
| 10–20 HU | Equivocal (MRI, magnetic resonance imaging, or CEUS) |
| <10 HU | Non-enhancing: cyst |

- **Pitfall:** pseudoenhancement of small (<10–15 mm) cysts; dual-energy CT iodine maps help.
- **Macroscopic fat = AML:** fat ≤ −10 to −20 HU on unenhanced CT is diagnostic. Fat **with calcification** suggests RCC (renal cell carcinoma).
- **Fat-poor AML:** hyperdense on unenhanced CT, homogeneous, avid early enhancement and washout.
- **Subtype clues:** clear cell RCC enhances early, avidly and heterogeneously (necrosis); papillary RCC is homogeneous and **hypoenhancing**; chromophobe and oncocytoma intermediate, with a central scar. Overlap is large.

### B. MRI
- **Indications:** contrast allergy, CKD (chronic kidney disease), equivocal CT enhancement, young patients, thrombus. Enhancement = signal rise ≥15–20%. Macrocyclic gadolinium carries very low NSF (nephrogenic systemic fibrosis) risk.

| Feature | Clear cell | Papillary | Chromophobe / oncocytoma | AML |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| T2 signal | High, heterogeneous | **Low**, homogeneous (haemosiderin) | Central T2-bright scar | Fat signal |
| Enhancement | Avid (>200%) | Weak | Intermediate / avid | Avid |
| Diffusion (ADC, apparent diffusion coefficient) | High | Lowest | Intermediate | – |
| Chemical shift | **Signal drop** (intracellular lipid, ~59%) | No drop | – | **India-ink** artefact; loss on fat saturation |

- **ccLS (clear cell likelihood score, 1–5):** structured MRI algorithm for solid cT1a masses using T2 signal, corticomedullary enhancement and microscopic fat, plus diffusion and **segmental enhancement inversion** (favours oncocytoma). Score 4–5 suggests clear cell RCC; 1–2 a non-clear-cell tumour. It guides biopsy versus surveillance.

### C. Ultrasound and CEUS (contrast-enhanced ultrasound)
- Grey-scale: cyst versus solid; poor below 2 cm. AML is hyperechoic, but so are some small RCCs.
- **CEUS** (microbubbles, not nephrotoxic): shows enhancement; used in CKD or allergy, indeterminate lesions, ablation and surveillance.

### D. Cystic masses: Bosniak 2019 (CT and MRI)
| Class | Key feature | Malignancy / action |
|---|---|---|
| I | Simple, thin wall ≤2 mm, no septa | Benign; none |
| II | 1–3 thin septa; homogeneous hyperdense (≥70 HU unenhanced) | Benign; none |
| IIF | Wall or septa 3 mm, or ≥4 thin septa | <10%; follow 6, 12 months, then yearly for 5 years |
| III | Wall or septa ≥4 mm, or irregular | ~50%; surgery or surveillance |
| IV | **Enhancing nodule** | ~90%; treat |

### E. Functional and molecular imaging
- **99mTc-sestamibi SPECT/CT** (single-photon emission CT): mitochondria-rich **oncocytoma** and hybrid oncocytic or chromophobe tumours take it up; clear cell RCC does not.
- **Girentuximab PET (positron emission tomography)** targets CA-IX (carbonic anhydrase IX) on clear cell RCC. **ZIRCON** (⁸⁹Zr-girentuximab, indeterminate cT1 masses): about 85% sensitivity and 87% specificity for clear cell RCC.
- **FDG-PET** (fluorodeoxyglucose PET): not recommended (low sensitivity). PSMA (prostate-specific membrane antigen) PET: experimental.

### F. Renal mass biopsy
- **Indications:** result may change management; suspected lymphoma, metastasis or infection; before ablation or surveillance; metastatic disease. Not needed if surgery is planned regardless.
- Cores: PPV (positive predictive value) 98.8%; subtype accuracy ≥90%, grade 60–80%; non-diagnostic ~14%. Seeding almost nil. Oncocytoma cannot be confirmed on cores.

### G. Staging and surgical planning
- **Venous thrombus:** CT sensitivity 78% (renal vein), 96% (IVC). MRI or multiplanar CT shows the cephalad tip; enhancing thrombus is tumour, non-enhancing is bland.

| Mayo level | Extent | T stage |
|---|---|---|
| 0 | Renal vein | T3a |
| I | IVC ≤2 cm above renal vein | T3b |
| II | IVC below hepatic veins | T3b |
| III | Intrahepatic IVC, below diaphragm | T3b |
| IV | Above diaphragm or atrium | T3c |

- Nodes ≥2 cm usually malignant. Chest CT; bone scan only if symptomatic.
- **Nephrometry:** R.E.N.A.L. (Radius, Exophytic/endophytic, Nearness to collecting system, Anterior/posterior, Location), PADUA, C-index: predict complexity and complications; guide partial versus radical nephrectomy.
- **After ablation:** persistent or new enhancement = residual or recurrence.
- **Recent:** radiomics and AI (artificial intelligence).

**Diagram to draw:** algorithm: incidental mass → renal-protocol CT/MRI → fat (AML) / cyst (Bosniak) / enhancing solid → biopsy or treat.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Enhancement ≥20 HU = solid tumour; macroscopic fat = AML.
> - MRI: clear cell T2-bright with chemical-shift drop; papillary T2-dark and hypoenhancing; ccLS 1–5.
> - Sestamibi for oncocytoma; ⁸⁹Zr-girentuximab PET (ZIRCON) for clear cell.
> - Biopsy PPV ~99%, non-diagnostic ~14%.

# Section 13 · Kidney & UTUC — Short answer questions (SAQ)

## Nephron Saving Surgery (NSS)
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2017-Winter Paper 4, Q3f

### Definition
- Complete tumour removal with **maximal preservation of functioning parenchyma**, mainly PN (partial nephrectomy).
- **Rationale**: RN (radical nephrectomy) causes CKD (chronic kidney disease), linked to cardiovascular events and death; PN gives equal cancer control with better function.

### Indications
| Category | Situations |
|---|---|
| Imperative | Solitary kidney, bilateral tumours, RN would mean dialysis |
| Relative | Contralateral kidney threatened (stone, stenosis, diabetes, hypertension); hereditary RCC (renal cell carcinoma) |
| Elective | **cT1a with normal contralateral kidney: standard of care** |

- Increasingly used for cT1b–T2 when feasible. AUA (American Urological Association) 2021: RN only if high complexity, no CKD or proteinuria, and post-RN eGFR (estimated glomerular filtration rate) likely >45.

### Pre-operative assessment
- Multiphase CT (computed tomography) or MRI; **R.E.N.A.L. nephrometry score** (radius, exophytic, nearness to collecting system, anterior/posterior, location) predicts complexity.

### Technique (open, laparoscopic or robotic)
1. Expose kidney; **intraoperative ultrasound** maps the tumour.
2. Hilar control; clamp artery (± vein), selective, or off-clamp.
3. Excise with a thin rim, or **enucleate** along the pseudocapsule (familial, multifocal tumours).
4. Close collecting system and vessels; renorrhaphy; unclamp; drain.
- **Robotic PN**: shorter warm ischaemia and fewer conversions than laparoscopy.

### Ischaemia
- Aim warm ischaemia **<25–30 minutes**; **cold ischaemia (ice slush)** if longer is expected, especially in a solitary kidney.
- Function depends mainly on **parenchyma preserved**. Randomised trials: no functional benefit of off-clamp, hypothermia or mannitol with a normal contralateral kidney.
- **Trifecta**: negative margin + no major complication + warm ischaemia ≤25 minutes.

### Complications
- **Urine leak** (stent, drain), **bleeding/pseudoaneurysm** (angioembolisation), AV (arteriovenous) fistula, renal insufficiency, hyperfiltration proteinuria (ACE (angiotensin-converting enzyme) inhibitors).

### Outcomes
- Local recurrence 1–2% (elective), 3–5% (imperative). Margin width does not matter if negative.
- **EORTC (European Organisation for Research and Treatment of Cancer) 30904 (2011)**: RN vs PN for ≤5 cm: PN better function, similar oncology; overall survival slightly better with RN, but trial flawed.
- **Also know:** thermal ablation for <3 cm; surveillance in frail patients.

**Diagram to draw:** tumour excised with rim, clamp on renal artery, two-layer renorrhaphy.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Imperative, relative, elective; elective PN is standard for cT1a.
> - Warm ischaemia ≤25 min; preserved parenchyma matters most.
> - EORTC 30904: PN better function, similar cancer control.

## Immuno modulation in Renal, cell carcinoma
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2025-Winter Paper 4, Q3a
(also answered in 2024-Winter Paper 2, Q3e)

*Assumed meaning: immunomodulation (immunotherapy) in renal cell carcinoma.*

### Rationale
- RCC (renal cell carcinoma) is immunogenic: rare (<1%) spontaneous regression after nephrectomy, tumour-infiltrating T cells.
- **Checkpoints:** PD-1 (programmed death-1) on T cells binds PD-L1 on tumour and switches T cells off; CTLA-4 (cytotoxic T-lymphocyte antigen-4) damps priming. Blockade **releases the brake** on existing immunity.
- VEGF (vascular endothelial growth factor) suppresses immunity, so TKIs (tyrosine kinase inhibitors) synergise.

### Cytokines (non-specific stimulation)
| | IFN-α (interferon-alpha) | High-dose IL-2 (interleukin-2) |
|---|---|---|
| Response | 10–15% | 15–20% |
| Durable complete response | <2% | **7–9%** |
| Toxicity | Flu-like | **Capillary leak**; intensive care |
| Role | Obsolete | Selected fit patients |

- High-dose IL-2: 600,000–720,000 IU/kg intravenously every 8 hours, maximum 15 doses; over 60% of complete responders stay disease-free.

### Checkpoint inhibitors (clear cell)
| Trial | Regimen vs comparator | Result |
|---|---|---|
| CheckMate 025 | Nivolumab vs everolimus, second line | OS (overall survival) 25.0 vs 19.6 months |
| **CheckMate 214** | Nivolumab + ipilimumab vs sunitinib | Intermediate/poor risk: OS HR (hazard ratio) 0.63; complete response 9% |
| KEYNOTE-426 / CheckMate 9ER | Pembrolizumab + axitinib / nivolumab + cabozantinib | Better PFS (progression-free survival) and OS |
| **CLEAR** | Pembrolizumab + lenvatinib | PFS 23.9 vs 9.2 months; response 71% |

- **First line:** IO/TKI (immuno-oncology plus TKI) for all IMDC (International Metastatic RCC Database Consortium) risk groups; nivolumab + ipilimumab for intermediate/poor only.
- After IO, switch to a VEGF-targeted TKI; IO rechallenge is not recommended.

### Adjuvant
- **KEYNOTE-564:** pembrolizumab for 1 year after nephrectomy in high-risk clear cell RCC (including resected metastases): disease-free survival HR 0.63; later OS benefit.

### Toxicity: irAEs (immune-related adverse events)
- Colitis, hepatitis, pneumonitis, thyroiditis, hypophysitis. Grade 2: hold, prednisolone; grade 3–4: stop, high-dose steroids.

**Also know:** PD-L1 does not select; sarcomatoid responds well; vaccines and allogeneic transplant failed.

**Diagram to draw:** T cell–tumour synapse with PD-1/PD-L1 and CTLA-4/B7 blocked.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - High-dose IL-2: durable complete response 7–9%.
> - CheckMate 214: nivolumab + ipilimumab, OS HR 0.63.
> - KEYNOTE-564: adjuvant pembrolizumab.

## Zero-ischaemia partial nephrectomy (laparoscopic and robotic)
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 3 Paper 4, Q3e
(clamping strategies and laparoscopic partial nephrectomy haemostasis also in 2019-Summer Paper 3, Q1)

### Definition and rationale
- **Zero-ischaemia PN (partial nephrectomy):** excision and renorrhaphy **without clamping the main renal artery**, so the kidney stays perfused (Gill and colleagues, 2011).
- **Why:** warm ischaemia injures nephrons (historical limit 30 minutes; trifecta ≤25). Most relevant in a **solitary kidney or CKD (chronic kidney disease)**.

### Techniques
| Technique | Method |
|---|---|
| **Off-clamp** | Hilum looped for rescue but not clamped; cold-scissor excision with strong suction |
| **Anatomical (super-selective)** | Microdissect the higher-order arterial branches entering the tumour; occlude only these with small bulldogs |
| **Selective segmental clamping** | Only the tumour's segment is ischaemic ("minimal ischaemia") |
| **Parenchymal compression** | Parenchymal clamp across a polar tumour |
| **Enucleation** | Pseudocapsule plane bleeds less, so suits off-clamp work |

### Aids
- **3D (three-dimensional) CT (computed tomography) reconstruction** maps segmental arteries; intraoperative Doppler ultrasound.
- **NIRF (near-infrared fluorescence) with ICG (indocyanine green)** on robotic consoles: after selective clamping, perfused kidney glows and the ischaemic tumour zone stays dark.
- **Robot:** wristed instruments and fast suturing ease off-clamp work.
- **Haemostasis:** clip or suture bed vessels as met; sliding-clip or barbed renorrhaphy; clamp if bleeding hides the margin.

### Selection
- **Ideal:** small (cT1a), exophytic, peripheral tumour; experienced surgeon.
- **Caution:** endophytic, hilar or central tumours.

### Evidence
- Off- versus on-clamp: **better early eGFR (estimated glomerular filtration rate), more blood loss**; **no difference beyond the early period**.
- **Robotic RCTs (randomised controlled trials, e.g. CLOCK):** no difference in eGFR or split function up to 2 years.
- RCT of super-selective clamping with NIRF: no 6-month gain; stopped for futility.
- **Concept:** parenchymal volume preserved, not ischaemia time within accepted limits, governs function.

- **Also know:** early unclamping; hypothermia for long ischaemia; mannitol no benefit.

**Diagram to draw:** hilum with a micro-bulldog on a tertiary branch; tumour zone dark on NIRF.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Zero ischaemia: no main-artery clamp (Gill, 2011); off-clamp or super-selective.
> - NIRF with ICG confirms the right branch.
> - RCTs: no long-term eGFR gain; more blood loss.
> - Parenchyma preserved matters more than clamp time.

## Rapid Revision Sheet – Section 13 · Kidney & UTUC

*All the key points from this section in one place. Read this in the last 30 minutes before the exam.*

#### Radiology for solid renal masses. (LAQ)

- Enhancement ≥20 HU = solid tumour; macroscopic fat = AML.
- MRI: clear cell T2-bright with chemical-shift drop; papillary T2-dark and hypoenhancing; ccLS 1–5.
- Sestamibi for oncocytoma; ⁸⁹Zr-girentuximab PET (ZIRCON) for clear cell.
- Biopsy PPV ~99%, non-diagnostic ~14%.

#### Nephron Saving Surgery (NSS) (SAQ)

- Imperative, relative, elective; elective PN is standard for cT1a.
- Warm ischaemia ≤25 min; preserved parenchyma matters most.
- EORTC 30904: PN better function, similar cancer control.

#### Immuno modulation in Renal, cell carcinoma (SAQ)

- High-dose IL-2: durable complete response 7–9%.
- CheckMate 214: nivolumab + ipilimumab, OS HR 0.63.
- KEYNOTE-564: adjuvant pembrolizumab.

#### Zero-ischaemia partial nephrectomy (laparoscopic and robotic) (SAQ)

- Zero ischaemia: no main-artery clamp (Gill, 2011); off-clamp or super-selective.
- NIRF with ICG confirms the right branch.
- RCTs: no long-term eGFR gain; more blood loss.
- Parenchyma preserved matters more than clamp time.

# Section 14 · Adrenal & Retroperitoneum — Long answer questions (LAQ)

## Adrenal mass evaluation and adrenocortical carcinoma: diagnosis and management
**Asked as:**
- Diagnosis and management for Adrenal Carcinoma. — *Summer 2018 Paper IV Q2 (LAQ)*
- Approach to evaluation of adrenal mass/s. — *Summer 2022 Paper IV Q3f (SAQ)*

**Type:** LAQ (25 marks); also asked as SAQ (10 marks)

### Introduction and epidemiology
- **Adrenal incidentaloma:** mass ≥1 cm found on imaging done for another reason.
- Two questions: **Is it malignant? (imaging)** and **Is it functioning? (biochemistry).**
- ACC (adrenocortical carcinoma): rare (0.7–2 per million per year), aggressive cortical tumour. **Bimodal** (children <10 years; adults 40–50 years); slight female excess.
- **Syndromes:** Li–Fraumeni (TP53), Beckwith–Wiedemann (IGF-2, insulin-like growth factor 2), Lynch, MEN-1 (multiple endocrine neoplasia 1), Carney complex, FAP (familial adenomatous polyposis). Surgery is the only cure.

### Differential diagnosis of an adrenal mass
- Non-functioning adenoma (commonest); cortisol-secreting adenoma (5.3%); phaeochromocytoma (5.1%); aldosteronoma (1%); ACC (adrenocortical carcinoma, 4.7%); metastasis (2.5%); myelolipoma, cyst, haemorrhage.
- **Bilateral:** metastases, lymphoma, **tuberculosis** and histoplasmosis, haemorrhage, CAH (congenital adrenal hyperplasia), macronodular hyperplasia, bilateral phaeochromocytoma (MEN-2 [multiple endocrine neoplasia type 2], VHL [von Hippel–Lindau]).

### Clinical features of ACC
- **Functional in 50–79% of adults** (90% of children):
  - Cortisol (commonest): rapidly progressive Cushing syndrome, often with virilisation.
  - Androgens: virilisation in women. **Oestrogen in a man (feminisation) suggests carcinoma.**
  - Aldosterone: rare.
- Non-functional: pain, fullness, weight loss, or incidental.

### Hormonal work-up (all lesions; ESE–ENSAT, European Society of Endocrinology–European Network for the Study of Adrenal Tumours)
| Axis / hormone | Tests |
|---|---|
| Glucocorticoid (cortisol) | **1 mg (1-mg) overnight dexamethasone suppression test (DST)** (or 24-h urine or late-night salivary cortisol): ≤1.8 µg/dL (50 nmol/L) excludes; >1.8 = **MACS** (mild autonomous cortisol secretion); >5 µg/dL autonomous; ACTH (adrenocorticotropic hormone) |
| Sex steroids and precursors | DHEA-S (dehydroepiandrosterone sulphate), androstenedione, testosterone, 17-OH-progesterone, 11-deoxycortisol; oestradiol in men and postmenopausal women (DHEA-S etc. only if ACC suspected) |
| Mineralocorticoid (aldosterone) | **ARR** (aldosterone-to-renin ratio; aldosterone:renin ratio) if hypertension or hypokalaemia |
| Catecholamine (catecholamines) | **Plasma free or 24-h urinary fractionated metanephrines** (plasma or urine **metanephrines**): must exclude phaeochromocytoma |
| Bilateral | 17-hydroxyprogesterone (CAH); exclude adrenal insufficiency |

- Why: confirms cortical origin, plans steroid cover, gives **tumour markers** for follow-up.
- **Exclude phaeochromocytoma before biopsy or surgery.** Biopsy only for suspected metastasis with known cancer.

### Imaging
- CT (computed tomography), HU (Hounsfield units), MRI (chemical-shift magnetic resonance imaging), MDT (multidisciplinary team), FDG-PET (fluorodeoxyglucose positron emission tomography).
```
Unenhanced CT -> <=10 HU or MRI signal drop-out -> lipid-rich adenoma
              -> >10 HU -> washout CT (15 min): APW >60% or RPW >40% -> adenoma
                         -> indeterminate -> MDT: repeat imaging 6-12 months, FDG-PET or surgery
```
- **APW** (absolute percentage washout) = (enhanced − delayed) ÷ (enhanced − unenhanced) × 100; **RPW** (relative) = (enhanced − delayed) ÷ enhanced × 100.
- Malignant features: heterogeneous, irregular, invasion, >4 cm. Macroscopic fat = myelolipoma.
- **Size and malignancy:** <4 cm 0.1%; 4–6 cm 2.4%; >6 cm 19.5% (another source: carcinoma in ~2% <4 cm, ~6% at 4–6 cm, ~25% >6 cm).
- **ACC size:** mean 10–12 cm; >90% exceed 5 cm.
- **CT in ACC:** irregular, heterogeneous, necrosis, calcification; **unenhanced >10 HU (Hounsfield units)** (mean ~39 HU vs ~8 HU in adenoma); **slow washout** (absolute <60%, relative <40% at 15 min). Growth ~1.9 cm/year.
- **MRI (magnetic resonance imaging):** no chemical-shift signal loss; best for IVC (inferior vena cava) thrombus and invasion.
- **FDG-PET/CT** (fluorodeoxyglucose positron emission tomography): avid, but cannot separate ACC from metastasis.
- **Staging:** CT chest, abdomen, pelvis; bone or brain only if symptomatic.
- **No biopsy** if ACC is suspected (seeding, poor accuracy); only for unresectable or metastatic disease, after excluding phaeochromocytoma.

### ESE–ENSAT 2023 (European Society of Endocrinology–European Network for the Study of Adrenal Tumors) for incidentaloma
- Homogeneous **≤10 HU: benign, no further imaging whatever the size**; metanephrines may be omitted.
- MACS: screen for hypertension, diabetes and osteoporosis; surgery individualised.
- Management: surgery for functioning tumour, suspicious imaging, >4 cm in fit patients, growth ≥5 mm and >20%.

### Pathology: Weiss score (≥3 of 9 = malignant)
| Domain | Criteria |
|---|---|
| Nuclear / cytology | High nuclear grade; >5 mitoses per 50 high-power fields; atypical mitoses |
| Architecture | ≤25% clear cells; diffuse architecture; necrosis |
| Invasion | Venous; sinusoidal; capsular |

- SF-1 (steroidogenic factor 1) confirms cortical origin. **Ki-67** and mitotic rate grade the tumour (low vs high grade).

### ENSAT staging
| Stage | Definition | 5-year disease-specific survival (ENSAT 2009) |
|---|---|---|
| I | T1 (≤5 cm), N0 M0 | ~80% |
| II | T2 (>5 cm), N0 M0 | ~60% |
| III | T3 (periadrenal fat) or T4 (adjacent organ, venous thrombus) or N1 | ~50% |
| IV | Distant metastasis only | ~13% |

- ENSAT differs from older UICC (Union for International Cancer Control) staging by moving T4 and N1 without metastasis into stage III.

### Management of ACC
**1. Surgery (stages I–III)**
- **Open transperitoneal adrenalectomy** for suspected ACC, especially >6 cm or invasive. Laparoscopy risks capsule rupture and peritoneal carcinomatosis; only for small organ-confined tumours by experts.
- **R0, intact capsule:** rupture gives dismal results. **En bloc** removal of involved kidney, liver, spleen, pancreas or IVC; IVC thrombectomy (bypass if needed).
- **Locoregional lymphadenectomy** (periadrenal fat, renal hilum, para-aortic or paracaval) improves staging and may reduce recurrence.
- High-volume surgeon (ESE–ENSAT: >6, preferably ≥20 adrenalectomies a year). Hydrocortisone cover if cortisol-secreting.

**2. Adjuvant therapy**
- **Mitotane** (adrenolytic DDT (dichlorodiphenyltrichloroethane) derivative) for **high risk**: stage III, R1/Rx, or Ki-67 >10% (ESE–ENSAT 2018), for at least 2 years.
  - Target blood level **14–20 mg/L**; needs **glucocorticoid replacement** (it destroys the cortex and raises cortisol-binding globulin).
  - Toxicity: gastrointestinal, neurological, hepatic, hypothyroidism, gynaecomastia.
- **ADIUVO** (RCT, randomised controlled trial; R0, stage I–III, Ki-67 ≤10%): no recurrence-free survival benefit. So observe low-risk patients.
- **Adjuvant radiotherapy** (tumour bed): R1 or Rx, or stage III. It cuts local recurrence but not overall survival.

**3. Advanced or metastatic (stage IV)**
- **EDP-M** (etoposide, doxorubicin, cisplatin + mitotane) is first line. **FIRM-ACT** (2012): versus streptozotocin–mitotane, response 23% vs 9% and PFS (progression-free survival) 5.0 vs 2.1 months; overall survival not significantly better.
- Mitotane alone for low-volume, slow disease.
- **Surgery:** resect oligometastases or late recurrence; debulk only if >90% removable.
- **Hormone control:** metyrapone, ketoconazole, mifepristone, etomidate. Radiotherapy for bone and brain metastases.

### Follow-up
- CT chest, abdomen, pelvis and baseline-raised hormones **every 3 months for 2 years**, then 6-monthly to 5 years.

### Prognosis
- Overall 5-year survival **20–47%**. Bad factors: stage, R1, high Ki-67 or mitoses, cortisol excess, age, size >12 cm.

### Recent advances
- Molecular classes (CIMP, CpG island methylator phenotype) predict prognosis. Checkpoint inhibitors give modest responses; IGF-1R (IGF-1 receptor) inhibitor linsitinib failed (GALACCTIC). ADIUVO-2 tests mitotane ± platinum–etoposide in high risk.

**Diagram to draw:** imaging and biochemistry algorithm above; algorithm: adrenal mass → hormones + CT (HU, washout) → suspected ACC → open R0 en bloc resection + LND (lymph node dissection) → risk-based adjuvant mitotane or radiotherapy.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - ≤10 HU = lipid-rich adenoma. APW >60%, RPW >40%.
> - 1-mg DST: >1.8 µg/dL = MACS.
> - Rule out phaeochromocytoma before biopsy.
> - Feminisation in a man, >6 cm, >10 HU, poor washout: think ACC. Never biopsy before resection.
> - Weiss ≥3/9; ENSAT stage IV = distant metastasis only.
> - Open, R0, intact capsule, en bloc + lymphadenectomy.
> - Mitotane level 14–20 mg/L; ADIUVO (low risk: no benefit); FIRM-ACT: EDP-M first line.

## Rapid Revision Sheet – Section 14 · Adrenal & Retroperitoneum

*All the key points from this section in one place. Read this in the last 30 minutes before the exam.*

#### Adrenal mass evaluation and adrenocortical carcinoma: diagnosis and management (LAQ)

- ≤10 HU = lipid-rich adenoma. APW >60%, RPW >40%.
- 1-mg DST: >1.8 µg/dL = MACS.
- Rule out phaeochromocytoma before biopsy.
- Feminisation in a man, >6 cm, >10 HU, poor washout: think ACC. Never biopsy before resection.
- Weiss ≥3/9; ENSAT stage IV = distant metastasis only.
- Open, R0, intact capsule, en bloc + lymphadenectomy.
- Mitotane level 14–20 mg/L; ADIUVO (low risk: no benefit); FIRM-ACT: EDP-M first line.

# Section 15 · Renal, Nephrology & Transplant — Long answer questions (LAQ)

## Allograft rejection, immunobiology of renal transplant, marginal kidney donor and newer immunosuppressant drugs
**Asked as:**
- Discuss pathophysiology of allograft rejection. Discuss newer immunosuppressive drugs in renal transplant. — *Winter 2017 Paper IV Q2 (LAQ)*
- Describe immunobiology of renal transplant and newer immunosuppressant drugs. — *Summer 2020 Paper IV Q1 (LAQ)*
- Immunobiology of renal transplant, describe. Also, describe newer immunosuppressant drugs — *Practice Set 3 Paper IV Q1 (LAQ)*
- Discuss marginal kidney donor and describe newer immunosuppressant drugs in renal transplantation — *Summer 2015 Paper IV Q2 (LAQ)*

**Type:** LAQ (25 marks)  
(also answered in 2020-Summer Paper 4, Q1)

### Introduction
- Rejection is the recipient's immune response against donor (allo) antigens. The recipient's immune system recognises donor antigens as foreign. Immunosuppression has cut acute rejection: it has fallen from at least 60% (pre-cyclosporine era; "over 60%") to **below 10%**. Slow, subclinical **antibody-mediated injury** (**antibody injury**) is now the main problem and the main cause of late graft loss.
- Organ shortage drives use of **marginal donors**. Their kidneys carry more DGF (delayed graft function) and shorter graft survival, but transplantation still beats dialysis.

### Part A: Immunobiology and pathophysiology of rejection

#### Transplant (target) antigens
| Antigen | Features |
|---|---|
| **HLA (human leucocyte antigen) = MHC (major histocompatibility complex)** | Class I (A, B, C) on all nucleated cells; class II (DR, DQ, DP) on antigen-presenting and endothelial cells. Class II mismatch is most immunogenic |
| **ABO** blood group | On endothelium; incompatibility causes hyperacute rejection unless desensitised |
| Minor and non-HLA antigens | Rejection despite HLA identity |

- **Sensitisation**: transfusion, pregnancy or previous transplant create (creates) preformed anti-HLA antibodies.
- **Testing**: CDC (complement-dependent cytotoxicity) and flow crossmatch (donor lymphocytes with recipient serum); **Luminex single-antigen beads** define each recipient's antibodies and allow a **virtual crossmatch**; cPRA (calculated panel-reactive antibody) expresses sensitisation.

#### Innate immunity: the first trigger
- **Ischaemia–reperfusion injury** activates innate immunity and up-regulates MHC: it releases danger signals (DAMPs, damage-associated molecular patterns). These activate **Toll-like receptors**, complement and macrophages, and up-regulate MHC on the graft. Hence DGF (delayed graft function) raises rejection risk.
- **Dendritic cells** bridge innate and adaptive immunity: activated, they up-regulate MHC and costimulatory B7 and migrate to lymph nodes.

#### Allorecognition (how T cells see the graft)
| Pathway | Mechanism | Main role |
|---|---|---|
| **Direct** | Recipient T cells recognise **intact donor MHC** on donor "passenger" dendritic cells | Very strong (high T-cell frequency); strong early acute TCMR (T-cell-mediated rejection) / early acute T-cell rejection |
| **Indirect** | Recipient APCs (antigen-presenting cells) present **processed donor peptides on self-MHC** | Lifelong; helps B cells make DSA (donor-specific antibody), so alloantibody and chronic rejection |
| **Semi-direct** | Recipient APCs acquire **intact donor MHC** (membrane transfer) and present it | Links the two pathways (both pathways) |

#### Three-signal model of T-cell activation
1. **Signal 1** (antigen): TCR (T-cell receptor) binds MHC–peptide → **calcineurin** dephosphorylates NFAT (nuclear factor of activated T cells) → IL-2 (interleukin-2) transcription.
2. **Signal 2 (costimulation)**: **CD28 binds CD80/86 (B7)**. Signal 1 without signal 2 causes **anergy**. CTLA-4 (cytotoxic T-lymphocyte antigen-4) is the natural brake.
3. **Signal 3**: IL-2 binds **CD25** (IL-2 receptor) → **mTOR (mammalian target of rapamycin)** → cell cycle G1 to S → clonal proliferation, which needs de novo purine synthesis.

#### Effector mechanisms
- **Cellular**: CD4 Th1 cells (IL-2, IFN-γ (interferon-γ)) recruit macrophages; CD8 cytotoxic T cells kill by perforin and granzyme → **tubulitis, endarteritis**.
- **Humoral**: B cells with T-cell help become plasma cells → **DSA (donor-specific antibody)** binds endothelium → complement, leaving (leaves) **C4d** in (on) peritubular capillaries → **microvascular inflammation** (glomerulitis, capillaritis). NK (natural killer) cell injury is complement-independent, so AMR (antibody-mediated rejection) may be C4d-negative.
- **Regulation**: **Tregs** (regulatory T cells, FoxP3+) suppress responses; the basis of tolerance research.

#### Types of rejection (brief; detail in 2020-Summer Paper 1, Q3d; context for drug choice)
| Type | Timing | Mechanism and features |
|---|---|---|
| **Hyperacute** | Minutes–hours | Preformed ABO/HLA antibody → complement, thrombosis; graft turns blue; **prevented by crossmatch** |
| Accelerated acute | 2–5 days | Memory (anamnestic) response in sensitised recipients |
| **Acute TCMR** | Days–months (mostly first 6 months) | Interstitial infiltrate, tubulitis, arteritis |
| **Acute AMR** | Any time | DSA, C4d, microvascular inflammation |
| **Chronic active AMR** | Months–years | **Transplant glomerulopathy** (GBM (glomerular basement membrane) double contours), arterial intimal fibrosis; commonest cause of late graft loss |

- **Also know:** **subclinical** rejection (histology, stable function). **Risk factors**: HLA mismatch, DSA, ABO incompatibility, long ischaemia and DGF, **non-adherence**, retransplant.

#### Banff classification (biopsy is the gold standard; Banff grades biopsy findings and classifies TCMR and AMR)
| Category | Key criteria |
|---|---|
| Borderline | Foci of tubulitis with mild interstitial inflammation |
| TCMR IA / IB | Interstitial inflammation >25% with moderate (IA) or severe (IB) tubulitis |
| TCMR IIA / IIB | Mild-moderate (IIA) or severe (IIB) intimal arteritis |
| TCMR III | Transmural arteritis or fibrinoid necrosis |
| Active AMR | All three: (1) tissue injury (microvascular inflammation, arteritis, TMA (thrombotic microangiopathy)); (2) antibody–endothelium interaction (C4d, microvascular inflammation or gene transcripts); (3) **circulating DSA** |

#### Diagnosis and monitoring
- Rising creatinine; **de novo DSA** (donor-specific antibody) predicts graft loss; biopsy; **dd-cfDNA (donor-derived cell-free DNA)** (negative predictive value >90%) avoids unnecessary biopsy; gene-expression panels.
- **Exclude**: obstruction, leak, renal artery stenosis, CNI (calcineurin inhibitor) toxicity, BK virus nephropathy.

#### Treatment
- **TCMR**: IV (intravenous) pulse steroids; **rATG (rabbit antithymocyte globulin)** / ATG (antithymocyte globulin) for Banff II–III.
- **AMR** (not standardised): steroids, **IVIG (intravenous immunoglobulin)**, **plasmapheresis**, rituximab; eculizumab in selected patients.

### Part B: Marginal kidney donor

#### Categories
**1. ECD (expanded criteria donor), UNOS (United Network for Organ Sharing) 2002**: age **≥60**, or **50–59** with **≥2** of **hypertension**, **creatinine >1.5 mg/dL**, **death from CVA (cerebrovascular accident)**. About 70% higher risk of graft loss.

**2. KDPI (Kidney Donor Profile Index)**: percentile of a 10-factor risk index. **KDPI >85%** replaced ECD in the US (December 2014). Low-KDPI kidneys go to recipients with the longest EPTS (Estimated Post-Transplant Survival): **longevity matching**.

**3. DCD (donation after circulatory death): Maastricht**
| Category | Description | Type |
|---|---|---|
| I / II | Dead on arrival / failed resuscitation | Uncontrolled |
| III | Arrest after withdrawal of therapy | **Controlled (commonest)** |
| IV | Arrest in a brain-dead donor | Either |
| V | Euthanasia | Controlled |

- Warm ischaemia needs rapid cannulation and cooling, and DCD kidneys usually go on machine perfusion. DGF is **higher (roughly 40–50%)**, but long-term survival is similar to DBD (donation after brain death).

**4. Others**: **AKI (acute kidney injury)** donors; **HCV (hepatitis C virus)-viraemic** donors into negative recipients with DAAs (direct-acting antivirals); **paediatric donors <15 kg** (en bloc); older or hypertensive living donors (KDIGO (Kidney Disease: Improving Global Outcomes) 2017 risk-based approach).
- **Absolute exclusions**: metastatic or haematological cancer, melanoma, untreatable viral infection. Treated bacterial infection is acceptable; screen by serology and nucleic acid testing.

#### Assessment and optimisation
- **Remuzzi biopsy score** (glomeruli, tubules, interstitium, arteries, each 0–3): **0–3 single**, **4–6 dual kidney transplant**, **≥7 discard**.
- **Cold storage** at 4 °C in UW (University of Wisconsin) or HTK (histidine–tryptophan–ketoglutarate) solution slows metabolism.
- **HMP (hypothermic machine perfusion)** reduces DGF (**Moers, NEJM 2009**: about 21% vs 27%; greatest benefit in ECD kidneys); Campbell notes no long-term survival difference. Normothermic perfusion allows viability testing (limited experience).
- **ESP (Eurotransplant Senior Program)**: donors **≥65** to recipients **≥65**, prioritising short cold ischaemia over HLA (human leucocyte antigen) matching. Informed consent is essential.
- **Ojo (JASN 2001)**: ECD transplantation improves survival over dialysis, although graft survival is lower than with standard kidneys.
- With DGF, give antibody induction and delay nephrotoxic CNI (calcineurin inhibitor) therapy.

### Part C: Newer immunosuppressive drugs (newer immunosuppressants)

#### Standard immunosuppression (current practice)
- **Three-signal model**: signal 1 is antigen (TCR (T-cell receptor) meeting MHC (major histocompatibility complex)); signal 2 is costimulation (CD28–CD80/86); signal 3 is IL-2 receptor signalling through mTOR, driving proliferation.
- **Induction** (>90%; over 90% receive induction) plus **tacrolimus + mycophenolate** ± steroids (mycophenolate: GI (gastrointestinal) upset, teratogenic). Acute rejection is below 10%; chronic **AMR (antibody-mediated rejection)** is now the main problem. Newer drugs aim to avoid CNI nephrotoxicity, improve adherence and treat AMR.

| Drug | Mechanism (site) | Key points |
|---|---|---|
| **Belatacept** | **CTLA4-Ig**: binds CD80/86, blocks **CD28 costimulation (signal 2)** | FDA (Food and Drug Administration) 2011; IV monthly: IV 10 mg/kg induction, then **5 mg/kg 4-weekly**; CNI (calcineurin inhibitor)-free. **BENEFIT**: better GFR (glomerular filtration rate), more early acute rejection; 7-year better patient and graft survival. **Contraindicated if EBV (Epstein–Barr virus)-seronegative** (PTLD (post-transplant lymphoproliferative disorder)) |
| **Extended-release tacrolimus** | CNI: binds FKBP12 → inhibits calcineurin (**signal 1**), so less IL-2 transcription | Once daily, better adherence, steadier levels; nephrotoxicity, NODAT (new-onset diabetes after transplant), tremor |
| Mycophenolate | Inhibits IMPDH (inosine monophosphate dehydrogenase), blocking lymphocyte purine synthesis | Standard partner of tacrolimus |
| **mTOR inhibitors** (sirolimus, everolimus) | Bind FKBP12 → block mTOR (**signal 3**), arresting G1 to S | Not nephrotoxic; fewer CMV (cytomegalovirus)/BK infections; antitumour effect. **TRANSFORM (2018)**: everolimus + reduced (low-dose, low) CNI non-inferior. Poor wound healing, **lymphocele**, proteinuria, dyslipidaemia |
| **Basiliximab** | Anti-CD25 (IL-2 (interleukin-2) receptor), non-depleting (**signal 3**) | Low-risk induction, **20 mg IV (intravenous) days 0 and 4** |
| **rATG** (rabbit antithymocyte globulin); **alemtuzumab** | T-cell depletion; anti-CD52 | High-risk induction, DGF; infection (CMV), PTLD |
| **Rituximab; bortezomib** | Anti-CD20 (B cells); proteasome inhibitor (plasma cells) | AMR, desensitisation |
| **Eculizumab** | Anti-C5 (complement) | Selected AMR; aHUS (atypical haemolytic uraemic syndrome) recurrence |
| **Imlifidase** | Enzyme that **cleaves IgG** | **EMA (European Medicines Agency) 2020**: crossmatch-positive, highly sensitised patients |
| **Anti-IL-6 therapy** (tocilizumab, clazakizumab) | Blocks IL-6 pathway | Chronic active AMR |

- **Also know:** mycophenolate inhibits IMPDH (inosine monophosphate dehydrogenase), blocking lymphocyte purine synthesis; anti-CD40 costimulation blockade under trial.
- **Voclosporin** is approved for lupus nephritis, **not transplantation**.

### Recent advances
- **Tolerance induction** by mixed chimerism (combined kidney and donor stem-cell transplant) and Treg therapy; normothermic repair; **pig-kidney xenotransplant** into a living human (March 2024).

**Diagram to draw:** three-signal T-cell activation (APC–T-cell synapse showing signals 1–3) with drug sites (CNI at signal 1 / after signal 1, belatacept at 2, basiliximab and mTOR inhibitors at 3, mycophenolate at DNA synthesis).

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Direct pathway drives early TCMR; indirect pathway drives antibody (DSA) and chronic rejection.
> - Signal 1 calcineurin, signal 2 CD28–B7, signal 3 IL-2R–mTOR.
> - Banff AMR = tissue injury + antibody–endothelium interaction (C4d) + DSA.
> - Chronic active AMR (transplant glomerulopathy) is the main cause of late graft loss.
> - Belatacept blocks signal 2; better GFR, avoid (contraindicated) if EBV-negative; imlifidase (IgG cleavage) for highly sensitised patients; dd-cfDNA NPV >90%.
> - ECD: ≥60, or 50–59 with ≥2 of hypertension, creatinine >1.5, CVA death; KDPI >85% since 2014.
> - Remuzzi 0–3 single, 4–6 dual, ≥7 discard; HMP reduces DGF; ESP ≥65 to ≥65.

# Section 15 · Renal, Nephrology & Transplant — Short answer questions (SAQ)

## Brain-stem death: guidelines for declaration and the Board of Medical Experts (brain dead committee)
**Asked as:**
- Brain dead committee. — *Summer 2018 Paper IV Q3c (SAQ)*
- Guidelines for declaration of brain stem death. — *Summer 2022 Paper IV Q3b (SAQ)*

**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
(also answered in 2018-Summer Paper 4, Q3c)

*Assumed meaning: the Board of Medical Experts that certifies brain-stem death under Indian law.*

### Definition and legal basis
- **Brain-stem death:** irreversible loss of consciousness and of the capacity to breathe; equivalent to death of the person.
- **India:** **THOA (Transplantation of Human Organs Act) 1994** legalised brain-stem death in India; **amended 2011** (Transplantation of Human Organs and Tissues Act); **Rules 2014**.
- Internationally: Harvard criteria (1968), AAN (American Academy of Neurology) criteria (updated 2023).

### Composition of the Board of Medical Experts (India; Rules 2014)
| Member | Role |
|---|---|
| 1. Registered medical practitioner **in charge of the hospital** | Hospital authority |
| 2. **Independent specialist**, nominated by the in-charge from a panel approved by the Appropriate Authority | Independent check |
| 3. **Neurologist or neurosurgeon** from the approved panel (if unavailable: surgeon, physician, anaesthetist or intensivist) | Expert |
| 4. Medical practitioner **treating the patient** | Clinical history |

- **No member may belong to the transplant team.**

### Certification procedure
- **Preconditions:** known irreversible cause, apnoeic coma on (a) ventilator; exclude **sedatives, relaxants**, hypothermia, metabolic or endocrine causes, shock.

#### Tests: all reflexes absent
| Brainstem reflex (all absent) | Method | Cranial nerves |
|---|---|---|
| Pupillary | Fixed pupils, no light reaction | II, III |
| Corneal | Touch cornea: no blink | V, VII |
| Oculocephalic | Turn head: eyes move with head | VIII, III, VI |
| Oculovestibular | Ice-cold water in each ear: no eye movement | VIII, III, VI |
| Motor in cranial nerve area | Supraorbital pressure: no grimace | V, VII |
| Gag and cough | Pharynx (touch) and tracheal suction: no response | IX, X |

- **Apnoea test (last):** pre-oxygenate with 100% oxygen; disconnect ventilator with oxygen insufflation; **no breathing effort at PaCO₂ (arterial CO₂; arterial carbon dioxide tension) ≥60 mmHg** (or ≥20 mmHg above baseline). Abort if (for) hypotension, desaturation or arrhythmia.
- **Two sets of tests, at least 6 hours apart**, recorded on **Form 10**.
- **Ancillary tests** if examination is impossible (facial trauma): angiography, perfusion scan, EEG (electroencephalogram).

### Linked duties (after declaration)
- **Mandatory request:** ICU (intensive care unit) staff must inform relatives of the option to donate (mandatory request for donation by ICU staff); a **transplant coordinator** counsels; next of kin give consent.
- **NOTTO** (National Organ and Tissue Transplant Organisation), with regional and state bodies (ROTTO, SOTTO: regional and state organ and tissue transplant organisations), allocates organs and keeps the registry.

**Diagram to draw:** flowchart: coma → preconditions → test 1 → 6 h → test 2 → Form 10 → consent → NOTTO.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - 4-member (Four-member) Board; no transplant-team member.
> - Two tests ≥6 h apart; Form 10.
> - Apnoea test: no breathing at PaCO₂ ≥60 mmHg.

## Vascular access for dialysis.
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2020-Summer Paper 4, Q3b

### Introduction
- Haemodialysis needs blood flow of **250–350 mL/min**; access failure is a major cause of morbidity in ESKD (end-stage kidney disease).

### Types
| Access | Construction | Ready | Comment |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Radiocephalic AVF** (arteriovenous fistula; Brescia–Cimino, 1966) | Radial artery to cephalic vein at wrist (vein end to artery side) | 6–8 weeks | **First choice**: best patency, fewest infections; most fail to mature |
| Brachiocephalic AVF | Elbow | 6–8 weeks | Higher flow, more steal |
| **AVG** (arteriovenous graft) | PTFE (polytetrafluoroethylene) loop or straight | 2–3 weeks | More stenosis, thrombosis, infection |
| **Tunnelled cuffed CVC** (central venous catheter) | Right internal jugular | At once | Bridge or last resort; infection, central stenosis |

- Non-tunnelled 10–12 Fr jugular or femoral catheters: acute dialysis only.

### Planning
- **Hierarchy**: AVF → AVG → CVC; **distal before proximal; non-dominant arm first**. KDOQI (Kidney Disease Outcomes Quality Initiative) 2019: individual "ESKD Life-Plan".
- **Timing**: refer at eGFR (estimated glomerular filtration rate) <30; create AVF about **6 months** before dialysis.
- **Vein preservation**: no venepuncture, cannula or PICC (peripherally inserted central catheter) in that arm; avoid subclavian lines.
- **Assessment**: pulses, **Allen test** (ulnar collateral flow), **Doppler vein mapping** (vessels about ≥2 mm).
- **Maturity "rule of 6s"**: flow >600 mL/min, diameter >6 mm, depth <6 mm, at 6 weeks.

### Complications
| Complication | Treatment |
|---|---|
| Failure to mature (juxta-anastomotic stenosis) | Angioplasty, ligate side branches |
| Stenosis (neointimal hyperplasia) | PTA (percutaneous transluminal angioplasty), stent graft |
| **Thrombosis** (usually on a stenosis) | Thrombectomy, then fix stenosis |
| **Steal syndrome** (hand ischaemia) | Banding, DRIL (distal revascularisation–interval ligation) |
| Aneurysm; infection (CVC > AVG > AVF) | Repair; antibiotics, excise graft |

- **Also know:** brachiobasilic transposition AVF; venous hypertension; high-output cardiac failure.

### Monitoring
- Thrill and bruit, arm-elevation test, prolonged bleeding after needling, rising venous pressure; duplex when abnormal.

### Recent advances
- **Endovascular AVF** (WavelinQ, Ellipsys); drug-coated balloons; bioengineered vessels.

**Diagram to draw:** radiocephalic end-to-side fistula at the wrist.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - AVF first, distal, non-dominant, 6 months ahead.
> - Rule of 6s: >600 mL/min, >6 mm, <6 mm deep, 6 weeks.
> - Thrombosis sits on stenosis; steal → banding or DRIL.

## Female kidney donors: special precautions
**Asked as:**
- Female kidney donors: enumerate special precautions — *Winter 2023 Paper IV Q3d (SAQ)*
- Female kidney donors: enumerate special precautions — *Practice Set 4 Paper IV Q3a (earlier version) (SAQ)*

**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
(also answered in 2023-Winter Paper 4, Q3d)

### Introduction
- Most living donors, especially in India, are **women** (wives, mothers). Guidance: **KDIGO (Kidney Disease: Improving Global Outcomes) 2017** living donor guideline.

### Before donation
1. **Free, uncoerced consent:** interview the donor alone; independent donor advocate; family pressure and gender inequity screened. In India the THOA (Transplantation of Human Organs Act) authorisation committee verifies this.
2. **Pregnancy:** pregnancy test before contrast CT (computed tomography) and surgery; **reliable contraception** during work-up. Pregnant women do not donate.
3. **Obstetric history:** previous **pre-eclampsia, gestational hypertension or gestational diabetes** signals higher future hypertension, diabetes and kidney risk; evaluate and counsel.
4. **Future pregnancy counselling (KDIGO):** donation **increases gestational hypertension and pre-eclampsia** (Garg et al., NEJM 2015: about 11% vs 5% in non-donors). Maternal and fetal outcomes are otherwise similar (Chapter 91).
5. **Oestrogen therapy:** combined OCPs (oral contraceptive pills) and HRT (hormone replacement therapy) raise **VTE (venous thromboembolism)** risk; stop them some weeks before surgery and give barrier or progestogen-only contraception; VTE prophylaxis.
6. **Gynaecological and cancer screening:** cervical cytology, breast examination or mammography (age-appropriate); pelvic ultrasound if indicated.
7. **Urine tests:** repeat urinalysis **outside menstruation** to avoid false haematuria; exclude recurrent UTI (urinary tract infection).
8. **GFR (glomerular filtration rate):** low muscle mass makes creatinine-based estimates unreliable; **measured GFR** preferred. Note nephron mismatch with a large recipient.
9. Correct **iron-deficiency anaemia**.

### Operative
- Laparoscopic donor nephrectomy; **Pfannenstiel** extraction for cosmesis; transvaginal extraction possible.

### After donation
- **Defer pregnancy** until recovery and stable function; many programmes advise at least 6–12 months (another source: often 6–12 months).
- In pregnancy: inform obstetrician; **high-risk antenatal care**; monitor BP (blood pressure), proteinuria and creatinine; low-dose aspirin may be considered.
- **Lifelong annual BP, creatinine and proteinuria**; avoid NSAIDs (non-steroidal anti-inflammatory drugs).
