**Diagram to draw:** flowchart: consent → pregnancy test → hormone review → GFR → surgery → pregnancy follow-up.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Ensure free consent (Indian gender gap).
> - Pregnancy test; contraception; stop oestrogens.
> - Donors: gestational hypertension or pre-eclampsia about 11% vs 5%.
> - Defer pregnancy; high-risk antenatal follow-up.

## Hospital authorization committee for live related kidney donation.
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2023-Winter Paper 4, Q3e

*Indian law; not covered in the chapter notes.*

### Legal framework
- **THOA (Transplantation of Human Organs Act) 1994**; amended 2011 as THOTA (Transplantation of Human Organs and Tissues Act); **Rules 2014**.
- Purpose: **prevent organ trade** while allowing genuine donation.

### Donor categories
| Donor | Approval |
|---|---|
| **Near relative:** spouse, son, daughter, father, mother, brother, sister, grandparents, grandchildren | Verified by the hospital's competent authority |
| **Other than near relative** (affection or attachment) | **Authorisation Committee approval mandatory** |
| **Swap (paired) donation** between near-relative pairs (2011) | Authorisation Committee |
| **Foreign donor or recipient** | Authorisation Committee; embassy certifies relationship |

### Constitution
- **Hospital-based** if a centre does more than **25 transplants a year**; otherwise **district or state committee**.
- **Members:** medical director or superintendent (chair); two senior doctors of the hospital **not in the transplant team**; two persons of high integrity and social standing (preferably one woman), nominated by the State; state committees add health-department nominees.

### Functions and procedure
1. Receive the **joint donor–recipient application** with consent forms, fitness certificates and identity and relationship documents.
2. **Personal interview** of donor, recipient and families; proceedings **video-recorded**.
3. Verify **relationship** (documents; **HLA [human leukocyte antigen] or DNA testing** if doubtful).
4. Exclude **commercial dealing**: financial status, reasons, long association, middlemen.
5. Confirm donor understands risks and consents freely, without coercion.
6. Check **domicile** (other-state donors).
7. Decide promptly; decision recorded and displayed; reasons given if refused. **Appeal** to the State Government within **30 days**.

### Restrictions
- **Minors and mentally ill people** not accepted as living donors except in exceptional, approved cases.
- **Foreign recipient with unrelated Indian donor** not approved.
- **Penalty** for commercial dealing: imprisonment up to 10 years and fine up to ₹1 crore; doctors' registration removed.

**Diagram to draw:** flowchart: application → near relative (competent authority) or other (committee) → interview, verify → decision → appeal.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - THOA 1994; amended 2011; Rules 2014.
> - Hospital-based committee if >25 transplants a year; no transplant team member.
> - Non-relatives, swap and foreigners need committee approval.
> - Interview video-recorded; appeal within 30 days.

## Marginal donor and Remuzzi score for pre-implantation biopsy
**Asked as:**
- Define Marginal donor. Mention components of Remuzzi scoring system for preemptive renal biopsy of marginal donor kidneys. — *Winter 2024 Paper IV Q3e (SAQ)*
- Define Marginal donor. Mention components of Remuzzi scoring system for preemptive renal biopsy of marginal donor kidneys. — *Practice Set 4 Paper IV Q3b (earlier version) (SAQ)*

**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
(also answered in 2024-Winter Paper 4, Q3e; 2016-Summer Paper 3, Q3f)

*"Preemptive" is read as pre-implantation (procurement) biopsy.*

### Definition
- A **marginal (extended) donor** is one whose kidney carries a **higher risk of DGF (delayed graft function) and shorter graft survival** than a standard donor. Such kidneys are used because of organ shortage.
- Still better survival than dialysis (Ojo, 2001).

### Categories
- **ECD (expanded criteria donor), UNOS (United Network for Organ Sharing):**
  - Age **60 or over**, or
  - Age **50–59 with 2 or more of:** hypertension, **creatinine >1.5 mg/dL**, death from **CVA (cerebrovascular accident)**.
  - Graft-loss risk about 1.7 times that of a standard donor.
- **KDPI (Kidney Donor Profile Index) >85%** replaced ECD in the US (2014); 10 donor factors (age, height, weight, ethnicity, hypertension, diabetes, cause of death, creatinine, HCV [hepatitis C virus], DCD).
- **Others:** DCD (donation after circulatory death); donor AKI (acute kidney injury); HCV-positive donors; older or hypertensive living donors.

### Remuzzi score (Karpinski/Remuzzi)
- Pre-implantation biopsy (ideally at least 25 glomeruli), read on rapid sections.
- **Four components, each 0–3: total 0–12.**

| Component | 0 | 1 | 2 | 3 |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Glomerular global sclerosis** | None | <20% | 20–50% | >50% |
| **Tubular atrophy** | None | <20% | 20–50% | >50% |
| **Interstitial fibrosis** | None | <20% | 20–50% | >50% |
| **Arterial and arteriolar narrowing** | None | Wall thickness < lumen diameter | Wall = or slightly > lumen | Wall far > lumen, severe narrowing or occlusion |

### Allocation by score
| Total | Grade | Use |
|---|---|---|
| **0–3** | Mild | **Single** kidney transplant |
| **4–6** | Moderate | **Dual** kidney transplant |
| **7–12** | Severe | **Discard** |

- Remuzzi (NEJM 2006): biopsy-guided older-donor kidneys, single or dual, had graft survival similar to young-donor kidneys.
- **Limitations:** sampling error; wedge biopsy overestimates sclerosis; interobserver variation.

### Optimising marginal kidneys
- Short cold ischaemia; **HMP (hypothermic machine perfusion)** reduces DGF; old-for-old matching; informed consent.

**Diagram to draw:** Remuzzi grid with four compartments scored 0–3.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - ECD: ≥60, or 50–59 with 2 of hypertension, creatinine >1.5, CVA death.
> - Remuzzi: glomeruli, tubules, interstitium, vessels; 0–3 each.
> - 0–3 single, 4–6 dual, ≥7 discard.

## Laparoscopic donor nephrectomy (including pros and cons)
**Asked as:**
- Laparoscopic donor nephrectomy — *Practice Set 2 Paper IV Q3a (SAQ)*
- Pros and cons of lap donor nephrectomy — *Practice Set 2 Paper IV Q3d (SAQ)*

**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
(living donation also in 2023-Winter Paper 1, Q3c; the pros and cons are asked again in Practice Set 2 Paper 4, Q3d)
**See Practice Set 2 Paper 4, Q3a (full answer).**

### Introduction
- **Ratner (1995)**; now the preferred approach (open under 2% in the United States). **Donor safety comes first.**
- Work-up includes measured GFR (glomerular filtration rate) and CT (computed tomography) angiography.

### Side selection
- **Left preferred:** longer renal vein, simpler dissection.
- **Right** if the left has multiple arteries or complex veins, or the right is smaller or abnormal: the donor keeps the better kidney. The right vein is short.

### Techniques
| Technique | Features |
|---|---|
| Pure transperitoneal laparoscopic | 3–4 ports; colon reflected; Pfannenstiel extraction |
| **Hand-assisted** (about two-thirds of US cases) | Hand port is also the extraction site; tactile feedback, quick haemostasis |
| Retroperitoneoscopic | Balloon-made space; no bowel mobilisation; direct hilum; less room |
| Robotic; LESS (laparoendoscopic single-site surgery) | Wristed dissection; single umbilical incision; cost |

### Key steps
1. Reflect colon; divide gonadal, adrenal and lumbar veins (left).
2. Keep periureteric tissue and the gonadal–ureter triangle (ureteric blood supply).
3. Hydration and diuretic before vessel division.
4. Divide ureter, artery, then vein (stapler; see Q3f on Hem-o-lok).
5. Extract quickly; cold flush.

### Warm ischaemia
- Arterial occlusion to cold flush: a few minutes, slightly longer than open, without effect on long-term graft function.

### Pros and cons (versus open)
| Pros | Cons |
|---|---|
| Less pain, shorter stay, faster (earlier) return to work | Longer operation; learning curve |
| Better cosmesis; **more people volunteer to donate** (more willing donors) | Slightly longer warm ischaemia |
| Magnified view of hilum | Shorter vessels, especially right vein (on the right) |
| Equal graft function and survival (graft function equals open) | Pneumoperitoneum lowers renal blood flow; cost |

- **Risks (safety):** complications about 16% (mostly gastrointestinal); mortality **3 per 10,000**; conversion 0.26–3.34%. Lifetime ESRD (end-stage renal disease) 90 per 10,000 versus 14 in matched non-donors.

**Diagram to draw:** left hilum with gonadal, adrenal and lumbar veins and the stapled artery and vein.

> **Key points to remember (including pros and cons angle):**
> - Ratner 1995; left kidney preferred (longer vein).
> - Hand-assisted in about two-thirds of US cases.
> - Pros: less pain, shorter stay, earlier return to work, better cosmesis, and more willing donors; graft function equals open.
> - Cons: longer operation, learning curve, slightly longer warm ischaemia, shorter vessels (especially on the right), cost.
> - Safety: mortality 3 per 10,000; conversion 0.26–3.34%.
> - Never rely on Hem-o-lok alone on the donor renal artery (do not use Hem-o-lok clips alone).

## Steroid-free immunosuppression in renal transplantation
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 3 Paper 4, Q3f
(immunosuppression also in Practice Set 3 Paper 4, Q1 and 2020-Summer Paper 4, Q1)

### Why avoid steroids
- Steroids block cytokine transcription (IL-1, IL-2 [interleukins]) and redistribute lymphocytes, but long-term use harms:
  - **Metabolic:** PTDM (post-transplant diabetes mellitus), hypertension, dyslipidaemia, weight gain, hence **cardiovascular death** (leading cause of death with a functioning graft).
  - **Bone:** osteoporosis, avascular necrosis; **growth failure** in children.
  - Cataract, infection, poor healing.

### Strategies
| Strategy | Timing |
|---|---|
| **Steroid avoidance** | No steroids, or perioperative doses only |
| **Early (rapid) withdrawal** | Stopped within the first week; most studied |
| **Late withdrawal** | Months later; more rejection, less favoured |

### Regimen
- **Induction is essential:** depleting rATG (rabbit antithymocyte globulin) or alemtuzumab; basiliximab (IL-2 receptor blocker) in low risk.
- **Maintenance:** tacrolimus at target levels plus MMF (mycophenolate mofetil).

### Selection
- **Suitable:** low immunological risk: first transplant, non-sensitised, no DSA (donor-specific antibody), prompt graft function, good adherence.
- **Unsuitable:** highly sensitised, DSA, retransplant after immunological loss, DGF (delayed graft function), early rejection, **recurring steroid-responsive disease** (IgA nephropathy, lupus), steroids needed for other illness.

### Evidence
- **Woodle et al. (2008):** withdrawal after day 7 versus low-dose maintenance: similar 5-year outcomes; **more mild, steroid-sensitive rejection**.
- **HARMONY (Lancet 2016):** steroids stopped on day 8: similar rejection and graft survival, **less PTDM**.
- **Cochrane review (2016):** more acute rejection, but no difference in patient or graft survival up to 5 years.
- **KDIGO (Kidney Disease: Improving Global Outcomes, 2009):** low-risk recipients with induction may stop steroids within the first week; if continued beyond it, continue rather than withdraw.
- **Practice:** over two-thirds still take maintenance steroids (Chapter 91); steroid-free maintenance increasing in low-risk children.

### Risks and safeguards
- Early rejection; possibly more chronic injury and recurrence. Safeguards: tacrolimus levels, adherence, DSA monitoring, biopsy for rising creatinine.

**Diagram to draw:** timeline: induction, steroids stopped by day 7, tacrolimus–MMF maintenance.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Avoidance, early (first week) or late withdrawal.
> - Needs induction plus tacrolimus–MMF; low-risk recipients only.
> - More mild rejection, same survival; less PTDM (HARMONY).
> - KDIGO 2009: stop within week 1, or continue.

## Artificial kidney
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 6 Paper 4, Q3e

*Read as devices that replace kidney function: the dialyser (classic "artificial kidney") and newer wearable, implantable and bioartificial kidneys.*

### Definition
- An **artificial kidney** removes solutes and water from blood across a semipermeable membrane. The haemodialysis dialyser is the standard example.
- Pioneered by **Kolff (1943)**; long-term use followed the Scribner shunt (1960) and Brescia–Cimino AV (arteriovenous) fistula (1966).

### Principles
| Process | Mechanism | Removes |
|---|---|---|
| **Diffusion** | Concentration gradient across membrane | Small solutes (urea, potassium) |
| **Convection** (haemofiltration) | Solute dragged with water | Middle molecules |
| **Ultrafiltration** | Hydrostatic pressure gradient | Water |
| Adsorption | Binding to membrane or sorbent | Some proteins, toxins |

- **Hollow-fibre dialyser**; blood and dialysate flow **countercurrent** to keep the gradient.
- **Schedule:** blood flow 250–350 mL/min, 3–5 hours, 3–5 times a week; adequacy by Kt/V (urea clearance × time ÷ volume), at least 1.2 per session.
- **Peritoneal dialysis** uses the peritoneum as membrane (glucose or icodextrin draws water).

### Limitations of current dialysis
- Intermittent: fluid and solute swings cause cramps.
- No endocrine (erythropoietin, vitamin D activation) or tubular metabolic function.
- Access thrombosis, infection, steal; cardiovascular disease, vascular calcification.
- **Transplantation remains the best renal replacement therapy.**

### Newer artificial kidneys
| Device | Principle | Status |
|---|---|---|
| **WAK (wearable artificial kidney)** | Belt-worn; sorbent regenerates a small volume of dialysate | Early human trials |
| Wearable or portable peritoneal dialysis | Continuous sorbent-regenerated peritoneal fluid | Development |
| **Implantable bioartificial kidney** | Silicon nanopore haemofilter plus bioreactor of renal tubule cells; driven by blood pressure, no pump | Preclinical |
| **RAD (renal assist device)** | Human proximal tubule cells in a hollow-fibre cartridge with continuous dialysis | Phase II in acute kidney injury; not adopted |
| Decellularised or organoid kidneys; pig kidney xenotransplant | Regenerative and xenograft routes | Experimental (see Practice Set 6 Paper 4, Q2) |

### Urologist's role
- AV fistula and graft work-up, laparoscopic PD (peritoneal dialysis) catheter placement with omental fixation, transplant.

**Diagram to draw:** hollow-fibre dialyser with countercurrent blood and dialysate flow.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Diffusion, convection, ultrafiltration across a semipermeable membrane.
> - Dialysis lacks endocrine and tubular function; transplant is best.
> - Wearable, implantable bioartificial and RAD devices are investigational.

## Robotic kidney transplantation
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 6 Paper 4, Q3f

### Introduction
- **RAKT (robot-assisted kidney transplantation)**: the graft is implanted intracorporeally on the iliac vessels using the robot.
- Milestones: Hoznek (2002, robot-assisted anastomosis); Giulianotti (2010, obese recipient); **Menon and Ahlawat (Vattikuti–Medanta, 2014)** standardised the transperitoneal technique with **regional hypothermia**, assessed by the IDEAL (idea, development, exploration, assessment, long-term study) framework.

### Indications and selection
- Living-donor grafts mainly; increasingly deceased donors.
- **Obese recipients** gain most, as open transplant carries more wound complications.
- **Contraindications (relative):** heavy iliac calcification on non-contrast CT (computed tomography), multiple graft vessels early in experience, previous transplant on that side, dense adhesions.

### Technique (Vattikuti–Medanta)
1. Lithotomy, steep Trendelenburg; transperitoneal ports.
2. Periumbilical **GelPOINT** incision for graft and ice insertion.
3. Peritoneal flap; expose the **external iliac vessels**.
4. Graft wrapped in gauze with ice slush; **ice slush in the iliac fossa** for regional hypothermia.
5. **Vein first:** renal vein end-to-side to external iliac vein, to shorten arterial ischaemia.
6. Renal artery end-to-side to external iliac artery; unclamp and reperfuse.
7. Extravesical **Lich–Gregoir** ureteroneocystostomy over a double-J stent.
8. Graft retroperitonealised with the peritoneal flap to prevent torsion.

### Advantages
- Small incision: **fewer wound infections, less lymphocele**, less pain, faster recovery, better cosmesis.
- Magnified 3D (three-dimensional) view and precise suturing in deep pelvis or obese patients.

### Disadvantages
- **Longer rewarming (anastomosis) time**; cost; learning curve; pneumoperitoneum and Trendelenburg effects.
- No tactile feedback for calcified vessels.

### Outcomes
- Multicentre and ERUS (European Association of Urology Robotic Urology Section) series: graft function and survival comparable with open transplant, with lower surgical-site complications.

### Complications
- Graft thrombosis, haemorrhage, urine leak, ureteric stricture, lymphocele (less), port-site hernia; DGF (delayed graft function).

**Diagram to draw:** pelvis with graft on external iliac vessels and ureter to bladder dome; ice slush via GelPOINT.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Vattikuti–Medanta technique with regional hypothermia (2014).
> - Vein first, then artery, then Lich–Gregoir.
> - Best for obese recipients; graft function equals open.

## Rapid Revision Sheet – Section 15 · Renal, Nephrology & Transplant

*All the key points from this section in one place. Read this in the last 30 minutes before the exam.*

#### Allograft rejection, immunobiology of renal transplant, marginal kidney donor and newer immunosuppressant drugs (LAQ)

- Direct pathway drives early TCMR; indirect pathway drives antibody (DSA) and chronic rejection.
- Signal 1 calcineurin, signal 2 CD28–B7, signal 3 IL-2R–mTOR.
- Banff AMR = tissue injury + antibody–endothelium interaction (C4d) + DSA.
- Chronic active AMR (transplant glomerulopathy) is the main cause of late graft loss.
- Belatacept blocks signal 2; better GFR, avoid (contraindicated) if EBV-negative; imlifidase (IgG cleavage) for highly sensitised patients; dd-cfDNA NPV >90%.
- ECD: ≥60, or 50–59 with ≥2 of hypertension, creatinine >1.5, CVA death; KDPI >85% since 2014.
- Remuzzi 0–3 single, 4–6 dual, ≥7 discard; HMP reduces DGF; ESP ≥65 to ≥65.

#### Brain-stem death: guidelines for declaration and the Board of Medical Experts (brain dead committee) (SAQ)

- 4-member (Four-member) Board; no transplant-team member.
- Two tests ≥6 h apart; Form 10.
- Apnoea test: no breathing at PaCO₂ ≥60 mmHg.

#### Vascular access for dialysis. (SAQ)

- AVF first, distal, non-dominant, 6 months ahead.
- Rule of 6s: >600 mL/min, >6 mm, <6 mm deep, 6 weeks.
- Thrombosis sits on stenosis; steal → banding or DRIL.

#### Female kidney donors: special precautions (SAQ)

- Ensure free consent (Indian gender gap).
- Pregnancy test; contraception; stop oestrogens.
- Donors: gestational hypertension or pre-eclampsia about 11% vs 5%.
- Defer pregnancy; high-risk antenatal follow-up.

#### Hospital authorization committee for live related kidney donation. (SAQ)

- THOA 1994; amended 2011; Rules 2014.
- Hospital-based committee if >25 transplants a year; no transplant team member.
- Non-relatives, swap and foreigners need committee approval.
- Interview video-recorded; appeal within 30 days.

#### Marginal donor and Remuzzi score for pre-implantation biopsy (SAQ)

- ECD: ≥60, or 50–59 with 2 of hypertension, creatinine >1.5, CVA death.
- Remuzzi: glomeruli, tubules, interstitium, vessels; 0–3 each.
- 0–3 single, 4–6 dual, ≥7 discard.

#### Laparoscopic donor nephrectomy (including pros and cons) (SAQ)

- Ratner 1995; left kidney preferred (longer vein).
- Hand-assisted in about two-thirds of US cases.
- Pros: less pain, shorter stay, earlier return to work, better cosmesis, and more willing donors; graft function equals open.
- Cons: longer operation, learning curve, slightly longer warm ischaemia, shorter vessels (especially on the right), cost.
- Safety: mortality 3 per 10,000; conversion 0.26–3.34%.
- Never rely on Hem-o-lok alone on the donor renal artery (do not use Hem-o-lok clips alone).

#### Steroid-free immunosuppression in renal transplantation (SAQ)

- Avoidance, early (first week) or late withdrawal.
- Needs induction plus tacrolimus–MMF; low-risk recipients only.
- More mild rejection, same survival; less PTDM (HARMONY).
- KDIGO 2009: stop within week 1, or continue.

#### Artificial kidney (SAQ)

- Diffusion, convection, ultrafiltration across a semipermeable membrane.
- Dialysis lacks endocrine and tubular function; transplant is best.
- Wearable, implantable bioartificial and RAD devices are investigational.

#### Robotic kidney transplantation (SAQ)

- Vattikuti–Medanta technique with regional hypothermia (2014).
- Vein first, then artery, then Lich–Gregoir.
- Best for obese recipients; graft function equals open.

# Section 16 · BPH — Long answer questions (LAQ)

## HoLEP (holmium laser enucleation of the prostate)
**Asked as:**
- Holep — *Winter 2023 Paper IV Q2 (LAQ)*
- HOLEP. — *Summer 2020 Paper IV Q3d (SAQ)*

**Type:** LAQ (25 marks); also asked as SAQ (10 marks)  
(also answered in 2019-Summer Paper 1, Q3c and 2020-Summer Paper 4, Q3d)

*Read as HoLEP (holmium laser enucleation of the prostate) for BPH (benign prostatic hyperplasia).*

### Introduction and principle
- **HoLEP** removes the whole transition-zone adenoma endoscopically **along the surgical capsule**. It is the endoscopic equivalent of open simple prostatectomy.
- **Evolution:** HoLAP (holmium laser ablation, 1994, slow) → HoLRP (resection, strips removed with a grasper) → **HoLEP (1998)**, made practical by the **tissue morcellator**.
- "Enucleation is enucleation": any energy giving a true **AEEP (anatomic endoscopic enucleation of the prostate)** gives durable results.

### Laser physics
- **LASER:** light amplification by stimulated emission of radiation. Ho:YAG (holmium:yttrium–aluminium–garnet) = holmium atoms in a YAG (yttrium–aluminium–garnet) crystal.
- **Wavelength about 2120 nm**; chromophore **water** (absorbed by water); **pulsed**; optical penetration **0.4 mm**. It cuts by vaporisation with a thin coagulation zone; heat spreads 2–3 mm and seals (coagulates) small and medium vessels.
- Flexible quartz fibres; **normal saline** irrigation, so no TUR (transurethral resection) syndrome.

| Laser | Wavelength | Chromophore | Penetration |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Holmium:YAG** | 2120 nm | Water | 0.4 mm |
| Thulium fibre | 1940 nm | Water | 0.2 mm |
| KTP (potassium-titanyl-phosphate, green light) | 532 nm | Haemoglobin | 0.8 mm |
| Nd:YAG | 1064 nm | – | About 1 cm (sloughing) |

### MOSES (pulse modulation)
- **Moses effect:** the first part of each pulse boils a **vapour channel** in the water. Vapour absorbs the light far less, so the rest of the pulse passes through to the tissue ("parting the water").
- **Moses technology** splits each pulse to exploit this: more energy reaches tissue, and it **may shorten operating time**.

### Indications
- **Size-independent** (<30, 30–80, >80 mL), with or without a median lobe (AUA [American Urological Association] Statement 38). Only enucleation spans every size band.
- **High bleeding risk or anticoagulated men** (AUA recommendation, Statement 43).
- Large glands otherwise needing open prostatectomy (alternative to open prostatectomy for large glands); retention; failed medical therapy.

### Technique (steps)
- **Equipment:** 60–150 W Ho:YAG laser; **550 µm end-firing fibre**; 24–28 Fr continuous-flow laser resectoscope with 30° lens; saline.
- **Settings:** 2.0 J × 40–50 Hz to enucleate; 1.0–2.0 J × 20 Hz to coagulate.
- **Three-lobe (bottom-up, early apical release):**
  1. Median lobe: **bladder-neck incisions at 5 and 7 o'clock** to the capsule, joined proximal to the verumontanum; median lobe enucleated into the bladder. No median lobe: one 6 o'clock incision.
  2. **Perimontanal inverted-U incision**; scope pushes laterally to open the lobe–capsule plane at the apex; **early apical release** to protect the sphincter.
  3. Plane carried (develop the capsular plane) anteriorly to 11–1 o'clock (to 12 o'clock); **apical mucosal strip incised early** to avoid sphincter traction.
  4. Anterior commissure divided; lateral lobes enucleated into the bladder (**en bloc, 2-lobe or 3-lobe**).
  5. **Haemostasis** with a defocused beam.
- **Two-lobe:** 6 and 12 o'clock grooves; each half enucleated with its share of median lobe.
- **En bloc:** whole adenoma in one piece after circumferential apical release; usually paired with early apical release.
- **Morcellation:** nephroscope, two open inflow lines, **full bladder** and good haemostasis. Morcellators: Piranha (rotating), Versacut (oscillating). Fragments sent for histology.
- **Catheter:** 22 Fr three-way; same-day or next-day removal.

### Outcomes and trials
- **Versus TURP (transurethral resection of the prostate):** RCT (randomised controlled trial) in glands <100 mL: better symptom score and PVR (post-void residual), less haemoglobin drop (less bleeding), shorter catheter time and stay, longer operation.
- **Meta-analyses:** better **IPSS (International Prostate Symptom Score) and Qmax (maximum flow rate)**, durable to (at) **3–8 years**. **HoLEP is the only endoscopic procedure with efficacy superior to TURP.**
- **Versus open prostatectomy** (Naspro 2006; Kuntz 2008, 5 years): equal function, less morbidity, less blood loss, shorter catheter time and stay.
- **Retreatment rare**: whole transition zone removed.
- **Anticoagulated men:** feasible; slightly longer stay; warfarin worse than DOACs (direct oral anticoagulants).
- **Guidelines:** EAU (European Association of Urology) and AUA list HoLEP as a first-line alternative to TURP and to open surgery for large glands.

### Complications
- **Intraoperative:** capsular perforation; ureteric orifice injury (glands >80 mL); **morcellator bladder injury** (stop and drain; intraperitoneal → repair); retained adenoma.
- **Early:** transient storage symptoms and dysuria; **transient (early) SUI (stress urinary incontinence)** from adenoma volume removed, falling over 6 months; persistent SUI rare.
- **Late:** **retrograde ejaculation about 75%**; BNC (bladder neck contracture) **0–3.2%** (small glands <40 mL, consider bladder-neck incision); meatal stricture; urethral stricture.
- Erectile function unchanged. Transfusion rare; conversion to TURP almost never.

### Learning curve (limitation and comparison)
- The main barrier (**steep learning curve**): from 20 supervised cases; **efficiency plateaus after about 50 cases**. Mentoring and simulators help.

### Recent advances
- High-power pulse modulation (**Moses pulse modulation**); **ThuLEP** (thulium laser enucleation) and bipolar enucleation.
- **Ejaculation-preserving and median-lobe-only HoLEP** (antegrade ejaculation about 90%).
- **Same-day discharge** and catheter removal.

**Diagram to draw:** sagittal prostate showing the enucleation plane along the surgical capsule, with 5 and 7 o'clock incisions.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Ho:YAG 2120 nm, water chromophore, 0.4 mm; saline, no TUR syndrome.
> - Size-independent; preferred (best) in anticoagulated men.
> - Only endoscopic method superior to TURP; retreatment rare.
> - Retrograde ejaculation about 75%; learning curve about 50 cases.

## Prostate anatomy, newer surgical concepts in CA prostate and neurovascular bundle preservation in radical prostatectomy
**Asked as:**
- Discuss in detail anatomy of prostate with special reference to newer concepts in surgical management of CA prostate — *Practice Set 2 Paper IV Q1 (LAQ)*
- Neurovascular bundle preservation in Radical prostatectomy: recent techniques — *Winter 2023 Paper IV Q3a (SAQ)*

**Type:** LAQ (25 marks); also asked as SAQ (10 marks)

(anatomy also answered in Practice Set 1 Paper 1, Q2; robotic prostatectomy in 2023-Winter Paper 1, Q3b)

### Introduction
- A 20-g fibromuscular gland around the proximal urethra, from bladder neck (**base**) to urogenital diaphragm (**apex**).
- Modern RP (radical prostatectomy) is anatomical: each new technique gives a clear margin while saving a named structure (nerves, sphincter or anterior support).

### Zonal anatomy (McNeal, 1981)
| Zone | Share of glands | Surgical relevance |
|---|---|---|
| Peripheral (PZ) | About 70% | **70% of cancers**; posterolateral, so ECE (extracapsular extension) runs along the nerves |
| Central (CZ) | About 25% | Cone around the ejaculatory ducts; few cancers |
| Transition (TZ) | About 5% | BPH (benign prostatic hyperplasia); anterior cancers missed by DRE (digital rectal examination) |
| AFMS (anterior fibromuscular stroma) | No glands | Anterior tumours reach it; margin risk in posterior approaches |
- There is **no true capsule**, so cancer breaching the gland is "extraprostatic extension".

### Fascial planes and nerve sparing (dissection planes)
| Plane | Lies (where) | Trade-off (use) |
|---|---|---|
| **Intrafascial** | Between capsule and prostatic fascia | Most nerves (maximal nerves); more positive margins |
| **Interfascial** | Between prostatic and levator fascia | **Standard** for organ-confined disease; **standard nerve-sparing plane** |
| **Extrafascial** | Outside levator and Denonvilliers fascia | Widest margin; ED (erectile dysfunction) likely |
- **Denonvilliers fascia** stays on the rectum when ECE risk is low; it is excised when posterolateral ECE is suspected.
- Chapter 160 (Walsh) calls intrafascial "never used"; Chapter 161 accepts it in selected men.

### Neurovascular bundle (NVB)
- **NVB (neurovascular bundle) of Walsh** (Walsh and Donker, 1982): capsular vessels plus **cavernous nerves** (parasympathetic, from the pelvic plexus). The pelvic plexus (S2–S4 parasympathetic plus hypogastric sympathetic) gives the **cavernous nerves**, running posterolaterally **3–5 mm from the capsule**, marked by capsular vessels. It lies posterolateral, in the lateral pelvic fascia between **prostatic and levator fascia**.
- Nerves are invisible; the **vessels mark them**.
- At the apex they lie at **5 and 7 o'clock**, swinging to 3 and 9 o'clock at the mid-urethra; most nerve injuries occur here.
- Nerve distribution is diffuse (varies): a main posterolateral bundle, a posterior "hammock" and anterolateral fibres (basis of high anterior release).
- **Injury mechanisms:** incision, entrapment (clip or suture), **heat** (**thermal**), **traction**; so dissect athermally without traction.

### Vessels and lymphatics
- Inferior vesical artery (urethral and capsular branches). Preserve **accessory pudendal arteries**; they may be the main penile supply.
- DVC (dorsal venous complex): CO₂ pneumoperitoneum tamponades it, so robotic blood loss is low.
- Lymph: obturator, external and internal iliac, common iliac, presacral.

### Continence anatomy
| Passive | Active |
|---|---|
| Smooth muscle of bladder neck and urethra; anterior suspensory system (endopelvic fascia, detrusor apron, puboprostatic ligaments, DVC) | Horseshoe striated sphincter at the apex; pudendal nerve branches enter it at the apex |

### Newer concepts in surgical management
1. **RARP (robot-assisted RP):** 3D, 10× view and wristed instruments removed the suturing barrier. **Yaxley 2016** RCT (randomised controlled trial): equal function and early oncology versus open; less blood loss (300 versus 1400 mL), shorter stay. Surgeon matters more than platform.
2. **Risk-graded nerve sparing (graded, risk-stratified):** each side is graded from full (intrafascial) through partial to none (for example the Tewari four-grade scale). The choice uses grade, positive cores and mpMRI (multiparametric magnetic resonance imaging) capsular contact; full, partial or none per side, from MRI (magnetic resonance imaging) and nomograms.
3. **NeuroSAFE:** intraoperative frozen section of each posterolateral surface (of the whole posterolateral margin); a positive section means resecting that NVB (if positive, that NVB is resected). It aims to allow more nerve sparing without more positive margins; the UK NeuroSAFE PROOF RCT (randomised controlled trial) tested its effect on erectile function.
4. **Anterior-support preservation:**
   - **Retzius-sparing** (Bocciardi, 2010): fully posterior via the pouch of Douglas. Earlier continence at 3 months, equal at 12; more positive margins, so avoid for anterior tumours.
   - **Hood technique** (Tewari 2021): anterior approach keeping detrusor apron, puboprostatic ligaments and DVC; early data.
   - Retzius-sparing and Hood are aimed at continence; potency equal at 12 months.
5. **Apical dissection:** the apex is the commonest positive-margin site. Keep maximal urethral length, watch for an apical lip, divide sharply without cautery; control the DVC distally or late. Posterior (Rocco) reconstruction aids early continence.
6. **PLND (pelvic lymph node dissection) templates:**

| Template | Nodes | Drainage captured |
|---|---|---|
| Limited | Obturator | — |
| Standard | + External iliac | About 38% |
| **Extended** | + Internal iliac | About 63% |
| + Common iliac | + Common iliac | About 75% |
- Extended template when nodal risk exceeds 5% (EAU [European Association of Urology]); it stages but does not improve survival. Lymphocele is commonest.
7. **Single-port robot (da Vinci SP, 2018):** one 2.5-cm cannula; extraperitoneal or transvesical; same-day discharge.
8. **Focal therapy anatomy:** ablate the **index lesion**. Transrectal HIFU (high-intensity focused ultrasound) suits posterior PZ lesions; anterior lesions need transperineal cryotherapy or TULSA (transurethral ultrasound ablation). Spare sphincter, NVB and rectum.
- **Also know:** PSMA PET (prostate-specific membrane antigen positron emission tomography) staging; salvage robotic RP; new platforms; ERAS (enhanced recovery after surgery).

### Nerve-sparing techniques (NVB preservation)
- **Walsh anatomic (open):** release apex to base from the rectal surface; small clips; **no thermal energy**.
- **High anterior release** (open) and **Veil of Aphrodite** (robotic): lateral prostatic fascia lifted high anteriorly as a "veil". Select by **Tsuzuki criteria**: ≥2 of PSA (prostate-specific antigen) >10 ng/mL, Gleason >6, abnormal DRE (digital rectal examination), perineural invasion, high core involvement → >10% risk, so avoid.
- **Antegrade release (robotic):** incise levator fascia, sweep NVB posterolaterally, clip pedicle at the "groove".
- **Athermal, traction-free:** clips or bulldogs, cold scissors.
- Graded nerve sparing, NeuroSAFE, Retzius-sparing and Hood: see above.

### Adjuncts
- **Penile rehabilitation:** PDE5i (phosphodiesterase type 5 inhibitor), then injections.
- **Also know:** sural nerve grafts and nerve stimulation tests (abandoned); amniotic membrane wraps, fluorescence nerve imaging (experimental).

**Diagram to draw:** axial prostate showing the three fasciae (fascial planes), the NVB (NVBs) at 5 and 7 o'clock and the intra-, inter- and extrafascial planes.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - PZ gives 70% of cancers; no true capsule.
> - Interfascial plane is the standard nerve-sparing plane; apex is the commonest margin and nerve-injury site.
> - Avoid heat and traction; clips, cold scissors.
> - Veil and high release for low-risk sides (Tsuzuki).
> - NeuroSAFE frozen section: more sparing, safe margins.
> - Retzius-sparing: earlier continence, more positive margins.
> - Extended PLND captures about 63% of drainage.

# Section 16 · BPH — Short answer questions (SAQ)

## Silodosin
**Asked as:**
- Silodosin — *Summer 2015 Paper IV Q3b (SAQ)*
- Silodosin. — *Practice Set 5 Paper IV Q3b (earlier version) (SAQ)*

**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)

### Introduction: definition and pharmacology
- **Uroselective α1-blocker**, the **most α1A-selective** blocker/agent (well over 100-fold vs α1B — another source: several hundredfold over α1B; about 50-fold vs α1D), for LUTS (lower urinary tract symptoms) due to BPH (benign prostatic hyperplasia).
- Sparing vascular α1B gives **minimal hypotension** (little α1B (vascular) blockade means few blood-pressure effects). Blocking α1A in the vas and seminal vesicles causes **failure of emission**.
- Onset: flow improves in (within) 2–6 hours (h), symptoms in (within) 3–4 days. CYP3A4 metabolism (metabolised by CYP3A4).

### Mechanism of action
- **α1A receptors** (about 70% of prostatic α1 receptors) dominate in the prostate, bladder neck and prostatic urethra. Noradrenaline on them causes the **dynamic component** of BOO (bladder outlet obstruction).
- Blockade relaxes this smooth muscle.

### Dose and contraindications
| Item | Detail |
|---|---|
| Dose | **8 mg once daily with a meal**; no titration |
| Renal: CrCl (creatinine clearance) 30–50 mL/min | **4 mg daily** |
| Contraindicated | CrCl <30; severe liver (hepatic) impairment; strong (potent) CYP3A4 inhibitors (e.g. ketoconazole) |
| Caution | Other α-blockers (avoid), PDE5 (phosphodiesterase 5) inhibitors, planned cataract surgery |

### Indications, efficacy and other uses
| Indication | Evidence |
|---|---|
| **LUTS (lower urinary tract symptoms) due to BPE (benign prostatic enlargement)** | Japanese, American and European 12-week RCTs (randomised controlled trials): IPSS (International Prostate Symptom Score) and flow better than placebo; **non-inferior to tamsulosin 0.4 mg** (**Chapple 2011**), not better; effective (works) in severe LUTS |
| **MET (medical expulsive therapy)** | Distal ureteric stones 5–10 mm; meta-analyses show higher, faster expulsion than tamsulosin; **SUSPEND (2015)** found no overall tamsulosin benefit |
| CP/CPPS (chronic prostatitis/chronic pelvic pain syndrome) | α-blocker-naive men with voiding symptoms; of recent onset |

- **Greatest fall in BOOI (bladder outlet obstruction index; BOO index)** of all α1-blockers (subgroup data).
- Does not shrink the prostate; add a 5-ARI (5α-reductase inhibitor) for large glands.
- **Also know:** before ureteroscopy (easier access); with propiverine for storage symptoms; nocturia (modest).

### Adverse effects
- **Ejaculatory dysfunction (signature) / anejaculation**: over a quarter of men in (registration) trials (about 14% vs 2% vs (with) tamsulosin head-to-head), yet few stop the drug; responders do better. Mechanism: **emission failure** (vas and seminal vesicle α1A blockade) and bladder-neck relaxation. Reversible. Counsel young men.
- **Cardiovascular:** dizziness; orthostatic hypotension rare; no QT change; no excess hypotension in the elderly or with antihypertensives (safe with antihypertensives).
- Falls in older men: falls and fractures rise in men ≥66 on any α-blocker.
- **IFIS (intraoperative floppy iris syndrome)**: reported; class effect; warn (tell the ophthalmologist) before cataract surgery (stopping the drug does not remove risk).

### Comparison
| Drug | Selectivity | Dose | Hypotension | Ejaculatory dysfunction (disorder) | Efficacy in LUTS |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Silodosin** | α1A (highest); α1A ≫ α1D, α1B | 8 mg OD | Lowest | **Highest** (more) | Equal |
| Tamsulosin | α1A, α1D > α1B (α1A and α1D) | 0.4 mg OD | Low | Moderate (less) | Equal |
| Alfuzosin | Clinically uroselective | 10 mg OD | Low | Lowest | |
| Naftopidil | α1D | 25–75 mg | Some | Less | |
| Doxazosin, terazosin | Non-selective; titrate | 1–8 mg, 1–10 mg | Highest | Low | |

**Diagram to draw:** α1A prostate and vas, α1B vessels, α1D bladder; prostate and bladder neck showing α1A receptor sites blocked by silodosin.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Most α1A-selective α-blocker; 8 mg with a meal (food); no titration.
> - 4 mg if CrCl 30–50 (halve to 4 mg at CrCl 30–50); avoid if (below) <30.
> - Equal efficacy to tamsulosin 0.4 mg; greatest BOOI fall; better for MET.
> - Anejaculation is the signature effect (more anejaculation); least hypotension.

## UroLift (prostatic urethral lift)
**Asked as:**
- Urolift. — *Winter 2024 Paper IV Q3d (SAQ)*
- Urolift. — *Practice Set 6 Paper IV Q3b (earlier version) (SAQ)*

**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
(also answered in 2024-Winter Paper 4, Q3d)

*Read as PUL (prostatic urethral lift, UroLift) for BPH (benign prostatic hyperplasia).*

### Introduction
- A **non-ablative MIST (minimally invasive surgical therapy)**. Permanent implants pull (retract) the lateral lobes apart and open the prostatic urethra.
- **FDA (Food and Drug Administration) approval 2013**; extended to ≤100 mL in 2020.
- **Main advantage:** preserves erectile and **ejaculatory** function.

### Implant and mechanism
- Spring-loaded implant with T-shaped ends:
  - **Capsular tab:** nitinol.
  - **Urethral end:** stainless steel (resists encrustation).
  - **Connector:** polyethylene terephthalate suture under tension.
- Delivered through a **19-gauge needle** from a cystoscopic device.
- The gland is more compliant than the capsule, so tension compresses the lobe outward; later local atrophy and fibrosis. Implants are MRI (magnetic resonance imaging)-conditional (≤3 T).

### Indications
- **AUA (American Urological Association): prostate 30–80 mL, no obstructing median lobe** (confirm by cystoscopy or ultrasound). EAU (European Association of Urology): <70 mL, no middle lobe, men wanting ejaculation preserved.
- Men wishing to stop medication.
- **Avoid:** obstructing median lobe (MedLift data non-randomised), large gland, active infection.

### Technique
1. Local anaesthesia; rigid cystoscope.
2. Compress lateral lobe, fire needle, deploy implant; repeat on the other side.
3. **Usually 4–6 implants**, anterolateral, from bladder neck to apex, avoiding the neurovascular bundles.
4. **Keep implants >1.5 cm distal to the bladder neck** (encrustation).
5. Most go home without a catheter.

### Evidence
| | **L.I.F.T. (2013)** | **BPH6 (2015)** |
|---|---|---|
| Design | PUL vs sham, 2:1, 19 centres | PUL vs TURP (transurethral resection of prostate), 10 European centres |
| Result | IPSS (International Prostate Symptom Score) about halved, twice the sham effect; Qmax (maximum flow) +4 mL/s; no new sexual dysfunction | Composite end point met 52.3% vs 20.0%; antegrade ejaculation 100% vs 60.6% |
| Durability / retreatment | 13.6% surgical retreatment at 5 years | 13.6% vs 5.7% at 2 years; TURP better IPSS, Qmax, PVR (post-void residual) |

- TURP wins on IPSS, Qmax and PVR (post-void residual).

### Complications
- Transient dysuria, haematuria, urgency (<2 weeks); encrustation; rare pelvic haematoma; device malfunction (misfire).
- Lower major adverse events but **higher retreatment than TURP**.

**Diagram to draw:** axial prostate with implants pulling lateral lobes to the capsule.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - 30–80 mL, no median lobe; local anaesthesia.
> - Ejaculation preserved; L.I.F.T. retreatment 13.6% at 5 years.
> - TURP wins (better) on IPSS, flow and durability.

## Green light lasers
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 2 Paper 4, Q3b
(also answered as KTP laser in Practice Set 1 Paper 4, Q3e; lasers in BPH in 2017-Winter Paper 2, Q3a)

### Physics
- A 1064 nm Nd:YAG (neodymium:yttrium-aluminium-garnet) beam is passed through a **KTP (potassium titanyl phosphate) or LBO (lithium triborate) crystal**. This doubles its frequency and halves the wavelength to **532 nm** (green).
- Green light is absorbed by **haemoglobin**, not water, so vascular prostate absorbs it avidly.
- Optical penetration **0.8 mm**, tissue effect 1–3 mm, thin (0.2 mm) coagulation rim. The result is bloodless **photoselective vaporisation**.

### Platforms
| System | Power | Note |
|---|---|---|
| KTP | 80 W (later 100 W) | Popularised PVP (photoselective vaporisation of the prostate) |
| LBO HPS (High Performance System) | 120 W | Faster vaporisation |
| LBO XPS | **180 W** | 750 µm MoXy fibre: metal tip, fluid cooling |

### Technique
- 23–24 Fr cystoscope; side-firing fibre emitting at **70°**; saline irrigation.
- Open the bladder neck first, away from the orifices; sweep 15–30° in 1–2 seconds at ≤2 mm.
- Large bubbles mean efficient vaporisation. Pulsatile bleeding: coagulation mode (about 30 W).
- More than 5 kJ/cc predicts complete vaporisation.

### Indications (AUA [American Urological Association])
- Prostates <30 cc and 30–80 cc; men at **higher bleeding risk**, who can continue anticoagulants. Not >80 cc (high retreatment).

### Evidence: GOLIATH trial
- RCT (randomised controlled trial), 29 European centres, about 270 men, prostate ≤100 cc: 180 W PVP versus TURP (transurethral resection of the prostate).
- PVP **non-inferior** for IPSS (International Prostate Symptom Score), Qmax and complications to 2 years. Shorter catheter time and stay; TURP faster. Retrograde ejaculation (about two-thirds) and reoperation equal.

### Complications
- Dysuria (highest of transurethral techniques); UTI (urinary tract infection) and epididymitis from coagulum; capsular perforation 0.2–1%.
- No TUR (transurethral resection) syndrome; transfusion very rare; **no tissue for histology**.

### Recent advances
- GreenLight enucleation; office and same-day catheter-free pathways.

**Diagram to draw:** side-firing fibre at 70° vaporising a lateral lobe.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - 532 nm, frequency-doubled Nd:YAG; haemoglobin chromophore; 0.8 mm penetration.
> - 180 W XPS with MoXy fibre.
> - GOLIATH: PVP non-inferior to TURP.
> - Best for anticoagulated men; not >80 cc.

## Rapid Revision Sheet – Section 16 · BPH

*All the key points from this section in one place. Read this in the last 30 minutes before the exam.*

#### HoLEP (holmium laser enucleation of the prostate) (LAQ)

- Ho:YAG 2120 nm, water chromophore, 0.4 mm; saline, no TUR syndrome.
- Size-independent; preferred (best) in anticoagulated men.
- Only endoscopic method superior to TURP; retreatment rare.
- Retrograde ejaculation about 75%; learning curve about 50 cases.

#### Prostate anatomy, newer surgical concepts in CA prostate and neurovascular bundle preservation in radical prostatectomy (LAQ)

- PZ gives 70% of cancers; no true capsule.
- Interfascial plane is the standard nerve-sparing plane; apex is the commonest margin and nerve-injury site.
- Avoid heat and traction; clips, cold scissors.
- Veil and high release for low-risk sides (Tsuzuki).
- NeuroSAFE frozen section: more sparing, safe margins.
- Retzius-sparing: earlier continence, more positive margins.
- Extended PLND captures about 63% of drainage.

#### Silodosin (SAQ)

- Most α1A-selective α-blocker; 8 mg with a meal (food); no titration.
- 4 mg if CrCl 30–50 (halve to 4 mg at CrCl 30–50); avoid if (below) <30.
- Equal efficacy to tamsulosin 0.4 mg; greatest BOOI fall; better for MET.
- Anejaculation is the signature effect (more anejaculation); least hypotension.

#### UroLift (prostatic urethral lift) (SAQ)

- 30–80 mL, no median lobe; local anaesthesia.
- Ejaculation preserved; L.I.F.T. retreatment 13.6% at 5 years.
- TURP wins (better) on IPSS, flow and durability.

#### Green light lasers (SAQ)

- 532 nm, frequency-doubled Nd:YAG; haemoglobin chromophore; 0.8 mm penetration.
- 180 W XPS with MoXy fibre.
- GOLIATH: PVP non-inferior to TURP.
- Best for anticoagulated men; not >80 cc.

# Section 17 · Prostate Cancer — Long answer questions (LAQ)

## Role and impact of Radiotherapy methods for management of focal and advanced carcinoma prostate?
**Type:** LAQ (25 marks)  
**Source:** 2021-Summer Paper 4, Q1
(also answered in 2017-Summer Paper 2, Q3f)

*"Focal" is taken to mean localised (organ-confined and locally advanced) disease.*

### Radiobiology
- RT (radiotherapy) causes DNA double-strand breaks. Prostate cancer has a **low α/β ratio (~1.5 Gy)**, lower than the rectum (~3 Gy). So larger fractions kill more tumour for the same late toxicity: the basis of **hypofractionation**.

### EBRT (external beam radiotherapy) techniques
| Step | Advance |
|---|---|
| 3D-CRT (three-dimensional conformal RT) | CT-planned beams shaped to the prostate |
| **IMRT (intensity-modulated RT)/VMAT (volumetric modulated arc therapy)** | Inverse planning, steep dose gradients; less rectal toxicity; now standard |
| **IGRT (image-guided RT)** | Daily fiducials or cone-beam CT (computed tomography); margins 3–7 mm |
| MRI fusion, PSMA (prostate-specific membrane antigen) PET (positron emission tomography) | Better targeting; PSMA PET changes plans in up to half of high-risk men |

| Schedule | Dose per fraction | Example | Evidence |
|---|---|---|---|
| Conventional | 1.8–2 Gy | 78 Gy in 39 | Dose escalation (RTOG 0126): better PSA control, no survival gain |
| **Moderate hypofractionation** | 2.25–4 Gy | **60 Gy in 20** | **CHHiP, PROFIT**: non-inferior |
| **SBRT (stereotactic body RT)** | ≥5 Gy | 36.25 Gy in 5 | **HYPO-RT-PC** non-inferior; **PACE-B** equal toxicity |

### Brachytherapy
| Feature | LDR (low dose rate) | HDR (high dose rate) |
|---|---|---|
| Source | Permanent ¹²⁵I (145 Gy) or ¹⁰³Pd seeds | Temporary ¹⁹²Ir via afterloader |
| Monotherapy | Low and favourable intermediate risk | 27 Gy in 2 or 34–38 Gy in 4 (single 19 Gy fraction fails) |
| Quality | Post-implant CT: **D90** (dose covering 90% of gland) **>90%** of prescription | Real-time planning |

- **Technique:** TRUS (transrectal ultrasound)-guided perineal template; peripheral loading spares the urethra.
- **Boost** (EBRT 45 Gy + implant) for unfavourable intermediate/high risk: **ASCENDE-RT** halved biochemical failure, with more grade 3 urinary toxicity (18% vs 5%). **RTOG 0232:** adding EBRT to implant gave no benefit in intermediate risk.
- **Contraindications:** IPSS (International Prostate Symptom Score) >20, Qmax <10 mL/s, recent TURP (transurethral resection of the prostate), pubic arch interference, prior pelvic RT, inflammatory bowel disease.

### ADT (androgen deprivation therapy) with RT
- **Why:** ADT radiosensitises (less DNA repair) and causes apoptosis; RT up-regulates androgen-receptor signalling, which ADT blocks.

| Risk group | ADT with EBRT |
|---|---|
| Low, favourable intermediate | None |
| Unfavourable intermediate | **4–6 months** |
| High/locally advanced | **18–36 months** (EORTC 22863, PCS IV); add **abiraterone** if very high risk (STAMPEDE) |

- **RT added to ADT** improves survival in locally advanced disease: **SPCG-7, NCIC PR.3/MRC PR07** (about 8–10% absolute).

### After radical prostatectomy
- **Adjuvant RT** (pT3/positive margin): EORTC 22911, ARO 96-02 (biochemical), **SWOG 8794** (metastasis-free and overall survival).
- **RADICALS-RT, RAVES, GETUG-AFU 17** (ARTISTIC meta-analysis): **early salvage at PSA (prostate-specific antigen) 0.1–0.2 ng/mL** equals adjuvant RT and spares over 60% of men RT.
- Salvage dose ≥64–66 Gy. **RTOG 9601:** 2 years bicalutamide improved overall survival. **SPPORT:** pelvic nodes + short ADT improve freedom from progression.

### Advanced and metastatic disease
| Setting | RT role | Evidence |
|---|---|---|
| Low-volume de novo mHSPC (metastatic hormone-sensitive) | **RT to prostate** + ADT | **STAMPEDE arm H**: overall survival HR (hazard ratio) 0.68 (3-year 73% → 81%); none in high volume |
| Oligometastases (≤3–5) | SBRT to all lesions (MDT, metastasis-directed therapy) | STOMP, **ORIOLE** (less progression), SABR-COMET |
| Painful bone metastases | Palliative EBRT, e.g. **8 Gy single fraction**; cord compression | Equal pain relief to multiple fractions |
| mCRPC (metastatic castration-resistant), bone-only symptomatic | **Radium-223** (α-emitter, calcium mimic, range <100 μm) 50 kBq/kg 4-weekly × 6 | **ALSYMPCA**: overall survival 14.9 vs 11.3 months; fewer skeletal events |
| mCRPC, PSMA-positive, after taxane and ARPI (androgen-receptor pathway inhibitor) | **¹⁷⁷Lu-PSMA-617** (β-emitter radioligand) | **VISION**: survival 15.3 vs 11.3 months (HR 0.62); **TheraP**: better PSA response than cabazitaxel |

- **Never combine radium-223 with abiraterone** (more deaths and fractures).
- **Also know:** protons (no proven gain over IMRT); strontium-89, samarium-153 (pain palliation); elective pelvic nodal RT (RTOG 94-13, GETUG-01: no survival gain).

### Response assessment
- **Phoenix failure:** PSA nadir + 2 ng/mL. **Benign bounce** after brachytherapy in about a third: watch, do not salvage.
- Biopsy-proven local failure: salvage prostatectomy, brachytherapy, HIFU (high-intensity focused ultrasound) or cryotherapy.

### Toxicity
| Type | Features and management |
|---|---|
| Acute (weeks) | Frequency, dysuria, diarrhoea; α-blockers |
| Late GI (gastrointestinal) | **Radiation proctitis** at 6–18 months: sucralfate/steroid enemas, argon plasma coagulation, hyperbaric oxygen |
| Late GU (genitourinary) | Cystitis, haematuria, stricture |
| Sexual | ED (erectile dysfunction), vascular; worse with ADT; PDE5 (phosphodiesterase type 5) inhibitors work in most |
| Rare | Second cancers, fistula |

### Recent advances
- **Hydrogel rectal spacer** (late rectal toxicity 2% vs 7%); MR-linac adaptive SBRT; **FLAME** focal boost to the MRI lesion; PSMA radioligands moving earlier (PSMAfore).

**Diagram to draw:** TRUS-guided perineal template brachytherapy.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - α/β ~1.5 Gy: basis of 60 Gy/20 (CHHiP) and SBRT 36.25 Gy/5.
> - ADT 4–6 months unfavourable intermediate; 18–36 months high risk.
> - Early salvage at PSA 0.1–0.2 = adjuvant (RADICALS, RAVES, GETUG-AFU 17).
> - STAMPEDE H: prostate RT for low-volume M1; ALSYMPCA, VISION for mCRPC.

## Discuss advances in management of CA prostate
**Type:** LAQ (25 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 5 Paper 4, Q2
(related: 2025-Winter Paper 3, Q3b; 2024-Winter Paper 4, Q3f)

### Introduction
- Three aims drive recent change: **find significant cancer and avoid diagnosing insignificant cancer**; **match local treatment to risk** with less morbidity; give **earlier, intensified and targeted** systemic therapy.

### 1. Screening and diagnosis
- **ERSPC (European Randomized Study of Screening for Prostate Cancer)** and Göteborg-1: PSA (prostate-specific antigen) screening lowers cancer-specific mortality, but PSA alone causes negative biopsies and overdiagnosis of GG (Grade Group) 1. Hence risk-stratified pathways.
- **Biomarkers** (reflex tests before biopsy):

| Test | Sample | Content |
|---|---|---|
| PHI (Prostate Health Index) | Blood | (p2PSA ÷ free PSA) × √total PSA |
| 4Kscore | Blood | Four kallikreins + clinical data; % risk of GG ≥2 |
| STHLM3 | Blood | 4Kscore kallikreins + 232 risk SNPs (single-nucleotide polymorphisms) + clinical variables |
| PCA3 (prostate cancer antigen 3), SelectMDx | Urine after DRE (digital rectal examination) | RNA ratio (PCA3); DLX1 and HOXC6 (SelectMDx) |
| ExoDx IntelliScore | Urine, no DRE | Exosomal RNA of PCA3, ERG, SPDEF |

- Tissue genomic classifiers (Decipher, Prolaris, Oncotype DX) refine risk for surveillance and adjuvant decisions.
- **MRI-first pathway:** mpMRI (multiparametric magnetic resonance imaging), scored by **PI-RADS (Prostate Imaging–Reporting and Data System)** 1–5, before biopsy.
  - **PROMIS (2017):** mpMRI sensitivity 93% vs 48% for TRUS (transrectal ultrasound) biopsy; NPV (negative predictive value) 89%; 27% could avoid biopsy.
  - **PRECISION (2018):** MRI with targeted biopsy of PI-RADS ≥3 found more significant cancer (38% vs 26%); 28% avoided biopsy.
  - So PI-RADS ≥3 lesions are biopsied, while PI-RADS 1–2 with low PSA density may safely avoid biopsy.
- **Transperineal biopsy** under local anaesthesia: near-zero sepsis; MRI–ultrasound fusion targeting. **Micro-ultrasound** (PRI-MUS scoring) matches mpMRI in registry data.
- **PSMA (prostate-specific membrane antigen) PET (positron emission tomography):** **proPSMA:** accuracy 92% vs 65% for CT (computed tomography) plus bone scan (sensitivity 85%, specificity 98%). Used for high-risk staging, biochemical recurrence and selecting radioligand therapy.

### 2. Localised disease
- **AS (active surveillance):** standard for GG1; extended to low-volume favourable intermediate risk (GG2), with MRI and PSA density. **ProtecT (15 years):** prostate cancer mortality about 2–3% in monitoring, surgery and radiotherapy arms; more metastasis on monitoring, no survival difference.
- **Robotic RP (radical prostatectomy):** **Retzius-sparing** (Bocciardi) gives earlier continence (1 week to 3 months; equal by 12 months); single-port robot; frozen-section-guided nerve sparing (NeuroSAFE).
- **Radiotherapy:**
  - Moderate hypofractionation **60 Gy in 20** (CHHiP) is standard.
  - **SBRT (stereotactic body radiotherapy) 36.25 Gy in 5:** PACE-B showed similar late toxicity at 2 years (chapter); its 5-year report (2024) showed non-inferior biochemical or clinical control. HYPO-RT-PC (42.7 Gy in 7) was also non-inferior.
  - **FLAME:** MRI-lesion boost improved biochemical PFS (progression-free survival) (HR [hazard ratio] 0.45). **Hydrogel rectal spacer** reduces rectal toxicity.
  - High-risk M0: **abiraterone added to ADT (androgen deprivation therapy) and RT (STAMPEDE):** MFS (metastasis-free survival) HR 0.53.
- **Focal therapy:** based on the **index lesion hypothesis** (the largest or highest-grade focus drives progression). Energies: HIFU (high-intensity focused ultrasound), cryotherapy, IRE (irreversible electroporation), focal laser, VTP (vascular-targeted photodynamic therapy, padeliporfin). **HEAT registry (1379 men):** failure-free survival 68% (intermediate risk); continence 90–95%. Still within trials or registries.

### 3. Recurrent and advanced disease
| Setting | Advance | Evidence |
|---|---|---|
| High-risk BCR (biochemical recurrence) | PSMA-guided salvage; enzalutamide + leuprolide | **EMBARK:** MFS HR 0.42 |
| mHSPC (metastatic hormone-sensitive) | ADT + ARPI (androgen receptor pathway inhibitor) | LATITUDE, TITAN, ENZAMET, ARCHES |
| | Triplet (ADT + docetaxel + ARPI) | **PEACE-1, ARASENS** |
| | Prostate RT, low volume | STAMPEDE arm H |
| | MDT (metastasis-directed therapy) | STOMP, ORIOLE |
| nmCRPC (non-metastatic castration-resistant) | Apalutamide, enzalutamide, darolutamide | SPARTAN, PROSPER, ARAMIS |
| HRR (homologous recombination repair)-mutated mCRPC | **PARP (poly-ADP-ribose polymerase) inhibitors** | **PROfound:** olaparib imaging PFS 7.4 vs 3.6 months; PROpel, MAGNITUDE, TALAPRO-2 |
| PSMA-positive mCRPC | **¹⁷⁷Lu-PSMA-617** | **VISION:** OS (overall survival) 15.3 vs 11.3 months (HR 0.62); TheraP: PSA response 65% vs 37% vs cabazitaxel |

- **ADT alone is no longer standard** for mHSPC; every fit man gets intensification.
- **Germline and somatic testing** for all metastatic disease; **pembrolizumab** for MSI-high (microsatellite instability-high) tumours.
- **Relugolix:** oral GnRH (gonadotropin-releasing hormone) antagonist; HERO showed better sustained castration than leuprolide.
- **Also know:** radium-223 (ALSYMPCA, OS HR 0.70); PSMAfore (lutetium before chemotherapy); AI in MRI reading and pathology.

**Diagram to draw:** MRI-first pathway: raised PSA → mpMRI → PI-RADS ≥3 targeted transperineal biopsy; PI-RADS 1–2 with low PSA density → surveillance.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - PROMIS and PRECISION: MRI before biopsy finds more significant cancer and avoids biopsies.
> - proPSMA: PSMA PET accuracy 92% vs 65%.
> - ProtecT: no mortality difference at 15 years; AS is standard for GG1.
> - VISION (Lu-PSMA, HR 0.62); PEACE-1 and ARASENS triplets; EMBARK.

# Section 17 · Prostate Cancer — Short answer questions (SAQ)

## Cryotherapy for carcinoma prostate
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2015-Summer Paper 4, Q3f

### Principle
- **Transperineal, image-guided** freezing; suits **anterior** tumours. HIFU (high-intensity focused ultrasound) is the main alternative.
- **Joule–Thomson effect**: **argon** freezes, **helium** thaws. Thermocouples guard sphincter and neurovascular bundles; a **urethral warmer** prevents sloughing.
- Generations: liquid nitrogen without imaging; TRUS (transrectal ultrasound) plus warmer; argon/helium 17-gauge probes; focal with MRI (magnetic resonance imaging) fusion.

### Cell death
- **Intra- and extracellular ice**, recrystallisation, **vascular stasis and ischaemia**, apoptosis, immune effects.
- **−40 °C**, fast freeze, slow thaw, **two cycles**; the ice-ball edge is sublethal.

### Indications
1. **Salvage after radiotherapy** (main role): biopsy-proven local recurrence, no metastases, **PSA (prostate-specific antigen) <10 (ideally <4)**, doubling time ≥16 months.
2. **Primary whole-gland**: men unfit for surgery or radiotherapy.
3. **Focal or hemi-gland**: MRI-visible grade group 2–3 lesion; rests on the index-lesion hypothesis.

### Technique
- TRUS-guided transperineal template: place cryoprobes, thermocouples and urethral warmer; freeze anterior to posterior until −40 °C is reached; helium thaw; repeat the cycle.

### Outcomes
- Salvage: biochemical control **55–71% at 3–5 years** (Phoenix: nadir + 2).
- **Donnelly, Cancer 2010** (RCT (randomised controlled trial)): **non-inferior** to external beam radiotherapy; worse sexual function.

### Complications
| | Primary / focal | Salvage |
|---|---|---|
| **ED (erectile dysfunction)** | **Almost always** (whole gland) | Very common |
| Incontinence | Rare | Single digit |
| **Rectourethral fistula** | ≤0.8% (focal) | 1–3.4% |
| Retention | Commonest after focal | ~4% |

- **Also know:** urethral sloughing, stricture; focal ablation preserves erections better (COLD (Cryo On-Line Data) registry).

| | Cryotherapy | HIFU | IRE (irreversible electroporation) |
|---|---|---|---|
| Energy | Freezing | Focused ultrasound heat (≥55 °C) | Electric pulses, non-thermal |
| Route | Transperineal | Transrectal | Transperineal |
| Best for | Anterior lesions | Posterior lesions | Lesions near nerves, vessels |

### Guidelines and advances
- **EAU (European Association of Urology)**: primary or focal only in trials or registries. PSMA (prostate-specific membrane antigen) PET selects salvage patients.

**Diagram to draw:** prostate with ice ball, −40 °C isotherm inside 0 °C edge.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Argon freezes, helium thaws; −40 °C, two cycles, urethral warmer.
> - Main role is salvage: PSA <10, doubling time ≥16 months.
> - Whole-gland ED near universal; fistula ≤0.8% focal, up to 3.4% salvage.

## Saturation biopsies of prostate
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2016-Summer Paper 4, Q3a

### Definition
- Systematic prostate biopsy with **more cores than the extended 12-core scheme**, usually **18–21 or more** (often about 20–30). It samples the peripheral zone, transition zone, anterior gland and apex.
- Route: transrectal, or **transperineal template mapping biopsy** through a brachytherapy grid with a core every **5 mm**.

### Rationale
- Sextant biopsy (Hodge, 1989) under-sampled the lateral peripheral zone. The 12-core scheme (adds apical and far-lateral cores) is today's standard.
- Saturation aims to cut sampling error, mainly for **anterior and apical cancers** (anterior-only cancer 6–20%).

### Indications
- **Repeat biopsy** after a negative biopsy with persistent suspicion: rising PSA (prostate-specific antigen), ASAP (atypical small acinar proliferation), negative or unavailable MRI (magnetic resonance imaging).
- **Active surveillance** confirmation and **focal therapy** mapping (shows multifocality).

### Templates and technique
| Template | Division | Cores |
|---|---|---|
| Original Barzell | Octants (anterior/posterior × apex/base, each side) | 4–8 per octant |
| Modified Barzell | Apex and base of each side × 5 sectors | 20 |
| Ginsburg | Anterior, mid, posterior sectors per side | 4 per sector |

- Lithotomy, stepper and grid; general or spinal anaesthesia, or periprostatic block (up to 22 mL of 1% lidocaine transrectally).

### Results
- **Initial biopsy**: 12 to 18–21 cores did not raise detection.
- **Repeat setting**: early series gained; a large prospective study did not. Extra cancers are often insignificant (overdiagnosis).
- **PROMIS (2017)** used 5-mm template mapping as the reference: mpMRI (multiparametric MRI) sensitivity 93% vs TRUS (transrectal ultrasound) biopsy 48%.

### Complications
- More haematuria, haematospermia, pain and **urinary retention** than standard biopsy; anaesthesia and theatre time. Transperineal route has less infection.

### Current position
- Largely replaced by **mpMRI with targeted biopsy**: **PRECISION (2018)** found more significant cancers (38% vs 26%) and 28% avoided biopsy.
- **EAU (European Association of Urology) 2023** prefers the transperineal route. Saturation remains for MRI-negative high suspicion and focal therapy mapping.

**Diagram to draw:** transperineal grid over the prostate with sampling sectors.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Saturation = >12 cores (18–21 or more); 5-mm template mapping.
> - No gain at initial biopsy; used after negatives.
> - PROMIS: template mapping as reference; mpMRI sensitivity 93%.

## Prostate cancer prevention trials
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2017-Summer Paper 4, Q3a

### Rationale
- Prostate cancer is common, slow and androgen-dependent. Men with 5α-reductase deficiency have rudimentary prostates. This supported **5ARI (5α-reductase inhibitor)** trials.

### Landmark trials
| | PCPT (2003) | REDUCE (2010) | SELECT (2009) |
|---|---|---|---|
| Agent | Finasteride 5 mg | Dutasteride 0.5 mg | Selenium 200 µg ± vitamin E 400 IU |
| Men | 18,882; PSA (prostate-specific antigen) ≤3 | PSA 2.5–10, prior negative biopsy | 35,533 |
| Design | 7 years; end-of-study biopsy | Biopsy at 2 and 4 years | Stopped early for futility |
| All cancers | ↓24.8% | ↓22.8% | No benefit |
| High grade | Gleason 7–10 ↑ (6.4% vs 5.1%) | Gleason 8–10 ↑ (0.9% vs 0.6%) | Vitamin E alone ↑ risk |

### The high-grade controversy
- Proposed artefacts: better PSA performance and a smaller gland make cancer easier to find.
- The FDA (Food and Drug Administration) did **not accept** these. In 2011 it refused a prevention licence and added a **high-grade warning** to 5ARI labels.
- PCPT at 18.4 years: non-significant 25% fewer prostate cancer deaths. Real-world data show no mortality effect.
- **Conclusion:** 5ARIs reduce detection of low-grade cancer, not death.

### Vitamins and other agents
- **ATBC** trial: low-dose vitamin E cut prostate cancer 32% in smokers (secondary end point). SELECT then failed.
- **VITAL** (vitamin D): no prostate effect. High-dose zinc, calcium and antioxidants may harm.
- **Aspirin/NSAIDs** (non-steroidal anti-inflammatory drugs): small effect. **Statins:** observational benefit only. **Metformin:** none.
- Diet: whole foods; avoid obesity.

### Current position
- No agent is approved for prevention. 5ARIs may be discussed with men taking them for BPH (benign prostatic hyperplasia).

**Diagram to draw:** bar chart: all cancers ↓ vs high-grade ↑ in PCPT and REDUCE.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - PCPT ↓24.8%; REDUCE ↓22.8%; both ↑ high grade.
> - FDA warning; no approved preventive agent.
> - SELECT negative; vitamin E alone harmful.

## GnRH (LHRH) analogues: agonists and antagonists
**Asked as:**
- GNRH Analogue — *Winter 2017 Paper IV Q3c (SAQ)*
- LHRH agonist — *Summer 2019 Paper IV Q3b (SAQ)*

**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)

*Read as: GnRH (gonadotrophin-releasing hormone, also LHRH, luteinising hormone-releasing hormone) agonists and antagonists, chiefly in prostate cancer.*

### Physiology
- **LHRH (luteinising hormone-releasing hormone, GnRH (gonadotrophin-releasing hormone))** from the hypothalamus is released in **pulses**. It (**pulsatile** GnRH) drives (stimulates) pituitary LH (luteinising hormone) and FSH (follicle-stimulating hormone). LH → Leydig cells → (Leydig-cell) testosterone (about 95% testicular; about 95% of total).
- **Continuous** exposure (stimulation) **desensitises and down-regulates** the pituitary receptor. This is the basis of agonist therapy.

### GnRH (LHRH) agonists
#### Drugs
| Drug | Formulation |
|---|---|
| Leuprolide | Depot 1, 3, 6 monthly |
| Goserelin | SC (subcutaneous) implant 1 or 3 monthly |
| Triptorelin | IM (intramuscular) depot |
| Histrelin | Implant, 1 year |
| Buserelin | Nasal or SC |

- Substitution at **position 6** of the decapeptide (position-6 substitution) raises potency and half-life; depots last 1 month to 1 year.

#### Mechanism
```
Agonist → initial LH surge (flare, testosterone up)
        → receptor down-regulation (1–2 weeks)
        → LH falls → castrate testosterone in 2–4 weeks
```
- Initial LH surge, then receptor **down-regulation** → low LH → castration in 2–4 weeks; survival **equals orchidectomy**.

#### Flare phenomenon
- LH rises up to **10-fold for 10–20 days** → bone pain, **spinal cord compression**, urinary retention (pain).
- Dangerous with spinal or weight-bearing metastases.
- **Prevent with an antiandrogen** (e.g. bicalutamide, flutamide) started with or before the agonist, for **21–28 days**.
- Impending cord compression → **antagonist** (degarelix) or orchidectomy instead.

### GnRH antagonists
- Immediate competitive receptor block: LH falls 84% in 24 hours, **no flare**, FSH falls.
- **Degarelix** (SC (subcutaneous) monthly): castrate by day 3 in >95%; injection-site reactions.
- **Relugolix** (oral daily, FDA 2020). **HERO** vs leuprolide: sustained castration 97% vs 89%; faster recovery after stopping; **fewer MACE (major adverse cardiovascular events)** (about 3% vs 6%).
- Preferred for **impending cord compression** or severe bone pain when orchidectomy is unsuitable.
- **Also know:** abarelix restricted (severe allergy); PRONOUNCE (degarelix vs leuprolide, stopped early) showed no MACE difference.

### Agonist vs antagonist
| Agonist | Antagonist |
|---|---|
| Flare; needs antiandrogen | No flare; castrate by day 3 (degarelix) |
| FSH rises | FSH falls |
| Depot to yearly | Monthly SC or oral (relugolix; HERO) |

### Castrate level
- **<50 ng/dL** (traditional); **<20 ng/dL** is achievable (orchidectomy median about 15) and may be better (aim **<20 ng/dL**). Check testosterone when PSA (prostate-specific antigen) rises.

### Indications in urology
- Metastatic hormone-sensitive prostate cancer (with ARPI (androgen receptor pathway inhibitor) or docetaxel).
- With radiotherapy for intermediate/high-risk and locally advanced disease.
- Recurrent prostate cancer: BCR (biochemical recurrence) with short doubling time; continued in CRPC (castration-resistant prostate cancer).
- **Also know:** BPH (benign prostatic hyperplasia) volume reduction (not routine); central precocious puberty; cryptorchidism (GnRH or hCG (human chorionic gonadotrophin): low success, not first-line); endometriosis; intermittent ADT (androgen deprivation therapy) acceptable (PR.7).

### Adverse (side) effects
- Hot flushes (50–80%), loss of libido, ED (erectile dysfunction).
- **Osteoporosis** (DEXA (dual-energy X-ray absorptiometry); calcium, vitamin D, denosumab or zoledronic acid).
- Diabetes, cardiovascular events, sarcopenia, anaemia, gynaecomastia (≤5%), cognitive change.
- Slow testosterone recovery after stopping.

**Diagram to draw:** hypothalamic–pituitary–testis axis showing agonist and antagonist sites, and the agonist site and flare.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Agonist: surge, then down-regulation; continuous stimulation → down-regulation → castration in 2–4 weeks.
> - Flare 10–20 days; cover flare with antiandrogen 21–28 days.
> - Antagonist: no flare; degarelix castrate by day 3; relugolix oral (HERO, fewer MACE).
> - Castrate <50 ng/dL; aim <20 ng/dL.

## Combined Androgen Blockade.
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2018-Summer Paper 4, Q3f

### Definition and rationale
- **CAB (combined androgen blockade)**, also called maximal or total androgen blockade: castration (orchidectomy or LHRH (luteinising hormone-releasing hormone) agonist) **plus a non-steroidal antiandrogen**.
- **Rationale (Labrie):** castration removes ~90–95% of testosterone, but **adrenal androgens** (DHEA, dehydroepiandrosterone; androstenedione) are converted to DHT (dihydrotestosterone) inside the prostate. An antiandrogen blocks these at the AR (androgen receptor).

### Antiandrogens
| Drug | Dose | Signature toxicity |
|---|---|---|
| Flutamide | 250 mg three times daily | Diarrhoea, hepatotoxicity |
| Nilutamide | 300 mg then 150 mg daily | Delayed dark adaptation, pneumonitis |
| **Bicalutamide** | **50 mg daily** (with LHRH agent) | Best tolerated; gynaecomastia |
| Cyproterone (steroidal) | 100 mg two to three times daily | Cardiovascular, hepatic; not used for CAB now |

- Class effects: gynaecomastia, hepatotoxicity, pneumonitis.

### Evidence
- **PCTCG (Prostate Cancer Trialists' Collaborative Group) meta-analysis, 2000** (27 trials, ~8,000 men): with non-steroidal antiandrogens, 5-year survival improved by only **~2–3%** (borderline significance). Cyproterone gave no benefit (slightly worse).
- So the benefit is small, with more cost and toxicity. **AUA (American Urological Association): long-term CAB not recommended**.

### Current uses
1. **Flare prevention:** an LHRH agonist raises LH (luteinising hormone) and testosterone for 10–20 days, risking cord compression, pain or obstruction. Start antiandrogen **with or before** the agonist for **21–28 days**. (Antagonists such as degarelix or relugolix cause no flare.)
2. **Antiandrogen withdrawal:** on progression, stopping bicalutamide gives PSA (prostate-specific antigen) fall >50% in 15–30% (mutant AR made the drug an agonist).

### Modern intensification (replaces CAB in mHSPC, metastatic hormone-sensitive prostate cancer)
| Trial | Addition to ADT (androgen deprivation therapy) | Result |
|---|---|---|
| LATITUDE, STAMPEDE (arm G) | Abiraterone + prednisone | OS (overall survival) HR (hazard ratio) 0.62 / 0.63 |
| ENZAMET | Enzalutamide (vs ADT + older antiandrogen) | OS HR 0.67 |
| ARCHES | Enzalutamide | Radiographic PFS (progression-free survival) HR 0.39 |
| TITAN | Apalutamide | OS HR 0.67 |
| PEACE-1, ARASENS | **Triplet**: + docetaxel + abiraterone or darolutamide | OS benefit (ARASENS HR 0.68) |

**Diagram to draw:** testis and adrenal androgen sources, with LHRH agonist and antiandrogen sites of action.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - CAB = castration + non-steroidal antiandrogen (adrenal androgens).
> - PCTCG: ~2–3% 5-year survival gain only.
> - Main use now: flare cover for 21–28 days.
> - ARPIs (androgen receptor pathway inhibitors) or triplets now standard in mHSPC.

## PSMA PET Ct scan
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2019-Summer Paper 4, Q3a

### Principle
- **PSMA (prostate-specific membrane antigen):** transmembrane glycoprotein over-expressed by prostate cancer, rising with grade.
- **PET/CT (positron emission tomography/computed tomography):** positron-labelled ligand binds PSMA; uptake as SUV (standardised uptake value).
- **Tracers:** ⁶⁸Ga-PSMA-11 (half-life 68 min); ¹⁸F-DCFPyL, ¹⁸F-rhPSMA-7.3, ¹⁸F-PSMA-1007 (110 min, better images; PSMA-1007 little urinary excretion).
- **Specificity >90%**; beats choline and fluciclovine.

### Indications
**1. Primary staging (high risk):**
- **proPSMA (2020):** accuracy **92% vs 65%** vs CT + bone scan.
- Nodal sensitivity only 30–40% against PLND (pelvic lymph node dissection): a negative scan does not exclude micrometastases.
- NCCN (National Comprehensive Cancer Network): may **replace** conventional imaging; AUA (American Urological Association): add-on.

**2. BCR (biochemical recurrence):**
- Detection rises with PSA (prostate-specific antigen): about **half positive below 0.5 ng/mL**, nearly all above 2.
- **Calais trial:** PSMA 56% vs fluciclovine 26%. **CONDOR:** management changed in 63.9%.
- Guides salvage radiotherapy (disease outside field in about 1 in 5).

**3. Intraprostatic detection:** **PRIMARY** trial: adding PSMA PET to MRI (magnetic resonance imaging) improved negative predictive value; 5-point PRIMARY score.

**4. Theranostics:** selects patients for **¹⁷⁷Lu-PSMA-617**. **VISION (2021):** mCRPC (metastatic castration-resistant prostate cancer) after ARPI (androgen receptor pathway inhibitor) and taxane: OS 15.3 vs 11.3 months.

### Reporting
- **PROMISE miTNM** (molecular imaging TNM): miT, miN, miM staging plus expression score (vs blood pool, liver, parotid). PSMA-RADS also used.

### Pitfalls
| False positive | False negative |
|---|---|
| Coeliac/stellate ganglia, fractures, Paget's disease, fibrous dysplasia | Small nodes (<5 mm) |
| Other tumours (RCC (renal cell carcinoma), HCC (hepatocellular carcinoma)) | PSMA-negative tumours (about 5–10%) |
| ¹⁸F-PSMA-1007 benign bone uptake | Neuroendocrine transformation; masking by bladder activity |

- **Also know:** no proof yet that PSMA-guided treatment improves survival (stage migration).

**Diagram to draw:** PSMA ligand binding the cell-surface antigen with fused PET/CT image of a nodal lesion.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - proPSMA: accuracy 92% vs 65%.
> - BCR: about half positive below PSA 0.5 ng/mL.
> - Nodal sensitivity 30–40%: negative scan does not exclude nodes.
> - VISION: ¹⁷⁷Lu-PSMA-617 improves OS in mCRPC.

## Methods to predict prognosis of patient of Cancer prostate
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2019-Summer Paper 4, Q3e

### 1. Clinical and pathological factors
- **PSA (prostate-specific antigen):** organ-confined about 80% at <4 but <50% at >10 ng/mL; density and velocity add information.
- **Clinical stage (DRE (digital rectal examination), TNM).**
- **Grade group (ISUP (International Society of Urological Pathology)) – strongest single factor:**

| Grade group | Gleason | 5-year BCR (biochemical recurrence)-free |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | ≤6 | 96% |
| 2 | 3+4 | 88% |
| 3 | 4+3 | 63% |
| 4 | 8 | 48% |
| 5 | 9–10 | 26% |

- Biopsy: % positive cores, **cribriform and intraductal** patterns, perineural invasion.
- Prostatectomy: margin (extent, grade), EPE (extraprostatic extension), seminal vesicle and node status.

### 2. Risk groups
| D'Amico | Low | Intermediate | High |
|---|---|---|---|
| PSA (ng/mL) | ≤10 | 10–20 | >20 |
| Gleason | ≤6 | 7 | 8–10 |
| Stage | T1–T2a | T2b | T2c |

- **NCCN (National Comprehensive Cancer Network):** very low, low, favourable/unfavourable intermediate, high, very high.
- **CAPRA (Cancer of the Prostate Risk Assessment)** score 0–10 (age, PSA, Gleason, stage, % cores); **CAPRA-S** after surgery.

### 3. Nomograms
- **Partin tables:** organ-confined disease, EPE, seminal vesicle, node involvement.
- **MSKCC (Memorial Sloan Kettering) nomograms:** pre- and post-treatment BCR and 15-year cancer-specific survival.
- **Briganti nomogram:** risk of nodal invasion → decides PLND (pelvic lymph node dissection).

### 4. Imaging
- mpMRI (multiparametric MRI): PI-RADS (Prostate Imaging Reporting and Data System) score, tumour contact length, EPE.
- PSMA PET (prostate-specific membrane antigen PET) for metastatic burden.

### 5. Tissue biomarkers
| Test | Basis | Use |
|---|---|---|
| **Decipher** | 22-RNA classifier | Metastasis risk; post-op radiotherapy |
| **Oncotype DX GPS** | 17 genes | Adverse pathology; active surveillance |
| **Prolaris** | Cell-cycle progression genes | Progression, cancer death |

- Germline BRCA2 (BReast CAncer gene 2): aggressive disease.

### 6. After treatment
- **PSA doubling time (PSADT):** <3 months highest risk; Pound: time to BCR <2 years, PSADT <10 months, Gleason 8–10 predict metastasis.
- **EAU (European Association of Urology) BCR risk groups:** low risk = PSADT >1 year and grade group <4.
- PSA nadir on ADT (androgen deprivation therapy); **Phoenix** failure (nadir + 2) after radiotherapy.

**Diagram to draw:** flowchart from risk group to nomogram, biomarker and treatment decision.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Grade group is the strongest predictor.
> - D'Amico, NCCN and CAPRA combine PSA, grade and stage.
